To understand the architecture of Elias and Catherine's quiet tragedy, one must first grasp the absolute poverty of their universe, a microscopic empire built not of gold, but of nine children and the relentless erosion of hope. They existed in a world where the sun rose and set with the same indifferent rhythm, yet for them, the turning of the earth meant the grinding of teeth and the hollowing of bellies. Their story is a testament to the obscure heroism of the forgotten, those whose names are erased from the grand annals of kings and conquerors, leaving behind only the faint, fossilized imprint of their struggle in the dust of history. While the great men of the fifteenth century carved their legacies in stone, Elias and Catherine were merely survivors, navigating a landscape where Fate dealt its cards with a cruel, uneven hand.

It was past the middle of the fifteenth century, an era defined by the iron fists of tyrants and the fractured hearts of nations. Louis XI ruled France with a calculating cruelty; Edward IV sat upon the throne of England, a rightful king in name but a usurper in the eyes of conscience; and across the channel, Philip "the Good," Duke of Burgundy, reigned over Holland with the cold efficiency of a machine that had ground down his cousin Jacqueline's spirit into dust. Into this storm of imperial ambition stepped our tale, beginning in the modest, cobblestoned town of Tergou, where the air smelled perpetually of wool, tanning hides, and the sharp tang of ambition.

Here lived Elias and Catherine, a couple of sturdy burghers whose prosperity was a fragile illusion sustained solely by the sheer volume of their offspring. They traded in cloth, silk, and the prized curried leather—a material so durable it could withstand twenty years of wear and the savage carving knives of a violent age. Yet, amidst the bustling commerce, their true currency was not silver, but the endless cycle of birth and loss. For nine years, their household had expanded like a fortress under siege, welcoming a child annually with prayers rather than protestations. In their youth, these children were divine playthings, sources of evening solace; but as the parents aged and the olive branches of their family tree shot upward, the reality of mortality set in. The massive loaves of bread, once sufficient for a dozen hungry mouths, now vanished from the trencher before dawn, leaving Elias and Catherine to stare at one another in the dim light of the kitchen, haunted by the question: *Who will find bread for them all when we are gone?*

The younger children, innocent of the economic calculus, viewed dinner as a natural law, as inevitable as the sunrise. But the elder sons, burdened by the weight of survival, bore the national thoughtfulness of their people. The conversation at the table shifted from laughter to a grim arithmetic of sustenance. "Kate, the children grow so, this table will soon be too small," Elias would murmur, his voice heavy with the dread of scarcity. Catherine, responding not to his words but to the terror in his eyes, would reply softly, "We cannot afford it, Eli." Their anxiety manifested in a peculiar, mortifying pride; they refused to let any drop of their blood descend into the lower classes, driven by a fierce desire to maintain their status as free burghers even in death.

Through a discipline born of necessity, they managed to clothe and feed their growing brood, accumulating a small hoard that brought them a secret, miser-like joy. But fortune, capricious as the winds of the North Sea, demanded payment. One day, the eldest surviving son, Richart, aged nineteen, approached his mother with a composure that masked a profound internal fracture. He begged to be sent to Amsterdam, to apprentice with a merchant. "Merchants are wealthy," he pleaded, his voice steady despite the tears gathering in his eyes. "I am good at numbers. Let me go, and I shall forever be your debtor."

Catherine threw up her hands, a gesture of dismay and disbelief. "Leave Tergou? Leave your poor father now he is no longer young?"

"There are enough in the house without me," Richart answered gravely, the resolve of granite hardening his features. "There will be one mouth less for you to feed."

In that moment, the first young bird tested its wings on the edge of the nest, trembling but determined to fly into a world that offered no guarantee of safety. Richart departed for Amsterdam with a face long and sad, never to cost his parents another penny, though fitting him out for the house of Vander Stegen consumed their entire savings save for a single gold crown.

The absence of Richart created a vacuum at the supper table that echoed louder than any shout. Elias, unable to bear the sight of the empty chair, would roughly command the remaining children to "sit wider," turning his head away to hide the tremor in his hands. Two years later, the pattern repeated itself. Jacob, following in his brother's footsteps, left for the city just after midday dinner. At supper, Elias whispered, "Sit wider, dears!" and Catherine, previously resolved not to grieve, broke down in sorrow. "Ay! the table will soon be too big for the children," she cried, her forced calm shattering into sobs. "'Tis the best that leave us," she sobbed, "that is the cruel part." Elias tried to comfort her with the stoic philosophy of their class, claiming that what God took was better than what He spared, dismissing women as silly and men as unthankful. But Catherine insisted, "Richart and Jacob were the flower of the flock," her grief refusing to be silenced by logic.

With the coffer emptied twice, the family descended into a frantic economy of ants. Elias adhered to the humble creed that "a penny saved is a penny gained," denying himself and his family the smallest luxuries until they could smile with a joy greater than indulgence ever could. In three more years, they had scraped together enough to establish their fourth son as a tailor and their eldest daughter as a robe-maker, providing for two more. Yet, the drain was inexorable. The shop bled in goods, and the coffers ran dry.

Now, two children remained unable to provide for themselves, and two were unwilling. Among the unable was Giles, a dwarf of a disturbing constitution—half stupidity, half malice, possessing only head, claws, and a shrill voice, rejected by dogs and women alike, yet fiercely protected by his mother. Beside him sat Little Catherine, a fragile child who moved on crutches, her face like marble, her eyes violet pools of suffering. She endured pain with a silent grace, offering no fretful complaints, a testament to a spirit too weary to complain.

Then there were the unwilling: Sybrandt, the youngest, lost in a dream of play that rendered him incapable of labor; and Cornelis, the eldest, who had calculated the odds and chosen to wait by the hearth for dead men's shoes. Worn out by the moral and physical infirmities of their remaining charges, Elias and Catherine often voiced their despair: "What will become of all these when we shall be no longer here to take care of them?" Yet, the domestic horizon would suddenly clear, and the refrain would return, half-mechanical now, punctuated by the bright, cheerful mention of Gerard.

Gerard was the anomaly, the son apart destined for the Church. In those days, the Church maintained her children by hook or by crook, regardless of merit or connections. Gerard's talents were not in theology, but in the trivialities of reading and penmanship. He was so absorbed in his craft that he often missed meals, carrying a tinder-box and brimstone matches to beg candle ends from neighbors, lighting them at unreasonable hours, even at eight o'clock at night in winter. While his family endured his nocturnal wanderings, the monks of a neighboring convent embraced him. They taught him penmanship, only to discover, in the midst of a lesson, that he was teaching them.

When confronted with this reversal of roles, Gerard blushed and hung his head, unsure of his own judgment. An elderly monk, however, saw potential beyond the script. "How is it," the monk asked gently, "that you, with an eye so true and a hand so subtle, do not color as well as you write? A scroll looks barren unless bordered by fruit, leaves, and rich arabesques... to say nothing of the pictures of holy men and women departed, which lift the heart by their effigies."

Confused and admitting his failures in illumination due to a lack of funds, Gerard's path changed with the arrival of a fellow-enthusiast in the guise of an old lady. Margaret, the sole survivor of the famous Van Eyck brothers, had fled Flanders to spend her final days in her native land. She purchased a small house near Tergou and, hearing of Gerard's talent, sent her servant, Reicht Heynes, to invite him.

Margaret and Reicht quickly turned Gerard inside out like a glove. They revealed the truth he had feared: he did not illuminate because he could not afford the gold, the blue, and the red, relying only on cheap earths, and he dared not ask his mother for the rest. With generosity that transcended mere charity, Margaret gifted him brushes loaded with gold leaf, vermilion, and ultramarine, along with sheets of fine vellum. As Gerard left her house, overwhelmed by gratitude, Reicht chased him with a candle and cheese, receiving kisses in return. But the greatest gift was not the pigment; it was the sympathy for the isolated enthusiast. In an age where the boundaries between fine art and mechanical craft were blurred, and where artists sought one another with a ferv

To truly comprehend the architecture of Gerard's early exile, one must first understand the absolute poverty of his universe at that precise moment. He existed within a microscopic empire of three, subjugated by hunger and the rigid, unforgiving laws of a world that offered no mercy. The setting was a cramped chamber, stifling and dim, where the very air seemed thick with the weight of their destitution. Yet, amidst this crushing atmosphere, a spark of defiance ignited—a small, defiant rebellion against the tyranny of starvation.

"The soup is hot," said Gerard, his voice cutting through the gloom with a clarity that belied his youth.

"But how are we to get it to our mouths?" inquired the senior, his voice trembling with despair, a despondency born of failing strength and the sheer physical impossibility of their situation.

"Father, the young man has brought us straws." And Margaret smiled, a sliver of cunning light breaking through the shadows.

"Ay, ay!" said the old man, his voice cracking with effort; "but my poor bones are stiff, and indeed the fire is too hot for a body to kneel over with these short straws. St. John the Baptist, but the young man is adroit!"

For, while he voiced this complaint, Gerard moved with the fluid precision of a man who understood that survival required more than mere presence—it demanded ingenuity. In a single, seamless motion, he untied the knot on his breast, removed his hat, and placed a stone into each corner of the brim. Wrapping his hand in the tail of his jerkin, he snatched the flask from the searing heat, wedging it securely between the stones to cool it rapidly, and then pressed the hat firmly under the old man's nose with a merry, triumphant smile. Trembling with anticipation, the elder inserted the pipe of rye-straw and drew.

Lo and behold, his wan, drawn face began to light up, the darkness receding inch by inch until it glowed with a renewed vitality. As soon as he had drawn a long, sustaining breath:

"Hippocrates and Galen!" he cried, his voice booming with unexpected vigor. "'Tis a *soupe au vin*—the restorative of restoratives. Blessed be the nation that invented it, and the woman that made it, and the young man who brings it to fainting folk. Have a suck, my girl, while I relate to our young host the history and virtues of this his sovereign compound. This corroborative, young sir, was unknown to the ancients: we find it neither in their treatises of medicine, nor in those popular narratives, which reveal many of their remedies, both in chirurgery and medicine proper. Hector, in the *Ilias*, if my memory does not play me false..."

"(Margaret. 'Alas! he's off.') ...was invited by one of the ladies of the poem to drink a draught of wine; but he declined, on the plea that he was just going into battle, and must not take aught to weaken his powers. Now, if the *soupe au vin* had been known in Troy, it is clear that in declining *vinum merum* upon that score, he would have added in the hexameter, 'But a *soupe au vin*, madam, I will degust, and gratefully.' Not only would this have been but common civility—a virtue no perfect commander is wanting in—but not to have done it would have proved him a shallow and improvident person, unfit to be trusted with the conduct of a war; for men going into a battle need sustenance and all possible support, as is proved by this, that foolish generals, bringing hungry soldiers to blows with full ones, have been defeated, in all ages, by inferior numbers. The Romans lost a great battle in the north of Italy to Hannibal, the Carthaginian, by this neglect alone. Now, this divine elixir gives in one moment force to the limbs and ardour to the spirits; and taken into Hector's body at the nick of time, would, by the aid of Phoebus, Venus, and the blessed saints, have most likely procured the Greeks a defeat. For note how faint and weary and heart-sick I was a minute ago; well, I suck this celestial cordial, and now behold me brave as Achilles and strong as an eagle."

"Oh, father, now? an eagle, alack!"

"Girl, I defy thee and all the world. Ready, I say, like a foaming charger, to devour the space between this and Rotterdam, and strong to combat the ills of life, even poverty and old age, which last philosophers have called the *summum malum*. Negatur; unless the man's life has been ill-spent—which, by the bye, it generally has. Now for the moderns!"

"Father! dear father!"

"Fear me not, girl; I will be brief, unreasonably and unseasonably brief. The *soupe au vin* occurs not in modern science; but this is only one proof more, if proof were needed, that for the last few hundred years physicians have been idiots, with their chicken-broth and their decoction of gold, whereby they attribute the highest qualities to that meat which has the least juice of any meat, and to that metal which has less chemical qualities than all the metals; mountebanks! dunces! homicides! Since, then, from these no light is to be gathered, go we to the chroniclers; and first we find that Duguesclin, a French knight, being about to join battle with the English—masters, at that time, of half France, and sturdy strikers by sea and land—drank, not one, but three *soupes au vin* in honour of the Blessed Trinity. This done, he charged the islanders; and, as might have been foretold, killed a multitude, and drove the rest into the sea. But he was only the first of a long list of holy and hard-hitting ones who have, by this divine restorative, been sustentated, fortified, corroborated, and consoled."

"Dear father, prithee add thyself to that venerable company ere the soup cools." And Margaret held the hat imploringly in both hands till he inserted the straw once more.

This act spared them the tedious enumeration of "modern instances," and gave Gerard an opportunity of telling Margaret how proud his mother would be that her soup had profited a man of learning.

"Ay! but," said Margaret, her voice softening with a mixture of affection and reproach, "it would like her ill to see her son give all and take none himself. Why brought you but two straws?"

"Fair mistress, I hoped you would let me put my lips to your straw, there being but two."

Margaret smiled, a flush rising to her cheeks. "Never beg that you may command," she said gently. "The straw is not mine, 'tis yours: you cut it in yonder field."

"I cut it, and that made it mine; but after that, your lip touched it, and that made it yours."

"Did it? Then I will lend it you. There—now it is yours again; your lip has touched it."

"No, it belongs to us both now. Let us divide it."

"By all means; you have a knife."

"No, I will not cut it—that would be unlucky. I'll bite it. There I shall keep my half: you will burn yours, once you get home, I doubt."

"You know me not. I waste nothing. It is odds but I make a hairpin of it, or something."

This answer, rather than provoking him, dashed the novice Gerard to fresh efforts, leaving him momentarily speechless. And now, the bread and soup being disposed of, the old scholar prepared to continue his journey. Then came a little difficulty: Gerard the adroit could not tie his ribbon again as Catherine had tied it. Margaret, after slily eyeing his efforts for some time, offered to help him; for at her age, girls love to be coy and tender, saucy and gentle, by turns, and she saw she had put him out of countenance but now. Then a fair head, with its stately crown of auburn hair, glossy and glowing through silver, bowed sweetly towards him; and, while it ravished his eye, two white supple hands played delicately upon the stubborn ribbon, and moulded it with soft and airy touches. Then a heavenly thrill ran through the innocent young man, and vague glimpses of a new world of feeling and sentiment opened on him. And these new and exquisite sensations Margaret unwittingly prolonged: it is not natural to her sex to hurry aught that pertains to the sacred toilet. Nay, when the taper fingers had at last subjugated the ends of the knot, her mind was not quite easy, till, by a manoeuvre peculiar to the female hand, she had made her palm convex, and so applied it with a gentle pressure to the centre of the knot—a sweet little coaxing hand-kiss, as much as to say, "Now be a good knot, and stay so." The palm-kiss was bestowed on the ribbon, but the wearer's heart leaped to meet it.

"There, that is how it was," said Margaret, and drew back to take one last keen survey of her work; then, looking up for simple approval of her skill, received full in her eyes a longing gaze of such ardent adoration, as made her lower them quickly and colour all over. An indescribable tremor seized her

To understand the architecture of Gerard Eliassoen's ascent, one must first confront the absolute poverty of his universe prior to this moment. He existed within a microscopic empire of two, subjugated by a man he was conditioned to call "Master"—a square-built, brutal artifact of a world he barely knew, roughly sixty years of age, though time on the island seemed measured only by the decay of his humanity. Now, however, the chains of servitude have been severed, replaced by the dizzying vertigo of proximity to royalty.

When the grave white-haired seneschal approached their table, his inquiry courteous yet laden with the weight of protocol, the atmosphere shifted instantly. Faces within hearing turned sharp, bending with a mixture of curiosity and venomous envy upon the man destined to walk beside a princess. Gerard rose to obey, the motion fluid despite the turmoil in his soul.

"I wager we shall not see you again," Margaret said calmly, though a flush betrayed her composure.

"That you will," was the reply, delivered with a confidence that bordered on arrogance. Then, leaning close, he whispered, "This is my good princess; but you are my queen." Turning to the crowd, he added aloud, "Wait for me, I pray you; I will presently return."

"Ay, ay!" Peter spoke simultaneously, awakening from his reverie.

With Gerard gone, the pair whose dress was so homely, yet who stood in the presence of the man summoned by the Princess, became the cynosure of neighboring eyes. Observing this spectacle, William Johnson stepped forward, feigning surprise to claim kinship.

"And to think that there was I at your backs, and you saw me not," he declared.

"Nay, cousin Johnson, I saw you long since," Margaret replied coldly, her tone cutting through the warmth of the reunion.

"You saw me, and spoke not to me?"

"Cousin, it was for you to welcome us to Rotterdam, as it is for us to welcome you at Sevenbergen. Your servant denied us a seat in your house."

"The idiot!" Johnson muttered, his face flushing purple. He sensed Margaret's keen edge and suspected her intent. Under the circumstances, wisdom dictated action over words. He insisted they accompany him immediately, vowing to demonstrate whether they were truly welcome to Rotterdam or not.

"Who doubts it, cousin? Who doubts it?" the scholar interjected.

Margaret thanked him graciously but demurred, citing a desire to hear the minstrels once more. Yet, in about a quarter of an hour, Johnson renewed his proposal, noting that many guests had departed. Only then did her true reason surface.

"It were ill manners to our friend; and he will lose us. He knows not where we lodge in Rotterdam, and the city is large, and we have parted company once already."

"Oh!" Johnson exclaimed. "We will provide for that. My young man, ahem! I mean my secretary, shall sit here and wait, and bring him on to my house: he shall lodge with me and with no other."

"Cousin, we shall be too burdensome."

"Nay, nay; you shall see whether you are welcome or not, you and your friends, and your friends' friends, if need be; and I shall hear what the Princess would with him."

Margaret felt a thrill of joy that Gerard might be lodged under the same roof as her, yet a slight misgiving clouded her happiness. "But if your young man should be thoughtless, and go play, and Gerard miss him?"

"He go play? Leave that spot where I put him, and bid him stay? Ho! Stand forth, Hans Cloterman."

A figure clad in black serge and dark violet hose arose, taking two precise steps to stand before them without moving a muscle—a solemn, precise young man, the very statue of gravity and starched propriety. At his aspect, Margaret, overwhelmed by joy, could hardly maintain her countenance. She whispered to Johnson, "I would put my hand in the fire for him. We are at your command, cousin, as soon as you have given him his orders."

Hans was instructed to sit at the table and await Gerard, conducting him to Ooster-Waagen Straet. He complied not with words, but by calmly occupying the indicated seat. Margaret, Peter, and William Johnson then withdrew together.

"And, indeed, it is time you were abed, father, after all your travel," Margaret said, a thought that had lingered in her mind throughout the evening.

Hans Cloterman sat waiting for Gerard, solemn and businesslike. Minutes flew by, exciting no impatience in that perfect young man. Johnson dismissed the notion of his secretary leaving his post or neglecting duty in pursuit of sport or youthful hilarity with a laugh of scorn.

As Gerard proved long in coming, the patient Hans, his employer's eye no longer upon him, improved the time by quaffing goblets of Corsican wine. The wine was strong, so was Cloterman's resolve; yet, after an hour, the model secretary imbibed the notion that Creation expected him to toast the health of all good fellows, and specifically the Duke of Burgundy present. With this view, he filled his bumper nine times, rising gingerly but solemnly and slowly. Having reached his full height, he instantly rolled upon the grass, goblet in hand, spilling the cold liquor on more than one ankle—their owners frisked, but not disturbing a single muscle in his own long face. In the total eclipse of reason, his visage retained its gravity, primness, and infallibility.

Meanwhile, the seneschal led Gerard through several passages to the door of the pavilion, where young noblemen, embroidered and feathered, sat sentinel, guarding the heir-apparent while playing cards by the red light of torches held by their servants. A whisper from the seneschal prompted one of them to rise reluctantly, staring at Gerard with haughty surprise before entering the pavilion. He presently returned, beckoning the pair, and led them through a passage or two, landing them in an ante-chamber where three more young gentlemen, feathered, furred, and embroidered like pieces of fancy work, were deep in that instructive and edifying branch of learning known as dice.

"You can't see the Princess—it is too late," said one.

Another followed suit: "She passed this way but now with her nurse. She is gone to bed, doll and all. Deuce—ace again!"

Gerard prepared to retire. The seneschal, with an incredulous smile, replied, "The young man is here by the Countess's orders; be so good as conduct him to her ladies."

On this, a superb Adonis rose with an injured look and led Gerard into a room where sat or lolloped eleven ladies, chattering like magpies. Two, more industrious than the rest, played cat's-cradle with fingers as nimble as their tongues. At the sight of a stranger, all tongues stopped like one piece of complicated machinery, and all eyes turned on Gerard, as if the same string that checked the tongues had turned the eyes. Gerard was ill at ease before, but this battery of eyes discountenanced him further, and down went his eyes to the ground. Then the cowards, finding, like hares running by ponds and frogs scuttling into water, that there was a creature they could frighten, giggled and enjoyed their prowess. A duenna said severely, "Mesdames!" and they were all abashed at once as though a modesty string had been pulled. This same duenna took Gerard and marched before him in solemn silence. The young man's heart sank, and he had half a mind to turn and run out of the place.

"What must princes be," he thought, "when their courtiers are so freezing? Doubtless they take their breeding from him they serve."

These reflections were interrupted by the duenna suddenly introducing him into a room where three ladies sat working, and a pretty little girl tuning a lute. The ladies were richly but not showily dressed. The duenna went up to the one hemming a kerchief and said a few words in a low tone. This lady then turned towards Gerard with a smile, beckoning him to come near. She did not rise, but she laid aside her work, and her manner of turning towards him, slight as the movement was, was full of grace, ease, and courtesy. She began a conversation at once.

"Margaret Van Eyck is an old friend of mine, sir, and I am right glad to have a letter from her hand, and thankful to you, sir, for bringing it to me safely. Marie, my love, this is the gentleman who brought you that pretty miniature."

"Sir, I thank you a thousand times," said the young lady.

"I am glad you feel her debtor, sweetheart, for our friend would have us to do him a little service in return."

"I will do anything on earth for him," replied the young lady with ardor.

"Anything on earth is nothing in the world," said the Countess of Charolois quietly.

"Well, then, I will—What would you have me to do, sir?"

Gerard had just discovered the nature of the high society in which he now found himself. "My sovereign demoiselle," he said gently, a little tremulously, "where there have been no pains, there needs no reward."

"But we must obey mamma. All the world must obey."

"That is true. Then, our demoiselle, reward me, if you will, by letting me hear the stave you were going

It was nearly four in the afternoon, the light slanting low and heavy across the cobblestones. Inside the workshop, Eli labored with the rhythm of a man bound to his trade, while his eldest and youngest sons wandered the streets beyond. Outside, the air hummed with a tension born of absence; Catherine and her diminutive, crippled daughter had already departed, driven by an anxious hope to scour the distant road for any sign of Gerard. Left alone in the sitting room was Giles, a figure of grotesque disproportion who served as both the room's guardian and its most unsettling ornament.

To understand the Hollanders, one must first discard the notion that they merely traded cloth or dyes. They claimed invention as their birthright—the press, the pigment, the very concept of liberty itself—but above all, centuries before the tales of this era began, they invented cleanliness. While the English gentry, clad in velvet jerkins and chicken-toed shoes, trampled upon floors of stale rushes—a foul receptacle for bones, rotting food, spittle, and the refuse of dogs and eggs—the hosier's chamber at Tergou gleamed with a defiant purity. Its floor was paved with Dutch tiles, glazed to such a luster and scrubbed with such constancy that they invited the touch of a diner's plate.

Dominating one wall was a single large window, its center framed by massive cross-stone work that rose in stark relief, mimicking a crucifix for the devout souls within. The panes were tiny, lozenge-shaped shards soldered together with strips of lead, a testament to medieval craftsmanship that remains visible even today in rural cottages. Within this sanctified space sat chairs of rude, primitive construction, save for one armchair of solid oak. Its back, rising at a rigid right angle to the seat, towered so high that a seated man's head hovered two feet below the apex, crowned with intricate carvings. Beside it stood a copper pail, waist-deep, filled with holy water, accompanied by a small besom to scatter the blessing. And looming over the long, narrow, massive oak table was Giles, clinging to the rim by his teeth, his eyes blazing with a predatory intensity, his claws raised like those of a pouncing vampire.

Nature, it seemed, had fashioned Giles not out of malice, but through a distracted hand. She had sculpted his head and torso with her usual diligence, yet her focus wavered, leaving the remainder to chaotic chance. The result was a human wedge, an inverted cone of flesh and bone. As the Roman poet Horace might have lamented regarding an amphora that failed to stand upright, Giles's center of mass was entirely absent. Bisected vertically, the upper half of his body would have outweighed the lower three times over. Yet, this very imbalance granted him powers that would have baffled the legendary Milo. His brawny arms, unburdened by the weight of his own torso, allowed him to scale vertical poles with the agility of a squirrel or hang suspended from a tree branch by a single hand, dangling like a fruit from its stem. Had he possessed the suction of a lizard's foot, he could have walked upon ceilings.

But this pocket-athlete found his greatest ecstasy in defying gravity atop the dinner table. He would grip the surface with both hands, swing wildly, and then, in a climax of pure delight, clamp his jaws shut on the wood. Releasing his hands, he would cling to the table edge with his teeth alone, holding on with the tenacity of a grim death. All joy, however elevating, is subject to interruption. Little Kate, spotting Sampsonet in this acrobatic tableau, froze in horror. Like her mother, her heart belonged to the stability of the furniture, not the twelve-mo gymnast performing upon it.

"Oh, Giles! How can you?" she cried. "Mother is here. Look at the dent you've made in the table."

"Go and tell her, little tale-bearer," snarled Giles, his voice thick with irritation. "You are the one destined for mischief."

"Am I?" Kate replied, her tone unnervingly calm. "That is news to me."

"The biggest troublemaker in Tergou," Giles grumbled, tightening his grip on the table.

"Oh, indeed!" Kate said dryly.

Having launched this unexpected barb without consequence, Giles settled back into his post. Kate sat quietly and began to weep. At that precise moment, her mother entered, and Giles hurled himself beneath the table, emerging only to glare from the shadows.

"What is to be done now?" the woman demanded sharply. Turning her experienced gaze from Kate to the dwarf, noting his contorted position and the sheepish flush on his face, she signaled a threat of corporal punishment.

"Nay, mother," the girl interjected softly. "It was but a foolish word Giles spoke. I had not noticed it at another time; but I was weary and consumed with worry for Gerard, you know."

Then, a faint voice echoed from the doorway. "Let no one be in care for me."

Gerard appeared, tottering into the room. Pale, dust-covered, and utterly drained, he stumbled amidst a chorus of uplifted hands, cries of delight, and mingled currents of curiosity and anxiety, collapsing exhausted into the nearest chair. The journey from Rotterdam, searching like a shadow for Margaret, and the arduous trek thereafter, had completely broken him. Yet, the elasticity of youth proved resilient; quickly, he became the gravitational center of an eager circle.

First, they demanded the details of the prizes. Gerard recounted how he had been granted access to view the competitors' works, displayed in an immense hall awaiting the judges' verdict.

"Oh, mother! Oh, Kate!" he exclaimed, his voice trembling with awe. "When I beheld the goldsmiths' creations, I felt as though I ought to have fallen to my knees. I scarcely believed that all the goldsmiths upon earth possessed such abundance of gold, silver, jewels, and such divine craft of design and facture. In truth, all the arts are sacred."

Seeking to delight the women, he proceeded to describe the reliquaries, feretories, chalices, crosiers, crosses, pyxes, monstrances, and other ecclesiastical wonders, alongside goblets, hanaps, watches, clocks, chains, and brooches, until their mouths watered with desire.

"But, Kate," he continued, his tone shifting to melancholy, "when I turned to the illuminated manuscripts from Ghent and Bruges, my heart sank. My own work was dirt by comparison. For the first minute, I could almost have wept; but I prayed for a purer spirit, and soon I was able to admire them, thanking God for those lovely works and for those skilled, patient craftsmen, whom I acknowledge as my masters. Well, the colored work was so breathtaking I forgot all about the black and white. But the following day, when the other prizes had been distributed, they came to the writing competition. Whose name do you suppose was called first?"

"Yours," Kate answered confidently.

The others scoffed at her presumption.

"You may well laugh," Gerard said gently, "but for all that, 'Gerard Eliassoen of Tergou' was the name the herald shouted. I stood stupefied; they pushed me forward. Everything swam before my eyes. I found myself kneeling on a cushion at the feet of the Duke. He spoke to me, but I was so fluttered I could not reply. Then, he placed his hand to his side—not drawing a glaive to sever my dull head, but granting me a gold medal, and here it is."

A roar erupted, followed by a scramble for possession.

"And then he gave me fifteen great, bright golden angels," Gerard declared triumphantly. "I had seen one before, but never handled one. Behold them."

"Oh, Gerard! Oh, Gerard!" the family cried out.

"There is one for you, our eldest; one for you, Sybrandt; one for you, Little Mischief; and two for thee, Little Lily, because God has afflicted thee; and one for myself, to purchase colors and vellum; and nine for her who nursed us all and risked the two crowns upon poor Gerard's hand."

The gold revealed their true natures. Cornelis and Sybrandt clutched their coins with alternating glares of greed and envy directed at Kate, who had secured two pieces. Giles seized his medal, rolled it across the pristine tiled floor, and gamboled after it. Kate set aside her crutches, sat down, and extended her small arms toward Gerard in a gesture of heavenly love and tenderness. The mother, initially stunned numbness by the shower of wealth falling upon her apron, suddenly broke into tears.

"Leave kissing him, Kate; he is my son, not yours," she cried, though her voice trembled. "Ah, Gerard! My boy! I have not loved you as you deserve."

Gerard threw himself to his knees beside her, and she flung her arms around him, weeping with overwhelming joy and pride upon his neck.

"Good lad! Good lad!" cried the hosier, moved to emotion. "I must go and tell the neighbors. Lend me the medal, Gerard; I will show it to my good friend Peter Buyskens. He is ever regaling me with tales of how his son Jorian won a tin mug for shooting at the butts."

"Ay, do, my man; and show Peter Buyskens one of the angels. Tell him there are fourteen

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten was a man of calculated artistry, a strategist who understood that the most effective trap is often disguised as an offer. He approached the novice with a bait that deliberately missed its mark, casting a line far wider than the hook he truly sought to set.

"The town records," he began, his voice smooth as polished oak, "are crabbedly written, the ink rusted by the slow corrosion of centuries." With a gesture that feigned benevolence, he extended the honor of transcription to Gerard. "You shall make them fair."

Gerard, ever pragmatic, inquired immediately of the wage. Ghysbrecht named a sum so meager it covered merely the cost of pens, ink, and parchment.

"But, Burgomaster," Gerard pressed, his tone sharpening, "what of my labor? Consider this a year's work."

"Your labor?" Ghysbrecht scoffed, a dismissive curl touching his lip. "Do you call the mere marking of parchment 'labor'? A little sweat goes to that, I trow."

"'Tis labor," Gerard countered, his stance firm despite the weight of the older man's presence, "and skilled labor to boot. In every craft, skill is valued higher than brute toil, regardless of the sweat shed. Furthermore, there is the matter of my time."

"Your time?" Ghysbrecht laughed softly, the sound devoid of warmth. "Why, what is time to you, at twenty-two?"

Fixing his eyes upon Gerard with a keen, predatory intensity designed to gauge the tremor in the boy's resolve, he delivered the blow. "Say, rather, you have grown idle. You are in love. Your body remains among these chanting monks, but your heart belongs to Peter Brandt and his red-haired girl."

"I know no Peter Brandt."

The denial only hardened Ghysbrecht's suspicion that the exorcist was playing a deeper, more dangerous game than he admitted.

"Ye lie!" Ghysbrecht roared, the mask of civility shattering. "Did I not find you at her elbow on the road to Rotterdam?"

"Aha!"

"And you were seen at Sevenbergen but the other day."

"Was I?"

"Aha! And at Peter's house."

"At Sevenbergen?"

"Aye, at Sevenbergen."

In the lexicon of modern intrigue, this maneuver constitutes a bold draw—a guess presented as undeniable fact, intended to force the subject's hand and reveal the truth through their reaction. The result of this psychological stratagem surpassed even the crafty Burgomaster's expectations.

Gerard sprang to his feet, seized by a strange, electric state of nervous excitation.

"Burgomaster," he said, his voice trembling not with fear, but with a fierce, defiant clarity, "I have not been to Sevenbergen these three years. I know not the names of those you claim to have seen me with, nor where they dwell. But since my time is precious—even if you choose to squander it—I bid you good day."

With a sudden, darting motion, he turned and fled, his eyes sparkling with a light that suggested not defeat, but revelation.

Ghysbrecht surged upward in a fit of huge, blinding ire, only to sink slowly back into his chair, the rage cooling into a colder, more calculating dread.

"He fears me not," he muttered, tracing the rim of his cup. "He knows something. If not everything, certainly enough to unsettle me."

Then, summoning his trusty servant with a hurried command, he almost dragged the man to the window.

"Do you see yon man?" Ghysbrecht cried, pointing a shaking finger toward the street below. "Haste! Follow him! But ensure he sees nothing of you. He is young, yes, but old in craft. Keep him in sight all day. Tell me whither he goes, and what he does."

Night had fallen when the servant returned, breathless and urgent.

"Well? Well?" Ghysbrecht demanded, leaning forward eagerly.

"Master," the servant replied, his voice low, "the young man went from here... straight to Sevenbergen."

Ghysbrecht let out a long, mournful groan that echoed in the silent room.

"To the house of Peter the Magician."

To truly comprehend the architecture of the human heart, one must first look into the deepest currents of the Rhine or the thickest waters of the Thames, painting the silt beneath rather than skimming the surface. Lower a bucket into the well of self-deception, and what rises must be the immortal truth, must it not? Yet, no son of Adam ever reads his own heart directly; such insight is merely a reflection, a sagacity borrowed from years of perusing the lives of others. Even armed with this reflected light, we can only decipher our own souls, never read them fluently. Thus, halfway to Sevenbergen, Gerard paused to interrogate his own spirit regarding his journey. His heart replied without hesitation: *"We go out of curiosity—to know why she spurned us, to prove our hearts remain unbroken, and to demonstrate our contentment with our honors and future benefice, needing neither her nor any ally of her fickle sex."*

Soon, Gerard located Peter Brandt's cottage. In the doorway sat a girl stitching with a needle, while a stalwart figure leaned upon a longbow, conversing with her. A sharp, unaccountable pang pierced Gerard at the sight of the man. He was past fifty, an old soldier whose reputation for shooting at the butts with admirable force and skill lingered in Gerard's memory. Within a heartbeat, the youth stood before them. Margaret looked up, dropping her work with a faint cry, her complexion shifting rapidly between pale and flushed. Yet, these fleeting signs of emotion were swiftly suppressed; she turned far colder and more indifferent than she would have if she had not already betrayed such agitation.

"What! Is it you, Master Gerard?" she asked, her tone laced with feigned surprise. "What on earth brings you here, I wonder?"

"I was passing by and saw you," Gerard replied, masking his turmoil. "So I thought I would give you good day and inquire after your father."

"My father is well. He will be here anon."

"Then I may as well stay till he comes."

"As you will. Good Martin, step into the village and tell my father here is a friend of his."

"And not of yours?"

"My father's friends are mine."

"That is doubtful," Margaret retorted, her eyes flashing. "It was not like a friend to promise to wait for me, and then make off the moment my back was turned. Cruel Margaret—you little know how I searched the town for you; how, for want of you, nothing was pleasant to me."

"These are idle words," she countered sharply. "If you had truly desired my father's company, or mine, you would have returned. There, I had a bed laid for you at my cousin's, and he would have made much of you. Who knows, I might have made much of you too. I was in the humour that day. You will not catch me in the same mind again, neither you nor any young man, I warrant me."

"Margaret, I came back the moment the Countess let me go; but you were not there."

"Nay, you did not," she insisted, "or you would have seen Hans Cloterman at our table; we left him specifically to bring you on."

"I saw no one there, only a drunken man who had just tumbled down."

"At our table? How was he clad?"

"Nay, I took little heed: in sad-coloured garb."

At this, Margaret's face gradually warmed, but she quickly assumed an air of incredulous severity, peppering Gerard with shrewd questions. He answered them all most loyally. Finally, the clouds parted, and they deduced the source of the misunderstanding. Then came a revulsion of tenderness, all the more potent because they had wronged one another; and following that, more dangerous still, came mutual confessions. Neither had been happy since; neither ever would have been happy but for this fortunate meeting.

Gerard spotted a manuscript of the Vulgate lying open on the table and pounced upon it like a hawk. Manuscripts were his delight, but before he could reach it, two white hands slammed flat upon the page, covering it, while a red face pressed over them.

"Nay, take away your hands, Margaret, that I may see where you are reading," Gerard pleaded softly. "I will read there too at home; so shall my soul meet yours in the sacred page. You will not? Nay, then I must kiss them away."

He kissed them so often that, overcome by sheer shame, they were forced to withdraw. Lo! the sacred book lay open to, *"An apple of gold in a setting of silver."*

"There, now," she exclaimed, "I had been hunting for it ever so long, and found it but even now—and to be caught!" With a touch of inconsistency, she pointed it out to Gerard with her white finger.

"Ay," he said, "but today it is all hidden in that great cap."

"It is a comely cap, I'm told by some."

"Maybe; but what it hides is beautiful."

"It is not: it is hideous."

"Well, it was beautiful at Rotterdam."

"Ay, everything was beautiful that day," she murmured, letting out a little sigh.

Now Peter entered, welcoming Gerard cordially and inviting him to stay for supper. Margaret disappeared, only to reappear moments later with her hair arranged in her silver net. She shot a glance that was half-arch, half-coy, gliding about them as she spread the supper, beaming with gaiety and happiness. In the cool evening, Gerard coaxed her outside; she objected, yet came. He urged her further along the road to Tergou; she declined, yet came. They strolled up and down, hand in hand. When he eventually had to depart, they pledged each other never to quarrel or misunderstand one another again, sealing the promise with a long, loving kiss. Gerard went home on wings.

From that day forward, Gerard spent most of his evenings with Margaret. Their attachment deepened on both sides until the hours they shared were the hours they truly lived; the rest of life they merely endured. At the outset of this profound bond, all went smoothly. Obstacles existed, but they seemed distant and trivial to the eyes of hope, youth, and love. The feelings and passions of so many others that this attachment threatened to thwart gave no warning smoke to reveal their volcanic nature and power. The course of true love ran smoothly, placidly, until it had drawn these two young hearts into its current forever. And then...

To witness the architecture of joy in Tergou requires understanding the sheer vibrancy of that specific morning, a day where the ordinary fabric of civic life was torn open by spectacle. Velvet sunlight, unwonted in its intensity, washed the cobblestones, while horses' hooves galloped with a metallic gleam that echoed through the narrow streets. Windows and balconies, usually reserved for quiet domesticity, were now studded with faces straining to catch every movement. The French Ambassador rode through the center of this tableau, not for diplomacy, but for sport, venturing into the neighboring forest. He was accompanied by a retinue that included several servants of the Duke of Burgundy, lent specifically to honor the foreign dignitary and minister to his pleasures. Among them rode the Duke's tumbler, a figure of grave, sedate majesty whose very presence rendered his more noble companions—men of higher station—to seem light, frivolous, and almost clownish. Yet, whenever the atmosphere grew too thick with respect and awe, the tumbler would roll off his horse in a manner so ignoble and absurd that even the stoic Ambassador was forced to burst into laughter. With a graceless agility, he would climb back aboard by the tail, playing his part in a pantomime of servitude that provoked mirth rather than reverence.

Trailing behind this procession rode a creature that commanded far greater attention: the Duke's leopard. Mounted on a Flemish steed of giant, prodigious power, a huntsman carried a long box strapped securely to his loins by cunningly devised harnesses. Upon this platform sat a bright leopard, crouching in anticipation, chained tightly to the rider. The populace pressed forward, mesmerized by the animal's glossy hide and spotted coat, eager to touch her or pull at her tail. But the huntsman's voice cut through the crowd with a terrible warning. "Beware!" he shouted. "In Antwerp, one man dared but throw a handful of dust at her, and the Duke made dust of him."

"Gramercy!" someone cried out.

"I speak truth," the huntsman confirmed. "The good Duke imprisoned him in a cell beneath the earth, and the rats cleaned the flesh from his bones in a single night. He served him right for molesting the poor beast."

A murmur of fear rippled through the Tergovians, shrinking instinctively from the idea of tickling the sovereign's leopard. Yet, the tension broke with the arrival of the final element of the parade: the Duke's giant. Standing seven feet four inches tall, a Hungarian brute, he brought up the rear. Like others of his kind, he possessed a treble, fluty voice of negligible power, a flaw he was entirely unaware of due to his vanity. As he passed, he caught sight of Giles perched atop a balcony. Stopping abruptly, the giant began to mock the dwarf.

"Hallo! Brother!" he squeaked, his voice piercingly high despite his massive frame. "I nearly passed without seeing thee."

"You are plain enough to see," Giles bellowed back, his bass tones vibrating with a depth that contrasted violently with the giant's shrill complaint.

"Come on my shoulder, brother," the giant squealed again, holding out a shoulder shaped like a fist of mutton to assist the smaller man down.

"If I do, I'll cuff your ears," roared Giles.

The giant, sensing the dwarf's irascibility and emboldened by the surrounding laughter, played upon him. He failed to perceive that the crowd laughed not at his wit, but at the ridiculous incongruity of the two voices—the gigantic body emitting a feeble flute note, and the tiny form unleashing a deep, resonant drumbeat. It was a mountain delivering a squeak, a molehill belching thunder.

The singular duet ended as abruptly as it began. Giles, losing all patience and self-command, driven by a rage devoid of fear, dropped upon the giant's neck. Seizing the giant's hair with one hand, he punched his head with the other. The giant's initial impulse was to laugh, but the weight and rapidity of the blows quickly extinguished that inclination.

"He! he! Ah! ha! Hallo! Oh! Oh! Holy saints! Here! Help! Or I must throttle the imp! I can't! I'll split your skull against the—"

He made a wild run backward toward the balcony. Giles, sensing imminent danger, seized the railing with both hands and whipped over it just as the giant's head collided with the wood with a stunning crack. The people roared with laughter and exultation at the little champion's address. Indignant, the giant seized two of the laughers, knocking them together like dumbbells before shaking them until they lay flat. Catherine shrieked, throwing her apron over Giles, while the giant strode wrathfully away after the departing party.

No one present foresaw the consequences of this incident, yet its immediate effects were agreeable. The Tergovians turned proudly toward Giles, listening with newfound affability to his pleas for parchment. He conducted a regular trade with his brother Gerard in this commodity, wandering about begging sheets gratis while Gerard provided copper coins in exchange.

That same afternoon, Catherine and her daughter engaged in conversation about their favorite topic: Gerard, his goodness, his benefice, and the brightened prospects of the entire family. Their fortune had arrived in the exact shape they desired. Beyond the advantage of a benefice that the Countess Charolois would not disdain to grant, there lay the feminine delight of having a priest, a holy man, within their own household.

"He will marry Cornelis and Sybrandt," Catherine declared. "For they can wed, good housewives, now, if they will. Gerard will take care of you and Giles when we are gone."

"Yes, Mother, and we can confess to him instead of to a stranger," Kate replied.

"Ay, girl! And he can give the sacred oil to your father and me, and close our eyes when our time comes."

"Oh, Mother! Not for many, many years, I do pray Heaven. Pray speak not of that; it always makes me sad. I hope to go before you, Mother dear. No; let us be gay today. I am out of pain, Mother, quite out of all pain. It seems so strange; I feel so bright and happy that..."

She paused, her eyes lighting up. "Mother, can you keep a secret?"

"Nobody better, child. Why, you know I can."

"Then I will show you something so beautiful. You never saw the like, I trow. Only Gerard must never know; for surely he means to surprise us with it. He covers it up so, and sometimes he carries it away altogether."

Kate took her crutches and moved slowly away, leaving her mother in an exalted state of curiosity. Soon she returned, carrying something wrapped in cloth. Uncovering it revealed a lovely picture of the Virgin, adorned with all her insignias, wearing her tiara over a wealth of beautiful hair flowing loosely over her shoulders. Catherine, at first, was struck with awe.

"It is herself," she cried. "It is the Queen of Heaven. I never saw one like her to my mind before."

"And her eyes, Mother: lifted to the sky, as if they belonged there, and not to a mortal creature. And her beautiful hair of burning gold."

"And to think I have a son that can make the saints live again upon a piece of wood!"

"The reason is, he is a young saint himself, Mother. He is too good for this world; he is here to portray the blessed, and then to go away and be with them forever."

Before they had finished admiring the image, a strange voice was heard at the door. Driven by one of the furtive instincts common to their sex, they hastily hid the picture in the cloth, though there was no real need. The next moment, a man entered, casting his eyes furtively around the room. It had been ten years since Ghysbrecht Van Swieten had set foot in the house.

The two women were so taken aback that they merely stared at him and each other, murmuring, "The burgomaster!" in a tone so expressive that Ghysbrecht felt compelled to respond.

"Yes! I own the last time I came here was not on a friendly errand. Men love their own interests—Elias's and mine were contrary. Well, let this visit atone for the last. Today I come on your business and none of mine."

Catherine and her daughter exchanged a swift glance of contemptuous incredulity. They knew the man better than he thought.

"It is about your son Gerard."

"Ay! Ay! You want him to work for the town all for nothing. He told us."

"I come on no such errand. It is to let you know he has fallen into bad hands."

"Now Heaven and the saints forbid! Man, torture not a mother! Speak out, and quickly: speak ere you have time to coin falsehood. We know thee."

Ghysbrecht turned pale at this affront, spite mingling with the other motives that had brought him there. "Thus it is, then," he said, grinding his teeth and speaking very fast. "Your son Gerard is more likely to be the father of a family than a priest: he is forever with Margaret, Peter Brandt's red-haired girl, and loves her like a cow her calf."

Mother and daughter both burst out laughing. Ghysbrecht stared at them, bewildered.

"What! You knew it?"

"Carry this

While the burgomaster exposed Gerard to the scrutiny of Tergou, a quieter, yet infinitely heavier storm brewed within the domestic walls of Sevenbergen. It was the distress of a housewife, born of scarcity, yet it carried a weight that transcends mere hunger. Margaret approached Martin Wittenhaagen, the veteran soldier, her eyes wet with unshed tears. "Martin, there is nothing left in the house," she confessed, her voice trembling. "Gerard returns, and he is so thoughtlessly absent-minded. He forgets to break bread at home. When his labor ceases, he rushes to me, poor soul, often collapsing from exhaustion. And to think, I possess nothing to offer the servant who loves me so dearly."

Martin scratched his head, the gesture betraying a mind racing through logistical impossibilities. "What can I do?" he murmured. "It is Thursday; it is your day to shoot. Sooth to say, I counted on you today."

"Nay," the soldier replied, his tone firm despite his weariness. "I may not draw my bow when the Duke or his cronies are out on the chase. Read else. I am no scholar of courtly etiquette." With these words, he produced from his pouch a parchment sealed with a grand crest. It bore the stipulation and license granted by Philip, Duke of Burgundy, to Martin Wittenhaagen, one of his trusted archers, in recompense for services rendered in war and a grievous wound sustained at the Duke's side. The document promised a stipend of four merks annually, payable by the Duke's almoner, alongside a license to release three arrows weekly—specifically on Thursdays—in any of the Duke's Holland forests. However, the proviso was absolute: the hunt was forbidden if the Duke or his friends were present. Under such circumstances, Martin was barred from disturbing the woods, lest he forfeit his salary, his life, and incur a fine of a penny.

Margaret sighed, the sound echoing the emptiness of her hearth, and fell silent. "Come, cheer up, mistress," Martin urged, his demeanor shifting from bureaucratic resignation to protective resolve. "For your sake, I will peril my carcass. I have done worse for women not worth your forefinger. It is no such mighty risk; I shall merely step into the skirts of the forest here. Odds are they will drive a hare or a fawn within range of my arrow."

"Well, if I permit you to go," Margaret insisted, her desperation overriding her caution, "you must promise me not to stray far and not to be seen. Far better that Gerard goes supperless than that ill should come to you, faithful Martin."

With the necessary vow extracted, Martin donned his bow and loaded three arrows, slipping cautiously into the wood, barely a furlong distant. The faint, mournful cry of horns drifted on the breeze, signaling that the game was already afoot. "Come," Martin thought, steeling his nerves, "I shall soon fill the pot, and no one will be the wiser." He positioned himself behind a thick oak commanding a view of an open glade, drawing his weapon—a formidable instrument of English yew, towering six feet two inches and thick as a thigh. Martin, broad-chested with arms forged of iron and cord, had mastered the bow since infancy. He could draw a three-foot arrow to the nape of his neck; when released, the projectile blurred beyond the eye's tracking ability, the bowstring twanging with a resonance akin to a harp. This bow had claimed the lives of countless soldiers in the wars of the Hoecks and Cabbel-jaws. In those eras, battlefields were not shrouded in clouds of smoke; combatants were few, yet the carnage was total, for they fought with clarity, seeing exactly what they wrought. Fewer bloodless arrows flew then than the hollow, silent projectiles of modern warfare.

A hare cantered into view, then sat sprightly, its ears forming a perfect capital 'V'. Martin leveled his tremendous weapon. The arrow flew, the string sang, but in his haste to secure the kill, he missed by an inch. The shaft appeared to strike the animal, yet it struck the earth beside her, passing beneath her belly like a flash of light, hissing through the short grass before vanishing. The hare leaped three feet vertically and bolted at maximum velocity. "Bungler!" Martin chided himself. A sure sign he was not an habitual bungler, for otherwise, he would have blamed the hare.

Scarcely had he fitted another arrow when a wood-pigeon settled directly upon the tree where he stood. "Aha!" he thought. "You are small, but dainty." This time, he exercised patience. He drew the arrow carefully, loosed it smoothly, and watched it appear to pierce the bird cleanly, sending feathers skyward like dust. Yet, the bird, its breast torn rather than pierced, fluttered feebly away. By sheer effort, it rose above the canopy, soared fifty yards, and dropped dead—but where, hidden by the dense foliage, he could not see. "Luck is against me," he muttered despondently.

He fitted another arrow and scanned the glade intently. Suddenly, a commotion erupted behind him. Turning just in time, he witnessed a noble buck crossing the open space, but it was too late; the animal had already vanished into the brush. Martin dashed his bow to the ground in an imprecation. At that precise moment, a long-spotted animal glided swiftly across the path, trailing the deer. Its belly seemed to graze the earth as it moved. Martin snatched up his bow instantly; he recognized the Duke's leopard.

"The hunters will not be far from her," he reasoned grimly. "And I must not be seen. Gerard must go supperless tonight." He plunged into the wood, following the trajectory of the buck and the leopard, the only route back to safety.

He had not traveled far when an unusual sound cut through the silence ahead—the violent rustling of leaves and the crunch of trampled earth. Hurrying forward, he discovered the leopard atop the buck, tearing at its flesh with teeth and claws. The deer ran in a frantic circle, bounding convulsively as blood poured down its hide. A desperate resolution crystallized in Martin's mind: he would secure the venison for Margaret. Drawing his arrow to the head, he buried it deep in the deer. Despite the predator clinging to its back, the buck bounded high into the air and fell dead. The leopard continued to tear, seemingly oblivious to the new reality. Martin hoped the creature would gorge itself until it succumbed, allowing him to claim the meat.

Minutes passed. Resolutely, he walked forward and placed his hand on the buck's leg. The leopard emitted a frightful growl, pausing her feeding. She sensed Martin's presence, becoming sulky and alert. What course of action remained? Martin recalled the ancient wisdom that wild beasts could not withstand the steady human gaze. Standing erect, he fixed his eyes upon the leopard. The beast returned a savage glare, refusing to break contact. As Martin maintained his unblinking stare, the leopard, brutally ignorant of natural law, lunged at his head with a deafening yell, her eyes aflame, jaws distended.

He had but a fraction of a second to react, catching her by the throat before her teeth could crush his face. One claw seized his shoulder, ripping through fabric and skin; the other, aimed at his cheek, would have been fatal. Martin was old-fashioned, wearing no hat, only a scapulary of the same sturdy stuff as his jerkin. He had thrown this garment over his head like a hood, and the brute's claw snagged in the loose leather.

Martin struggled to keep her teeth from his face, gripping her throat with iron force while she raked his shoulder. It felt like blunt reaping hooks grinding and tearing. The pain was excruciating, yet instead of cowering, it ignited his blood. He gnashed his teeth in rage nearly as fierce as hers, squeezing her neck with crushing strength. Two pairs of eyes flared in the dim light—the man's burning with fury, the brute's with primal instinct. Realizing she was being throttled, the leopard made a wild attempt to free herself, dragging his cowl across his face, blinding him, and tearing her claw free from his shoulder, taking flesh and bone with it. Still, he throttled her with hand and arm of iron.

Presently, her long tail, previously held high, drooped. "Aha!" cried Martin, joyfully, and gripped her like death. Next, her body lost its elasticity; he held a choked, powerless thing. He maintained his grip until all motion ceased, then dashed her corpse to the earth. Panting, he removed his cowl. The leopard lay mute at his feet, tongue protruding, a bloody paw twitching. For the first time, terror seized Martin. "I am a dead man," he whispered. "I have slain the Duke's leopard."

Hastily, he gathered handfuls of leaves and threw them over the carcass, then shouldered the buck and staggered away, leaving a trail of mingled blood—his own and the deer's. He burst into Peter's house a horrifying figure, bleeding and stained, and flung the deer's carcass onto the floor. "There—no questions," he rasped. "But broil me a steak on it, for I am faint."

Margaret did not notice his wounds; she assumed the blood belonged entirely to the deer. She busied herself at the fire, while the stout soldier stanching and binding

The moonlight fractured across the garden wall as Margaret severed a massive chunk of venison, tossing it outward with a decisive flick of her wrist. The meat sailed through the air, landing squarely before the emerald eyes of the leopard. With a savage snarl that vibrated through the night, the beast lunged, tearing and crunching with primal hunger. Suddenly, a hound bayed—a sound so piercing and close it echoed violently within the timbered halls—and the three animals at the window recoiled in unified terror. Fear seized the leopard; her supper secured, she melted away into the shadows of the woods with stolen grace. Instantly, the chaos erupted outside: horses reared, men shouted, and dogs barked, forming a frenzied pursuit that swept past the window. Inside, Martin and his companions exhaled, their chests rising in relief. The leopard was swift; she would vanish beyond a league of their reach before dawn. Seizing the brief respite, Margaret allowed herself a small, trembling sob. Gerard stepped forward, pressing a kiss to wipe the tears from her cheeks, his touch gentle amidst the lingering adrenaline.

They returned to the table, the tension replaced by a fragile warmth. Gerard raised his cup, toasting the unique fortitude of the female spirit. "'Tis stronger than man's force," he declared, his voice thick with admiration.

"Aye," Margaret replied, her gaze steady, "only when those she loves stand in peril. Not otherwise."

That evening, Gerard lingered with her longer than custom dictated, departing home intoxicated by a pride that felt regal, his heart light as a prince's. Yet, mere distances from the sanctuary of his childhood home, beneath the canopy of ancient oaks, two figures emerged to block his path.

It was his father and mother.

*So late,* Gerard thought, a chill seizing his spine. *What could possibly drive them here?*

He halted, staring at them. They stood rigid, grim faces carved from stone, silence hanging between them like a physical weight. He stammered, offering tentative inquiries.

"Why ask?" his father growled, the question itself an accusation. "You know why we are here."

"Oh, Gerard!" his mother interjected, her voice a complex tapestry of reproach and buried affection.

Gerard's heart hammered against his ribs; he fell silent, the weight of their presence crushing his resolve. Then, his father softened, sensing the boy's confusion.

"Nay, do not bow your head," the elder said gently. "You are not the first young fool ensnared by a rosy cheek and a pair of azure eyes."

"Nay, nay!" Catherine chimed in, her tone shifting to superstition. "It was witchcraft! Peter the Magician is renowned for such sorcery."

"Come, Sir Priest," the father resumed, addressing Gerard with paternal authority. "You know you must not consort with women of ill repute. But grant us this promise: do not set foot in Sevenbergen again. Let this be the end. We shall not punish you severely for one lapse."

"I cannot promise that, father."

"Not promise it, you young hypocrite!" the father snapped, his patience fraying.

"Nay, father, do not misname me," Gerard pleaded, his voice cracking. "I lacked the courage to tell you what I knew would grieve you. I am eternally grateful to that good friend, whoever he may be, who revealed your truth to you. It is a burden lifted from my soul. Yes, father, I love Margaret. Call me not a priest, for a priest I shall never be. I would sooner die."

"That we shall see, young man," the father retorted, his composure returning like a shield. "Come, deny me no more; you will learn the price of disrespecting a father."

Gerard fell silent. The trio walked home enveloped in a gloomy quiet, broken only by the occasional, heavy sigh of Catherine.

From that hour, the modest dwelling at Tergou ceased to be a haven of peace. The following day, Gerard was summoned before the entire family, subjected to a barrage of accusations. Every voice rose in condemnation save for little Kate's and the dwarf's, who instinctively mirrored her silence without understanding why. Cornelis and Sybrandt were fiercer than their sire. Dismayed by the sudden proliferation of enemies, Gerard cast a longing glance at his younger sister. Her eyes swelled with tears as harsh words rained down upon him, a boy who had been the universal favorite just yesterday. Yet, she offered no solace. Turning her head away, she spoke with chilling finality: "Dear, dear Gerard, pray to Heaven to cure you of this folly!"

"What, are you against me too?" Gerard asked, his voice hollow with despair. He rose, letting out a deep, ragged sigh, and fled the house, walking straight toward Sevenbergen.

The genesis of a quarrel, where parties remain bound by affection despite opposing interests and sentiments, is relatively innocuous. Often, both sides hold partial truths at the outset, waiting for a calm, judicious friend—one capable of perceiving the full spectrum of the conflict—to intervene as a divine gift. However, as discord endures, it widens and deepens, fed by the inherent fallibility and irritability of human nature. These flaws are not exclusive to either faction; inevitably, the conclusion arrives where both stand equally in the wrong.

The combatants were mismatched: Elias burned with righteous anger, while Cornelis and Sybrandt festered with spite. Gerard, possessing a broader and more cultivated mind, perceived the nuances both sides missed. He suffered from fits of irresolution, feeling neither wrath nor triumph, but merely profound unhappiness. He was utterly alone in this struggle, unable to confide in anyone. Margaret was a spirited girl; he dared not reveal the torment he endured at home. She might well side with his relations by demanding his resignation, sacrificing her own happiness to preserve the family bond. Though Margaret Van Eyck had once been a great comfort to him, he now feared making her his confidante. Her own history was well-known: in her youth, she had rejected numerous suitors for the sake of her art, a pursuit incompatible with the duties of wife and mother. Consequently, she remained single, painting alongside her brothers. How could he confess that he had declined the ecclesiastical benefice she had secured for him, doing so precisely because of the very thing she had once despised and sacrificed so lightly?

Gerard appeared poised to succumb to despair. But the opposition harbored a terrible ally in Catherine the Elder. This good-hearted yet uneducated woman could not, unlike her daughter, act with quiet firmness or strategic planning. At times, she irritated Gerard, inadvertently strengthening him, for anger serves as a potent sustainer of courage. At other moments, however, she would suddenly pivot and launch attacks upon her own household. Consider this single instance among many: one day, gathered at home, Cornelis declared, "Shall our Gerard wed Margaret Brandt? Why, it is hunger marrying thirst."

"And what will happen when you marry?" Catherine cried, her voice sharp. "Gerard can paint, Gerard can write, but what can you do to keep a woman, ye lazy loon? Nothing but wait for your father's shoes. Oh, we see clearly why you and Sybrandt refuse to let the poor boy marry. You fear he will come to us for a share of our substance. Say he does, say we give it to him—it is not yours we part with, and perhaps never will be."

On such occasions, Gerard would smile slyly, regaining his composure, leaving Catherine's unfortunate allies momentarily confused. But eventually, after more than half a year of simmering irritation, the situation reached its climax. The father announced to the assembled family that he had ordered the burgomaster to imprison Gerard in the Stadthouse rather than allow him to marry Margaret. Gerard turned pale with rage, yet through sheer effort, he maintained his silence. His father pressed on, "And before the year is out, you shall be a priest, nilly-willy."

"Is it so?" Gerard cried, his voice breaking. "Then listen to me, all. By God and St. Bavon, I swear I will never be a priest while Margaret lives. If force is to decide this, and not love and duty, then try force, father. But force shall fail you. The day the burgomaster comes for me, I leave Tergou forever, and Holland too, and my father's house, where it seems I have been valued these many years not for myself, but for what can be extracted from me."

With that, he stormed out of the room, his face flushed white with anger and desperation.

"There!" Catherine exclaimed, her voice trembling. "That comes of driving young folks too hard. But men are crueler than tigers, even to their own flesh and blood. Now, Heaven forbid he should ever leave us, married or single."

As Gerard emerged from the house, his cheeks pale and his heart pounding, he encountered Reicht Heynes. She bore a message: Margaret Van Eyck desired to see him. He found the elderly woman seated, grim as a judge. She wasted no time on pleasantries, querying coldly why he had not visited recently. Before he could respond, she spoke in a sarcastic tone, "I thought we had been friends, young sir."

At this, Gerard looked the very picture of doubt and consternation.

"It is because you never told her you were in love," Reicht Heynes said, her expression softening with pity for his confusion.

"Silence

To understand the architecture of this betrayal, one must first grasp the absolute fragility of the union it sought to sanctify. In the annals of Tergou, the banns of marriage were a ritual of three readings, a bureaucratic incantation designed to ensure no hidden kinship or prior vow obscured the path to the altar. Yet, in the fervor of youth and the novelty of the Cure's arrival, the young couple had manipulated the system with reckless brilliance, persuading the clergyman to compress the requisite triad of readings into a single, frenzied twenty-four-hour cycle. They were proclaimed on Monday at Matins and Vespers, a triumph of timing that ensured the church remained empty save for the eager witnesses of their own devotion. By Tuesday morning, however, the atmosphere had curdled into a suffocating dread. As Margaret and Gerard stood before the altar, their hearts hammering against their ribs like trapped birds, a stranger rose from the congregation—a shadow cast by the law itself. With a voice devoid of warmth, he declared the union void, citing the parties' minority and the absence of parental consent. The sanctuary, moments before a temple of joy, became a courtroom of ruin.

Outside, beneath the gray Dutch sky, Margaret and Gerard huddled in a trembling consultation, their despair bordering on paralysis. Before they could formulate a defense, the accuser approached, feigning a sorrowful reluctance to intervene. He spun a web of plausible deniability, claiming his interference was born of a desire to protect their happiness, yet admitting his livelihood depended entirely on the prohibition of such unions. "The young people give me a crown," he whispered, his eyes gleaming with the greed of a professional obstructionist, "and I shall undo my work handsomely. Tell the Cure I was misinformed, and all returns to smooth sailing."

"A crown!" Gerard exclaimed, his desperation momentarily eclipsing his pride. "I will give you a golden angel to do this!" The transaction was sealed with the alacrity of thieves meeting in the dark. The man departed with his payment, a golden angel purchased from the future misery of two lovers, and hastened to the Cure. With a flourish of contrition, he confessed a "ridiculous mistake," corrected by the mere sight of the aggrieved pair. The Cure, relieved of his burden, agreed to the wedding for the following day at ten. Thus, the professional assassin of bliss retired to a tavern in Tergou, where a green dedicated to archery served as a backdrop for his drunken boasting. There, amidst the raucous laughter of the common folk, he recounted his exploit to a notorious loiterer named Sybrandt. Sybrandt, a creature of idleness and malice, fled home to relay the news to his father, who was away in Rotterdam purchasing cloth from the merchants. Seizing the opportunity, Sybrandt signaled his elder brother, and together they became the vanguard of a conspiracy born of envy.

These two were the black sheep of the Van Swieten lineage, Gerard's darker reflections. Idleness is a slow poison, and the waiting for the death of loved ones a festering wound; these two one-dimensional curs were ready to tear anyone limb from limb who threatened their miserable inheritance, a phantom wealth that occupied their thoughts by day and haunted their dreams by night. While their parents viewed parsimony as a virtue rooted in industry and love, these twisted hearts perverted that same thrift into avarice, a fertile soil for crimes far more destructive than any natural vice. They plotted in secret, deciding not to inform their mother, whose sentiments were too volatile, but to bypass her entirely and seek the Burgomaster. They were cunning enough to sense his aversion to the match, though the reason remained obscure to them.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten saw through their scheme instantly, yet he masked his insight behind a veil of magisterial indifference. Hearing their story, he adopted an air of cold authority, declaring, "Since the father of the family is not here, his duty falls upon me, who am the father of the town. I know your father's mind; leave all to me. Above all, tell not a woman a word of this, least of all the women in your own house, for chattering tongues mar the wisest counsels." With a dismissal laced with condescension, he sent them away, ashamed of his complicity with such foolish conspirators.

Upon returning home, they discovered a scene of profound transformation. Gerard sat on a low stool at his mother's knee, her hand caressing his hair, her voice filled with tender promises to support him against his father's wrath. The cause of this sudden capitulation was characteristic of the woman's capricious nature. In a fit of female irritation, she had previously torn Margaret's portrait to shreds, watching with misgiving as Gerard turned pale as death, sitting motionless like a bereaved ghost, tears blinding his vision. Terrified by her own cruelty, her heart had smote her bitterly, and she wept in secret, resolving to make amends without confession. Her maternal instincts, fierce and forgiving, had already transferred her fatal alliance to Gerard when the two black sheep entered the room. Unaware of the immediate catalyst, and inexperienced in the intricate machinations of the feminine heart, Gerard mistook her kindness for absolution. Ashamed of his suspicion that she was the destroyer of his love, he kissed her repeatedly, retreating to his bed a prince once more, believing his mother had reclaimed her rightful place beside him at the very crisis of his fate.

The next morning, at ten o'clock, Gerard and Margaret stood in the church of Sevenbergen. He was radiant with joy; she burned with blushes. Only Peter and Martin Wittenhaagen were present, for secrecy was paramount. Margaret had renounced Italy; she could not abandon her father, who was too learned and too helpless to travel. It was settled they would retire to Flanders for a few weeks until the storm in Tergou passed. The Cure did not keep them waiting long.
