Standing at the altar, he called them forward, and they walked hand in hand, the happiest souls in Holland. He opened his book, preparing to speak the sacred rites.

But ere a single syllable of the ceremony escaped his lips, a harsh voice shattered the silence: "Forbear!" Constables of Tergou surged up the aisle, seizing Gerard in the name of the law. Martin's long knife flashed out in an instant. "Forbear, man!" cried the priest, indignant. "What! Draw your weapon in a church? Ye who interrupt this holy sacrament, what manner of impiety is this?"

"There is no impiety, Father," replied the Burgomaster's servant respectfully. "This young man seeks to marry against his father's will, and his father has prayed our Burgomaster to deal with him according to the law. Let him deny it if he can."

"Is this so, young man?" the priest demanded.

Gerard hung his head, defeated. "We take him to Rotterdam to abide the sentence of the Duke," the servant announced.

At this, Margaret uttered a cry of despair. The young creatures, so happy a moment before, collapsed into each other's arms, sobbing pitifully. Even the instruments of oppression recoiled, ashamed of their cruelty, until one, of a better nature, stepped forward under the pretext of separating them. He whispered to Margaret, "Rotterdam? It is a lie. We merely take him to our Stadthouse."

They mounted him on horseback, riding the road to Rotterdam. After a dozen halts and sly detours, they arrived back at Tergou. Just outside the town gates, a rough vehicle covered in canvas awaited. Gerard was forced inside, and by five in the evening, he was secretly conveyed into the prison of the Stadthouse. He was hoisted up several flights of stairs and thrust into a small room illuminated only by a narrow window barred with vertical iron. The entire furniture consisted of a massive oak chest. Imprisonment in that age was a highway to death; even in its mildest form, it implied cold, unbroken solitude, torture, starvation, and often poison. Gerard felt he was in the hands of an implacable enemy.

"Oh, the look that man gave me on the road to Rotterdam," he murmured, kneeling to commend his soul to God. "There is more here than my father's wrath. I doubt I shall see the light of day again."

Presently, he rose and leaped at the iron bar of the window, clutching it tightly. This allowed him to press his knees against the wall and peer outward. For a fleeting minute, he witnessed a sight reserved only for the captive: Martin Wittenhaagen's back. Martin sat quietly fishing in the brook near the Stadthouse. Gerard sprang again at the window and whistled. Martin instantly ceased his pretense of fishing, turning hastily to see Gerard, making a signal before rising with his line and bow and departing quickly. Gerard realized his friends were not idle, yet he wished Martin had stayed; the sight of him was a comfort. Holding on, he gazed at the soldier's retreating form until he could no longer see, then fell back heavily. With a wrench, he pulled the rusty iron bar, held fast only by corroded nails, away from the stonework. At that precise moment, Ghysbrecht Van Swieten opened the door stealthily behind him. The Burgomaster's eye fell instantly on the dislodged iron, then glanced at the window, but he said nothing. The window was a hundred feet from the ground; if Gerard fancied jumping, why should he balk? The Burgomaster brought a brown

The burgomaster's gaze, a venomous coil of hatred locked upon Gerard, sealed the young man's conviction: Ghysbrecht was not merely an adversary, but a sworn executioner waiting in the shadows. Imprisonment had curdled hope into a singular, driving certainty. As Gerard glided silently around the tower's sharp angle, he expected no miracle, only the cold calculus of malice—a treacherous mechanism devised by a wicked man to ensure his demise within those stone walls, an escape so improbable it would remain unknown even if achieved. Yet, as he moved through the darkness, a hand, soft yet resolute, slipped into his. Margaret stood beside him, sharing the weight of this newly forged peril.

Before the haunted tower could fully reveal itself to their eyes, a vision struck them, numbing their very souls. Halfway up the ascent, a creature mounted the wall with steady, unnatural grace. Its head burned with a fiery luminescence, resembling an enormous glowworm, casting a stark silhouette against the night. The body was shrouded in obscurity, defined only by the glare of that infernal crown, measuring nearly four feet in length. Below, at the tower's base, stood a figure clad in white, indistinguishable from a living woman. Gerard and Margaret stood paralyzed, their hearts hammering against their ribs.

"The rope! The rope!" Gerard gasped, his voice cracking. "It ascends the rope."

As they watched, the glowing entity vanished into the mouth of Gerard's former prison, its light flooding the interior and turning the barred window a sickly crimson. The white figure remained motionless beneath. Those who survive the initial shock of the supernatural often succumb to a peculiar, primal terror—a wild, magnetic urge to throw oneself upon the terrible object, drawn by a fascination akin to a bird fixated on a serpent. Great tragedians knew this well; Macready, portraying Banquo's second appearance, had captured it perfectly, hurling himself backward with averted head at the horror. Now, this strange compulsion seized Margaret. She released Gerard's hand, standing bewildered until, in a single, frantic motion, she cried out and darted toward the specter.

Gerard, unaware of this natural instinct, saw only the devil dragging his friend to perdition. He fell to his knees, screaming the ancient rite: *"Exorcizo vos. In nomine beatae Mariae, exorcizo vos."* While the exorcist shrieked his incantations in the extremity of his terror, a profound relief washed over him when the specter emitted a feeble cry of fear. To discover that hell possessed its own vulnerabilities was an encouragement. Redoubling his efforts, Gerard soon saw the ghastly shape kneeling at Margaret's feet, hearing it pray pitifully for mercy.

Kate and Giles arrived shortly thereafter, finding a new rope dangling from the prisoner's window to the earth. "I see how it is," muttered the lesser intelligence, accepting reality as it unfolded. "Our Gerard descended this rope. He is free. I ascend, and I see."

"No, Giles, no!" commanded the superior intelligence, blinded by prejudice. "Do you not perceive this is glamour? This rope is a line cast by the Evil One to lure thee to destruction. He knows the weaknesses of every heart; he sees how fond thou art of climbing. Where would our Gerard procure such a rope? How fasten it in the sky thus? It defies nature. Holy saints protect us tonight, for hell is abroad."

"Stuff!" retorted the dwarf. "The road to hell is down, and this rope leads up. I have never had the fortune to climb such a long rope. It may be years before I encounter one prepared for me. Better to be knocked on the head at once than to wait forever for happiness." With a cry of delight, he sprang onto the rope, moving with the agility of a cat leaping for a table laden with fish. Every muscle ignited with energy; the only concession Kate secured was permission to fasten a lantern to his neck. "Light frightens ill spirits," she reasoned. Thus, with arms like iron and legs like feathers, Giles climbed upward faster than his brother descended. The lantern at the nape of his neck transformed him into a living glowworm. His sister watched his progress with trembling anxiety.

Suddenly, a female figure burst from the solid masonry, flying at her with superhuman velocity. Kate uttered a weak cry—the limit of her vocal cords under the weight of terror. Dropping her crutches, she sank to her knees, burying her face and moaning, "Take my body, but spare my soul!"

"Why, it is a woman!" Kate quivered.
"Why, it is a woman!" Margaret echoed.
"How you scared me!"
"I am scared enough myself. Oh! Oh! Oh!"

"This is strange," Margaret breathed, recovering her composure. "But the fiery-headed thing? It was with you, and you are harmless! Why are you here at this hour?"
"Nay, why are *you*?"
"Perhaps we share the same errand? Ah! You are his good sister, Kate!"
"And you are Margaret Brandt."
"Yes."
"All the better. You love him; you are here. Then Giles was right. He has won his freedom."

Gerard stepped forward, putting the mystery to rest. But their conversation was abruptly severed by a horrible, unearthly noise, sounding like a sepulcher giving vent to its contents: *"PARCHMENT! PARCHMENT! PARCHMENT!"* Each repetition rose in intensity. Looking up, they beheld the dwarf, clutching bundles of parchments, his face alight with fiendish joy and flushed with diabolical fire. The lantern burning at his neck rendered him a more infernal transparency than any mortal eye had ever witnessed. With the shout, the awful imp hurled the documents at the astonished figures below. Records rained down like wounded wild ducks; some collapsed immediately, others fluttered helplessly, while others spread wide, wheeling slowly in airy circles before settling. Hardly had they landed when the sepulchral roar returned—"Parchment! Parchment!"—and another flock pattered and sailed down, followed by another, blanketing the grass in white.

Finally, the fire-headed imp, his body light and hands horned, slid down the rope like a falling star. Prioritizing business over sentiment, he proposed an immediate settlement for the merchandise he had just delivered.

"Hush!" Gerard warned. "You speak too loudly. Gather them up and follow us to a safer place than this."

"Will you come home with me, Gerard?" asked little Kate.
"I have no home."
"You shall not say so. Who is more welcome than you, after such cruel wrong, to your father's house?"
"Father! I have no father," Gerard replied sternly. "He who was my father has become my gaoler. I have escaped his hands; I will never come within their reach again."
"An enemy did this, not our father." Kate recounted what she had overheard between Cornelis and Sybrandt. Yet the injury was too fresh to be soothed. Gerard displayed a bitterness of indignation he had previously seemed incapable of.

"Cornelis and Sybrandt are two ill curs that have shown me their teeth and their heart for a long while, but they could do no more. My father gave the burgomaster authority, or he dared not lay a finger on me, a free burgher of this town. So be it. I was his son; I am his prisoner. He has played his part; I shall play mine. Farewell to the burgh where I was born, lived honestly, and was imprisoned. While there remains another town in creation, I will never trouble you again, Tergou."

"Oh! Gerard! Gerard!" Margaret whispered urgently. "Do not give way to anger now. Let his choler cool!"

Kate turned quickly toward her. "May I look at your face?" The inspection seemed favorable, prompting her to whisper, "It is a comely face, and no mischief-maker's."
"Fear me not," Margaret said in the same gentle tone. "I could not be happy without your love, as well as Gerard's."
"These are comforting words," Kate sobbed. Then, lifting her eyes, she added, "Little did I think to like you so well. My heart is willing, but my infirmity forbids me to embrace you."

At this hint, Margaret gently wound her arms around Gerard's sister and kissed her lovingly. "Often he has spoken of you to me, Kate; and often I longed for this."
"You, too, Gerard," Kate said, "kiss me ere you go; for my heart lies heavy at parting with you this night."

Gerard kissed her, and she departed on her crutches toward home. The last sound they heard of her was a little, patient sigh. Then tears welled thick in Margaret's eyes. But Gerard was a man, and he noticed nothing of his sister's sorrow. Turning toward Sevenbergen, the dwarf nudged Gerard with his bundle of parchments and extended a concave claw. Margaret dissuaded him. "Why take what is not ours?"
"Oh, spoil an enemy however you can."
"But may they not make this a pretext for fresh violence?"
"How can they? Think you I shall remain in Tergou after this? The burgomaster robbed me of my liberty; I doubt I should take his life for it, if I could."
"Oh, fie! Gerard."
"What! Is life worth more than liberty

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten clutched the iron key to Gerard's prison within the leather pouch of his tunic, waiting until the clock tolled ten. He reasoned to himself that a little hunger served as a sharpening agent, breaking the spirit of the captive just as surely as a hammer breaks bone. At the appointed hour, he ascended the stairwell, carrying a loaf of bread and a pitcher of water, trailed by his trusted servant, Dierich Brower, whose arm rested heavily upon the hilt of his weapon.

Ghysbrecht pressed his ear against the wooden door. Silence answered him, a heavy, suffocating quiet. A grim smile twisted his features. *By this time,* he thought, *he will be as downhearted as Albert Koestein was.* He turned the lock and pushed the door open.

There was no Gerard.

Ghysbrecht stood stupefied. Though his face remained hidden in shadow, his entire body seemed to lose all motion, suspended in a peculiar paralysis. Moments later, he collapsed forward, trembling violently. Behind him, Dierich sensed the shift in the air, stepped closer, and peered over his master's shoulder. Upon seeing the empty cell, the discarded rope, and the removed iron bar, the servant let out a loud exclamation of wonder. Yet his surprise doubled when his master, disregarding everything else, suddenly flung himself to his knees before the vacant chest. With quivering hands, Ghysbrecht frantically raked the surface, as if refusing to trust his own eyes in a matter of such catastrophic importance.

Dierich gazed at him in utter bewilderment. "Why, master, what is the matter?"

Ghysbrecht's pale lips worked, forming shapes of speech, but no sound emerged. His hands fell limp at his sides, and he stared blankly into the hollow cavity of the chest.

"Why, master, what avails glaring into that empty box? The lad is not there. Look here! Note the cunning of the young rogue; he hath taken out the bar—"

"Gone! What is gone, Holy saints! He is planet-struck!"

"STOP THIEF!" shrieked Ghysbrecht, suddenly turning on his servant and collaring him, shaking him with a rage born of desperation. "Do you stand there, knave, and watch your master robbed? Run! Fly! A hundred crowns to him who brings it back to me. No, no! 'Tis in vain. Oh, fool! Fool! To leave that in the same room with him. But none ever found the secret spring before. None ever would but he. It was to be. It is to be. Lost! Lost!"

His years and infirmities now overpowered his short-lived frenzy, and he sank upon the chest, muttering the word like a broken prayer. "Lost! Lost!"

"What is lost, master?" the servant asked, his kindness cutting through the storm.

"House and lands and good name," groaned Ghysbrecht, wringing his hands feebly.

"WHAT?" cried the servant, the word striking Ghysbrecht's ear with the force of a bell.

The tone of eager curiosity struck a chord, reviving Ghysbrecht's natural cunning. "I have lost the town records," he stammered, looking askance at the man like a fox cornered near a hen-roost.

"Oh, is that all?"

"Is't not enough? What will the burghers say to me? What will the burghs do?"

Then he suddenly burst out again, his voice rising. "A hundred crowns to him who shall recover them; all, mind, all that were in this box. If one be missing, I give nothing."

"'Tis a bargain, master: the hundred crowns are in my pouch. See you not that where Gerard Eliassoen is, there are the pieces of sheepskin you rate so highly?"

"That is true; that is true, good Dierich: good, faithful Dierich. All, mind, all that were in the chest."

"Master, I will take the constables to Gerard's house, and seize him for the theft."

"The theft? Ay! Good; very good. It is theft. I forgot that. So, as he is a thief now, we will put him in the dungeons below, where the toads are and the rats. Dierich, that man must never see daylight again. 'Tis his own fault; he must be prying. Quick, quick! Ere he has time to talk, you know, time to talk."

In less than half an hour, Dierich Brower and four constables entered the hosier's house, demanding young Gerard of the panic-stricken Catherine.

"Alas! What has he done now?" she cried, tears welling in her eyes. "That boy will break my heart."

"Nay, dame, but a trick of youth," said Dierich smoothly. "He hath but made off with certain skins of parchment, in a frolic doubtless. But the burgomaster is answerable to the burgh for their safekeeping, so he is in care about them; as for the youth, he will doubtless be quit for a reprimand."

This polished speech completely imposed upon Catherine, but her daughter was more suspicious. Her suspicion was strengthened by the disproportionate anger and disappointment Dierich displayed the moment he learned Gerard was not at home, nor had he been seen that night.

"Come away then," he said roughly. "We are wasting time." He added vehemently, "I'll find him if he is above ground."

Affection sharpens the wits, and often it has made an innocent person more than a match for the wily. As Dierich moved to depart, Kate made him a signal to speak with him privately. He bade his men proceed and waited outside the door. She joined him.

"Hush!" she whispered. "My mother knows not. Gerard has left Tergou."

"How?"

"I saw him last night."

"Ay! Where?" cried Dierich eagerly.

"At the foot of the haunted tower."

"How did he get the rope?"

"I know not; but this I know: my brother Gerard bade me there farewell, and he is many leagues from Tergou ere this. The town, you know, was always unworthy of him, and when it imprisoned him, he vowed never to set foot in it again. Let the burgomaster be content, then. He has imprisoned him, and he has driven him from his birthplace and from his native land. What need now to rob him and us of our good name?"

This might at another moment have struck Dierich as good sense; but he was too mortified at this escape of Gerard and the loss of a hundred crowns.

"What need had he to steal?" he retorted bitterly. "Gerard stole not the trash; he but took it to spite the burgomaster, who stole his liberty; but he shall answer to the Duke for it, he shall. As for these skins of parchment you keep such a coil about, look in the nearest brook or stye, and 'tis odds but you find them."

"Think ye so, mistress? Think ye so?" Dierich's eyes flashed.

"Mayhap you know 'tis so."

"This I know, that Gerard is too good to steal, and too wise to load himself with rubbish, going a journey."

"Give you good day, then," said Dierich sharply. "The sheepskin you scorn, I value it more than the skin of any in Tergou." And he went off hastily on a false scent.

Kate returned into the house and drew Giles aside. "Giles, my heart misgives me; breathe not to a soul what I say to you. I have told Dirk Brower that Gerard is out of Holland, but much I doubt he is not a league from Tergou."

"Why, where is he, then?"

"Where should he be, but with her he loves? But if so, he must not loiter. These be deep and dark and wicked men that seek him. Giles, I see that in Dirk Brower's eye makes me tremble. Oh, why cannot I fly to Sevenbergen and bid him away? Why am I not lusty and active like other girls? God forgive me for fretting at His will; but I never felt till now what it is to be lame and weak and useless. But you are strong, dear Giles," she added coaxingly, "you are very strong."

"Yes, I am strong," thundered Perpusillus; then, catching sight of her meaning, "but I hate to go on foot," he added sulkily.

"Alas! Alas! Who will help me if you will not? Dear Giles, do you not love Gerard?"

"Yes, I like him best of the lot. I'll go to Sevenbergen on Peter Buyskens' mule. Ask you him, for he won't lend her me."

Kate remonstrated. The whole town would follow him. It would be known whither he was gone, and Gerard be in worse danger than before. Giles parried this by promising to ride out of the town the opposite way, and not turn the mule's head towards Sevenbergen till he had got rid of the curious. Kate then assented and borrowed the mule. She charged Giles with a short but meaning message,

To truly grasp the architecture of their impending exile, one must first witness the intoxicating, delirious height of their freedom. Gerard and Margaret descended upon Sevenbergen in the first flush of recovered liberty, their spirits buoyant by the sheer audacity of their escape. Yet, this euphoria was a fragile veneer, quickly cracking under the weight of grim realities. Gerard was a fugitive, a ghost haunting the edges of the law, liable to be recaptured and subjected to the brutal machinery of punishment. Thus, separation became not merely a necessity, but a cruel imperative. Furthermore, a profound loathing for his native soil had taken root in Gerard's soul; he viewed the land itself as an antagonist. Margaret, while wishing him to flee the country entirely, recoiled at the specific destination of Italy, her heart fainting at the thought of him crossing the channel alone. Conversely, Gerard's resolve to abandon Margaret was forged solely in the crucible of his ambition to visit Italy—a pilgrimage where he believed he could secure wealth, renown, and the removal of every insurmountable barrier to their union.

He recounted to her the chilling counsel of the demoiselle Van Eyck, repeating her warnings until the logic was undeniable: the gold pieces bestowed upon him were currency for a journey, not a dowry. The path forward was clearly laid out for his survival and elevation. As a craftsman and artist stifled in this boorish, provincial cage, he knew that Italy alone possessed the eyes to recognize his worth. On this pragmatic foundation, the selfless girl granted her consent, though the agreement was sealed with a torrent of tender tears. Hand in hand, they sat until the clock struck midnight, oscillating between despairing eulogies of their hard fate and painting luminous, hopeful canvases of a future that felt increasingly distant. In the midst of these visions, Margaret's sorrow would flood her eyes, silencing even Gerard's eloquent pleas, leaving them to wonder what sin had summoned such a coalition of enemies to conspire against their love.

Dawn broke with a resignation that neither dared fully articulate; the hour of parting hovered like a suspended sentence, neither spoken nor inevitable. But at approximately three in the afternoon, the silence was shattered by the arrival of Giles. Having circled many miles to evade suspicion, he rode up to the door, his presence a thunderclap that turned two pale faces toward the terrible messenger.

"Brother Gerard!" Giles cried, his voice booming with the authority of a prophet delivering doom. "Kate bids you run for your life. They charge you with theft; you have handed them a handle. Do not attempt explanation. Seek not justice in Tergou; the parchments you seized are but a blind. She has seen your death written in the men's eyes; a price hangs over your head. Fly! For Margaret's sake, and for all who love you, do not loiter another second!"

It was a verdict delivered without trial. Giles, breaking from his rote instructions, added his own counsel: "All the constables swarmed our house after you, and so did Dirk Brower. Kate is wise, Gerard. Listen to her advice and fly!"

"Oh, yes, Gerard!" Margaret screamed, her composure shattering. "Fly on the instant! Ah! Those parchments... my mind misgave me. Why did I let you take them?"

"Margaret, they are but a ruse," Giles insisted. "No matter. That old caitiff shall never see them again. I will not depart until I have hidden his treasure where he can never find it."

After thanking Giles warmly, Gerard bid him farewell, instructing him to return and tell Kate he was already gone—"For I shall be gone ere you reach home," he declared. He then summoned Martin, briefing him on the crisis and begging him to patrol the road toward Tergou. "Ay!" Martin agreed, his demeanor shifting to one of grim readiness. "And if I spot Dirk Brower or any of his men, I will shoot an arrow into the oak-tree in our garden. On that signal, you must bolt into the nearby forest and meet me at the Weird Hunter's Spring. There, I will guide you through the woods."

Armed with this contingency plan, Gerard drew a breath he hadn't known he needed. He returned to Margaret, who stood trembling before the oak tree, watching every branch as if expecting an arrow to strike. Gerard began digging a deep hole, burying the parchments one by one. Most were charters and records of the burgh, but one document caught his eye—a private deed between Floris Brandt, father of Peter, and Ghysbrecht.

"Why, this is as much yours as his," Gerard noted, reaching for it. "I will read this."

"Oh, not now, Gerard! Not now!" Margaret cried, her voice rising in panic. "Every moment you waste fills me with terror. Look—the rain begins to fall in great drops, and the clouds lower!"

Yielding to her urgent reprimand, Gerard shoved the deed into his bosom, covering the others with earth and stamping the soil down firmly. Just as he worked, a flash of lightning illuminated the sky, followed immediately by a roll of distant thunder, and the rain descended in sheets. Margaret and Gerard fled into the house, swiftly followed by Martin.

"The road is clear," Martin reported, "but a heavy storm is brewing."

His prophecy proved accurate. Thunder grew louder, crashing overhead like a collapsing roof; flashes strobed in rapid succession, striking like the lashes of a whip, while rain poured in torrents. Margaret buried her face, refusing to witness the violence of the heavens. Inside, Gerard raised the rough shutter and lit a candle. The lovers conferred in the flickering light, Gerard blessing the storm that granted them a few stolen hours together. The sun set unnoticed, swallowed by the darkness, yet the thunder continued to peal and the lightning to flash relentlessly.

Supper was laid out, yet neither could eat; the knowledge that this might be their final meal together choked them. As the storm momentarily lulled, Peter retired to rest, but Gerard and Margaret could not afford sleep. Martin lingered, carefully fitting a new string to his bow, a task requiring his utmost precision. The lovers murmured their sorrows and their undying love beside him.

Suddenly, Martin raised a hand, signaling for silence. The room fell quiet, but the air remained thick with tension. Then, a footstep cracked faintly upon the autumn leaves scattered across the garden behind the house. To those unburdened by fear, such a sound is trivial; to those hunted, it is a death knell. It was a foot attempting to be noiseless.

Martin nocked an arrow and snuffed out the candle. In that sudden darkness, to their horror, they heard more than one footstep approaching the other door of the cottage—not quite as silent as the first, but moving with deliberate stealth—and then, a dead, suffocating pause. Their blood froze in their veins.

"Oh, Kate, oh, Kate! You said fly on the instant!" Margaret moaned, wringing her hands in anguish, terror, and wild remorse for having delayed Gerard's departure.

"Hush, girl!" Martin whispered, his tone stern and commanding.

A heavy knock fell upon the door, resonating not just against the wood, but directly upon the hearts within.

The back door yielded to a blow delivered with the crude finality of a gavel strike, sealing their fate within the confines of the house. Yet, amidst the thunderous assault, Margaret seemed to reclaim a fragment of her composure. With a whisper that cut through the chaos, she commanded, "Tell them he was here, but is gone." Seizing Gerard, she nearly dragged him up the rough-hewn steps leading to her father's chamber, her own resting just beyond. The pounding intensified, a relentless rhythm demanding entry. "Who dares knock at this hour?" Martin retorted, his voice steady despite the intrusion. "Open, and you shall see!" came the demand. "I yield not to thieves—honest men sleep now." "Open to the law, Martin Wittenhaagen, or face the consequences." The voice belonged to Dirk Brower. "What brings you so far from Tergou?" "Open, and you shall know." Slowly, deliberately, Martin drew the bolt. In surged Dierich and four others, letting in their companion who had entered through the rear. "Now, Martin, where is Gerard Eliassoen?" "Gerard Eliassoen? He was here but moments ago!" "Was he?" Dierich's face fell, the color draining from his features. "And where is he now?" "They say he has fled to Italy. Pray, what is to be done?" "It matters not. When did he leave? Do not tell me he departed in such a storm!" "A coil indeed surrounds Gerard Eliassoen," Martin sneered, his tone dripping with disdain. He lit the candle, seated himself calmly beside the fire, and began to whet a fine silk cord around his bowstring at the point where the arrow's notch fretted it. "I shall inform you," he said casually. "Do you know his brother Giles? A misbegotten imp, all head and limbs? Well, he tore over here on a mule, bellowing words I could not catch from my distance, only the noise itself. Regardless, he startled Gerard. Immediately after his departure, there was such weeping and kissing, and then Gerard vanished. They claim he has gone to Italy—perhaps you know where that lies, for I do not." Dierich's expression sank further at this report. No flaw marred his tale; a more cunning man might have spun too many lies and invited suspicion, but Martin executed his duty perfectly. He uttered only the single falsehood he was instructed to speak, inventing nothing else from his own mind. "Comrades," Dierich murmured, "I fear he speaks truth. I warned the burgomaster how this would unfold. He encountered the dwarf galloping Peter Buyskens's mule from Sevenbergen. 'They have sent that imp to Gerard,' he declared, 'so Gerard must be at Sevenbergen.' 'Ah, master!' I replied, 'It is too late now. We should have sought Sevenbergen before wasting our time hunting the obscure corners of Tergou for those cursed parchments we shall never find until we locate the man who stole them. If he was at Sevenbergen, and they dispatched the dwarf to him, it must have been to warn him we hunt him. He is leagues away by now,' I concluded. Confound that chalk-faced girl! She has outwitted us bearded men; and so I told the burgomaster, yet he refused to hear reason. A wet jerkin apiece—that is all we shall gain from this venture." Martin grinned coolly in Dierich's face. "However," the latter added, "to satisfy the burgomaster, we must search the house." Martin's demeanor shifted instantly to gravity. This transformation did not escape Dierich's notice. He paused, reflecting. "Station two of you outside, one on each flank of the house, to ensure no one leaps from the upper windows. The rest follow me." Taking the candle, he ascended the stairs, trailed by three comrades. Martin was left alone. The stout soldier bowed his head. Everything had proceeded smoothly at first; now came this fatal reversal. Suddenly, a spark of hope ignited. Gerard must be in Peter's room or Margaret's; neither was excessively high from the ground. Gerard would leap. Dierich had left a guard below, but what then? For half a minute, Gerard and he would stand two to one, and in that fleeting interval, anything might occur. Martin held the back door ajar and watched. Light spilled into Peter's room. "Curse the fool!" he muttered. "Is he allowing them to capture him like a maiden?" The illumination drifted into Margaret's bedroom. Still, no window opened. Had Gerard intended to flee that way, he would not have waited until the men occupied the room. Martin grasped this immediately and descended to the foot of the stairs, listening intently. He began to believe Gerard must have escaped via the window while the men searched the interior. The longer the silence persisted, the stronger this conviction grew. Then, it was shattered abruptly and rudely. Faint cries emanated from the inner bedroom—Margaret's. "They have taken him," Martin groaned. "They have secured him." Instantly, it flashed across his mind that if they removed Gerard, his life would be worthless; and if harm befell him, Margaret's heart would shatter. Casting his eyes wildly around like a savage beast seeking an exit, he formed a resolution in a twinkling, terribly characteristic of those iron times and a soldier driven to the horns. He moved to each door in turn, mimicking Dierich Brower's voice sharply, "Watch the window!" Then, quietly, he closed and bolted both doors. He picked up his bow and six arrows; fitting one to the string, he stowed the others in his quiver. His knife he placed upon a chair behind him, hilt facing inward, and there he waited at the foot of the stairs with the calm determination to slay those four men, or perish by their hands. Two, he knew, he could dispatch with his arrows before they could approach, leaving Gerard and him to face the remaining pair hand-to-hand. Moreover, he had witnessed men panic-stricken by such a sudden assault. Should Brower and his men hesitate even an instant before engaging him, he would shoot three instead of two, tipping the odds decisively in their favor. He need not wait long. Heavy footsteps echoed in Margaret's room, drawing nearer and nearer. The light advanced, accompanied by voices. Martin's heart, though stout, hammered violently to hear men marching thus to their deaths—and perhaps his own; more likely so than not: for four against one in a battlefield ten feet square was long odds, and Gerard might be bound, powerless to aid. Yet this man, whom we have seen tremble in his shoes at the mere mention of a Giles-o'-lantern, never wavered in this awful moment of peril. He stood there, body braced for combat, eye glowing, equally prepared to take life and lose it. A desperate gamble! To win meant instant exile for life; to lose meant death upon that very floor. Dierich Brower and his men discovered Peter in the first stages of sleep. They ransacked his cupboards, drove knives into an alligator he had nailed to his wall, and peered beneath his bed. It was a spacious room, seemingly replete with hiding places, yet Gerard was nowhere to be found. Proceeding to Margaret's room, the sight proved disheartening—it was small and bare, devoid of cupboards, save for a large fireplace and chimney. Dierich's eye fixed immediately upon these. Here they found the beauty of Sevenbergen sleeping on an old chest scarcely a foot high, unshielded by any covering; yet the sheets were snow-white, as was Margaret's own linen. There she lay, resembling a lily fallen into a rut. Soon she awoke, sitting upright in bed, amazed; then, spotting the intruders, she began to scream faintly, praying for mercy. Her plea made Dierich Brower ashamed of his mission. "Here is a disturbance," he said, slightly flustered. "We intend no harm to you, my pretty maid. Lie still, shut your eyes, and dream of your wedding night while I inspect this chimney to see if Master Gerard hides therein." "Gerard! In my room?" "Why not? They say you and he—" "Cruel! You know they have driven him away from me—expelled him from his native land. This is a ruse. You are thieves; you are wicked men; you are not men of Sevenbergen, or you would know Margaret Brandt better than to seek her lover in this room of all others in the world. Oh, brave! Four great hulking men, armed to the teeth, to insult one poor honest girl! The women dwelling in your own homes must be naught, or you would respect them too much to defile a girl of good character." "Enough! Depart, before we hear worse," Dierich hastened, saying. "He is not in the chimney. Plaster may mend what a cudgel breaks, but a woman's tongue is a double-edged dagger, and a girl retains her mother's milk." He beat a hasty retreat. "I told the burgomaster how this would play out."

Where is the woman who cannot perform the role demanded of her? Where is she who refuses to play the part, yet fails to execute it with the necessary artifice to save the life of the man she adores? In moments of such catastrophic gravity, nature itself seems to conspire against the simplicity of the female spirit, forcing her to scatter dust in the eyes of Solomon, blinding reason with the grit of despair.

As the male figures withdrew, retreating into the shadows of their own rooms and descending the stairwell, Margaret rose from the bed. She moved with a fluid, ghostly grace, stripping away the layers of her disguise—the skirt, the petticoats, the stockings, the nightgown that had served as her shroud. She pushed the heavy chest, laden with linens and her own form, against the far wall until it stood flush with the timber. Then, she glided to the threshold, pressing her ear to the wood, listening for the echo of footsteps. Silence answered her; the sounds of the household had faded into the night.

Within that chest lay a deception so profound it defied detection by any eye unaccustomed to the mechanics of survival. A section of the floorboards above had been fractured, and when Gerard had requested repairs to restore the room's dignity, lacking proper materials, he had ingeniously cut away a precise aperture. This opening was sized exactly to accommodate Margaret's slender frame, allowing her to slide beneath the planks while the stolen wood sealed the gap behind her. The chest itself, appearing merely a foot deep, actually rested on the rafters two feet below the surface, creating a hollow tomb disguised as furniture.

Now, under the cover of absolute stillness, Margaret knelt and offered a silent prayer to the heavens. Rising, she crept to the edge of the hidden cavity and whispered, tenderly, "Gerard."

There was no response. She pressed her voice slightly higher, desperation creeping into her tone. "Gerard, all is safe. Thank Heaven! You may rise; but oh, be cautious!"

Silence hung heavy in the air. She placed her trembling hand upon his shoulder. "Gerard!"

Still nothing. Panic, cold and sharp, pierced her resolve. "Oh, what is this?" she cried, her fingers flying wildly across his face, searching his chest for a pulse. She gripped his shoulders, shaking him violently, lifting him as if he weighed nothing more than a bundle of rags. But he did not stir. He slid from her grasp, falling back not with the resistance of a living man, but with the final, weightless collapse of a corpse.

A primal dread seized her. The lid had been down. She had lain upon him. The intruders had occupied the room for some time. With the frenzied strength of a cornered animal, she tore him from the darkness of the chest and dragged him to the window. She flung the sash wide, inviting the cool, fragrant night air to rush in. She laid him there, exposed to the silver wash of the moonlight.

His complexion was the color of ash; his limbs were slack, utterly devoid of motion. She pressed her palm to his breast. Horror! It was as still as death itself. The horror of horrors washed over her: she had suffocated him with her own body. The human mind struggles to accept such a sudden, grotesque calamity. Gerard, who had barely managed to squeeze into that cramped confinement mere minutes prior, how could he already be dead?

She called out to him using every endearing name her heart could conjure, every phrase her tongue could frame. She kissed him, caressed him, coaxed him, imploring him to speak. There was no answer to words of love she had never dared utter before, nor ones she believed capable of escape. Trembling violently, she began to babble over his ashen features, words that were simultaneously terrible and pitiful.

"Oh, Gerard! I am so sorry you are dead. I am so sorry I have killed you. Forgive me for not letting the men take you; it would have been kinder than this. Oh, Gerard! I am very, very sorry for what I have done."

Suddenly, her composure shattered into madness. "No! No! Such things cannot be, or there is no God. It is monstrous! How can my Gerard be dead? How can I have killed my Gerard? I love him. Oh, God! You know how I love him. He does not. I never told him. If he knew my heart, he would speak to me; he would not be so deaf to his poor Margaret. Is it all a trick to make me cry out and betray him? But no! I love him too well for that. I'll choke myself first."

She seized her own throat, silencing the wild urge to scream in her terror and anguish. "If he would but say one word. Oh, Gerard! Don't die without a word. Have mercy on me and scold me, but speak to me! If you are angry with me, scold me! Curse me! I deserve it: the idiot who killed the man she loved better than herself. Ah, I am a murderess. The worst in all the world. Help! Help! I have murdered him. Ah! Ah! Ah! Ah! Ah!"

She tore at her hair, unleashing shrieks so wild, so piercing, they rang out like funeral knells, striking the ears of Dierich Brower and his men. Instantly, the group sprang to their feet, turning in unison, their faces illuminated by the shock of the sound.

Martin Wittenhaagen stood poised at the base of the staircase, a figure of taut tension, his bowstring pulled tight enough to snap the air, the arrowhead hovering inches from the ceiling, while a dagger rested heavy against his hip. He froze, struck dumb by the absurdity of the scene unfolding above: the men returned, yet Gerard was nowhere to be seen. With a jolt of bewildered realization, he lowered the weapon, his mouth agape, staring upward at the group who now regarded him with equal parts confusion and suspicion.

"Why, mates, was the old fellow making ready to shoot at us?" someone demanded, their voice laced with disbelief.

"Stuff!" Martin retorted, forcing his stolid composure back into place like armor settling over skin. "I was merely testing my new string. There! If you deem it necessary, I will unstring the bow myself."

"Humph!" Dierich grunted, his eyes narrowing with a predatory suspicion that cut deeper than any blade. "There is something more in you than I understand. Put a log on, and let us dry our hides a bit ere we go."

A roaring fire erupted quickly, casting dancing shadows against the rough timber walls. The men clustered around its warmth, the smoke rising from their wet garments and tangled, long hair mingling with the heat. It was amidst this crackling intimacy that the shrieks tore through Margaret's chamber—a sound so primal it shattered the moment. Every head snapped upward. One man snatched the candle, sprinting up the narrow steps toward the sleeping quarters. Martin surged forward, driven by a sudden, visceral dread of the man's intrusive curiosity, attempting to bar his path. But Dierich moved with the speed of a coiled spring, throwing his arms around Martin's waist from behind, shouting orders for the others to hold him fast.

The candle-bearer slipped past the blockade, and the remaining men descended upon Martin. What followed was a chaotic, violent tangle of limbs and fury. For a long, fierce struggle, they rolled across the floorboards, a writhing mass of desperation, with Martin pinned in the center, fighting with the strength of a cornered lion. Yet, they eventually subdued the old Samson, binding his hands and feet tightly with a rope intended for Gerard. Martin groaned aloud, a raw sound of defeat, realizing with horror that the intruder had already breached the sanctuary of Margaret's room while they fought. Now, he was powerless, trapped in the very space he sought to protect.

"Ay, grind your teeth, you old rogue," Dierich panted, his chest heaving from the exertion, his voice dripping with triumph. "You shan't use them."

"It is my belief, mates, that our lives were scarce safe while this old fellow's bones were free," another man observed, wiping sweat from his brow.

"He makes me think this Gerard is not far off," a third added, his tone shifting to one of anxious speculation.

"No such luck," Dierich replied sharply, cutting short the hope. "Hallo, mates. Jorian Ketel has been in that girl's bedroom a long time. Best go and see after him, some of us."

The crude laughter sparked by this remark had barely died away when the sound of hasty footsteps echoed overhead, racing along the gallery.

"Oh, here he comes, at last," Dierich announced, turning toward the stairwell. "Well, Jorian, what is to do now up there?"

Jorian Ketel pressed forward without hesitation, driven by a singular purpose, until he reached the threshold of Margaret's chamber. There, however, the reality of his quest shattered against a tableau of profound stillness. In his place, he found the very man he sought, Gerard, rendered ghostly and inert, his head resting in the lap of Margaret. She knelt over him, a figure carved from marble by grief, utterly mute and petrified. Her eyes, once vibrant, were now dilated pools of glass, reflecting neither the flickering candlelight nor the presence of the intruder; she possessed no awareness of the earthly realm save for the pallid countenance nestled beneath her hands.

Jorian stood transfixed, the candle trembling violently in his grip, casting dancing shadows that seemed to mock the silence. "Where was he, then, all this time?" he demanded, his voice breaking the heavy air. Margaret did not stir, her spirit suspended in a paralysis of terror. Ignoring her, Jorian approached the empty chest, his mind racing to connect the dots of the unfolding tragedy. As he examined the hollow space, comprehension dawned upon him, heavy and grim. The girl's dumb, frozen despair struck him with the force of a physical blow. "This is a sorry sight," he muttered, the weight of the situation settling on his shoulders. "It is a black night's work—all for a few skins! Far better to have gone with us than to end thus. She is past answering me, poor wretch."

He paused, his instinct shifting from observation to action. "Stop! Let us try whether—" Reaching out, he retrieved a small, circular mirror, no larger than his palm, and pressed it gently against Gerard's lips and nostrils, holding it steady against the stillness. When he withdrew it, the surface remained dull, devoid of the mist of exhalation. Yet, in that moment of inspection, a spark ignited within Jorian. "THERE IS LIFE IN HIM!" he cried out, the realization exploding from his chest.

The words, though spoken aloud, seemed to bypass the ears and strike directly at Margaret's consciousness. Instantly, she reacted, a low mutter escaping her lips, as if a statue suddenly infused with blood and passion. With a surge of energy that defied her previous rigidity, she rose and flung her arms around Jorian's neck. "Oh, bless the tongue that tells me so!" she exclaimed, clutching the large, rough-hewn man with an intensity that bordered on ferocity. "There, there! Let us lay him warm," Jorian urged, guiding her frantic movements. In a heartbeat, he lifted Gerard and deposited him onto the bedclothes.

From his belt, Jorian produced a flask, pouring a measure of Schiedamse brandy into his hand twice, and flinging the pungent liquor sharply into Gerard's face. The harsh alcohol acted as a catalyst for recovery; Gerard gave a faint, ragged sigh. Never had such a sound been more joyful to the human ear. Margaret lunged toward him, but halted abruptly, quivering with the fear that her touch might crush the fragile life returning to him. She had lost all confidence in her own strength. "That is right—let him alone," Jorian instructed firmly. "Do not go cuddling him as you did me, or you'll drive his breath back again. Let him alone: he is sure to come to. 'Tisn't like as if he was an old man."

Gerard sighed deeply, and a faint streak of color began to steal across his lips. Seizing the opportunity, Jorian made for the door. Hardly had he reached the threshold when his legs were seized from behind. It was Margaret! She curled around his knees like a serpent, kissing his hand and fawning upon him with desperate devotion. "You won't tell? You have saved his life; you have not the heart to thrust him back into his grave, to undo your own good work?"

"No, no! It is not the first time I have done you two a good turn; 'twas I told you in the church whither we had to take him," Jorian replied smoothly. "Besides, what is Dierich Brower to me? I'll see him hanged ere I'll tell him. But I wish you'd tell me where the parchments are! There are a hundred crowns offered for them. That would be a good windfall for my Joan and the children, you know."

"Ah! They shall have those hundred crowns," Jorian promised. "What! Are the things in the house?" he asked eagerly.

"No; but I know where they are; and by God and St. Bavon I swear you shall have them tomorrow. Come to me for them when you will, but come alone."

"I were made else," Jorian declared, his resolve hardening. "What! Share the hundred crowns with Dirk Brower? And now may my bones rot in my skin if I let a soul know the poor boy is here."

With that, he bolted from the room, fleeing lest any further delay arouse suspicion and draw the authorities to them both. Left alone, Margaret knelt, quivering from head to toe, praying fervently beside Gerard and for Gerard.

Outside, the dynamic shifted again. "What is to do?" Dierich Brower queried, his tone laced with suspicion. "Why, we have scared the girl out of her wits. She was in a kind of fit."

"We had better all go and doctor her, then," someone suggested.

"Oh, yes! And frighten her into the churchyard," Jorian countered with an offhand ease that disarmed all doubt. "Her father is a doctor, and I have roused him, and set him to bring her round. Let us see the fire, will ye?"

His casual manner worked wonders, dissolving the tension. Soon, the group agreed that the kitchen of the "Three Kings" offered far greater warmth than Peter's house, and they departed, having first untied Martin. At the door, Dierich Brower remarked, "Take note, mate, that I was right, and the burgomaster wrong; I said we should be too late to catch him, and we were too late."

Thus, Gerard escaped the twin terrors of prison and grave. How did he achieve freedom? Not through cunningly contrived hiding places nor Margaret's ready wit, but by a sudden impulse in one of his captors—a fragment of humanity remaining in a somewhat reckless heart, aided by a desire for gain. So mixed and seemingly incongruous are human motives, so shortsighted our shrewdest counsels. Those whose moderate natures or gentle fates keep them from the fierce extremes of joy and anguish that our nature is capable of, are perhaps the best, and certainly the happiest of mankind. But to such readers, I should try in vain to convey the bliss unspeakable that now settled upon these persecuted lovers. Even to those who have enjoyed greatly and suffered greatly, my feeble art can present but a pale reflection of Margaret's and Gerard's ecstasy. To sit and watch a beloved face return from the grave to the world, to health and beauty, by swift gradations; to see the roses return to the loved cheek, love's glance to the loved eye, and his words to the loved mouth—this was Margaret's joy, a happiness to balance years of sorrow. It was Gerard's to awaken from a trance, finding his head pillowed on Margaret's arm, to hear the woman he adored murmur new words of eloquent love, and to be showered with tears, tender kisses, and caresses. He never knew, until this sweet moment, how ardently, how tenderly, she loved him. He thanked his enemies. They wreathed their arms sweetly round each other, and trouble and danger seemed a world, an age behind them. They called each other husband and wife. Were they not solemnly betrothed? Had they not stood before the altar together? Was not the blessing of Holy Church upon their union—and her curse on all who would part them?

But as no woman's nerves can bear with impunity so terrible a strain, presently Margaret turned faint and sank on Gerard's shoulder, smiling feebly but quite, quite unstrung. Then Gerard grew anxious and sought to seek assistance. But she held him with a gentle grasp, imploring him not to leave her for a moment. "While I can lay my hand on you, I feel you are safe, not else. Foolish Gerard! Nothing ails me. I am weak, dearest, but happy, oh! so happy!"

Then it was Gerard's turn to support that dear head, with its great waves of hair flowing loose over him, to nurse her and soothe her, quivering on his bosom, with soft encouraging words and murmurs of love and gentle caresses. Sweetest of all her charms is a woman's weakness to a manly heart. Poor things! They were happy. Tomorrow they must part. But that was nothing to them now. They had seen Death, and all other troubles seemed light as air. While there is life there is hope; while there is hope there is joy. Separation for a year or two, what was it to them, who were so young, and had caught a glimpse of the grave? The future was bright, the present was Heaven: so passed the blissful hours. Alas! Their innocence ran other risks besides the prison and the grave. They were in most danger from their own hearts and their inexperience, now that visible danger there was none.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten was consumed by a feverish insomnia, his mind trapped in a loop of dread that refused to yield to rest. A superstitious terror held him fast; he feared the very elements that now raged outside—the thunder and lightning—and thus, paralyzed by this irrational awe, he had withheld himself from joining the party scouring Peter's dwelling. Yet, as the storm's fury finally subsided into a trembling silence, the urgency seized him. He crept downstairs, saddled his mule with trembling haste, and galloped to the "Three Kings" inn at Sevenbergen.

There, he found his men slumbering in a chaotic tableau of exhaustion: some sprawled across chairs, others atop tables, and a few curled directly upon the floorboards. Ghysbrecht roused them with furious shouts, demanding answers for their failure. Their responses were a disjointed tapestry of unsuccessful searching, clumsily interwoven with boasts of their own zealousness.

"Fool! To let you go without me!" the burgomaster cried, his voice cracking with indignation. "My life depended on him being there all along. Did you look under the girl's bed?"

"No; there was no room for a man there," one replied dismissively.

"How can you claim such certainty if you did not look?" Ghysbrecht snarled, his patience evaporating like mist before the sun. "You should have looked beneath her mattress, inside the folds of the bedding, and probed every panel with your knives until the wood sang. Come, rise now, and I shall teach you the art of searching."

Dierich Brower rose, shaking the dust from his clothes with a defiant posture. "If you find him, call me a horse and no man."

In moments, Peter's house was once more encircled by the group. Ghysbrecht, the fiery old man, dismounted and entrusted his mule to Jorian Ketel. Clad in determination, he stepped forward alongside Dierich Brower and the others, entering the silent, hollow shell of the home. Inside, nothing stirred. No creature moved, not even Peter himself. They ascended the stairs, the air thick with anticipation, when suddenly one of the men broke the tension with a sharp shout, pointing a finger through the open window of Peter's chamber.

The others turned, their eyes widening as they gazed outward. There, at a modest distance, walking calmly across the golden fields accompanied by Margaret and Martin, stood the fugitive they had hunted. Seizing the moment, Ghysbrecht erupted in an exultant yell. He scrambled down the stairs and threw himself onto his waiting mule. With a unified cry, he and his men spurred their animals, launching themselves into a hot, relentless pursuit.

Dawn broke not with a gentle light, but with the sharp, metallic tang of impending violence, waking Gerard before the sun had fully claimed the sky. He roused Martin, the old soldier, whose eyes widened in astonishment at the hour. In his mind, Gerard had already vanished through the window during the chaos of the previous night. Yet, reality demanded action. As they conferred on the only viable route to safety—a narrow passage through the great forest leading to a known bridle-road and a hostelry that might offer a swift steed—they moved with urgent precision. Their goal was clear: to cross the border of Holland before the populace stirred from their slumber.

Peter's dwelling stood but a short distance from the tree line. They departed immediately, Martin armed with his bow and three arrows, while Gerard carried only a stout oak staff, a gift from Peter for the journey ahead. Margaret, practical and quick, pinned up her kirtle and farthingale against the wetness of the road. Peter accompanied them only to the edge of his garden hedge, where, with an emotion rarely seen on his stoic face, he bestowed his blessing upon the young man.

As they crossed the stony field toward the wood, the sun merely peeked over the horizon, casting long, uncertain shadows. Halfway across, Margaret's composure shattered. Her eyes darted nervously backward, catching something unseen, and a cry tore from her throat. Driven by primal instinct, she turned and sprinted toward the forest, screaming with terror. Behind her, the thunderous hoofbeats of Ghysbrecht and his men echoed like judgment. Resistance was folly; flight was the only option. Martin and Gerard followed suit, their hearts pounding in unison. Though the pursuers gained ground, Martin's shouts pierced the rising panic: "Only win the wood! Only win the wood!"

Hope surged within them. With their superior start, the great trees seemed to stretch their branches like welcoming arms, their leaves forming a living shield against the open field. But nature offered no sanctuary from human malice. The fiery old burgomaster, Ghysbrecht, flung himself upon his mule, spurring the beast into a gallop that defied reason. He did not pursue his own men alone; he targeted the fugitives. His intent was to cut them off, to crush them between his force and the timberline.

Ghysbrecht rode in a wide semicircle, positioning himself directly before Gerard, leaving the others exposed to mercy. Margaret shrieked, throwing herself against Gerard in a futile attempt to shield him, but he shook her off without ceremony, driven by a singular focus. In his ardor, Ghysbrecht forgot the ancient truth that hunted beasts turn upon their hunters, nor did he consider that hatred breeds a reciprocal desire to destroy. Expecting Gerard to dodge, the burgomaster prepared to strike. Instead, Gerard flew straight at him, issuing a savage, exulting cry. With all the heart, soul, and strength he possessed, he swung the oak staff.

The weapon descended upon Ghysbrecht's face with a frightful crash. The impact knocked the old man beneath his own mule, which beat the devil's tattoo with its heels as it stumbled forward. Blood streamed from the burgomaster's face, spattering his collar, marking the end of his command. In that same instant, the three fugitives vanished into the tree line. The yells of dismay and vengeance that erupted from Ghysbrecht's men signaled a shift from pursuit to a desperate race for survival.

"Why run?" Gerard panted, turning to his companions. "You have your bow, and I have this," shaking the blood-stained staff.

"Boy!" Martin roared, his voice cutting through the din. "The GALLOWs! Follow me!" He fled deeper into the woods.

Soon, the baying of dogs, like a pack tearing at fresh meat, filled the air. The pursuers had entered the wood, spotting the fugitives flitting among the trunks. Margaret moaned and panted, her lungs burning, while Gerard clenched his teeth, gripping his staff until his knuckles turned white. They reached a stiff hazel coppice, where Martin dashed in, shouldering aside the young wood as if it were standing corn. Within fifty yards, they faced four blind paths. Martin chose one, commanding, "Bend low." They glided along, half-creeping, until their path intersected with other tortuous trails. Taking one, they expected to circle back, but the path twisted, eventually depositing them in a thick pine grove where the walking was hard and the visibility nil. The young fir-trees grew so densely that one could not see three yards ahead.

Exhaustion finally claimed Martin. Having learned in war to dissociate from danger amidst its presence, he sat down, pulled a piece of bread and a slice of ham from his wallet, and began to eat his breakfast calmly. The younger pair stared at him with dismay.

"All Sevenbergen could not find you now," Martin said, chewing deliberately. "You will lose your purse, Gerard, long before you reach Italy; is that the way to carry a purse?"

Gerard looked down. Entangled by its chains to the buckle and strap of his wallet hung a large triangular purse. "This is none of mine," he muttered, attempting to free it. But the friction of the coppice had rendered it inextricable.

"It seems loath to leave me," Gerard sighed, resorting to his knife to sever the connection. Upon examination, the purse was well-provided with silver coins of various sizes, though its bloated appearance was largely due to numerous folded sheets of brown paper. A light dawned in Gerard's eyes. "Why, it must be that old thief's! See! Stuffed with paper to deceive the world!"

The mystery of its acquisition solved itself quickly. The purse had rested at Ghysbrecht's saddle-bow; in his rush to strike, Gerard had unconsciously torn it away, felling his enemy and robbing him in a single, fatal gesture. Delighted by this feat, Gerard hesitated to discard the spoils.

"Throw it away, Gerard, or let Martin take it back," Margaret urged, her voice trembling. "Already they call you a thief. I cannot bear it."

"Throw it away? Give it back? Not a stiver!" Gerard retorted fiercely. "This is spoil lawfully won in battle from an enemy. Is it not, Martin?"

"Of course," Martin agreed readily. "Send him back the brown paper, but the purse or the coin—that were a sin."

"Oh, Gerard!" Margaret pleaded. "You are going to a distant land. We need the goodwill of Heaven. How can we hope for that if we take what is not ours?"

But Gerard viewed the situation through a different lens. "It is Heaven that gives it me by a miracle, and I shall cherish it accordingly," he declared, invoking scripture. "Thus the favored people spoiled the Egyptians, and were blessed."

"Take your own way," Margaret submitted humbly, recognizing his resolve. "You are wiser than I am. You are my husband," she added softly, "is it for me to gainsay you?"

These words, coming from a woman who had previously held the whip-hand, surprised Martin momentarily. Later, they would surprise him less. Gerard kissed her tenderly for her wife-like docility, and they continued their journey hand in hand, led by Martin into the depths of the massive forest.

With every step, the feeling of security grew. The townspeople never ventured so far into the trackless wilds. Impetuous natures repent quickly, and Gerard, once clear of immediate danger, felt his conscience prickle.

"Martin, would I had not struck quite so hard," he confessed.

"Whom? Oh! Let that pass; he is cheap served," Martin dismissed.

"I saw his grey hairs as my stick fell on him," Gerard insisted, haunted by the image. "I doubt they will not fade from my sight this while."

Martin grunted with contempt. "Who spares a badger for his grey hairs? The greyer your enemy is, the older; and the older the craftier, and the craftier the better for a little killing."

"Killing? Killing, Martin? Speak not of killing!" Gerard shuddered.

"I am much mistaken if you have not," Martin replied cheerfully. "Now Heaven forbid!"

"The old vagabond's skull cracked like a walnut. Aha!"

"Heaven and the saints forbid it!"

"He rolled off his mule like a stone shot out of a cart. Said I to myself, 'There is one wiped out,'" Martin grinned ruthlessly.

Overcome by guilt, Gerard fell to his knees, praying fervently for his enemy's life. Martin's patience snapped.

"Here's mummery. What! You that set up for learning, know you not that a wise man never strikes his enemy but to kill him? And what is all this coil about killing of old men? If it had been a young one, now, with the joys of life waiting for him, wine, women, and pillage! But an old fellow at the edge of the grave, why not shove him in? Go he must, today or tomorrow; and what better place for greybeards? Now, if ever I should be so mischancy as to last so long as Ghysbrecht did, and have to go on a mule's legs instead of Martin Wittenhaagen's, and a back like this (striking the wood of his bow) instead of this (striking the

Courage, much like talent in the common sort, flows within a rigid channel; push it but an inch beyond its banks, and the vessel breaks. Martin's fortitude was absolute within the confines of his experience. He had weathered storms and faced perils that hardened the spine of any rustic soul, confronting them with a Spartan indifference born of familiarity. Yet, here, the landscape shifted beneath his boots. He had never been hunted by a bloodhound, nor witnessed that beast's unerring, primal instinct thwarted by human guile. Now, the supernatural merged with the novel to unravel his composure. Within moments, he leaned heavily upon his bow, his vitality draining away like water through cracked earth.

Gerard, whose perception of danger was inversely proportional to its proximity, urged flight. "What avail does it serve?" Martin murmured, his voice heavy with resignation. "If we flee the woods, we die cheap; here, amidst the cover, we may die with dignity."

"Alas, good Martin," Gerard cried, panic edging his tone, "do not surrender to despair so swiftly; surely there is a path to escape."

"Oh, Martin!" Margaret interjected, her voice trembling yet sharp with resolve. "What if we were to part ways? Gerard's life stands forfeit alone. Is there no strategy to draw the pursuit upon us both, leaving him free to go?"

"Girl, you misunderstand the bloodhound's nature," Martin replied, his head sinking low. "It does not track this man or that; it tracks blood. From the trail of Ghysbrecht to the corpse, and from the corpse to the man who spilled the blood, that cursed hound will lead them inevitably. Even if Gerard ran through an army or swam the Meuse itself, the beast would find him."

Once more, Martin leaned on his bow, his spirit crushed. Through the canopy, the hound's mellow, resonant bark echoed—a sound so tuneful it seemed designed to lull rather than terrify. *Strange,* the thought lingered, *that beauty should be so terrible.* Consider the eye of the boa constrictor: it fascinates its prey with a loveliness that paralyzes, a ruby set with the emerald's green fire. Yet the deer, seeing it, loses all motion, trembling in anticipation of death. So too did this sweet, mellifluous sound ensnare Martin Wittenhaagen. He stood bewildered, unnerved, paralyzed by the sweetness of the doom approaching. Gerard, too, faltered; Martin's grim prophecy had struck him dumb. He had struck an old man, shed his blood, and now, by that very crimson stain, the four-legged avenger of vengeance closed in. Was not the finger of Heaven pressing down upon them?

While the men were numbed by dread, the woman's mind ignited with frantic clarity. Her love hung in the balance.

"Lend me your knife," she commanded Martin.

He handed it over. "It will be little use in your hands," he warned.

Margaret performed a deed of sleight-of-hand. Stepping behind Gerard, she drew the blade across her forearm, letting it bleed freely. Stooping low, she smeared the blood onto her hose and shoes, moving with such adroitness that neither Gerard nor Martin perceived the act until the red trickle ceased. Seizing the soldier's arm, she spoke urgently. "Come, be a man! Let this end. Take us to some thick copse where numbers mean nothing to our foes."

"I am going," Martin grumbled sullenly. "Hurrying serves no purpose; we cannot outrun the hound, and the place is nearby." Turning left, he led them forward, walking as men walk to execution.

Soon, they reached a dense hazel coppice, reminiscent of the sanctuary they had sought earlier. "Here," Martin declared, "it spans but a furlong, yet it will suffice."

"What is our plan?"

"Pass through this, wait on the other side. As they emerge straggling, shoot three, club two, and the rest will finish us off."

"That is all you can devise?" Gerard challenged.

"That is all."

"Then, Martin Wittenhaagen, I shall take the lead; you have lost your wits. Can you obey a youth like myself?"

"Oh, yes, Martin," Margaret cried, seizing the opportunity. "Do not gainsay Gerard! He is wiser than his years."

Martin yielded a reluctant assent. "Do as I see you do," Gerard instructed. Drawing his massive knife, he chopped hazel shoots close to the ground at every step, twisting them upright behind him to form a living wall among the standing trunks. Martin mimicked the action, though his movements carried a dogged, hopeless weight.

As they painfully navigated the majority of the coppice, the bloodhound's deep bay grew louder, losing its melodic quality to become stern and relentless. Margaret trembled visibly. Martin dropped to his stomach, listening intently. "I hear horse's hooves."

"No," Gerard corrected sharply. "I suspect a mule. That cursed Ghysbrecht is still alive; no other would pursue me with such bitter determination."

"Never strike your enemy unless you intend to slay him," Martin growled gloomily.

"I shall hit harder this time, if Heaven grants me the chance," Gerard vowed.

Finally, they emerged into an open woodland. The trees were large but widely spaced, offering no refuge. Now, mingled with the hound's bay, came a chorus of voices hooping and hallooing.

"The entire village hunts us," Martin stated flatly.

"I care not," Gerard retorted. "Listen, Martin. I have smoothed the track for the dog but roughened it for the men, allowing us to engage them separately. The hound will gain on the humans; once it exits the coppice, we must kill it."

"The hound? There are several."

"I hear but one."

"Aye! Only one speaks; the others run silent. Should the leader lose the scent, another will take up the cry. There will be at least two dogs, or devils wearing dog skins."

"Then we must kill two instead of one. The moment they fall, back into the coppice and reverse direction."

"That is a sound strategy, Gerard," Martin admitted, a spark returning to his eyes.

"Hush! The men are entering the wood."

Gerard whispered his orders. "You stand with your bow by the coppice edge, in the ditch. I shall move only a few yards to that oak and hide behind it. The dogs will follow me; shoot as many as you can as they exit; I will brain the rest as they circle the tree."

Martin's eyes flashed with renewed purpose. They assumed their positions. The hooping and hallooing drew nearer, accompanied by the rustling of young wood. Every now and then, the bloodhound issued a single, piercing bay. It was terrible—the branches brushing closer, the inevitable struggle for life tightening its grip, the death knell ringing out.

A trembling hand rested on Gerard's shoulder, causing him to start violently, his nerves fully strung. "Martin says if we are forced to separate, make for the high ash-tree we entered by."

"Yes! Yes! Yes! But go back, for Heaven's sake! Do not come here, exposed in the open!"

She turned to flee toward Martin, but before she could reach him, a huge dog burst from the coppice, standing erect for a heartbeat. Margaret cowered, but the beast ignored her entirely. Scent was to him what sight was to man. He lowered his nose for an instant, then, with an awful yell, launched himself straight at Gerard's hiding spot, rolling head-over-heels dead as a stone, impaled by an arrow from the bow that twanged beside the coppice in Martin's hand.

Simultaneously, a second hound emerged, sniffing his fallen comrade. Gerard lunged at it, but before he could raise his cudgel, a streak of white lightning seemed to strike the beast. It collapsed into the dust, mortally wounded but not dead, howling pitifully. Gerard lacked the time to dispatch it; the coppice rustled too dangerously close, seeming to breathe with life. Pointing wildly at Martin to retreat, Gerard sprinted a few yards to the right, creeping cautiously into the thick undergrowth just as three men burst forth. These had outpaced their companions significantly; the rest trailed at varying distances. Gerard crawled back almost on all fours. Instinct drove Martin and Margaret to mimic his movement along their line of retreat.

Within mere yards, pursuer and pursued passed each other on parallel tracks. A loud cry signaled the discovery of the dead and wounded hounds. This triggered a babble of voices, swelling as fresh hunters arrived. The hunters, caught off guard, wasted precious time; the hunted used it to their advantage.

"I hear no more hounds," Martin whispered to Margaret, regaining his composure.

"It was your turn to tremble and despair," she replied, her voice thin. "Oh, why did we leave Gerard? They will kill my Gerard, and I shall not be near him."

"Nay, nay! The head responsible for catching him lies elsewhere. Did you bid him meet us at the ash-tree?"

"And so I did. Bless you, Martin, for thinking of that. To the ash-tree!"

"Aye! But with less noise."

They approached the coppice's edge when suddenly, hooping and hallooing erupted behind them. The villagers, convinced

Speech is the familiar vent of human thought, yet there exist emotions so primal and overwhelming that they erupt not in articulate words, but in raw, eloquent cries. In such moments, man sheds his civilized veneer, revealing himself as nothing more than a higher beast; for when driven to the brink of agitation, even the most refined creatures reduce themselves to mere ejaculation, stripped of speech. There was something terrifyingly bestial in the triumphant roar that erupted from the thicket as Dierich's men burst forth upon their quarry, mirrored instantly by the piercing shriek of terror that drove Gerard and the mule into flight. The pursuers' hands clawed at the empty air, grasping at ghosts mere feet behind them, as they chased a phantom of survival. For a fleeting second, confused and disoriented like lions that have missed their spring, Dierich and his followers faltered, allowing Gerard and the beast to carve out a ten-yard sanctuary. Then, fueled by adrenaline, they surged forward with weapons raised, certain of capture. This was not the first time these factions had tested their mettle against one another; earlier that morning, in the open fields, they had visibly closed the gap on the trio. Now, however, fate had reset the board for a contest decided solely by velocity.

A hundred yards vanished in the blink of an eye, yet those crucial ten yards persisted, a stubborn barrier between hunter and hunted. This sudden acceleration baffled Dierich Brower. The truth lay in the mechanics of motion itself: when three flee together, their pace is dictated by the slowest link. From Peter's house to the forest's edge, Gerard had been tethered to Margaret's slower stride; now, unburdened by her presence, he ran with his own youthful vigor, aided by a mule capable of leaving the entire party in the dust. Furthermore, the benefits of youth and chastity began to manifest physically. Light began to bleed perceptibly into the space separating the hunted from the hunters. Desperate to close the distance, Dierich made a final, valiant effort, gaining two yards, only for Gerard to steal them back in a heartbeat. The pursuit dissolved into a chorus of curses. Martin heard them, and a spark ignited in his eyes. "Courage, Gerard! Courage, brave lad! They are breaking apart!" And indeed, the formation fractured. Dierich was now led by a single man, while another lagged entirely to the rear.

They ascended a long, gentle rise, and here, the advantages of youth, grit, and sober living proved insurmountable. Before reaching the summit, the weight of Dierich's forty years crushed him, feeling heavier than forty leaden bullets. "Our prey is turning to dough," he gasped, abandoning the sprint to follow at a walking pace. Jorian Ketel trailed off next, followed by others until one by one, Gerard ran them all to a halt, save for a solitary figure who pressed on with the relentless tenacity of a bloodhound, though steadily losing ground. His name, if my memory serves, was Eric Wouverman. Following him, they approached a sharper, steeper rise within the woods. "Hold fast to the mane!" Martin shouted. Gerard obeyed, and the mule propelled him upward with a speed surpassing his previous running. Witnessing this maneuver, Dierich's remaining follower lost his nerve. Now fully eighty yards behind Gerard and significantly ahead of his nearest comrade, he halted abruptly. Obedient to Dierich's silent command, he lowered his crossbow, leveled it with deliberate precision, and just as the trio disappeared over the crest of the hill, released the bolt. It whizzed through the air with deadly intent. A cry of dismay shattered the silence, and the next instant, as if struck by a thunderbolt, they all lay prone upon the earth—the men and the mule alike, silenced by the arrow.

The discharge was instantaneous, a sudden, magical rupture in the air that left the shooter momentarily stupefied. Only then did he hail his companions to join him in the pursuit, launching himself up the hill. But scarcely had he covered half the ascent when the silhouette of Martin Wittenhaagen emerged, a bent bow drawn taut in his hand. Upon seeing Martin poised thus, Eric Wouverman instinctively darted behind the nearest tree, shrinking his form until he was nothing more than a shadow against the bark. Martin's mastery with the weapon was legendary, and the lifeless dog lying nearby served as a grim testament to his lethal precision.

Wouverman peered cautiously around the rough hide of the tree; there, fixed in the wood, was the arrowhead, gleaming with a malevolent intent. It was aimed directly at him. Terrified, he dared not shift his position. Minutes passed in tense silence as he held his breath, waiting for the inevitable strike. Finally, his companions arrived in overwhelming numbers. With a derisive laugh, Martin vanished from sight, and shortly thereafter, the sound of his mule's hooves echoed as he rode away. The remaining hunters surged forward, only to find themselves outwitted and outrun.

From the commanding height, the glade stretched out—a narrow corridor seemingly without end. Far below, they spotted Gerard and Margaret sprinting across the grass, appearing no larger than gnats. Behind them thundered Martin, driving the mule *ventre à terre*, his legs pumping furiously. The hunters had been bested not merely by speed, but by cunning.

To understand Martin's audacious strategy, one must look beyond mere coincidence, for while chance often mimics design, it rarely orchestrates such precise chaos. Just as a startled hare might tumble over a briar precisely when a shot rings out, raising false hopes before vanishing unharmed, so too did fate intervene here. At the very moment the crossbow bolt whistled harmlessly overhead, the burgomaster's mule stepped squarely into a hidden rabbit-hole. She stumbled, collapsing under the combined weight of her riders. Gerard caught Margaret in his arms, but her momentum dragged him down with her, scattering the principal actors across the soil like fallen leaves.
