The docile mule rose immediately, trembling violently, while Martin scrambled up beside her. Seeing that only one pursuer remained, he executed a brilliant tactical pivot: he urged the terrified mule onward, forcing the young lovers to flee on foot, while he personally engaged the lone hunter. Once he had secured his companions, he mounted them both, running alongside the mule until his lungs burned, then swapping places with Gerard. In this relentless rotation, the runners never fatigued. Long before the exhausted hunters could recover, every trace of the fugitives was erased from the landscape. Defeated and crestfallen, Dierich and his men turned back, leaving behind their leader and his winged bloodhound to mourn their failure.

Life and liberty, when secure, are invisible luxuries, taken for granted until the shadow of loss reveals their true worth. Like sunlight, their brilliance is unnoticed at noon's zenith, only appreciated in the twilight when fractured rays of topaz dance beneath the elm branches, fragile yet vivid against the encroaching gloom. Thus, Gerard and Margaret, trembling at every rustle of leaves that sounded too harsh, radiated a luminous gratitude as they drifted through the sanctuary of the woods, the phantom echoes of baying hounds and brutal voices still haunting their minds.

Yet, the illusion shattered when Gerard spotted crimson stains blooming on Margaret's ankles. "Martin! Martin! Help! They have wounded her—the crossbow!" he screamed, his composure fracturing.

"No, no!" Margaret replied, offering a smile meant to soothe his terror. "I am neither wounded nor hurt."

"But what is it, then, in Heaven's name?" Gerard cried, agitated beyond measure.

"Do not scold me!" Margaret blushed, her voice trembling. "Did I ever scold you?"

"No, dear Gerard."

"Well, then," she confessed, her tone shifting to one of desperate explanation, "Martin said the cruel dogs followed the scent of blood. So, thinking if I could but offer them a little red upon my shoes, they might follow me instead, leaving my Gerard free to go. I scratched my arm with Martin's knife—forgive me! Whose else could I take? Yours, Gerard? Ah, no. You forgive me?" she pleaded, her expression a mixture of love, fear, and fawning submission.

"Let me see this scratch," Gerard choked out, unable to hold back his emotion.

"There," he breathed, recoiling slightly. "I thought so. A scratch? I call it a cut—a deep, terrible, cruel wound." He shuddered visibly at the sight.

"She might have done it with her bodkin," the soldier muttered, his voice dripping with disdain. "Milksop! That sickens at the mere sight of a scratch and a little blood."

"No, no," Margaret insisted softly. "I could stare upon a sea of blood, but not upon hers. Oh, Gerard! How could you be so cruel?"

Margaret smiled, a look of ineffable tenderness softening her features. "Foolish Gerard," she murmured, "to make such a fuss of nothing." She flung her guilty arm around his neck. "As if I would not give all the blood in my heart for you, let alone a few drops from my arm." Under the weight of his recent peril, she wept upon his shoulder, driven by pity and love; he wept with her.

"And I must part from her," he sobbed, the reality crashing down. "We two who love so dearly—one must remain in Holland, the other in Italy. Ah me! Ah me! Ah me!"

At this, Margaret wept anew, but her tears were patient and silent. Instinct, sharp and unerring, guarded her heart alongside her selflessness. To voice her thoughts now would only deepen Gerard's agony of departure, so she surrendered to silence.

Suddenly, they emerged onto a well-worn path, and Martin halted. "This is the bridle-road I spoke of," he said, hanging his head low. "And there, lying ahead, is the hostelry."

Margaret and Gerard exchanged terrified glances. "Come a step with me, Martin," Gerard whispered urgently.

Drawing the soldier aside, he spoke in a broken voice. "Good Martin, watch over her for me! She is my wife, yet I leave her. Look, Martin! Here is gold—it was for my journey. It serves no purpose to ask her to take it; she would refuse. But you will take it for her, will you not? Oh, Heaven! Is this all I can offer her? Money? But poverty is a curse. You will ensure she wants for nothing, dear Martin? The burgomaster's silver is sufficient for me."

"Thou art a good lad, Gerard," the soldier replied warmly. "Neither want nor harm shall come to her. I care more for her little finger than for all the world; and were she naught to me, even for thy sake, I would be a father to her. Go with a stout heart, and may God be with thee, going and coming."

With unusual emotion, the rough soldier wrung Gerard's hand and turned his head away. For a moment, he hesitated to return to Margaret, but Gerard stopped him. "No, good Martin; pray, stay here behind this thicket, and turn your head away from us, while I..."

By this ruse, Gerard escaped witnessing the raw anguish of leaving the woman he loved, and Martin spared himself the sight of their mutual devastation. He saw nothing of the poor souls kneeling to renew before Heaven those holy vows cruel men had interrupted. He witnessed no scene of them clinging together as one entity, struggling to part, failing, returning, and embracing again like drowning creatures grasping at straws. He heard only Gerard's relentless sobbing and Margaret's mournful moans.

Finally, a hoarse cry pierced the air, accompanied by the patter of feet on the hard road. Martin sprang up to find Gerard running wildly, both hands clasped above his head in fervent prayer, while Margaret tottered backward toward him, palms extended in pitiful supplication, her cheeks ash-gray, her eyes fixed on empty space.

Gerard caught her in his arms, speaking words of comfort, but her mind refused to receive them. Only the sound of his voice caused her to moan and clutch him tightly, her body trembling violently. He lifted her onto the mule, wrapping his arm around her frame. Once strong as a boy, she had now become relaxed and powerless. Supporting her, he led her slowly and sadly home. She shed not a single tear, uttered not a single word.

At the edge of the wood, he dismounted her and commanded her to cross to her father's house. She obeyed immediately. Martin continued to Rotterdam; Sevenbergen was too stifling for him. Gerard, severed from the love of his life, moved forward like one in a trance.

At the little hostelry, he hired a horse and a guide, riding swiftly toward the German frontier. Yet, everything was mechanical; his senses felt dulled, as if viewing the world through a thick veil. Trees, houses, and men slid past him like ghosts. His companions spoke to him twice, receiving no answer. Only once did he cry out savagely, "Shall we never be out of this hateful country?"

After hours of riding, they reached the brow of a steep hill, with a small brook flowing at the bottom. "Halt!" the guide cried, pointing across the valley. "Here is Germany."

"Where?" Gerard asked, bewildered.

"On the other side of the stream. No need to ride down the hill, I trow."

Gerard dismounted without a word, retrieving the burgomaster's purse from his girdle. As the guide opened it, he remarked half-sulkily, "You will soon be out of this hateful country. Perhaps the one you seek will like you no better anyway. Though it be a church you have robbed, they cannot take you once you cross that bourn."

These words, at another time, would have earned the speaker an admonition or a blow. Now, they fell upon Gerard like idle air. He paid the lad in silence and descended the hill alone.

The brook was silvery, murmuring over pebbles varnished by the clear water. Gerard sat down, staring stupidly at them. Then he drank, washing his hot feet and hands in the icy current. The shock woke him. Rising, he took a run and leaped across the stream into Germany.

Even as his foot touched the strange soil, he turned abruptly and looked back. "Farewell, ungrateful country!" he cried. "But for her, it would cost me nothing to leave you forever, along with all my kin and kindred—and the mother who bore me, and my playmates, and my little native town. Farewell, fatherland—welcome the wide world! *Omne solum fortipatrat*."

With these brave words on his lips, he suddenly slumped, arms and legs weak, sitting down to sob bitterly upon the foreign earth. When the young exile had sat thus for a while, bowed down by grief, he rose and wiped the tears from his eyes like a man shedding a burden. Casting not a single glance behind to weaken his resolve, he stepped out into the wide world. His love and heavy sorrow left no room for vulgar misgivings. Compared with the tearing apart of his soul from Margaret, the prospect of walking to Italy in that rude age seemed trivial. All nations meet in a convent. Thanks to his good friends the monks and his own thirst for knowledge, he could speak most of the languages required on that long road.

"I will soon be at Rome," he told himself. "The sooner the better."

After walking a good league, he arrived where four serpentine country roads met, confusing many an inexperienced neighbor traveling between villages. Gerard took out the little dial Peter had given him, setting it in the autumn sun. By this compass, he steered unhesitatingly for Rome, inexperienced yet determined as a young swallow flying south—but unlike the swallow, wandering south entirely alone.

The road stretched out before him, a ribbon of mud and dust leading nowhere certain, until Gerard encountered a tableau of human folly. Two men in sober, dark suits leaned with the languid indifference of predators resting between kills, flanking a horse that stood as the centerpiece of their amusement. Between them lay the Count, a figure of grotesque excess dressed in a silk doublet and a bright green jerkin of English cloth, glossy as a mole's hide. He sprawled flat upon his stomach in the afternoon sun, looking less like a nobleman and more like an enormous, discarded lizard. A velvet cloak, flaming yellow, was carefully draped over the horse's loins, a ridiculous attempt at warmth for a beast that required none.

"Is aught amiss?" Gerard inquired, his voice cutting through the lazy atmosphere.

"Not that I wot of," one of the servants replied, his tone dripping with feigned concern.

"But your master," Gerard pressed, unable to suppress his indignation, "lies like a corpse. Are ye not ashamed to let him grovel on the ground?"

The servant's response was immediate and dismissive. "Go to; the bare ground is the best cure for his disorder. If you get sober in bed, it gives you a headache; but you leap up from the hard ground like a lark in spring. Eh, Ulric?"

"He speaks sooth, young man," Ulric agreed warmly, his eyes gleaming with a shared malice.

"What, is the gentleman drunk?" Gerard asked, the question hanging in the air with naive simplicity.

The servants erupted into a hoarse, raucous laughter, mocking the very concept of Gerard's inquiry. Suddenly, however, Ulric halted, his expression shifting from joviality to grave scrutiny as he eyed Gerard from head to toe. "Who are you, and where born, that know not the Count is ever drunk at this hour?" The question transformed Gerard instantly from a concerned traveler into a suspect character, an outsider whose ignorance marked him as dangerous.

"I am a stranger," Gerard declared, straightening his spine, "but a true man, and one that loves knowledge; therefore ask I questions, and not for love of prying."

"If you be a true man," Ulric countered, his voice sharpening with shrewdness, "then give us trinkgeld for the knowledge we have given you."

Gerard blinked, confused, but quickly masked his bewilderment with a polite facade. "Trinkgeld you shall have, such as my lean purse can spare, an if you will tell me why ye have ta'en his cloak from the man and laid it on the beast."

Inspired by the prospect of payment, the two men immediately offered Gerard two competing theories, each more absurd than the last. The first suggested that since the Count was a seasoned toper prone to rapid sobriety, finding his horse sweating in the cold while a cloak lay idle nearby would inevitably provoke a fit of cursing and violence. The second, more pretentious explanation posited that a horse is a poor milksop, requiring constant warming, whereas a master, filled with the internal heat of good beer, rendered a cloak a mere shred of idle vanity. Each speaker fell in love with their own logic, having clearly taken a bite from the dog that had bitten their master to the brain. Their voices rose in a heated debate until the green sot began to growl instead of snore, oblivious to the fact that his audience was arguing over his comfort.

Before long, the intellectual discourse devolved into physical combat. Hans, gripping the bridle with his right hand, delivered a solid cuff to Ulric's jaw with his left. Ulric returned the favor with equal enthusiasm, his right hand free. They engaged in a chaotic dance of blows, *ding dong*, over the horse's mane, pummeling one another and jostling the poor beast until it backed away, stumbling over a promontory of the green lord's body. Like a toad stung by Ithuriel's spear, the Count started up howling, clutching his smarting side with one hand and tugging at his hilt with the other. The servants, seized by terror, released the reins. The horse galloped off, whinnying in panic, while the men chased after him, crying out in fear. The green noble followed, volleying curses, his naked sword drawn, his body rebounding wildly from hedge to hedge in a headlong, zigzag career down the narrow lane.

"In which hurtling," Gerard muttered, turning his back on the chaos, he walked calmly south. He was glad to have saved the four tin farthings he had prepared for trinkgeld, yet his heart remained heavy, burdened by the sight of their drunken extravagance.

The sun was nearing its setting, painting the sky in hues of bruised purple. Gerard, who had hoped in vain for an inn along the route, felt a profound unease settle in his bones. Black clouds gathered overhead, swallowing the light. He quickened his pace, almost running, but it was futile. Down came the rain in torrential sheets, drenching the bewildered traveler and seemingly extinguishing the sun itself; its fading rays could not compete with this new assailant.

Gerard trudged on, dark, wet, and lost in an unknown region. "Fool! to leave Margaret," he chided himself, the words tasting bitter on his parched lips.

Soon, the darkness thickened into an impenetrable veil. He was entering a great wood. Huge branches shot across the narrow road like skeletal fingers, and the benighted stranger groped his way through what seemed an interminable, inky cave with a rugged floor. He stumbled constantly, his shivering limbs and empty stomach betraying him, his heart fainting with every step. Then, the wolves rose from their lairs, bayed all around the perimeter of the forest, their cries echoing like death knells.

Gerard's hair bristled, but he gripped his cudgel tightly, preparing to sell his life dearly if necessary. There was no wind, yet his excited ears caught the patter of light feet over the newly fallen leaves and the rustle of low branches as creatures glided swiftly past. Suddenly, in the sea of ink, a great fiery star appeared close to the ground. He hailed it as he would his patron saint. "CANDLE! a CANDLE!" he shouted, attempting to run toward it. But the dark, rugged path soon thwarted his efforts; the light proved more distant than he had dared hope. Finally, in the very heart of the forest, he found a house, illuminated by candles and filled with loud voices. He looked up for a signboard, but there was none. "Not an inn after all!" he said sadly. Yet, reasoning that no Christian would turn a dog out into this wood tonight, he resolved to seek shelter. He approached the door that led to the voices, opening it slowly and peering in timidly. He withdrew abruptly, as if slapped in the face, and recoiled into the rain and darkness.

He had glimpsed a large but low room, the center dominated by a huge round stove or clay oven reaching to the ceiling. Around it, wet clothes hung to dry—some on lines, others more compactly on rustic pegs. These garments, soaked with the day's rain and stained with the dirt of travel, lined with what locals called "rammish clowns," emitted rank vapors and compound odors inexpressible, rising in steaming clouds. In one corner sat a traveling family, large and numerous, releasing a peculiar, sickly smell of neglected brats into the common air. Garlic filled the interstices of the space. With windows closed and the intense heat of the central furnace battling the breath of at least forty people, the atmosphere was suffocating.

They had just finished supper.

Like many artists, Gerard possessed sensitive organs, and the potent effluvia struck dismay into him. Yet, the relentless rain lashing him outside, combined with the temptation of light and fire, drew him back. He could not force his way through the palpable perfumes all at once, returning to the doorway repeatedly like a singed moth. Eventually, he discerned that the various smells did not entirely mix, no fiend stirring them together. The odor of family predominated in two corners; stewed rustic reigned supreme in the center; and garlic in the noisy group by the window. By hasty analysis, he noted that the garlic described the smallest aerial orbit, while the scent of reeking rustic darted farthest—a flavor as if ancient goats, or the fathers of all foxes, had been dragged through a river and dried by Nebuchadnezzar.

So Gerard crept into a corner close to the door. Though the solidity of the main fetors isolated him somewhat, the heat and reeking vapors circulated, making the walls drip. The home-nurtured novice felt something like a cold snake wind about his legs, his head turning to a great lump of lead. Next, he felt like choking, sweetly slumbering, and dying, all in one.

He was within an ace of swooning, but recovered to a deep sense of disgust and discouragement. Settling on a resolution to return to Holland at peep of day, he plucked up a little heart. Being faint with hunger, he asked one of the men of garlic whether this was indeed an inn.

"Whence come you, who know not 'The Star of the Forest'?" was the reply.

"I am a stranger; and in my country inns have aye a sign."

"Droll country yours! What need of a sign to a public-house—a place

The momentum of Gerard's fall was arrested only by Denys's desperate grasp, yet the physics of gravity remained unforgiving; without the interposition of the dying bear, whose hairy carcass served as a final, tragic buffer, Gerard's neck or limbs surely would have shattered. Denys tore the animal from the boy, a futile gesture born of instinct rather than necessity. The bear panted, her limbs trembling in the twilight, but she was already beyond redemption. Within moments, her breathing ceased, leaving the forest floor littered with the evidence of a failed hunt. Denys, displaying a pragmatic cruelty suited to survival, propped Gerard against the soft, cooling fur and fanned him with frantic energy. Consciousness returned to the boy in fragments, clouded by confusion and the phantom sensation of the bear's weight, until he rolled away in a scream of terror.

"Courage," Denys cried, his voice cutting through the panic. "Le diable est mort."

"Is it dead? Quite dead?" Gerard stammered, hiding behind the trunk of a nearby oak. His courage was a feverish delusion, and the chill of the coming night had already settled deep in his bones.

"Behold," Denys said, pulling the bear's ear with playful insolence. He prised open her jaw and stuffed Gerard's head inside, performing other acts of grotesque mockery. In the midst of this absurdity, Gerard vomited violently.

Denys laughed, a harsh, braying sound. "What ails you now? And why did you tumble from your perch just as we secured victory?"

"I swooned, I trow," Gerard gasped.

"But why?"

Receiving no answer, Denys pressed on. "Green girls faint merely at the sight of you, yet they possess the wisdom to choose their time and place. What woman in her right mind ever faints while scaling a tree?"

"She sent her filthy blood all over me," Gerard retched. "I believe the scent alone was enough to overpower me! Faugh! I loathe blood."

"I daresay it was potent."

"Look at the mess she has made of me."

"But with her blood, not yours. I pity the enemy who sought to satisfy you."

"You need not boast, Maitre Denys," Gerard muttered, wiping his face. "I saw you beneath the tree; your shirt was stained."

"Let us distinguish," Denys said, flushing slightly. "It is permissible to tremble for a friend."

In response, Gerard flung his arms around Denys's neck in a silent embrace.

"Look here," whined the stout soldier, visibly moved by this sudden eruption of youthful affection. "Was anything in creation ever so reminiscent of a woman? I love thee, little milksop—go on. Good heavens, behold him on his knees now. What fresh caprice is this?"

"Oh, Denys, ought we not to return thanks to Him who has spared both our lives against such fearful odds?" Gerard knelt, raising his voice in prayer. Soon, he found Denys kneeling quietly beside him, his hands crossed over his chest in the manner of his homeland, his face drawn long and thin. When they rose, Gerard's countenance was radiant.

"Good Denys," he said, "Heaven will reward thy piety."

"Ah, bah! I did it out of politeness," the Frenchman dismissed, waving a hand. "It was to please thee, little one. *C'est égal*, it was prayed well and orderly, and it edified me to the core while it lasted. A bishop could scarcely have managed the affair better. Now that our vespers are sung and the saints enlisted on our side—*marchons*."

Before taking two steps, Denys halted. "By-the-by, the cub!"

"Oh, no, no!" Gerard protested.

"You are right. It is late. We have squandered precious time climbing trees, tumbling from them, swooning, vomiting, and praying. Furthermore, the brute is heavy to carry. And now I recall, we shall likely have Papa hunting for it next; these bears make such a racket over an odd cub. What is this? You are wounded! You are wounded!"

"Not I."

"He is wounded; miserable that I am!"

"Be calm, Denys. I am not touched; I feel no pain anywhere."

"You? You only feel when another is hurt," Denys cried, his voice thick with emotion. Throwing himself to his knees, he examined Gerard's leg with glistening eyes.

"Quick! Quick! Before it stiffens!" he urged, dragging the boy forward.

"Who makes the fuss about nothing now?" Gerard asked, his composure returning.

Denys's reply was indirect. "Be pleased to note," he said, "that I have a bad heart. You were a man enough to save my life, yet I must sneer at you, a novice in war. Was I not a novice once myself? Then you fainted from a wound, and I assumed you swooned from fear, calling you a milksop. Briefly, I have a bad tongue and a bad heart."

"Denys!"

"Plait-il?"

"You lie."

"You are very kind to say so, little one, and I am eternally obliged to you," mumbled the remorseful Denys.

As they walked many furlongs, the muscles of the wounded leg contracted and stiffened until Gerard could barely lift his toe, doing so with excruciating agony. Finally, he could endure it no longer.

"Let me lie down and die," he groaned. "This is intolerable."

Denys argued that it was afternoon and the nights were growing frosty; that cold and hunger were ill companions, and that it would be unreasonable to lose heart, especially since a certain great personage was notoriously deceased. Thus, Gerard leaned upon his axe and hobbled on, but soon he gave in completely, collapsing helplessly into the road.

Denys dragged him aside into the cover of the woods. To Gerard's astonishment, the soldier handed him his crossbow and bolts, commanding him strictly to remain motionless. "If any ill-looking fellows discover you and approach," Denys instructed, "bid them keep aloof. Should they refuse, shoot them dead at twenty paces. Honest men keep the path; knaves in a wood are fools to parley." With this, he snatched up Gerard's axe and set off running—not, as Gerard expected, toward Düsseldorf, but back along the road they had traveled.

Gerard lay aching and smarting. Rome, which had seemed so imminent at the start, now appeared impossibly distant, despite him being two hundred miles closer. Yet, soon his thoughts drifted toward Sevenbergen. How sweet it would be one day to hold Margaret's hand and recount every trial he had endured for her! The mere thought of her soothed him; amidst the pain and the irritation of his nerves, he lay resigned, offering a sweet, faint smile.

He had lain thus for more than two hours when suddenly shouts erupted. The next instant, something struck a tree hard by and vibrated violently.

Gerard looked up. It was an arrow.

He sprang to his feet. Several missiles rattled among the branches, and the wood echoed with battle cries. Their source was indeterminate; noises in these vast forests reverberate so wildly that a stranger is perpetually at fault regarding their origin, yet they seemed to fill the entire air. Presently, there was a lull, followed by the fierce gallop of hooves. Louder shouts and cries arose, mingled with shrieks and groans, accompanied by strange, terrible sounds like fierce claps of thunder, bellowing loudly before dying off in cracking echoes. Red tongues of flame shot intermittently among the trees, and clouds of sulfurous smoke drifted overhead. Then, all was still.

Gerard was struck with awe. "What will become of Denys?" he cried. "Oh, why did you leave me? Oh, Denys, my friend! My friend!"

Just before sunset, Denys returned, nearly collapsing under a hairy bundle—the bear's skin.

Gerard welcomed him with a burst of joy that startled him. "I thought never to see you again, dear Denys. Were you in the battle?"

"No. What battle?"

"The bloody conflict of men, or fiends, that raged in the wood a while ago," Gerard explained, describing it vividly, more fully than history might record.

Denys patted him indulgently on the back. "It is well," he said. "Thou art a good painter; and fever is a great spur to the imagination. Once, I lay in a cart-shed with a cracked skull and watched two hosts maneuver and fight for a good hour on eight feet of space, which I fairly described to my comrade in proper order, only not so gorgeously as thou, for lack of book learning."

"What, then, you believe me not? When I tell you the arrows whizzed over my head, and the combatants shouted, and—"

"May the foul fiends fly away with me if I believe a word of it."

Gerard took his arm and quietly pointed to a tree close by. "Why, it looks like—it is—a broad arrow, as I live!" He approached and looked up.

"It came from the battle. I heard it, and saw it."

"An English arrow."

"How do you know that?"

"Marry, by its length. The English bowmen draw the bow to the ear; others only

The afternoon sun filtered weakly through the shutters, casting a pale, indifferent light upon the room. Gerard, though no longer reeling from the initial shock of his injury, was consumed by a feverish irritability, his mind racing through a labyrinth of delirious fantasies. In one such haze, he demanded a lemon, a sour necessity to quell the dryness in his throat. Denys, transformed from a rough-hewn soldier into a figure of tender, grandfatherly devotion, rose with alacrity. With a soothing gesture to calm his young charge, he promised the fruit would arrive in the blink of an eye, vanishing immediately to scour the local markets.

The search proved arduous; lemons were rare luxuries in the North, unlike the abundance known today. Time stretched agonizingly as Gerard's patience frayed, until finally, the door swung open. Yet, it was not Denys who entered. Instead, a commanding silhouette glided softly into the room—an elderly gentleman clad in the solemn regalia of high medicine. He wore a long, sober gown trimmed with rich fur, paired with cherry-colored hose and pointed shoes. At his hip rested a sword sheathed in fine morocco leather, and encircling his neck was a ruff, starched with rigid severity and artificially deepened by hidden wooden frameworks known as *rebatoes*. Upon his head sat a four-cornered cap bordered with fur, framing a majestic white beard that cascaded over his chin and bosom. Following closely at his heels was a boy carrying a basket brimming with phials, lint, and surgical instruments, their metallic gleam inviting scrutiny.

There was no ambiguity regarding the visitor's profession; save for the weapon, this attire was the full ceremonial dress of a physician. Approaching the bedside with gentle precision, the old man spoke in a mild, hushed tone, "How fares thee, my son?"

Gerard responded with gratitude, noting that while his wound caused little pain, his throat felt parched and his head weighed down by a heavy fog.

"A wound!" the physician exclaimed, feigning surprise. "They failed to inform me of that. Allow me to inspect it. Indeed, a clean, sharp bite. I wager the mastiff possessed sound teeth capable of such extraction;" and the doctor's sympathy seemingly evaporated, transferring entirely to the canine predator he had conjured—a jackal of his own making.

"This requires immediate cauterization," he declared, his voice sharpening. "Otherwise, you shall find yourself retreating from water and performing somersaults beneath our very hands. This is the season of rabid curs, and one has already claimed your service; yet, we shall thwart him. Boy, hasten to heat the iron."

"But, sir," interjected Gerard, "it was no dog, but a bear."

"A bear!" the senior corrected severely, his brow furrowing. "Consider your words carefully; it is ill jesting with the man of art who brings his gray hairs and lifelong studies to heal you. A bear, quotha! Had you dissected as many bears as I, or even a tenth thereof, and examined their dentition to maintain your own skill, you would know that no bear's jaw could inflict such a trivial wound. I assure you, it was a dog—and since you have forced my hand, I deny it was even a large one, but merely one of those furious, small curs that plague us, running deviously to bite the legs of men and lay them low. Were it not for me and my learned brethren, wielding knife and cautery, the mischief would be total."

"Alas, sir!" Gerard protested. "When did I claim it was a bear's jaw? I said, 'A bear'; it was his paw, now."

"And why did you not inform me immediately?"

"Because you insisted on telling me otherwise."

"Never conceal anything from your leech, young man," the senior continued, displaying the paradox of being an eloquent speaker yet a notoriously poor listener. "Well, this is a grievous affair. The hardy excrescences of beasts—to wit, the claws of tigers, panthers, badgers, cats, and bears, alongside the horns of deer and the nails of humans, particularly children—are imbued with the direst poisons. You would have fared better bitten by a cur, however you argue, than gored by a bull or stag, or scratched by a bear. Nevertheless, you shall receive a potent biting cataplasm for your leg. Meanwhile, keep the limb cool: extend your tongue!—Good!—You have a fever. Let me feel your pulse!—Indeed, a fever. I prescribe flebotomy, and without delay."

"Flebotomy! That is bloodletting," Gerard muttered. "Humph! Very well, if it guarantees a cure, for I refuse to remain idle here." The doctor assured him that the procedure was infallible, especially in his condition.

"Hans, retrieve the necessary implements, and I shall entertain the patient with reasoned discourse."

The man of art then embarked upon a lecture for Gerard, explaining that in disease, the blood becomes heated, disturbed, and increasingly toxic. By removing a portion of this corrupted fluid, nature is compelled to generate a purer substance to replace it. Thus, bleeding served as both a coolant and a purifier, acting as a universal remedy for all ailments, for all diseases were fundamentally febrile, regardless of what empirical practitioners might claim.

"Do not suppose," he said warmly, "that mere bleeding suffices. Any paltry barber can incise a vein (though not all possess the skill to seal it again). True art lies in discerning which vein to evacuate for which ailment. Recently, a man plagued by earache was brought to me. I bled him in the right thigh, and his earache vanished instantly. Regrettably, he passed away shortly thereafter. Another arrived suffering from toothache; I bled him behind the ear, relieving him in a trice. Fate, however, claimed him as well. I bled our bailiff between the thumb and forefinger for rheumatism. Soon, he returned complaining of headaches and ringing in the ears, holding his hand over the basin. I smiled at his folly, bled him in the left ankle against his will, and rendered his head as light as a nut."

Branching from the immediate crisis with the enthusiasm of a zealot, the revered teacher expanded further:

"Know, young man, that two schools of art currently vie for supremacy across Europe. The Arabian school, whose ancient oracles include Avicenna, Rhazes, and Albucazis, revived by Chauliac and Lanfranc; and the Greek school, whose modern champions are Bessarion, Platinus, and Marsilius Ficinus, though whose primordial doctors were medicine's very prophets: Phoebus, Chiron, Aesculapius, and his sons Podalinus and Machaon, along with Pythagoras, Democritus, and Praxagoras, who discovered the arteries, and Dioctes, 'who first gave spirit to urine.' All these taught orally. Then came Hippocrates, the eighteenth descendant of Aesculapius, from whom we possess manuscripts; to him we owe 'the vital principle.' He also invented the bandage and tapped for water on the chest; above all, he dissected—yet only quadrupeds, for the brutal prejudices of the pagan multitude withheld the human body from the knife of science. Him followed Aristotle, who identified the aorta, the largest blood vessel in the human frame."

"Certainly, sir," Gerard objected humbly, "the Almighty granted us all that resides within our bodies, not Aristotle, nor any Grecian scholar."

"Child! Of course He provided the thing itself; but Aristotle bestowed the name. Yet, young men persist in chatter. The next great luminary was Galen, who studied at Alexandria, then the heart of science. Justly dissatisfied with quadrupeds, he dissected apes, viewing them as closer kin to man, and bled like a Trojan. Then came Theophilus, who revealed the nerves, the lacteal vessels, and the pia mater."

This troubled Gerard. "I cannot lie still while hearing mortals claim they bestowed the very parts which Adam, our father, received from Him, who fashioned him from clay, and us, his sons."

"Has such perversity ever been witnessed?" the doctor snapped, his color rising. "Who is the true donor of a thing to man? Is it he who plants it secretly in the dark recesses of the human body, or the learned sage who unveils it to his intelligence, thereby enriching his mind with knowledge? Comprehension is your only true possession. Have I answered?"

"I am silenced, sir."

"And that is preferable; for garrulous patients are difficult to cure, especially during fever. I assert, then, that Eristratus gifted us the cerebral nerves and milk vessels; indeed, he invented lithotomy, whatever detractors may claim. Then came another whose name escapes me; you somewhat disturb me with your petty objections. Next came Ammonius, the author of lithotrity, and here arrives Hans with the basin—to curb your verbosity. Blow your charcoal, boy, and present the basin; excellent. Arabs, quotha! What are they but a sect of yesterday who, circa the year 1000, stumbled upon the writings of those very Greeks, reading them askew due to lacking their own concurrent light? For their demi-god and camel-driver, Mahound, an impostor in both science and religion, strictly forbade them anatomy

The worthy physician retreated to his sanctuary, confessing to his housekeeper that he suffered from a grievous burn—a phrase chosen with surgical precision. In the economy of human deceit, we often cauterize our neighbor's digits while searing our own fingers; he had merely burned his own. Yet, the housekeeper, wielding remedies as mild as milk, treated him with the deference due a lamb submitting to experience. While he feigned recovery, his mind was already drafting bills for shattered phials and orchestrating the immediate imprisonment of his guests. He swore before a magistrate that these strangers, owing him money, plotted instant flight from the township. Alas, it was his unluckiest day. His sincere, honest endeavor to commit perjury was thwarted by a circumstance he had never foreseen, nor deemed possible: he had spoken the truth. And in an affidavit!

When the officers arrived at "The Silver Lion," they discovered the birds had flown. Descending to the river, they learned from local intelligence and launched a hot pursuit up the bank. This fleeting escape owed everything to Denys's sharp sense and keen observation. After a peal of laughter that served as a cordial tonic, and after venting his watchword thrice, Denys abruptly grew grave. "Gerard Dusseldorf is no place for us," he declared. "That old fellow went off unnaturally silent for such a babbler. We are strangers here; the bailiff is his friend. Within five minutes, we shall lie in a dungeon for assaulting a Dusseldorf dignity. Are you strong enough to hobble to the water's edge? It is hard by. Once there, you have but to lie down in a boat instead of a bed; and what is the odds?"

"The odds, Denys? Untold, and all in favor of the boat," Gerard replied, his voice trembling with anticipation. "I pine for Rome; for Rome is my road to Sevenbergen. Then we shall lie in the boat, but *on* the Rhine—the famous Rhine, the cool, refreshing Rhine. I feel its breezes coming; the very sight will cure a little hop-o'-my-thumb fever like mine. Away! Away!"

Finding his excitable friend in this fervent mood, Denys hastily settled accounts with the landlord, and they hurried to the riverbank. Inquiry revealed dismay: the public boat had departed an hour prior, and no others would launch that afternoon. Yet, by dint of relentless questioning and gathering a crowd, they secured an offer of a private vessel from an elderly man and his two sons. A bystander ridiculed the arrangement. "The current is too strong for three oars."

"Then my comrade and I will help row," the invalid insisted.

"No need," the old man countered kindly. "Bless your silly heart, he owns the other boat."

A powerful breeze filled the sails from astern; the boatmen set them broad and began rowing, propelling the vessel at a spanking rate. "Are ye better, lad, for the river breeze?"

"Much better. But indeed the doctor did me good."

"The doctor? Why, you rejected all his cures."

"No, but I mean—you will say I am naught—but knocking the old fool down somehow soothed me."

"Amiable dove! How thy little character opens more and more every day, like a rosebud. I read thee all wrong at first."

"Nay, Denys, mistake me not. I trust I bore with his idle threats, though in sooth his voice pierced my poor ears. But he was an infidel, or next door to one, and such I have been taught to abhor. Did he not as good as say we owed our inward parts to men with long Greek names, and not to Him, whose name is but a syllable, yet whose hand is over all the earth? Pagan!"

"So you knocked him down forthright—as a good Christian."

"Now, Denys, you will still be jesting. Take not an ill man's part. Had it been a thunderbolt from Heaven, he had met but his due; yet he took but a sorry bolster from this weak arm."

"What weak arm?" Denys inquired, his eyes twinkling.

"I have lived among arms, and by Samson's hairy paw, I never saw one more like a catapult. The bolster wrapped round his nose, and the two ends kissed behind his head, and his forehead resounded. Had he been Goliath, or Julius Caesar, instead of an old quacksalver, down he had gone. St. Denys, guard me from such feeble opposites as thou! And above all from their weak arms—thou diabolical young hypocrite."

The river wound through the landscape, occasionally shifting the wind from astern to their side. Then, all hands moved to the weather side to prevent the boat from heeling dangerously. Among them was a child of about five years old, the grandson of the boatman and his darling. This urchin had slipped aboard at the moment of departure; being too light to affect the boat's trim, he existed above, or rather below, the strict laws of navigation. They sailed merrily on, little conscious that they were pursued by a whole posse of constables armed with the bailiff's writ, closing in rapidly. For if the wind was strong, so was the current.

Suddenly, Gerard remembered that this was a fine road to Rome, but a fatal one to Burgundy. "Oh, Denys," he said, with an almost alarmed look, "this is not your road."

"I know it," Denys replied quietly. "But what can I do? I cannot leave thee till the fever leaves thee; and it is on thee still, for thou art both red and white by turns. I have watched thee. I must e'en go on to Cologne, I doubt, and then strike across."

"Thank Heaven," Gerard exclaimed joyfully. Adding eagerly, with a touch of self-deception, "'Twere a sin to be so near Cologne and not see it. Oh, man, it is a vast and ancient city such as I have often dreamed of, but ne'er had the good luck to see. Me miserable, by what hard fortune do I come to it now?"

"Well then, Denys," the young man continued, less warmly, "it is old enough to have been founded by a Roman lady in the first century of grace, sacked by Attila the barbarous, and afterwards sore defaced by the Norman Lothaire. And it has a church for every week in the year, besides chapels and churches innumerable, convents and nunneries. Above all, the stupendous Minster, yet unfinished, wherein, but in their own chapel, lie the three kings that brought gifts to our Lord: Melchior with gold, Gaspar with frankincense, and Balthazar, the black king, who brought myrrh. Over their bones stands the shrine, the wonder of the world—it is of ever-shining brass, brighter than gold, studded with images fairly wrought and inlaid with exquisite devices, brave with colors. Two broad stripes run to and fro, of jewels so great, so rare, each might adorn a crown or ransom its wearer at need. Upon it stand the three kings curiously counterfeited: two in solid silver, richly gilt, bareheaded; but he of Aethiop, ebony-skinned, bears a golden crown. In the midst, our Blessed Lady, in virgin silver, holds Christ in her arms. At the corners, in golden branches, four goodly waxen tapers burn night and day. Holy eyes have watched and renewed that light unceasingly for ages, and holy eyes shall watch them in saecula. I tell thee, Denys, the oldest song, the oldest Flemish or German legend, found them burning, and they shall light the earth to its grave."

"And there is St. Ursel's church, a British saint's, where lie her bones and all the other virgins, her fellows. Eleven thousand were they who died for the faith, being put to the sword by barbarous Moors on the twenty-third day of October, two hundred and thirty-eight. Their bones are piled in the vaults, and many of their skulls are in the church. St. Ursel's is in a thin golden case, standing on the high altar, but shown to humble Christians only on solemn days."

"Eleven thousand virgins!" cried Denys. "What babies German men must have been in days of yore. Well, would all their bones might turn to flesh again, and their skulls sweet faces, as we pass through the gates. 'Tis odds but some of them are wearied of their estate by this time."

"Tush, Denys!" said Gerard. "Why wilt thou, being good, still make thyself seem evil? If thy wishing-cap be on, pray that we may meet the meanest she of all those wise virgins in the next world, and to that end let us reverence their holy dust in this one. And then there is the church of the Maccabees, and the cauldron in which they and their mother Solomona were boiled by a wicked king for refusing to eat swine's flesh."

"Oh, peremptory king! And pig-headed Maccabees! I had eaten bacon with my pork liver rather than change places at the fire with my meat."

"What scurvy words are these? It was their faith."

"Nay, bridle thy choler, and tell me, are there nought but churches in this thy so-vaulted city? For

The equilibrium of good and evil, that ancient and relentless accountant, balances its ledger with a precision that borders on the divine. Yet, in the chaotic theater of the present moment, the scales tipped violently toward survival. The steel bow, strapped to the arbalestrier's back and raised high above his head, had become the instrument of his undoing. It was precisely that elevated position that rendered the weapon accessible to Gerard. One hand seized the cold metal, while the other slipped deftly between the bow and the cord, securing the prize. With a single, eager jerk of his crossbow, Denys was hauled from the churning depths until half his torso broke the surface, gasping for air.

"Now, grip me not! Grip me not!" Gerard screamed, his voice cracking with the mortal terror of a man who knew that a single misjudgment could mean drowning forever.

"Pas si bete," Denys gurgled, the words bubbling up like foam from a broken dam.

Seeing the raw, animalistic stuff of which his companion was made, Gerard's fear evaporated, replaced by a steely calm born of necessity. "On thy back," he commanded sharply. Seizing the arbalest, he drove a powerful stroke forward, aiding the desperate maneuver. "Hand on my shoulder! Slap the water with the other! No—with a downward motion. Just so. Do nothing more than I bid thee."

Gerard had already latched onto Denys's long, sodden hair, twisting it tight and biting the end firmly between his side teeth. With the formidable strength of his youthful neck, he held the soldier's head aloft, striking out lustily against the roaring current. For a fleeting second, hesitation paralyzed him—a terrible indecision regarding which bank to seek—but then the west shore appeared, slightly nearer. He committed to it, choosing not to swim safely to jail like a dutiful child, but rather to forge a path that would offer a novel incident to history.

Driven by the river's fury, they slanted dangerously across the flow. Having covered nearly a hundred yards, Denys murmured uneasily, "How much more of this?"

"Courage," Gerard mumbled, his voice steady despite the chaos. "Whatever a duck knows, a Dutchman knows; you are safe as in bed."

And indeed, the next moment brought shock and relief: they found themselves in shallow water, wading ashore onto solid ground. Once on *terra firma*, they stared at one another from head to toe, as if eyes alone could devour the magnitude of their deliverance. Then, by a singular, overwhelming impulse, they flung arms around each other's necks, panting there, hearts swelling too full for speech.

At this sacred threshold, life tasted sweeter than heaven. Perhaps most intensely so for the poor exiled lover, who had just secured the life of his friend. Oh, the joy to whose heights had any poet yet soared, or dared attempt to soar? To save a human life—and specifically, a beloved one—is a reward worth living for, yes, three score years and ten.

Calmer now, they clasped hands and walked along the bank, hand in hand like a pair of sweethearts, scarcely knowing or caring whither their feet led them. Behind them, the boatmen were all secure on the late-concave, now-convex craft. Herr Turnip-face, the "Inverter of things," stood in the middle, seemingly untouched by the fray. At least, he greeted them cheerfully when they shot into the Rhine and again when they emerged.

"Shall we wait till they right the boat?"

"No, Denys. Our fare is paid; we owe them naught. Let us move on, and briskly."

Denys assented, noting that they could walk the entire way to Cologne along this bank.

"I fare not to Cologne," came the calm reply.

"Why, whither then?"

"To Burgundy."

"To Burgundy? Ah, no! That is too good to be true."

"Sooth 'tis, and sense into the bargain. What matters it to me how I go to Rome?"

"Nay, nay; you but say so to please me. The change is too sudden. Think me not so ill-hearted as to take you at your word. Besides, did I not see your eyes sparkle at the wonders of Cologne? The churches, the images, the relics..."

"How dull art thou, Denys; that was when we were to enjoy them together. Churches! I shall see plenty, going Rome-ward however I will. The bones of saints and martyrs; alas! the world is full of them. But a friend like thee—where on earth's face shall I find another? No, I will not turn thee farther from the road that leads to thy dear home, and her that pines for thee. Neither will I rob myself of thee by leaving thee. Since I drew thee out of the Rhine, I love thee better than I did. Thou art my pearl: I fished thee, and must keep thee. So gainsay me not, or thou wilt bring back my fever; but cry courage, and lead on; and hey for Burgundy!"

Denys gave a joyful caper. "Courage! Va pour la Bourgogne. Oh! Soyez tranquille! Cette fois il est bien décidément mort, ce coquin-là."

And they turned their backs on the Rhine.

As this decision crystallized, a commotion rippled across the river among the small party that had been watching the discussion. The friends had not taken many steps before a voice cut through the air from the water.

Gerard turned, seeing one of the four figures holding out a badge of office and a parchment slip. His heart sank; he was a good citizen, accustomed to obeying the voice that now bade him turn back toward Düsseldorf—the Law's domain. Denys shared none of his scruples. He was a Frenchman, despising every other nation, its laws, inhabitants, and customs alike. He was a soldier, viewing the situation through a strictly military lens: superior force opposed, a river barrier, the rear open—it was a retreat made easy. He saw at a glance that the boat still drifted in mid-stream and that no ferry lay nearer than Düsseldorf.

"I shall beat a quick retreat to that hill," he declared, "and then, being out of sight, quick step."

They sauntered off casually.

"Halt! In the bailiff's name!" cried a voice from the shore.

Denys turned and ostentatiously snapped his fingers at the bailiff, proceeding undeterred.

"Halt! In the archbishop's name!"

Denys snapped his fingers at his Grace and moved on.

"Halt! In the emperor's name!"

Denys snapped his fingers at his Majesty and continued.

Gerard watched this needless pantomime with regret. As soon as they cleared the brow of the hill, he said, "There is now but one course: we must run to Burgundy instead of walking." He set off, running the best part of a league without stopping. Denys was fairly blown, inquiring what on earth had become of Gerard's fever.

"I begin to miss it sadly," he said drily.

"I dropped it in the Rhine, I trow," was the reply.

Presently, they reached a little village. Here, Denys purchased a loaf and a huge bottle of Rhenish wine. "For," he explained, "we must sleep in some hole or corner. If we lie at an inn, we shall be taken in our beds."

This was no mere common prudence on the old soldier's part. The official network for catching lawbreakers, especially plebeians, was incredibly dense in that age, though the cooperation of the public was almost null, particularly on the Continent. Innkeepers were everywhere under close surveillance regarding their travelers, for whose acts they were even in some degree responsible, more so it would seem than for their sufferings.

Both friends were glad when the sun set, and delighted when, after a long trudge under the stars—for the moon, if memory serves, did not rise until about three in the morning—they arrived at a large barn belonging to a house some distance away. A quantity of barley had recently been thrashed; the heap of straw on one side of the floor was almost as high as the unthrashed corn on the other.

"Here be two royal beds," said Denys. "Which shall we lie on, the mow, or the straw?"

"The straw for me," said Gerard.

They sat on the heap, eating their brown bread and drinking their wine. Then Denys covered his friend in straw, heaping it high above him, leaving only a breathing hole. "Water, they say, is death to fevered men; I'll make warm water on it, anyhow."

Gerard bade him make his mind easy. "These few drops from the Rhine cannot chill me. I feel heat enough in my body now to parch a kennel, or boil a cloud if I were in one."

With this epigram, his consciousness faded so rapidly that he might truly be said to "fall asleep." Denys, lying awake for a while, heard something that made him nestle closer. Horses' hooves rang up from Düsseldorf, and the wooden barn vibrated as they rattled past, howling in a manner too well known and understood in the fifteenth century, but as unfamiliar in modern Europe as a Red Indian's war-whoop. Denys shook where he lay. Gerard slept like a top. It all swept by, and troop and howls

Gerard stood transfixed, the sudden intelligence striking him with such violent force that his complexion flickered between pallor and crimson. With a grinding of teeth born of suppressed fury, he prepared to lash out. Men of spirit possess the ferocity of the wild boar; they may flee when cornered by a superior host, driven merely by instinct rather than cowardice, but if the pursuer presses relentlessly, closing the distance until escape becomes impossible, the beast turns. It whirls, charging blindly at the multitude of hunters, ready to gore them all. Gerard had set his jaw for this very charge, his eyes hardening for battle, yet the resolve shattered in an instant. His face crumpled into a look of profound desolation, and he whispered, "But my axe remains in Rhine."

Together, they weighed their options. Prudence urged them to skirt the village entirely, while hunger roared its imperative command: *Buy food.* In the cacophony of survival, hunger spoke the loudest, though prudence argued with the most convincing logic. Ultimately, they resolved to cut directly across the open fields. They paused beside a towering haystack, borrowing two bundles to form a makeshift pallet within a dry ditch, hidden from view yet stinging beneath layers of nettles. Taking turns, they ventured forth into the gloom and returned laden with turnips, gnawing at these bitter roots at irregular intervals throughout their retreat until the sun surrendered to the horizon.

Soon, they emerged once more, shivering violently into the encroaching rain and impenetrable darkness, merging onto the roadway beyond the village. It was a night of dismal despair, black as pitch and whipped by a gale that howled with relentless fury. Visibility vanished; sound was swallowed whole. Neither party could see, hear, be seen, or be heard. For all anyone knew, they drifted past their enemies like spectral phantoms, invisible in the void. Yet, amidst the natural terrors of this storm-lashed obscurity—a night where they seemed to grope and hack their path through solid black marble—their adversaries faded from their minds.

When the moon finally ascended, casting a pale, trembling light, they had traveled leagues beyond Düsseldorf. Still, they pressed onward, trudging through the mud. Suddenly, a massive structure loomed ahead. "Courage!" cried Denys, his voice cutting through the wind. "I believe I recognize this convent. Yes, it is. We have entered the diocese of Jülich. Here, Cologne holds no sway." And in that single declaration, the next moment found them secure within the protective walls.

Gerard's path led him into the quiet orbit of a monk whose hands spoke a language older than words. This brother, a craftsman of the divine mechanics, had conjured the great sundial that governed the Prior's garden, engineered a wheel to lift water from the earth's thirst, and devised a winnowing machine to separate grain from chaff. His workshop was a sanctuary of ingenuity, perpetually humming with the potential of creation. He had already birthed several psalteries and two dulcimers, and now, his fingers danced over the reeds, attempting to coax life from a set of regalles—a small organ destined for the choir's praise.

Gerard, humbled by the disparity between his own clumsy attempts and the monk's mastery, plucked a few notes on the humble psaltery. Yet, the monk's touch transformed the instrument; he played it with a divinity that laid bare Gerard's profound ignorance of music, turning the novice's failure into a lesson in grace. In illumination, the monk was skilled, though he lacked the delicate hand required to match Gerard's beauty. As they compared their disparate talents—the earnestness of the young traveler against the seasoned simplicity of the age—it became clear that Youth and Age had found kinship. They were like brothers, bound by a shared reverence for craft, and the pressure to remain was heavy upon Gerard's heart.

He sought counsel from Denys, who assented with a rueful shrug, acknowledging the pull of the moment. Gerard recounted his journey to the old man, softening the edges of his recent trials. "Alack!" sighed the good old man, his eyes clouding with memory. "I have wandered far in my day, yet no shadow ever molested me." He urged Gerard to shun the inns, places teeming with rogues and rioters where the soul could be poisoned even if the body escaped. Instead, he advised finding refuge in the peace of a monastery for every night's rest. Suddenly, his demeanor shifted; he looked at Gerard with the sharp intensity of a needle, asking, "How long hast thou been shriven?"

Gerard flushed, his reply weak and halting. "Better than a fortnight."

"And thou an exorcist!" the monk exclaimed, disbelief coloring his tone. "No wonder peril has hunted thee. Come, thou must be absolved immediately."

"Yes, father," Gerard agreed fervently, sinking to his knees, palms joined in supplication. But the monk halted him, half-fretful. "Not to me! Not to me! Not to me! My soul is as cluttered with the world as any living man's. My entire devotion lies in these wooden pipes and sorry leather stops, instruments that shall perish with those whose minds are fixated on such vanities."

"Dear father," Gerard pleaded gently, "these are for the service of the Church. Surely that sanctifies the pain and labor poured into them?"

"That is precisely what the Devil has been whispering in my ear lately," the monk retorted, placing one hand behind his back and shaking his finger at Gerard with a gesture that was both threatening and playful. "He was so kind, so thoughtful, to remind me that Solomon built the Lord a house adorned with rare hangings, and that this act was deemed gracious and sinless. Oh! He quotes Scripture with remarkable flair. But I am not so simple a monk as you suppose, my lad," he cried, his voice rising in sudden defiance, addressing not Gerard but the empty space beside him. "This one toy finished, vigils, fasts, and prayers await me. Prayers standing, prayers lying on the chapel floor, and prayers submerged in a right good tub of cold water."

He nudged Gerard, winking with conspiratorial knowingness. "Nothing he hates and dreads more than seeing us monks at our orisons, up to our chins in freezing water. *Corpus domat aqua*—the body dwells in water. So now, go confess thy trifling sins, those pardonable errors of youth and secular wandering, and leave me to mine. They are sweet to me as honey, to be expiated in due measure."

Gerard bowed his head, unable to suppress the question, "Where shall I find a confessor more holy and merciful?"

"In each of these cells," the monk replied simply, gesturing down the corridor. "There, seek Brother Anselm, yonder."

Gerard moved toward the indicated cell, but the doors stood in tight succession, and fate seemed to conspire against him. Just as he raised his hand to knock, he heard his old friend cry out in an agitated whisper, "Nay! Nay! Nay!" He spun around to find the monk pacing frantically before his own cell door, beating the air with doubled hands like a man sawing at a phantom log. Gerard feared the room housed some ferocious beast, or perhaps the very personage whose presence in the convent had been so loudly proclaimed. He glanced back, bewildered, and stepped toward the next door.

Then, his old friend nodded vigorously, his face bursting into a blissful expression before he retreated swiftly into his sanctuary. Inside, he seized his hourglass, flipped it, and returned to his regalles. Often, he would pause, gazing upward, murmuring to himself, "Well-a-day, the sands flow so swiftly when the man bends over earthly toys."

Father Anselm awaited him. He was a venerable figure, his ample head crowned with dignity and love. As Gerard confessed, responding to the priest's gentle yet piercing inquiries, he felt a strange mixture of awe and absurdity. "Here is a head!" he thought, panic rising. "Oh dear, oh dear! I wonder if you will allow me to sketch it once the confession is ended." His own mind grew foggy, crimes forgotten in the haze of admiration. Yet, he did not soften his account of the pagan nature of his travels, nor did he diminish the truth of his experiences.

The penance assigned was brief but solemn: Gerard was to enter the convent church, prostrate himself, and kiss the lowest step of the altar three times. Then, kneeling on the floor, he was to recite three Our Fathers and a Creed. Upon completion, he was to return instantly.

True to his word, Gerard performed his short mortification and returned to find Father Anselm mixing plaster. "After the soul comes the body," the monk declared. "Know that I am the surgeon here, lacking a better title. This is for thy leg; to cool it, not to burn it. The saints forbid otherwise."

During the treatment, the monastic leech, having taken a keen interest in the Düsseldorf episode of Gerard's confession, leaned toward Denys's view on "bleeding." "We Dominicans seldom let blood these days," he explained, his voice steady. "The lay leeches claim it stems from timidity and lack of skill, but in truth, we have long discovered that simples cure most ailments that can be cured at all. Furthermore, they never kill in capable hands, whereas other remedies strike like thunderbolts. As for blood, the Vulgate states expressly that it is the life of a man. And in medicine, as in law or divinity, to be wiser than the All-Wise is to be a fool. Moreover, simples are mighty. Consider the little four-legged creature that kills the poisonous snake; if bitten itself, it finds an herb powerful enough to quell that venom, stronger and swifter than any mortal remedy. Taught by her wisdom and our own traditions, we continue to search and test the virtues of the plants God has strewn across this earth—some to feed the body, others to heal it. Only in desperate cases do we mix heavenly with earthly virtue, steeping the hair or bones of departed saints in the medicine to work miracles."

"Do you truly believe it is the relics, father?" Gerard asked. "For Peter a Floris, a learned physician and no pagan, denies it stoutly."

"What does Peter a Floris know? And what do I know?" the monk countered calmly. "I do not presume to claim we can command the saints, nor that they will, regardless of our will, draw corporal virtue from their blessed remains. Yet, I observe that the patient who drinks thus in faith is often improved as by a charm. Doubtless, faith in the recipient plays a vast role in all such cures. Thus it has always been. A sick woman whom all Jewish physicians failed to heal merely touched Christ's garment and was cured in an instant. Had she not touched that sacred cloth, she would never have recovered. If she had worn it to her grave without faith, I suspect she would have been no better. But we do ill to probe these matters too curiously. Everything around us demands faith. Have then a little patience. We shall soon know all. Meanwhile, I, thy confessor for this nonce, strictly forbid thee, under penalty to thy soul's health, to listen to learned laymen on religious matters. Arrogance is their bane; with it, they shut heaven's open door against their own faces. Mark my words: learned laics. The unlearned have often been my masters in humility, and may become thine."

"The wound is tended; in three days it shall be but a scar," the monk concluded warmly. "Now, God speed thee, and may the saints make thee as good and as happy as thou art thoughtful and gracious."

Gerard lingered, hoping to delay departure for the refectory dinner, but Father Anselm, with a shade of regret barely perceptible, informed him he would not leave his cell that week, being himself in penitence.

To truly grasp the architecture of Gerard's despair, one must first witness the absolute tyranny of the monastic machine that awaited them. Denys, a man whose patriotism had curdled into a visceral hatred of foreign soil, viewed the approaching Burgundian lands with a mixture of longing and disdain. He constantly chanted the praises of his homeland, using every minor inconvenience to remind Gerard that "things were done better in Burgundy." Above all, he exploited Gerard's deepest foible—the terror of sleeping on bare straw—by promising the pristine, white linens of the polished nations beyond. "I ask no more," the Hollander would declare, his voice dripping with mock piety, "than to think I have not lain once in a naked bed since I left home! When I gaze upon their linen, instead of removing my habit and hose, I wish my very eyes and nose were closed forever." Denys carried this love of country to absurd lengths; he once walked twenty leagues in shoes that had long since exploded rather than purchase new ones from a German churl who would erect insurmountable barriers of incivility between customer and commodity.

As the sun began to dip below the horizon, casting long shadows across the landscape, they found themselves equidistant from a small town and a monastery, the latter lying ominously off the main road. Denys instinctively favored the inn, while Gerard, drawn by the promise of spiritual refuge, leaned toward the convent. Denys yielded, but only on the condition that once they crossed into Burgundy, they would invariably choose the tavern over the temple. Gerard agreed all too readily, for his map, annotated with a list of convents, ended precisely where the road forked. Turning off the path, the air grew thick with unspoken tension. Suddenly, Gerard asked with genuine surprise where this sudden aversion to places that had fed and lodged them freely so often stemmed from. The soldier hesitated, hemming and hawing, until finally, the dam of his suppressed grievances burst forth. It was not a fleeting dislike but an ancient, abiding horror, held at bay only by infinite difficulty and a veneer of politeness.

"I saw they had put poison in your drink," he confessed, his voice trembling with repressed rage, "so I forbade them. However, being the last chance, why not ease my mind? Know then I have been like a fish out of water in all those great dungeons. You straightway leave with some old shaveling; thus you see not my purgatory." "Forgive me! I have been selfish," Gerard murmured. "Ay, ay, I forgive thee, little one; 'tis not thy fault: thou art not the first fool that has been priest-ridden and monk-hunted. But I will not forgive them my misery." Then, speaking as if a century before Henry VIII's commissioners arrived, he delivered his indictment against the gloomy piles of stone. These structures were all built alike; inns might differ, but here everything was a suffocating monotony. A great gate, a little gate, countless steps leading inevitably to a gloomy cloister. There lay the dortoir, and there the great, cold refectory, where one was forced to sit in silent submission, or at least inaudibility, lest one dare to speak their mind aloud.

"And then," he continued, his voice rising in indignation, "no one is a man here, but all are slaves. Slaves to what? To a peevish, tinkling bell that never sleeps. If it were a trumpet now, sounding constant alarms, it wouldn't freeze a man's heart quite so thoroughly. *Tinkle, tinkle, tinkle*, and you must sit to meat with perhaps no stomach for food. Ere your meal settles in your belly, *tinkle, tinkle!* and you must rush to church with perhaps no stomach for devotion. I am not a hog at prayers, for one. *Tinkle, tinkle,* and now you must go to bed with your eyes wide open. Well, by then you have contrived to shut them, some uneasy imp of darkness has seized the bell-rope, and *tinkle, tinkle!* it behoves you to recite a prayer in the dark, whether you know one or not. If they heard the sort of prayers I mutter when they shatter my rest with their incessant ringing! Well, you drop off again and gain a sliver of sleep: lo, it is *tinkle, tinkle* for matins."

"And the only clapper you seem to love is a woman's," Gerard interjected half-contemptuously. "Because there is some music in that even when it scolds," was the stout reply. "And then to be constantly checked. If I merely put my finger in the salt-cellars, straightaway I hear, 'Have you no knife that you touch the salt?' And if I but wipe my blade on the cloth to save time, then it is, 'Wipe thy knife dirty on the bread, and clean upon the cloth!' Oh, small of soul! These petty, peevish pedantries fall chill upon good fellowship like tiny icicles melting down from straw-thatched eaves."

"I hold cleanliness no pedantry," Gerard countered firmly. "Thou shouldst learn better manners once for all." "Nay; 'tis they who lack manners. They stop a fellow's mouth at every word." "At every other word, you mean; every obscene or blasphemous one." "Exaggerator, go to! Why, at the very last of these dungeons, I found poor travelers sitting all chilled and mute around one shaveling, like rogues awaiting their turn to be hanged. So to cheer them up, I did but cry out, 'Courage, tout le monde, le diable...'" "Connu! What befell?" "Marry, this. 'Blaspheme not!' quoth the executioner. 'Plait-il,' I said. Does he not wheel and wryte on me in a sort of Alsatian French, turning all the P's into B's? I had much ado not to laugh in his face." "Being thyself unable to speak ten words of his language without a fault," Gerard noted dryly. "Well, all the world ought to speak French. What avail so many jargons except to put a frontier between men's hearts?"

"But what did he say?" "What signifies it what a fool says?" "Oh, not all the words of a fool are folly, or I should not listen to you." "Well, then, he said, 'Such as begin by making free with the devil's name, aye end by doing it with all the names in heaven.' 'Father,' I said, 'I am a soldier, and this is but my consigne or watchword.' 'Oh, then, it is just a custom?' said he. I, not divining the old fox and thinking to clear myself, said, 'Ay, it was.' 'Then that is ten times worse,' he replied. ''Twill bring him about your ears one of these days. He still comes where he hears his name often called.' Observe! No gratitude for the tidings which neither his missals nor his breviary had ever allowed him to know. Then he was so good as to inform me that soldiers commonly commit the crimes for which all other men are broken on the wheel—a savoir murder, rape, and pillage."

"And is't not true?" "True or not, it was ill manners," Denys replied guardedly. "And so says this courteous host of mine: 'Being the foes of mankind, why make enemies of good spirits into the bargain, by still shouting the names of evil ones?' and a lot more such nonsense." "Well, but, Denys, whether you heed his advice or slight it, wherefore blame a man for raising his voice to save your soul?" "How can his voice save my soul when he keeps turning my P's into B's?" Gerard was staggered; ere he could recover from this thunderbolt of Gallicism, Denys went triumphantly off at a tangent, stigmatizing all monks as hypocrites. "Do but look at them, how they creep about and cannot eye you like honest men." "Nay," said Gerard eagerly, "that modest, downcast gaze is part of their discipline, 'tis custodia oculorum." "Cussed toads eating hoc hac horum? No such thing; just so looks a cutpurse. Can't meet a true man's eye. Doff cowl, monk; and behold, a thief; don cowl, thief, and lo, a monk. Tell me not they will ever be able to look God Almighty in the face when they can't even look a true man in the face down here."

"Ah, here it is, black as ink! Into the well we go, comrade. Misericorde, there goes the tinkle already. 'Tis the best of tinkles though; 'tis for dinner: stay, listen! I thought so: the wolf in my stomach cried 'Amen!'" This last statement he confirmed with two oaths, and marched like a victorious gamecock into the convent, thinking by Gerard's silence he had convinced him, and not dreaming how profoundly he had disgusted him.

In the dim, candlelit expanse of the refectory, where the air hung thick with the scent of stale bread and incense, conversation drifted toward the sudden vacancy at the scriptorium. A monk spoke of the brother who had perished—a victim of fate or fever—who had been entrusted with the monumental task of transcribing the monastery's charter and rule. The news rippled through the assembly until it reached Gerard, whose heart leaped with a mixture of terror and hope. Rising with trembling knees, he offered his services, his voice barely audible above the rustle of habits.

"Nay, not for hire, my lords," he pleaded, his eyes darting nervously to the high table, "but for love. As a trifling return for the countless nights of shelter the brethren of your order have bestowed upon this poor wayfarer."

A murmur of approval rose from the lower ranks, but a senior monk, his face lined with skepticism, shook his head. "The late brother was a master of the pen," he intoned, his tone carrying the weight of institutional tradition. "His work cannot be continued save by a master. Your skill, stranger, is unknown here."

Gerard felt the heat rise in his cheeks. From the folds of his tunic, he drew forth a vellum deed, the back of which he had painstakingly cleaned and inscribed with a flourish of his own making—a specimen of his craft. He held it out, his fingers stiff.

The monk's reaction was instantaneous. His eyes widened, the skepticism evaporating into awe. He snatched the document, his movements jerky with excitement, and hurried up the long hall to the high table. Bending his knee, he brushed against the fifth and tenth steps—the sacred thresholds of the Prior's domain—and presented the deed with a reverence usually reserved for relics.

Instantly, the atmosphere shifted. A dozen knowing eyes locked onto the page, and a low hum of voices erupted, growing into a buzz of speculation. Gerard watched, breathless, as the Prior pointed repeatedly at the script, his finger tracing the elegant curves of the ink. The senior monk returned, his face flushed with triumph, bearing a savory *crustade royale* and a game pie spiced with exotic herbs for Gerard, alongside a silver grace cup brimming with rich *pimentum*.

Gerard accepted the feast, bowed low first to the distant Prior, then to his new companions, and raised the cup. As he quaffed and passed it along, a transformation occurred. The hostility dissolved; the table hailed him as kin.

"Art convent-bred? Deny it not?" the voices demanded in unison.

Gerard acknowledged the truth, giving silent thanks to Heaven. Without this revelation, he would have remained as rough and ignorant as his fellow travelers, Sybrandt and Cornelis.

"But 'tis passing strange how you could know," the Prior remarked, leaning forward.

"You drank with the cup in both hands," two monks chimed in, their laughter ringing clear.

For some time, the lower tables had been the source of raucous noise, but now a burst of mirth so obstreperous and prolonged threatened to tear the roof from the hall. The Prior, losing patience, dispatched the Sub-Prior to quell the uproar and impose penance upon the riotous monks. Yet, amidst the chaos, Gerard's face burned with shame. In the heart of the unruly revelry, his ear had caught a specific word—"courage!"—and the trumpet-toned voice of Denys of Burgundy cutting through the din.

Soon, Gerard was settled in Feu Werter's cell. Wax lights flickered against the stone walls, illuminating a small easel capable of adjusting to any angle, surrounded by the finest materials of calligraphy. Yet, the work proved too arduous for a single evening. The question gnawed at him: how could he ask Denys, the self-proclaimed monk-hater, to remain longer?

He approached Denys, offering to abide by whatever decision the soldier wished. To his delight, Denys responded with unexpected grace. "A day's rest will do neither of us harm. Write thou, and I'll pass the time as I may."

Gerard's work was swiftly admired. The community agreed that the monastery's records had suffered a grievous loss with poor Werter's death. The Sub-Prior pressed a rix-dollar into Gerard's hand, along with brushes and pigments from the convent's ample stores. Warm-hearted and buoyed by this good omen, Gerard believed his fortune—and his recovery of his well-beloved—lay in the power of the pen.

"Come, Denys," he said, his demeanor lightening, "see what the good monks have given me. Now, do try to be fairer to them; for to be round with you, it chilled my friendship for a moment to hear even you call my benefactors 'hypocrites.'"

"I recant," Denys declared immediately.

"Thank you! Thank you! Good Denys."

"I was a scurrilous vagabond."

"Nay, nay, say not so!"

"But we soldiers are rude and hasty. I give myself the lie, and I offer those I misunderstood all my esteem. 'Tis unjust that thousands should be defamed for the hypocrisy of a few."

"Now you are reasonable. Have you pondered what I said?"

"Nay, it is their own doing."

Gerard crowed softly, for everyone delights in being proven right, and listened intently as Denys recounted the genesis of his conversion.

"Well then," Denys began, his voice animated, "at dinner the first day, a young monk beside me opened his jaws and laughed right out, most musically. 'Good,' said I, 'at last I have fallen on a man and not a shorn ape.' So, to sound him further, I slapped his broad back and administered my consigne. 'Heaven forbid!' says he. I stared. For the dog looked as sad as Solomon; a better mime you never saw, even at a Mystery play. 'I see war is no sharpener of the wits,' he replied. 'What are the clergy for but to fight the foul fiend? and what else are the monks for? "The fiend being dead, The friars are sped." You may plough up the convents, and we poor monks shall have nought to do—but turn soldiers, and so bring him to life again.' Then there was a great laugh at my expense. 'Well, you are the monk for me,' said I. 'And you are the crossbowman for me,' quoth he. 'And I'll be bound you could tell us tales of the war should make our hair stand on end.' 'Excusez! the barber has put that out of the question,' quoth I, and then I had the laugh."

"What wretched ribaldry!" Gerard observed, his brow furrowing pensively.

The candid Denys admitted at once that he had witnessed merrier jests hatched with less cacophony. "'Twas a great matter to have got rid of hypocrisy," he continued. "'So,' said I, 'I can give you the chaire de poule, if that may content ye.' 'That we will see,' was the cry, and a signal went round."
