Then Denys burst into a tale of glee regarding his bedtime. Instead of the great dormitory, he had been ushered into a private cell, strictly forbidden to sleep. To aid his vigil, a book of pictures depicting a hundred merry adventures of monks pursuing the female laity was lent to him. In due course, he was taken barefoot down to the parlour, where a supper fit for a duke awaited. Twelve jolly friars, the roaringest boys he had ever met in peace or war, sat around the table. The story, the toast, the jest, and the wine-cup circulated freely. Some played cards with a gorgeous pack where Saint Theresa and Saint Catherine, bedizened with gold, stood for the four queens; while black, white, grey, and crutched friars represented the four knaves. They staked their very rosaries, swearing like troopers when they lost.

At midnight, a sly monk stole out, only to be cunningly followed by others into the garden, where he was seen thrusting his hand into the ivy and pulling out a rope-ladder. He scaled the ten-foot wall with such nimbleness that a russet kirtle popped up from the outer world as quickly as he. Thus began a scene of billing and cooing that struck Denys as rather feline than ecclesiastical, drawing from him the appropriate comment of a "mew!" The monks joined the musical chorus, and the lay visitor shrieked in discomfort. But Abelard merely cried, "What, are ye there, ye jealous miauling knaves? Ye shall caterwaul to some tune tomorrow night. I'll fit every man-jack of ye with a fardingale."

This brutal threat had reconciled Denys to staying another day—at Gerard's request. Gerard groaned inwardly. Meanwhile, unable to disconcert such a brazen monk, and the demoiselle beginning to whimper, they had danced caterwauling in a circle before bestowing a solemn benediction on the two wall-flowers and retreating to the parlour. There, they found a pair lying dead drunk and two others affectionate to tears. They had straightway carried off the inanimate bodies, dragged the loving and lachrymose ones kicking and screaming into their cells, and shut them up in measureless content.

To truly grasp the architecture of this encounter, one must first understand the absolute poverty of Gerard's universe prior to this moment. He existed within a microscopic empire of two, subjugated by a man he was conditioned to call "Master"—a term that felt less like address and more like a brand of ownership. Now, however, the landscape of his captivity had shifted violently from the silent, star-gazing void of the wilderness to the cacophonous, performative theater of human society. They met the landlord in the passage, a figure who bowed with a mechanical precision that suggested he was acting out a role rather than offering genuine hospitality.

"Welcome, messieurs," the landlord intoned, removing his cap with a low bow that seemed to require more energy than the gesture itself warranted.

"Come, we are not in Germany," Gerard retorted, his voice cutting through the air with the sharp edge of someone accustomed to silence.

In the public room, they encountered the mistress, a buxom woman of forty whose presence immediately filled the space with a chaotic warmth. She curtsied to them, smiling with a cordiality that bordered on the predatory. "Give yourself the trouble of sitting ye down, fair sir," she addressed Gerard, dusting two chairs with her apron—a theatrical flourish that Gerard observed with a mixture of amusement and weary resignation. *This is a polite nation,* he thought, cataloging the absurdities of civilized interaction. *The trouble of sitting down? That will I endure with singular patience; and presently the labour of eating, also the toil of digestion, and finally, by Hercules' aid, the strain of going to bed, and the struggle of sinking fast asleep.*

"Why, Denys, what are you doing? Ordering supper for only two?" the mistress asked, her tone shifting effortlessly from deference to command.

"Why not?" Denys replied, embodying the reckless spirit of the traveler.

"What, can we sup without waiting for forty more? Burgundy forever!"

"Aha! Courage, camarade. Le dia—"

"C'est convenu."

Here, the Salic Law seemed to have dissolved entirely, leaving behind a realm where wimple and kirtle reigned supreme. Doublets and hose were few in number, and those present moved with a feeble, unconvincing act. The landlord himself wandered objectless, eternally taking off his cap to folk for want of thought, while the women, passing him in turn, thrust him quietly aside without looking at him, much like one removes a live twig while bustling through a dense wood.

Supper arrived, carried in by a maid whom the mistress followed, empty-handed yet commanding. "Fall to, my masters," she said cheerily, her voice ringing with false intimacy. "Ye have but one enemy here; and he lies under your knife." (Gerard suspected this to be mere formula, a script recited by the trade.)

They fell to. The mistress drew her chair slightly toward the table, providing company as well as meat, gossiping genially with them like old acquaintances. Yet, once this performance was exhausted, the busy dame departed, sending in her daughter, a beautiful young woman of about twenty who claimed the vacant seat. She was not quite so broad and genial as the elder, but gentle and cheerful, displaying a womanly tenderness for Gerard upon learning the distance the poor boy had traveled and the journey ahead. She stayed nearly half an hour, a brief interlude of softness before the storm broke again. When she left, Gerard remarked, "This an inn? Why, it is like home."

"Qui fit Francois il fit courtois," Denys burst out, swelling with gratified pride.

"Courteous? Nay, Christian," Gerard corrected him. "To welcome us like home guests and old friends, us vagrants, here today and gone tomorrow. But indeed, who better merits pity and kindness than the worn traveler far from his folk? Hola! Here comes another."

The newcomer was the chambermaid, a woman of about twenty-five with a cocked nose, a large laughing mouth, and a sparkling black eye. Her bare arm was stout but not shapely, a testament to labor rather than vanity. The moment she entered, one of the travelers passed a somewhat free jest on her; instantly, the whole company erupted in roaring laughter at his expense, so swiftly had her practiced tongue dismantled his defense. It was like a passage of arms between a novice and a master of fence; the foils clashed, and the novice was pinked. One by one, others broke lances with her, but Marion stood her ground, planting her great arms upon her haunches and holding the entire room in play.

This country girl possessed in perfection that rude and ready humor which looks mean and vulgar on paper but carries all before it when spoken aloud. It was not wit's rapier; it was its bludgeon. Nature had done much for her in this regard, and daily practice in the inn completed the rest. Yet, she shall not be photographed by me, but feebly indicated, for it was just four hundred years ago, the raillery was coarse, and she returned every stroke in kind. Though a virtuous woman, she said things without winking that no decent man of our day would utter even among men.

Gerard sat gaping with astonishment. This was to him almost a new variety of *homo*. He whispered, "Denys, now I see why you Frenchmen say 'a woman's tongue is her sword.'" Just then, she leveled another assailant, and the chivalrous Denys, to console and support "the weaker vessel"—the iron kettle among the clay pots—administered his consigne: "Courage, ma mie, le..." etc.

She turned on him directly. "How can he be dead as long as there is an archer left alive?" (General laughter at her ally's expense.)

"It is 'washing day,' my masters," she declared with sudden gravity.

"After? We travelers cannot strip and go bare while you wash our clothes," objected a peevish old fellow by the fireside, who had kept mumchance during the raillery but crept out into the sunshine of commonplaces.

"I aimed not your way, ancient man," replied Marion superciliously. "But since you ask me" (here she scanned him slowly from head to foot), "I trow you might take a turn in the tub, clothes and all, and no harm done" (laughter). "But what I spoke for, I thought this young sire might like his beard starched."

Poor Gerard's turn had come; his chin crop was thin and silky. The loudest of all the laughers this time was the traitor Denys, whose beard was of a good length, singularly stiff and bristly; so that Shakespeare, though he never saw him, hit him in the bull's-eye.

"Full of strange oaths, and bearded like the pard." —*As You Like It*.

Gerard bore the Amazonian satire mighty calmly. He had little personal vanity. "Nay, 'chambriere,'" he said with a smile, "mine is all unworthy your pains; take you this fair growth in hand!" pointing to Denys's vegetable.

"Oh, time for that, when I starch the besoms."

While they shouted over this palpable hit, the mistress returned, and in no more time than it took her to cross the threshold, the Amazon turned to a seeming Madonna, meek and mild. Mistresses are wonderful subjugators; their like, I think, breathes not on the globe. Housemaids, decide! It was a waste of histrionic ability, though, for the landlady had heard and did not at heart disapprove the peals of laughter.

"Ah, Marion, lass," she said good-humoredly, "if you laid me an egg every time you cackle, 'Les Trois Poissons' would never lack an omelet."

"Now, dame," said Gerard, "what is to pay?"

"What for?"

"Our supper."

"Where is the hurry? Cannot you be content to pay when you go? Lose the guest, find the money, is the rule of 'The Three Fish.'"

"But, dame, outside 'The Three Fish' it is thus written—'Ici-on ne loge...'"

"Bah! Let that flea stick on the wall! Look hither," and she pointed to the smoky ceiling, which was covered with hieroglyphics. These were accounts, vulgo scores; intelligible to this dame and her daughter, who wrote them at need by simply mounting a low stool and scratching with a knife so as to show lines of ceiling through the deposit of smoke. The dame explained that the writing on the wall was put there to frighten moneyless folk from the inn altogether, or to be acted on at odd times when a non-paying face should come in and insist on being served. "We can't refuse them plump, you know. The law forbids us."

"And how know you mine is not such a face?"

"Out fie! It is the best face that has entered 'The Three Fish' this autumn."

"And mine, dame?" said Denys; "dost see no knavery here?"

She eyed him calmly. "Not such a good one as the lad's; nor ever will be. But it is the face of a true man. For all that," added she drily, "an I were ten years younger, I'd as lieve not meet that face on a dark night too far from home."

Gerard stared. Denys laughed. "Why, dame, I would but sip the night dew

The question rang out, sharp and demanding: "Where be the true men?" The answer returned not as a whisper, but as a roar of solidarity. "Here be we. God bless you all! God bless you!" A surge of movement erupted toward the stairs, where half a dozen hands, hardened by violence yet warm with brotherhood, reached out to grasp the rescuers. "Y'have saved our lives, lads," cried Denys, his voice thick with relief, "you've saved our lives this very night."

Before the echoes of gratitude could fade, the reality of the scene assaulted the senses. The room was a cavern of flickering torchlight, illuminated by the gleaming breastplates of the archers and the bronzed, battle-worn faces of their kin. In stark contrast lay the white, terrified cheeks of the bound thieves and the grotesque tableau of the bleeding giant, whose lifeless form the hard men had discarded amidst its own gore. Gerard moved through the circle of warriors, gripping each hand with eyes glistening with unshed tears. In response, the archers pressed their lips to his palm; this time, he returned the gesture, sealing a bond forged in blood. Turning to a handsome archer of his own age, Gerard's voice trembled. "Prithee, good soldier, watch over me. A strange drowsiness overtakes me. Let no one cut my throat while I sleep—for pity's sake." The archer laughed, dismissing the plea as jest, unaware that death was already knocking. Within moments, Gerard succumbed to a profound slumber. Denys observed the collapse with surprise but chose silence; the unconscious boy served his immediate purpose perfectly.

Elsewhere, two archers prodded the Abbot's body, flipping it with their boots to examine the wound. "Which of the two flung this enormous rogue from the upper storey?" they muttered, seeking the mechanics of the fall. Denys initially scoffed, feigning ignorance, fearing that admitting his role would invite the young vagabond to claim divine intervention and render him a fool. But seeing Gerard lost to dreams, Denys shifted tactics. "Well, comrades," he declared, his tone shifting to authoritative bravado, "I drove my sword through this one to the hilt. Others swarmed about me; I needed steel or I needed death. So I planted my foot against his stomach, gave a tug with my hand, and launched myself upward. I sent him flying to kingdom come! He died in the air, and his carrion rolled down among you without ceremony. Made you jump, I warrant me." He raised his cup. "But pikes and pillage! What avail prattle of trifles once they are gone? Buvons, camarades, buvons!"

The archers noted the irony of drinking where no liquor existed. "Nay," Denys countered, his nostrils flaring, "I'll soon find you wine. My nose has a natural alacrity for scenting vintage. Follow me." Leading the way with a torch, they descended a short flight of stone steps into a large, low cellar. The air was thick and dank, the walls encrusted with what looked like cobwebs but proved to be crystallized saltpeter oozing from the damp stones. "Oh! the fine mouldy smell," Denys murmured. "In such places, good wine still lurks. Advance thy torch."

Suddenly, a shape crouched in the corner, pale as ash and shivering violently. "Why, it is the landlord," Denys announced. "Get up, thou craven heart!" shouted an archer. "The thieves are bound, and we are thirsty. Rise and show us your wine; no bottles are visible here." "What, be the rascals bound?" the landlord stammered, his voice cracking. "Good news... wine? That will I, honest sirs." He rose with uncertain joints, offering to guide them. Denys intercepted him sharply. "You are all in the dark, comrades. He is in league with the thieves." "Alack, good soldier, me in league with the accursed robbers! Is that reasonable?" "The girl said so anyway." "The girl! What girl? Ah! Curse her, traitress!"

"The girl is gone to the bailiff," another archer interjected. "Let the burghers decide if this craven is guilty; we caught him not in the act. Let him draw us our wine." "One moment," Denys said, his eyes narrowing. "Why cursed he the girl? If he be a true man, he should bless her as we do." "Alas, sir!" the landlord pleaded. "I have but my good name to live by. I cursed her because you accused her of bellying me." "Humph! I trow thou art a thief, and where is the thief that cannot lie with a smooth face? Hold him, comrades. A prisoner can draw wine if his hands are not bound." The landlord offered no resistance, hoping to sell his remaining stock despite his overdue rent. The archers smiled grimly at his naivety. One laid a firm hand on his shoulder; the other led the way with the torch.

At the threshold, Denys halted. "Wait. Here be bottles in this corner; advance thy light." The torch-bearer approached, removing his scabbard to probe the heap where the landlord had crouched. "Nay, nay," cried the landlord. "The wine is in the next cellar. There is nothing here." "Nothing is mighty hard, then," Denys replied, reaching into the pile and pulling out an object. It was a bone. He tossed it onto the floor; it rattled. "There is nought there but the bones of the house," the landlord insisted. "Just now 'twas nothing. Now that we have found something, 'tis nothing but bones. Look at this one, comrade. Come, look, and bring your smooth-faced accomplice."

Philippe, the archer with the torch, examined the bone closely. "If this were a battlefield," he mused, "I'd say it was a man's shankbone. But 'tisn't a battlefield, nor a churchyard; 'tis an inn." "True, mate; but yon knave's ashy face is as good a light as any field. Read the bone by it. When the chine is missing and the house dog hides his tail, who was the thief?" Denys continued, his voice dropping to a menacing rumble. "My mind misgives me. The deeper I thrust, the more there be. Mayhap if these bones could speak, they'd make true men's flesh creep." "Alas! young man, what hideous fancies!" the landlord protested. "These are bones of beeves, sheep, and kids. Holy saints preserve us!" "Hold thy peace! Thy words are air. Thou hast not got burghers by the ear, but soldiers who sought their comrades and found their bones picked clean by crows—and perhaps by thee."

Denys thrust a small object into the landlord's face. "Men and women, saidst thou? Prithee, when spake I of women's bones? Wouldst make a child suspect thee? Field of battle, comrade! Was not this house a field of battle half an hour ago?" He grabbed the landlord and forced him closer. "Read his face. Now, what is this, thou knave?" He shoved a tiny fragment into the man's eyes. "Alas! I know not." "Too like the thumb bone of a man's hand. Mates, my flesh creeps. Churchyard! How know I this is not one?" Drawing his sword, Denys began raking the heap of earth and broken crockery. The landlord assured him it was futile, claiming they were merely sinners giving short measure, not assassins. "How could we afford to kill our customers? May Heaven's lightning strike me dead if there be any bones there but such as have been used for meat. 'Tis the kitchen wench who flings them here. I swear by God's holy mother, by holy Paul, by holy Dominic, and by Denys my patron saint..."

Denys held up a bone under the torch in dead silence. It was unmistakably human. The sight silenced the lying lips and paralyzed the heartless heart. The landlord's hair rose on his head like spikes, and his knees buckled as if his limbs had been severed. The archers dragged him upright, forcing him to stare at the dead skull, white as the living cheek opposite it, glaring back at its murderer. The landlord's pale lips parted, unable to utter a sound. "Ah!" Denys roared, trembling with rage. "Look on the sockets out of which thou hast plucked the eyes, and let them blast thine own, that crows may pick them out ere this week ends." Holding the skull, he threatened the quaking wretch with his naked sword until the man, groaning, took hold of the grisly relic. Denys wanted every murderer to feel this terror, to see the cold skull and fear their own heads becoming like it.

Soon, the heap yielded many more skulls, each discovery drawing a moan from the culprit. Suddenly, Denys, usually bold and hard, emitted a cry of distress. He held up a mass of long, glossy, golden hair—a woman's beautiful hair. The archers shook the craven wretch instinctively; he whined. "I have a little sister with hair just so fair and shining," gulped Denys. "Jesu! If it should be hers! Quick, take my sword and

Gerard awoke to the sharp, anxious scrutiny of Denys, whose eyes betrayed a lingering unease. "It is you for sleeping! Why, 'tis high noon," Denys exclaimed, his voice cutting through the haze of Gerard's stupor.

"It was a blessed sleep," Gerard replied, his voice thick with relief. "Methinks Heaven sent it to me. It hath placed a veil between me and that awful night. To think that you and I sit here alive and well... How terrible a dream I seem to have had!"

"Ay, lad," Denys responded, his tone softening as he accepted the reality of their survival. "That is the wise way to view such things once they are past; they become merely dreams, fleeting shadows. Break thy fast, and thou shalt think no more on't. Moreover, I promised to bring thee to the town by noon, to present thee to his worship."

Gerard dipped a piece of coarse rye bread into a cup of red wine, consuming it to break his fast. Then, accompanied by Denys, he walked over the scarred landscape of the recent combat. He returned shuddering, the phantom weight of violence still clinging to his limbs, until they finally turned toward the road leading to the little town. As they traveled, Gerard kept peering anxiously through the hedgerows, expecting sudden ambushes with an unreasonable intensity, until the town gates came into view.

Denys led him straight to "The White Hart." "No fear of cut-throats here," Denys assured him, gesturing to the inn's sturdy facade. "I know the landlord; he has been a burgess for many a year and looks the part of a bailiff. 'Twas here I intended to make my stand yesterday evening. But we lost time, and night overtook us—and—"

"And you saw a woman at the door," Gerard interrupted, his heart leaping. "And would be wiser than a Jeanneton; she told us they were nought."

"Why, what saved our lives if not a woman?" Gerard pressed, his gratitude swelling like a tide. "Ay, and she risked her own life to do it."

"That is true, Denys," Gerard continued, his voice trembling with emotion. "Though women are nothing to me in battle, I long to thank this poor girl and reward her, ay, even though I share every doit in my purse with her. Do not you?"

"Parbleu," Denys agreed without hesitation.

"Where shall we find her?"

"Mayhap the alderman will tell us. We must go to him first."

The alderman received them with a countenance of singular and inexplicable gravity. However, after a moment's reflection, a grim smile softened his features, and he proceeded to interrogate Gerard, taking down their answers with meticulous care. Once finished, he informed them that they must remain in the town until the thieves were tried, requiring them to be at hand to give evidence under penalty of fine and imprisonment. They looked very blank at this decree, bewildered by the sudden confinement.

"However," the alderman said, dismissing their confusion, "'twill not be long; the culprits having been taken red-handed." He added, with a touch of bureaucratic finality, "And you know, in any case, you could not leave the place this week."

Denys stared at this remark, seemingly unable to grasp the logic, while Gerard smiled inwardly at what he perceived as the old gentleman's simplicity—the naive belief that a provincial town of Burgundy held any attraction capable of detaining men driven by the urgent summons of Rome and Margaret.

Now, Gerard turned to that which was nearest both their hearts. "Your worship," he said, bowing low, "we cannot find our benefactress in the town."

"Nay, but who is your benefactress?" the alderman asked, intrigued.

"Who? Why the good girl that came to you by night and saved our lives at peril of her own," Gerard declared, his passion rising. "Oh sir, our hearts burn within us to thank and bless her; where is she?"

"In prison, sir? Good heavens, for what transgression?"

"She is a witness, and potentially a necessary one."

"Why, Messire Bailiff," interjected Denys, "you do not lay all your witnesses by the heels, I trust."

The alderman, pleased by the title of bailiff, grew communicative. "In cases of blood, we detain every testimony likely to provide legal leverage and thus thwart justice; hence we still hold the womenfolk. A man may hide in a single mindset for a week, but a woman, if she fulfills her duty to the realm on Friday, shall undo it before Sunday, or perish trying. Could you see that wench now, you would find her sobbing over having betrayed five males to the gallows. Had they been females, we might have trusted a subpoena. They despise one another, showing some sense. But now I recall, there were other reasons for binding this one. Hand me those depositions, young sir." He adjusted his spectacles. "Ah! She was implicated; she was part of the band."

A loud denial erupted simultaneously from Denys and Gerard.

"No need to deafen me," snapped the alderman. "It is written in black and white. 'Jean Hardy (one of the thieves), when questioned, confessed that—hmm? Yes, here it is. 'And that the girl Manon was the decoy, and her sweetheart was Georges Vipont, one of the band; and hanged last month: and that she has been depressed ever since, openly blaming the band for his death, stating if they had not been rank cowards, he would never have been caught, and it is his opinion she betrayed them out of pure spite, and—'"

"'His opinion,'" Gerard cried indignantly. "What significance does the opinion of a cutthroat hold, burning to exact revenge on the woman who delivered him to justice? If you rely on that, what value is his testimony? Is a thief never a liar? Is he not always a liar? And here is a motive to lie: revenge, which is the strongest of all passions. Oh, sir, what madness to question a detected felon and listen to him fabricate an honest life—as if he were a truthful man swearing in broad daylight with his true hand on the Gospel!"

"Young man," the alderman said, "restrain your heat in the presence of authority! By your tone, I find you are a stranger. Know then that in this land we question everyone. We are not so weak as to hope to reach the truth by closing either our left ear or our right."

"And so you would listen to Satan defaming the saints!"

"Nonsense! The law deals only with men and women, and these cannot utter a story entirely of falsehoods, however hard they try. Therefore, we do not shut the barn-door against any man's grain. Once admitted, we merely winnow and sift. Who told you I swallowed the thief's story whole like clear water? Not so. I credited only so much as was corroborated by superior proof."

"Superior proof?" Gerard looked bewildered. "Who else but the thieves would speak against her?"

"Marry, herself."

"Herself, sir? Did you question her too?"

"I tell you we question everyone. Here is her deposition; can you read? Read it yourself, then."

Gerard glanced at Denys and handed him Manon's deposition.

"I am a native of Epinal. I left my home two years ago due to misfortune: I could not endure the man they forced upon me. My father beat me. I fled from my father. I went into service. I left service because the mistress was jealous of me. Her reason for dismissing me was that I was saucy. Last year I stood in the marketplace to be hired alongside other girls. The landlord of 'The Fair Star' employed me. I served him for eleven months. A young man courted me. I loved him. I discovered that travelers arrived and never departed again. I told my lover. He commanded me to hold my peace. He threatened me. I learned my lover belonged to a band of thieves. When travelers were to be robbed, the landlord went out and signaled the band to assemble. Then I wept and prayed for the travelers' souls. I never spoke. A month ago my lover died.

"The soldier reminded me of my lover. He was bearded like the one I had lost. I cannot say whether I should have intervened if he had lacked a beard. I regret speaking now."

The paper nearly slipped from Gerard's grasp. For the first time, he realized Manon's life hung by a thread. Knowing the doggedness of the law and the relentless men who enforced it, he threw himself suddenly to his knees at the alderman's feet. "Oh, sir! Consider the chasm between those cruel men and this poor, fragile woman! Could you possess the heart to send her to the same death as them? Could you have the heart to condemn us to watch her slaughter, who, risking her life for ours, had not otherwise been in peril? Alas, sir! Show me and my comrade some pity, if you have none for her, poor soul. Denys and I are true men, and you will rend our hearts if you kill that poor, simple girl. What can we do? What remains for us but to cut our throats at the foot of her gallows?"

The alderman was tough, yet mortal; the prayers and agitation of Gerard first astonished, then moved him. He displayed this in a peculiar manner, becoming peevish and fretful. "There, rise, do," he said. "I doubt anyone would speak so many words for me. What ho, Daniel! Fetch the town clerk." As the functionary entered from an adjoining room, the alderman continued, "Here is a foolish lad fretting about that girl. Can we stretch a point? Say we admit her to bear witness and question her favorably."

The town clerk was a man of "impossibilities."

"Nay, sir, we cannot do that: she was not involved in this affair. Had she been an accessory, we might have offered her a pardon to testify."

Gerard burst forth, "But she did better. Instead of being an accessory, she halted the crime; and she volunteered as a witness by rushing here with the tale."

"Tush, young man, 'tis a matter of law." The alderman and the clerk then engaged in a lengthy debate, the one maintaining, the other denying, that she stood as legally valid as if she had been an accomplice to the attempted robbery of the travelers. This proved fortunate for Manon: for the alderman, irritated by the clerk's repetition that he could not do this, nor that, nor the other, declared, "I will show him I can do anything I choose." He summoned Manon forth. Upon the landlord of "The White Hart" acting as her bondsman, and Denys depositing five gold pieces with him, and the girl promising, not without some coaxing from Denys, to appear as a witness, he released her, easing his conscience by explaining his leniency in his own terms.

"The town had to purchase a new rope for every hanged person and present it to the executioner, or compound with him in money: and she was, in his opinion, not worth this municipal expense, whereas determined characters like her late confederates certainly were." Thus, Denys and Gerard carried her off, Gerard dancing around her in joy, Denys bolstering her spirits by assuring her of the demise of a troublesome figure, while she wept ominously. However, on the road to "The White Hart," the public discerned her plight. Having heard the entire story from the archers, who naturally recounted it passionately in her favor, they followed, cheering and encouraging her until, finding herself backed by numbers, she plucked up courage. The landlord also saw at a glance that her presence in the inn would attract custom, and he received her politely, assigning her an upper chamber: here she secluded herself, and being alone, shed tears anew.

Poor little mind, it was like a ripple, rising and falling, falling and rising, endlessly oscillating. Instructing the landlord to be exceedingly kind to her and keep her confined without making her feel imprisoned, the friends departed: and behold! As they stepped into the street, they witnessed two processions approaching from opposite directions. One was a large throng, accompanied by noise, howls, and those indescribable cries by which rough natures occasionally reveal a kinship to the beasts of the field and forest—a connection we usually succeed in suppressing. The other, sparsely attended by a few nuns and friars, moved slowly and silently.

These were the prisoners destined for exposure in the marketplace. These were the gathered bones of the victims being transported to the churchyard.

The two met in the narrow street nearly at the inn door and could not pass each other for a considerable time, and the bier bearing the relics of mortality became wedged against the cart carrying the men who had turned those bones into what they were, men who in a few hours must die for the same sin. The mob lacked the quick wit to immediately grasp the grim symmetry of this encounter: but eventually, a woman cried, "Behold your work, ye dogs!" and the crowd seized the sentiment like wildfire. There ensued a horrific yell, and the culprits groaned, attempting to hide their heads upon their bosoms, but failing, their hands being bound. There they stood, images of pale, hollow-eyed despair, and oh, how they gazed upon the bier, env

In the shadowed heart of the marketplace, justice was performed not with the solemnity of law, but with the brutality of fear. Four figures were condemned to stand pinioned, exposed to the prying eyes of the populace for two hours—a grotesque spectacle designed to ensure that any witness to their guilt, or any knowledge of their other crimes, might be extracted during the trial. Yet, despite the weight of their exposure, the crowd remained silent. No new accusations arose. This passivity was hardly surprising; these were creatures of the night, vampires who fed on the exhaustion of weary travelers, preying upon strangers in the dim recesses of the city.

But as the officers prepared to release them, a sound shattered the air—a scream of such primal terror that it seemed to tear the fabric of reality itself. A woman in the throng pointed a trembling finger at one of the accused, her eyes wide and bulging with a horror that defied human comprehension. She spoke not a word before collapsing, her spirit fleeing her body in sheer revulsion.

Men and women surged forward, torn between the instinct to aid the fallen and the morbid curiosity of the mob. As consciousness slowly returned to the woman, a cacophony of speculation erupted among the bystanders.

"'Twas at him she pointed!" someone cried.

"Nay, 'twas at this one!" countered another.

"No, no," a third insisted, gesturing wildly, "'twas at yon hangdog with the hair hanging loose around his neck."

Before these conjectures could spiral into chaos, the woman regained her wits entirely. With the ferocity of a lioness denied her kill, she lunged at the nearest officer—the landlord of the tavern—and screamed, "My child! Man! Man! Give me back my child!" Her fingers clawed at the glossy golden hair the officers had tied around the accused's neck, tearing it free with a violent snap. She buried her face in the strands, kissing them with a delirious desperation. Only then did the fog of grief clear from her mind. In that terrible clarity, she realized the truth: her lost daughter was dead. Overcome by the weight of this revelation, she sank to the ground, shrieking and sobbing over the stolen lock of hair until the entire crowd rose in a savage roar, turning their fury upon the accused.

His life ended swiftly and brutally. In those days, every citizen carried the means of instant execution at their girdle. But Denys, ever vigilant, drew his sword and shouted, "A moi, camarades!" rallying his companions to form a wall of steel between the mob and the victim. "Who lays a finger on him dies!" he commanded, his voice cutting through the din. Other archers fanned out, backing him up. With great difficulty, they held the line, keeping the assassins at bay while Denys appealed to the rioters.

"What sort of vengeance is this?" he roared, his face grim. "Would you be so mad as to rob the wheel of justice and grant this vermin an easy death?"

The mob, though growling with flashing eyes, was held passive not by Denys's words, but by the cold certainty of the archers' steel. Seeing the situation spiraling, the municipal officers gathered, forming a protective ring with the soldiers, and prudently escorted the accused back to the dungeon.

As the mob hooted at both the prisoners and the guards indiscriminately, Denys ensured the detainees were safely lodged. He then turned his steps toward "The White Hart," expecting to find Gerard waiting inside. On his path, however, he paused beneath a first-floor window where two girls were working. He saluted them, and they smiled back, their manners easy, their complexions radiant. Intrigued, he engaged them in conversation, inviting them to a meal at the inn. They initially objected, but after a brief exchange, they relented. In this fleeting moment of charming society, Denys momentarily forgot the plight of Gerard, who was currently being dragged away to prison.

Yet, fate intervened. Just as Gerard was led off, he suddenly halted, his presence of mind returning amidst the confusion. Demanding to know by whose authority he was arrested, he confronted his captors.

"By the vice-baillie's," the constable replied flatly.

"The vice-baillie?" Gerard exclaimed, his voice rising in indignation. "Alas! What have I, a stranger, done to offend a vice-baillie? This charge of sorcery must be a blind. No sorcerer am I; I am merely a poor, true lad far from my home."

Such vagueness disgusted the officer. "Show him the capias, Jacques," he ordered.

Jacques stepped forward, holding the writ aloft in both hands, extending it nearly a yard and a half from Gerard's face. Simultaneously, the large constable pinned Gerard down. Both officers stood on tenterhooks, fearing the prisoner might snatch the document—an act they attributed with superstitious dread.

But the poor prisoner had no such treacherous thoughts. One might ask if he would have touched it even with tongs. Instead, he simply craned his neck, reading the parchment with wide, startled eyes. To his infinite astonishment, he discovered that the vice-bailiff who had signed the writ was none other than the friendly alderman. Courage surged within him. "There is some error here," he assured his captor. Finding no response, he demanded to be taken immediately before the alderman.

"What say you to that, Jacques?" the officer asked.

"Impossible. We have no orders to take him before his worship. Read the writ!"

"Nay, but good kind fellows, what harm can it be? I will give you each an ecu."

"Jacques, what say you to that?"

"Humph!" Jacques grunted. "I say we have no orders *not* to take him to his worship. Read the writ!"

"Then say we take him to prison round by his worship."

Agreement was reached. They accepted the money, bidding Gerard observe that they were doing him a favor. He saw clearly that they desired gratitude as much as silver. He attempted to satisfy their cupidity, but the words stuck in his throat; feigning was never his forte.

Entering the alderman's presence, Gerard felt his heart hammering against his ribs. Begging with a faltering voice, he pleaded to know what offense he had committed since leaving that very room with Manon and Denys.

"Nought that I know of," the alderman replied calmly.

When the writ was produced, the alderman admitted signing it at daybreak. "I grow old, and my memory faileth me," he confessed. "A discussion regarding the girl... I quite forgot your own offense. But I remember now. All is well. You are the one I committed for sorcery. Wait! Before you go to gaol, you shall hear what your accuser says. Run and fetch him, you."

The man could not locate the accuser immediately. Growing impatient, the alderman turned to Gerard. "The main charge is that you set a dead body ablaze with diabolical fire—that flame which burns yet consumes nothing. And if 'tis true, young man, I am sorry for thee, for thou wilt assuredly burn with the fire of good pine logs in the marketplace of Neufchâteau."

"Oh, sir, for pity's sake let me have speech with his reverence the cure!" Gerard implored.

The alderman advised against it. "The Church was harder upon sorcerers than was the corporation."

"But, sir, I am innocent," Gerard protested, his voice shifting between a snarl and a whimper.

"Oh, if you think you are innocent—" the alderman interrupted sharply. "Officer, go with him to the cure; but see he escape you not. 'Innocent,' quotha?"

They located the cure in his doublet, repairing a wheelbarrow. Gerard recounted everything, appealing piteously to the priest. "Just for using a little phosphorus in self-defense against cut-throats, they intend to hang me."

It proved fortunate for the magician that he had already detailed his tale fully to the cure. Thus, that shrewd personage held the stick at the right end, while the corporation gripped it by the ferule. His reverence assumed an exceedingly grave demeanor. "I must question you privately on this untoward business," he declared. He ushered Gerard into a private room, ordering the officer to stand outside and guard the door, ready to enter if summoned. The big constable stood sentinel, quaking, expecting the room to vanish in a puff of brimstone.

Instantly alone, the cure's countenance unlocked, and he returned to himself. "Show me the trick on't," he said, his curiosity piqued.

"I cannot, sir, unless the room be darkened."

The cure swiftly closed out the light with a wooden shutter. "Now, then."

"But on what shall I put it?" Gerard asked. "Here is no dead face. 'Twas that which made it look so dire."

The cure groped about the room. "Good; here is an image: 'tis my patron saint."

"Heaven forbid! That were profanation," Gerard objected.

"Pshaw! 'Twill rub off, will't not?"

"Ay, but it goes against me to take such liberty with a saint," the sorcerer hesitated.

"Fiddlestick!" the divine dismissed him.

"To be sure, by putting it on his

The snow outside the town was a fresh, unbroken blanket, save for the chaotic tracks of innumerable wolves that had trampled every foot of the road into a muddy scarlet tapestry.

"We did well to heed the old man's counsel, Denys," Gerard murmured, his voice barely rising above the crunch of hooves and falling flakes.

"Aye, we did," Denys replied, his breath pluming in the freezing air. "For now that I recall, I heard them last night scurrying beneath our window, howling and whining for man's flesh in that market-place. Yet not a single fat burgher showed pity, dropping silver from a window to buy off the poor vagabones."

Gerard offered a smile, but it lacked warmth, clouded by an air of profound abstraction. They continued their plodding journey in a heavy silence, the weight of the world pressing down upon their shoulders.

"What dost thou meditate upon so profoundly?" Denys asked, breaking the quiet.

"Thy goodness," Gerard answered softly.

Denys bristled, his good humor evaporating instantly. Among his many quirks, he possessed a tolerance for genuine impertinence, a trait born of his inherent kindness. Yet, he would flare up at times where not even the shadow of a cause for anger existed.

"A civil question merits a civil reply," he said, his tone dry and sharp.

"Alas, I meant no other," Gerard pleaded.

"Then why pretend thou wert contemplating my virtue, knowing full well there is no goodness beneath my skin?" Denys demanded, his eyes flashing.

"Had another spoken thus, I would have retorted, 'Thou liest,'" Gerard countered gently. "But to thee, I say, 'Thou hast no eye for men's qualities, but only for women's.' Once more, I defy thy unreasonable choler and affirm I was thinking of thy goodness of yesterday. Thou wouldst have wedded me to the 'Tête d'Or,' or rather to the 'tête de veau dorée,' and left thyself solitary."

"Oh, are ye there, lad?" Denys recovered his cheerfulness in a heartbeat. "Well, to speak the truth, I meant it not for goodness, but for friendship and true fellowship, no more. And let me confess, my young master, my conscience pricks me even now for allowing thee to turn thy back upon fortune and peaceful days. A truer friend than I might have taken thee aside and somewhat hamstrung thee. Then hadst thou been fain to lie smarting at the 'Tête d'Or' for a month or so; yon skittish lass would have nursed thee tenderly, and all had been well. I had the blade in hand to do it, but remembering how thou hatest pain, though it be but a scratch, my craven heart failed me at the pinch." Denys wore a look of humble apology, as if lacking virtuous resolution when the path of duty lay so clear was a sin in itself.

Gerard raised his eyebrows in astonishment at this monstrous yet thoroughly characteristic revelation. This new, delicate point of friendship—the question of whether one ought, in all love, to sever the tendon of Achilles of one's friend—was never discussed further. An incident intervened.

"Here cometh one in our rear, riding on his neighbor's mule!" Denys shouted.

Gerard turned. "And how knowest 'tis not his own, pray?"

"Oh, blind fool! Because he rides it with no discretion."

In truth, the man came galloping like a fury. But what astonished the friends most was that upon reaching them, the rustic rider's eyes widened saucer-like. He drew the rein so suddenly and powerfully that the mule stumbled, sticking out her forelegs before sliding between the pedestrians like a four-legged table on castors.

"I trow ye are from the 'Tête d'Or'?" the stranger asked, panting. They assented. "Which of ye is the younger?"

"He that was born the later," Denys winked at his companion.

"Gramercy for the news."

"Come, divine then!"

"And shall. Thy beard is ripe, thy fellow's is green; he shall be the younger. Here, youngster." He held out a paper packet. "Ye left this at the 'Tête d'Or,' and our mistress sends it ye."

"Nay, good fellow, methinks I left nought." Gerard felt his pouch, confirming his emptiness.

"Would ye make our burgess a liar?" the rustic reproached, his voice dripping with indignation. "And shall I have no *pourboire*?" He repeated the demand, even more reproachfully, and charged forward, belly to earth.

"Nay, thou shalt have *pourboire*," Gerard said, pulling a small coin from his belt and tossing it.

"*A la bonne heure*!" cried the clown, his features beaming with disproportionate joy. "The Virgin go with ye; come up, Jenny!" Back he went, stomach to earth, as his nation is pleased to call it.

Gerard undid the packet. It was about six inches square, and inside it he found another packet, which contained a packet, and so on. At the fourth layer, he hurled the whole thing into the snow. Denys took it out and rebuked his petulance. He excused himself on the grounds of hating affectation.

Denys attested, "'The great toe of the little daughter of Herodias'—there was no affectation here, but only woman's good wit. Doubtless the wraps contained something which, out of delicacy, or her sex's lovely cunning, she would not have her hind see bestowed upon a young man; thy garter, to wit."

"I wear none."

"Her own then; or a lock of her hair. What is this? A piece of raw silk, fresh from the worm. Well, of all the love tokens!"

"Now who but thee ever dreamed that she is so naught as to send me love tokens? I saw no harm in her—barring her hands."

"Stay, here is something hard lurking in this soft nest. Come forth, I say, little nestling! Saints and pike-staves! Look at this!"

It was a gold ring with a great amethyst glowing and sparkling, full-colored yet pure as crystal.

"How lovely!" said Gerard innocently.

"And here is something writ; read it thou! I read not so glib as some, when I know not the matter beforehand."

Gerard took the paper. "'Tis a posy, and fairly enough writ." He read the lines, blushing like a girl. They were very naive, and may be thus Englished:

*'Youth, with thee my heart is fledde,
Come back to the golden Hedde!
Wilt not? yet this token keep
Of hir who doeth thy going weep.
Gyf the world prove harsh and cold,
Come back to 'the Hedde of gold.'*"

"The little dove!" purred Denys.

"The great owl! To go and risk her good name thus. However, thank Heaven she has played this prank with an honest lad that will ne'er expose her folly. But oh, the perverseness! Could she not bestow her nauseousness on thee?" Denys sighed and shrugged. "On thee that art as ripe for folly as herself?"

Denys confessed that his young friend had harped his very thoughts. 'Twas passing strange to him that a damsel with eyes in her head should pass by a man and bestow her affections on a boy. Still, he could not but recognize in this the bounty of Nature. Boys were human beings after all, and but for this occasional caprice of women, their lot would be too terrible; they would be out of the sun altogether, blighted, and never come to anything; since only the fair could make a man out of such unpromising materials as a boy.

Gerard interrupted this flattering discourse to beg the warrior-philosopher's acceptance of the lady's ring. He refused it flatly, insisting on Gerard going back to the "Tête d'Or" at once, ring and all, like a man, and not letting a poor girl hold out her arms to him in vain.

"Her hands, you mean."

"Her hand, with the 'Tête d'Or' in it."

Failing in this, he was for putting the ring on his friend's finger. Gerard declined. "I wear a ring already."

"What, that sorry gimcrack? Why, 'tis pewter, or tin at best: and this virgin gold, forbye the jewel."

"Aye, but 'twas Margaret gave me this one; and I value it above rubies. I'll neither part with it nor give it a rival," and he kissed the base metal, bidding it fear nought.

"I see the owl hath sent her ring to a goose," said Denys sorrowfully. However, he prevailed on Gerard to fasten it inside his bonnet. To this, indeed, the lad consented very readily. For sovereign qualities were universally ascribed to certain jewels; and the amethyst ranked high among these precious talismans.

When this was disposed of, Gerard earnestly requested his friend to let the matter drop, since speaking of the other sex to him made him pine so for Margaret, and almost unmanned him with the thought that each step was taking him farther from her. "I am no

Rough travel holds a peculiar allure for the English soul, even when the traveler bears no pilgrim's badge of romantic devotion. Yet, the chronicles of such journeys possess a fascination akin to that of a fossil; they preserve the strata of human experience. Nevertheless, since the true axis of this narrative turns upon Holland, it becomes incumbent upon us to return to that soil—to revisit the ordinary personages and mundane incidents that constitute the fabric of life across all eras.

Jorian Ketel arrived at Peter's house intent upon claiming Margaret's pledge. However, Margaret lay confined to her bed, succumbing to illness. Upon learning of Jorian's errand, Peter, inflamed with indignation, affronted him and ordered him from the premises. Those present, emboldened by the crowd's fervor, threatened to dunk him in the water. Both father and daughter were objects of intense local affection, and the entire saga had become public property. The citizens of Sevenbergen, caught in a state of heated, indiscriminate agitation fueled by popular sympathy, demanded justice. Thus, Jorian departed in a fury, regretting his earlier benevolence. Such repentance is not uncommon, possessing the virtue of sincerity.

Meanwhile, Dierich Brower, discovered at "The Three Kings" attempting to intoxicate a talkative individual to extract information regarding Martin Wittenhaagen's whereabouts, was apprehended and cast into a horse pond. He faced threats of further brutality should he ever appear in the borough again. Municipal jealousy, stirred to life, prompted the burgomaster of Sevenbergen to dispatch a formal missive to his counterpart in Tergou. The letter admonished the latter for overstepping legal boundaries and requested that any future complaints, real or imagined, directed against Sevenbergen's townsfolk be lodged directly with Sevenbergen's authorities.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, suppressing his rage at this rebuke, returned a courteous reply. He claimed the man he had tracked to Sevenbergen was merely a Tergovan named Gerard who had stolen the town records. Asserting that Gerard had fled into foreign lands, likely carrying the documents with him, Ghysbrecht declared the matter concluded. In doing so, he made a virtue of necessity. Yet, beneath this veneer of composure lay a storm of frustration. Baffled by the resistance in Sevenbergen, he redirected his gaze elsewhere, dispatching emissaries to the household in Tergou to ascertain Gerard's destination. Their response—"they did not know"—filled him with infinite surprise and deepening unease. He feared Gerard was merely lurking nearby, waiting to make a discovery and exact a terrible vengeance.

From this point forward, Dierich and his associates noted a palpable deterioration in Ghysbrecht's demeanor. He became a moody, irritable figure, haunted by a pervasive dread. His eyes darted furtively, scanning the horizon as if expecting a blow from an unknown quarter. The act of making others wretched had failed to bring him happiness, for rarely does inflicting pain heal the inflictor. The small family at Tergou, which might otherwise have resolved its differences without exiling *spem gregis* (hope of the flock) to a distant land, retained its outward appearance but lost its inner simplicity. The joyful unity that opened this tale had evaporated.

Little Kate understood the role Cornelis and Sybrandt played in banishing Gerard. Though she withheld this knowledge from her mother to prevent further discord, there were moments when she shuddered at their mere presence and blushed at their hypocritical expressions of regret. Catherine, exercising a woman's vigilant subtlety, observed these shifts without comment, quietly pondering the signs and watching for confirmation. The "black sheep," in their attempt to share the prevailing gloom, managed to deceive their father and Giles temporarily. However, the demure satisfaction simmering beneath their surfaces could not evade the keen scrutiny of the feminine gaze. "Noting all, seeming to note nothing," they masked their true feelings. Consequently, mistrust and suspicion settled at the dinner table, serving as poor substitutes for Gerard's intelligent visage, which had once illuminated the entire circle, remaining unnoticed until it vanished.

As for the elderly hosier, his pride had been wounded by his son's disobedience. He maintained a stiff exterior, striving never to utter Gerard's name. Yet, beneath his Spartan cloak, Nature tugged relentlessly at his heartstrings. One anxiety he never feigned to conceal: "If I but knew where the boy is, and that his life and health remain secure, my cares would be minimal," he would declare, followed invariably by a profound sigh. One cannot help but imagine that had Gerard opened the door at that precise moment and stepped inside, the scene would have been filled with tears and embraces rather than reproaches.

Then came a shock to the old couple: publicity. Within a week of Gerard's departure, his adventures dominated every conversation. Worse still, popular sympathy rallied passionately behind the lovers and against Gerard's "cruel" parents, as well as the meddling burgomaster who inserted himself into affairs beyond his jurisdiction.

"Mother," Kate remarked softly, "it is known throughout the town that Margaret is afflicted with a fever—a burning fever—and her father fears greatly for her."

"Margaret? Which Margaret?" Catherine queried, feigning a treacherous calm.

"Oh, Mother! Whom else could I mean? Why, Gerard's Margaret."

"Gerard's Margaret!" Catherine shrieked. "How dare you utter such a word to me? I warn you never to mention that hussy's name within these walls, for she has laid herself open to ruin. She is the destruction of my poor boy, the flower of my entire flock. Because of her, he is not a holy priest among us but roams the world, leaving me a desolate, broken-hearted mother. There, do not weep, my child; I speak harshly because I am distressed. Oh, Kate, you do not comprehend the turmoil within a mother's heart. I endure before you all; I must swallow my fears. Yet, at night, I see him in my dreams, always plagued by some new tribulation. Sometimes he is torn by wild beasts; other times, he falls into the grasp of robbers, their cruel knives raised to strike his pale face—one might think it would move a stone. Oh! When I recall that, while I sit here in comfort, my poor boy may lie dead in some savage land, all because of that girl... her very name is ratsbane to me. I tremble whenever I hear it."

"I shall say nothing, nor do anything to grieve you further, Mother," Kate replied tenderly, though a sigh escaped her lips.

She whose name was so fiercely proscribed within this home was widely discussed and even pitied outside. All of Sevenbergen felt sorrow for her, and young men and maidens cast numerous pitying glances as they passed the small window where the village beauty lay "dying for love." In this familiar phrase, they underestimated her spirit and selflessness. Gerard was alive, and she was too loyal to doubt his constancy. Her father was dear to her and helpless. Had not her physical weakness held her back, all her love for Gerard would not have prevented her from fulfilling her duties, though she might have performed them with a drooping head and a heavy heart.

However, the combination of physical and mental strain had triggered a violent fever. Only her youth and robust constitution saved her. The malady eventually receded, leaving her in a terrible state of debility where life felt like a burdensome weight. It is during such fragility that love and friendship become mortal angels, offering comfort in their voices and healing in their palms. Yet, this poor girl had to find a path back to vigor on her own. For many days, she lay alone as heavy hours rolled over her like leaden waves. In her enfeebled state, existence seemed a burden, and life appeared as something already passed. Unable to strive for recovery, she often lay motionless for hours, tears welling gently from her eyes.

One day, awakening from an uneasy slumber, she found two women in her chamber. One was a servant; the other, distinguished by the deep fur lining her collar and sleeves, was a woman of considerable standing. A narrow band of silvery hair, spared by her coiffure, indicated she had passed the age when sensible women conceal their years. Both women's expressions were kind and friendly. Margaret attempted to rise, but the older lady placed a gentle hand upon her arm.

"Lie still, sweetheart. We come not here to disturb you, but to offer comfort, God willing. Now, cheer up a little and tell us, first, who do you think we are?"

"Nay, madam, I recognize you, though I have never seen you before. You are the demoiselle Van Eyck, and this is Reicht Heynes. Gerard has often spoken of you and your kindness to him. Madam, he has no friend like you near him now," she said, collapsing back onto the pillows as tears instantly welled in her eyes.

The good-natured Reicht Heynes began to weep for companionship, prompting her mistress to chide her lightly. "Well, you are a pretty addition to a sickroom," she remarked, deploying a world of innocent artistry to lift the patient's spirits, achieving modest success. An old woman who has witnessed life's trials is a sovereign blessing beside a sorrowful youth. Knowing precisely what to say and what to omit, how to soothe and engage, she achieved remarkable results within an hour. Margaret's head rested on the elder's shoulder instead of the pillow, her soft eyes fixed upon her with gentle gratitude.

In the alchemy of the human spirit, age is merely a variable, irrelevant to the temperature of the soul. At forty, some hearts calcify into the brittle amber of regret; others remain fluid, burning with the volatile heat of youth. Margaret Van Eyck belonged to the latter category. Her heart was an evergreen, defying the seasonal decay that claimed so many. She loved her young namesake, Margaret Brandt, with a fervor that mirrored the sun's direct gaze. Yet, this affection was no fleeting caprice born of loneliness. Margaret Van Eyck possessed a sharp, surgical intuition for character, and in the girl before her, she recognized a rare alloy: genuineness. But beyond this sterling virtue, Margaret Brandt held a more potent asset within the bosom of the elder artist—a debt incurred not by service rendered, but by kindness bestowed.

Human nature operates on a paradoxical current; our affections often warm most rapidly not toward those who give, but toward those who receive. The ancient Greeks noted this inversion, but it was the bard of Britain who etched it into the marrow of history:

The poet recalled shielding a friend from the storm, bearing wounds meant for another, sucking venom from an arrow's bite. These memories, like torrents, swept away the speaker's resolve to harm. Observe the mechanism: the assassin's hand is stayed not by gratitude for past favors, but by the sheer weight of benefits conferred.

Margaret Van Eyck had performed miracles for Margaret Brandt. She had shattered her own rigid habits to cross the threshold of the sickroom, nursing the fevered brow, soothing the delirium, and petting the fragile spirit until the illness fled. Consequently, her heart had opened wide to the recipient of her grace. She loved the girl now with a tenderness that eclipsed anything she had ever felt for Gerard, even though her initial visits had been motivated solely by concern for her son.

Thus, when Margaret Van Eyck beheld the crimson roses blooming on Margaret's cheek—the physical manifestation of the love letter—and read the parchment that commanded them, she surrendered her own strategic vision without a murmur.

"Sweetheart," she said, her voice softening like velvet over steel, "I desired he should linger in Italy for five or six years, returning rich and renowned as an artist. But your happiness precedes all else. I see you cannot breathe without him; therefore, we must summon him home as swiftly as possible."

"Oh, madam! You see the very thoughts dancing in my mind," the young woman confessed, hanging her head and flushing with a shame that was not hers. "But how to inform him? That surpasses my skill. He has departed for Italy, though I know not his destination. Wait—he listed the cities he must traverse. Florence... and Rome." A pause stretched, filled with the anxiety of uncertainty. Finally, the sensible girl realized that a letter addressed vaguely to "My Gerard—Italy" risked getting lost in the labyrinth of foreign streets. She turned imploring eyes to her mentor for counsel.

"You have arrived at the precise place and the exact hour," the old lady declared. "Behold, this Hans Memling sits with me today. He departs for Italy no later than next week, 'to refine his hand,' he claims. Not before necessity demanded it, I assure you."

"But how shall he locate my Gerard?"

"Why, he knows your Gerard, child. They have shared tables here more than once; they fit together like hand and glove. Since their trade is identical, their paths will inevitably converge. Soon, fate will drop them into one another's orbit. Therefore, compose a lengthy missive, copy this pardon into it, and I vouch for the messenger. Within six months at the utmost, Gerard shall possess it. Upon receiving it, he will press it to his bosom and fly home. What are you smiling at? And why do your cheeks burn? What are you stifling? I cannot guess. Yes! Happy days approach for my little pearl."

Meanwhile, in the shadowed warmth of the kitchen, Martin sat before the black-jack, while Reicht Heynes spun beside him. The atmosphere crackled with tension, and Reicht pumped Martin relentlessly that night, extracting secrets with the diligence of a interrogator.

Hans Memling, an old disciple of Jan Van Eyck and his sister, was indeed a painter, despite Margaret's earlier sneer, and a man of considerable integrity save for one fatal flaw. He possessed an insatiable hunger for the "nipperkin, canakin, and the brown bowl"—wine, ale, and beer—which kept him perpetually destitute. This singular vice prevented the accumulation of fortune, forcing him frequently to seek sustenance and coin from Margaret Van Eyck. Yet, this dependence forged a bond; she knew he would honor any commission she entrusted to him, for pride would not allow him to fail his patron.

The letter was penned and delivered to Margaret Van Eyck. The following week, Hans Memling returned from Flanders. With the document in hand and a piece of gold provided for his travels, he seemed driven by an urgent impulse to depart.

"All the better," the old artist remarked. "He will reach Italy the sooner."

Yet, as with horses that burn with a frenzied urge to start only to stall immediately after leaving the stable, Hans's haste cooled into inertia once he reached the principal inn of Tergou and spotted two of his boon companions seated in the bay window. He entered for a final drink, but when he attempted to settle the tab, they refused payment. "No," they insisted. "You are embarking on a long journey; we must treat you. Everyone must treat you, including the landlord."

The result was a loosening of the tongue akin to pouring oil over a dry reed. Confident in his inebriation, Hans boasted of showing the Italians how to paint, recounted his exploits in battle wielding a pike, and detailed his amorous conquests with women present to contradict his version of events. In short, *plenus rimarum erat*; he flowed with stories in every direction. Among the miscellaneous debris of his rambling, he blurted that he carried a letter for a townsman of theirs, a certain Gerard, a fine fellow. He added, "You are all good fellows," and to emphasize his praise, he slapped Sybrandt on the back with such forceful enthusiasm that he drove the air from the man's lungs.

Sybrandt circled the table to evade this muscular greeting, yet he absorbed every syllable, learning for the first time that Gerard had departed for Italy. Determined to verify the intelligence, he feigned skepticism.

"My brother Gerard is never in Italy."

"Ye lie, ye cur!" roared Hans, instantly shifting to an irascible mode, failing to recognize that the man sitting opposite him was the very individual he had just lauded and assaulted. "If he is ten times your brother, he is in Italy. What proof do you offer? There, read me that superscription!" He flung the letter across the table.

Sybrandt picked it up, examining it with grave deliberation, before laying it down with the excuse that he could not read. However, one of the company, blessed by some immense fortuity with literacy, seized the opportunity. Proud of this rare accomplishment, he took the paper and read aloud:

"To Gerard Eliassoen, of Tergou. These by the hand of the trusty Hans Memling, with all speed."

"'Tis excellently well writ," observed the reader, scrutinizing every stroke.

"Ay!" Hans proclaimed bombastically. "And small wonder! 'Tis penned by a famous hand; by Margaret, sister of Jan Van Eyck. Blessed and honored be her memory! She is an old friend of mine, is Margaret Van Eyck."

Afterward, Miscellaneous Hans diverged into forty disparate topics.

Seizing the moment, Sybrandt slipped from the company and sought out Cornelis.

Together, they plotted over the news. Italy lay an immense distance away. Could they not detain him there?

"Detain him there? Nothing would hold him long from his Margaret."

"Curse her!" Sybrandt spat. "Why didn't she perish when she was about it?"

"She die? She would outlive the plague to vex us." Cornelis raged at her selfishness, unable to conceive of her death as a favor to them.

These two black sheep continued to confer, poisoning each other's minds until their corrupt hearts conceived a diabolical scheme: to keep Gerard in Italy forever, thereby securing his inheritance for themselves.

However, planning was easy; execution required talent, and iniquity stalled. Then, as if Satan had intervened, whispering simultaneously into the right ear of one and the left of the other, they both erupted in unison:

They approached Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, who received them immediately. The man writhing under the torture of suspense grasps eagerly at any fragment of knowledge. Certainty is often painful, but rarely, like suspense, is it intolerable.

"You have news of Gerard?" he asked eagerly.

They recounted the letter and Hans Memling. He listened with restless eyes. "Who wrote the letter?"

"Margaret Van Eyck," they replied, assuming the content matched the signature.

"Are ye sure?" Ghysbrecht moved to a drawer and produced a paper written by Margaret Van Eyck during negotiations for her house. "Was it written thus?"

"Yes. 'Tis the same handwriting

Denys, positioned squarely between his companions like a sacrificial offering to prevent any further attempts at flight, was consumed by an agony of grief and remorse that bordered on madness. He bore the crushing weight of betrayal; for his sake, Gerard had forsaken the direct German road to Rome, only to be abandoned utterly in the heart of Burgundy. This realization—the sheer isolation of his friend amidst a hostile landscape—was the poison that drove Denys to rage against the heavens and the earth alike, oscillating between frenzied shouting and a silence so dark and menacing that his captors felt compelled to bind him tightly.

They seized their leader just beyond the town limits, halting the entire cavalcade at the *Tête d'Or*. The young landlady, though distracted by the Count and increasingly wary of the Bastard, spotted Denys and approached with anxious inquiry regarding his missing companion. Overcome by a fresh torrent of sorrow, Denys confessed everything, imploring her to dispatch someone immediately to retrieve Gerard. "Now that he is severed from me, he may yet heed my counsel," he pleaded, his voice breaking. "Poor wretch, he cannot endure solitude." The landlady tossed her head dismissively, her cheeks flushing with irritation. "I believe I have shown you enough kindness already," she retorted sharply. "Will you not?" "Certainly not," Denys snapped. "Then"—he unleashed a barrage of curses and vitriol upon her. She spun on her heel, fleeing the scene with whimpers, claiming she was unaccustomed to such abuse, yet she swiftly commanded her stablehand to saddle two mules.

Denys rode north with his troop, head bowed low over his saddle, oblivious to the fact that the truthful young woman behind him was already preparing an equestrian disguise in a mere forty minutes. Driven by urgency, she spurred away with her servant in the exact opposite direction. That evening, after a grueling march, the Bastard and his men arrived at *The White Hart*, sparking a commotion of unprecedented scale. It took considerable time before Manon discerned her old friend amidst the throng; when she finally recognized him, her reaction was subtle—a deepening flush across her cheeks—but she made no overt claim to their shared history. The poor soul, however, began to recoil, his pride hardening into scorn for the humble origins from which she had risen. Denys observed this transformation but could not muster a smile. The inn itself seemed a grotesque echo of Gerard's fate.

Before night fully claimed the sky, the wind shifted. Manon entered the room, beckoning him with a single, urgent finger. He rose sullenly, dragging his guards in tow. "Nay, I wish to speak a word to thee in private," she insisted. Drawing him to a shadowed corner, she lowered her voice. "Wouldst thou do me a kindness?" Denys answered loudly, refusing to whisper, "No. I am not in the mood to bestow kindnesses upon man or woman. Were I to grant a favor, it would be to a dog—and even then, only if necessity demanded it."

"Alas, good archer," Manon said humbly, her voice trembling, "thou hast done me one favor once ere now, thou and thy pretty comrade." "Thou hast, dame, thou hast," Denys replied coolly. "Well then, for his sake—what is required?" "Thou knowest my tale. I was unfortunate, but now I am worshipful. Yet a woman cast him in my teeth today, and so it shall remain while he hangs there. I desire him taken down; alas!" "With all my heart," Denys declared. "But none dare I ask save thee. Wilt thou do it?" "Not I, even were I not a prisoner." Upon hearing this stern refusal, the tender Manon sighed, clasping her palms together in despair. Denys reassured her, telling her not to fret. There were soldiers of a lower caliber who would not hesitate to accept such a task for coin. It was merely a matter of money; if she could provide two angels, he would secure two men to perform the dirty work of *The White Hart*.

Though the prospect was distasteful, Manon reflected that soldiers were transient creatures, drunk in one tavern tonight and silenced in another tomorrow. Softly, she conceded, "Send them out to me. But pray, tell them it is for a friend; let them not think it is for me. I should sink into the earth; times have changed." Denys readily found warriors eager to win an angel each so effortlessly. He dispatched them, instantly dismissing the subject with contempt, and sat brooding on his lost friend. Soon, Manon and the hired hands reached a general accord. But what of the body once removed? She murmured, "The river lies near—the place." "Cast him in, then?" "Nay, nay; be not so cruel! Could ye not lay him gently—with something weighty beneath him?" She must have pondered the logistics in detail, for she was not one whose ideas arrived swiftly. Everything was settled rapidly, save for the timing of payment. The mail-clad warriors itched for their due, seeking it in advance. Manon hesitated. Did she doubt their word? Then let her accompany them, or watch from a distance. "Me?" Manon cried out in horror. "I would sooner die than witness it." "Yet that is precisely what thou wouldst have done." "Ay, for sore is my need. Times have changed." She had already forgotten her earlier admonition to Denys.

An hour later, the disagreeable relic of their caterpillar-like existence ceased to mar the worshipful matron's public life, and the grim eyes of the past stopped casting malignant glances down into the white hind's clover field. She had secured herself an average husband and a prime house-dog, destined to outlive everyone else. Her troops, returning from executing the medieval maxim "Off wi' the auld love" with naive brutality, received a profound shock. They found the marketplace choked with black-clad groups; it had stood empty an hour prior. Conscience struck them hard. Such was the price of meddling with the dead. Nevertheless, the bolder of the two, emboldened by the encroaching darkness, stole forward alone. He slily merged into a gathering crowd, then returned to his companion with a tone of reproach that lacked strict reasonableness. "Ye born fool," he muttered, "it is nothing but a miracle."

The letters of fire scrawled upon the church wall had interrogated the silence with a genuine, burning curiosity, demanding to know why no Mass was held for the Duke during this season of turmoil. This supernatural exclamation had been witnessed by many, lingering briefly before fading into the gray mist, leaving the spectators staring with mouths agape at the empty space where divine favor should have burned. Yet, the Corporation, unwilling to lag behind celestial powers in their fealty to a temporal sovereign, generously funded the services. From this transaction, an old friend of ours, the Cure, derived a substantial profit—a sum he possessed a distinct and refined taste for. However, I shall not detain you further with such trivialities as miracles, nor with the mundane economics of piety.

Denys pleaded for his weapons. "Why disgrace him as well as shatter his heart?" he demanded, his voice tight with the friction of conflicting loyalties.

"Then swear upon the cross of thy sword," the commander replied, his tone leaving no room for negotiation, "not to abandon the bastard's service until the sedition is extinguished." Necessity, that cruel architect, compelled Denys to yield. He delivered three volleys of oaths, binding his soul to the cause, and in exchange, recovered his arms and his liberty.

The troops halted at an inn known as "The Three Fish." Upon seeing Denys, Marion cried out, "I am out of luck; who would have thought to see you again?" Her greeting was blunt, lacking the polish of courtly romance. Seeing that Denys wore a mask of sorrow rather than amusement at her jest, she pressed him gently. "What troubles thee?"

He confessed his burden. Marion, however, possessed a bright, unyielding optimism regarding the trajectory of fate. "Gerard is too handsome and too well-behaved to come to harm," she reasoned, her logic as sturdy as the stone walls of the city. "Moreover, the women will always stand by his side. Things must either go well or ill with him. If fortune smiles, he joins the noble companies marching to Rome; if ill, he returns home, perhaps arriving before his friend, for you have a trifling campaign in Flanders awaiting you by all accounts."

She produced her purse, offering gold pieces to ease his journey. Denys steadfastly refused to accept a single coin, despite her repeated urging. Yet, Denys found himself swayed by her argument, not merely because she aligned with his own desires, but because her honesty warmed him. It made him regard the world in which his poor, solitary friend wandered with a newfound respect.

Scattered foot soldiers, reduced to small bands of two or three, were already striding lazily toward Flanders. Many were penniless, drifting from town to town, relying on bailiffs to secure food and lodging from innkeepers along the way. Anthony of Burgundy intercepted these wandering groups, gathering them beneath his banner until he led a formidable host of six hundred men into the province. Crossing the frontier, he was greeted by his brother Baldwyn, who arrived laden with men, arms, and provisions. Anthony organized his entire force, marching in battle array through several towns. Their passage was marked not only by a lack of impediment but by great acclamations from the populace. Such overt loyalty, however, drew comments that were far from gracious.

"This rebellion of ours is a bite," growled a soldier named Simon, who had elected himself Denys's comrade, his voice dripping with cynicism.

Denys said nothing, but he made a silent vow to Saint Mars: should this uprising, which had cost him Gerard, prove to be anything less than a true rebellion, he would shoot Anthony of Burgundy dead.

That afternoon, the army came into sight of a strongly fortified town. A whisper rippled through the little army, identifying the place as disaffected. Yet, when they approached, the great gate stood wide open, and the flanking towers were manned by a single sentinel each. Confident and careless, the advancing force broke their formation.

Within a furlong, the drawbridge across the moat rose slowly, creaking with the groan of timber, until it stood vertical against the fortifications. The very moment it settled into this warlike posture, the portcullis rumbled downward at the gate, and the towers and curtains bristled with lances and crossbows.

A stern hum vibrated through the bastard's front rank and spread to the rear.

"Halt!" cried Denys. The command traveled down the line, and they froze. "Herald to the gate!" A pursuivant spurred out of the ranks, halting twenty yards from the entrance. Raising his bugle with his herald's flag hanging low beside him, he blew a summons. A tall figure in brazen armor appeared over the gate. Fiery words exchanged between the herald and the gatekeeper were inaudible, yet their intent was clear; the herald blew a single, keen, threatening note at the walls and galloped back, war written plainly upon his face.

The bastard moved out of the line to meet him. Within two seconds of their heads coming together, he turned in his saddle and shouted, "Pioneers, to the van!" Instantly, hedges were leveled, the force took the field, and encamped just out of range of the walls. Mounted officers flew south, east, and west to friendly towns, scouring for catapults, palisades, mantelets, raw hides, tar-barrels, carpenters, provisions, and every material necessary for a siege.

This bright prospect greatly cheered the drooping spirits of the soldiers. At the first clang of the portcullis, eyes brightened and temples flushed.
