When the herald returned with battle in his gaze, the realization hit them in a moment, and for the first time in many days, a cry rang out: "Courage, *tout le monde*, le diable est mort."

If that great warrior heard, how he must have grinned!

The besiegers established their camp a furlong from the walls and began constructing roads. Pikemen remained in camp, ready for assault when practicable, while sappers, pioneers, catapultiers, and crossbowmen moved forward. These engineers initiated the siege by filling the moat, mining, and preparing to breach the walls. Since much of this work required execution under the close fire of arrows, quarrels, bolts, stones, and rocks, these artisans needed a hundred eyes. They operated with a concert of vigilance, individual intelligence, daring, and skill that rendered the siege not only fascinating but, to the observers, almost amusing.

First, they advanced their carpenters behind rolling mantelets to erect a high, strong stockade right on the edge of the moat. Some lives were lost, but not many. A strong force of crossbowmen, including Denys, rolled their mantelets up and fired over the workmen's heads, targeting every besieged who exposed himself and every loophole, arrow-slit, or aperture commanding the specific spot where the carpenters labored. Shielded by this condensed fire, they soon raised a high palisade between themselves and the ordinary missiles raining down from the pierced masonry.

But the besieged anticipated this move. At night, they ran out boards or wooden penthouses atop the curtains. The curtains were constructed with square holes near the top designed to receive beams supporting these structures—the true defense of medieval forts. From these positions, the defenders delivered missiles with far greater freedom and variety of range than the besiegers could achieve through the oblique, immovable loopholes of the curtain or the sloping crenelets of the higher towers. In response, the besiegers brought up mangonels, setting them to hurl huge stones at these woodworks, battering them to pieces. Simultaneously, they erected a triangular wooden tower as high as the curtain, keeping it ready for use just out of immediate shot.

To the besieged, this was a terrible sight. Wooden towers had taken many a town. They began mining underneath the section of the moat where the tower stood frowning, making other preparations to give it a warm reception. The besiegers also mined, aiming to undermine the square barbican and bring it crashing down. Throughout this chaos, Denys remained behind his mantelet alongside another arbalestrier, protecting the workmen and delivering excellent shots. These actions eventually earned him the esteem of an unseen archer, who periodically sent a winged compliment quivering into his mantelet. One bolt struck within an inch of the narrow slit through which Denys was squinting at that precise moment.

"Peste!" cried Denys. "You shoot well, my friend. Come forth and receive my congratulations! Shall merit such as thine hide its head? Comrade, it is one of those cursed Englishmen, with his half-ell shaft. I will not die until I have had a shot at London Wall."

On the side of the besieged, a figure soon attracted great notice by promenading under fire. It was a tall knight, clad in complete brass, carrying a light but prodigiously long lance with which he directed the movements of the defenders. Whenever disaster befell the besiegers, this tall knight and his long lance were invariably implicated.

"My young reader" might ask, "Why did not Denys shoot him?" Denys did shoot him; every day of his life. Other arbalestriers shot him; archers shot him. Everyone shot him. He was there to be shot, seemingly by design. But the abomination was that he did not mind being shot. Worse still, he grew so demoralized by the barrage that he seemed unaware when he was struck. He walked his battlements under fire, much like

A profound metamorphosis seized Margaret Brandt, transforming her spirit until she moved through her domestic sphere like a ghost haunting her own life. Her household duties were performed with the detached rhythm of a dreamer, her movements devoid of their usual vitality. When Peter, ever the blunt observer, spoke to her with any rapidity, she flinched violently, locking two wide, terrified eyes upon him as if he were a specter. Her visits to her beloved friend Margaret Van Eyck dwindled; when she did arrive, the warmth of the old lady's embrace met only with a trembling, passive resistance, causing Margaret to recoil as though burned. Yet, the most harrowing symptom of her affliction was her refusal to step beyond the sanctuary of her own roof under the sun. She ventured to Tergou only after dusk had swallowed the world, returning before the dawn broke, shunning even the sacred light of matins. Finally, Peter, though naturally unobservant, pierced the veil of her silence. "Methinks the folk all look at me," she confessed, her voice barely a whisper.

One afternoon, driven by concern, Margaret Van Eyck pressed her friend for the source of her torment. A look of sheer terror and a cascade of tears were the only response; the elder woman pleaded gently, asking why she feared to confide her sorrows. "I have no sorrows, madam, save those of my own making," Margaret replied, her voice breaking. "I am treated with a kindness I do not deserve, especially within these walls." "Then why not come oftener, my dear?" urged the old lady. "I come oftener than I deserve," Margaret sighed, the weight of her guilt crushing her further. "There, Reicht is crying for you," Margaret Van Eyck interrupted, pointing to the child. "Go, child—what on earth could possibly be wrong?"

Margaret Van Eyck, turning the possibilities over in her mind, concluded that Margaret must be mortified by the contemptuous treatment she endured from Gerard's family. Resolute, she vowed to reprimand the women responsible. The following day, clad in her hood and cloak, accompanied by the weeping Reicht, she marched to the hosier's house. Catherine received her with feigned respect, thanking her profusely with tears for her past kindness to Gerard. However, the moment Margaret attempted to pivot the conversation to Margaret Brandt, the hosier's demeanor shifted grotesquely. Her eyes dried, her lips thinned to a rigid line, and she adopted the stony visage of an obstinate, ignorant woman—a mask so impenetrable that one might as well try to soften a brick wall. Margaret Van Eyck, unused to such defiance, grew provoked. With an abrupt, mutilated curtsey that lacked all grace, she turned and fled, leaving behind Catherine's defiant glare.

Outside, Margaret Van Eyck encountered Reicht speaking with a pale girl leaning on crutches. Pushing past them with flushed cheeks and a scornful toss of her head directed at the entire family, she was suddenly halted by a small, delicate hand slipping timidly into hers. Looking around, she beheld two dove-like eyes filled with tears, gazing at her with a mixture of grateful relief and desperate pleading. Reading this complex, heartbreaking expression, the old lady's anger dissolved instantly. She stopped, kneeling to kiss Kate's brow. "I see," she murmured softly. "Very well, then, I leave it to you."

Returning home, Margaret recounted her journey with a mixture of pity and awe. "I visited a house today where I witnessed both the commonplace and the miraculous," she declared. "I saw a stupid, obstinate woman, and I also saw an angel incarnate, possessing a face so beautiful that if I held my paints here, I would take down my brushes once more and strive to capture its likeness."

Little Kate did not betray the high regard so swiftly formed of her. Seizing a better opportunity, she revealed what she had learned from Reicht Heynes: that Margaret Brandt had shed her very blood for Gerard in the woods. "See, Mother, how she loves him," Kate whispered. "Who would not love him?" "Oh, Mother, think of it! Poor thing." "Ay, wench. She has her own troubles, no doubt, just as we have ours. I cannot bear the sight of blood, let alone my own." This concession was a victory, yet when Kate pressed further, she was abruptly silenced.

About a month later, a soldier of the Dalgetty tribe, returning from service in Burgundy, delivered a letter to the hosier's house. Though he departed immediately to attend to business in Sevenbergen, the family gathered around the table as supper was served. The soldier placed the envelope beside Catherine, refusing any reward. Unfolding the paper, they discovered, with curious unanimity despite their inability to read, that the handwriting belonged to Gerard. "And your father must be away," cried Catherine, her voice rising in indignation. "Are you not ashamed? Not one among you can read your brother's letter." Although the words remained hieroglyphics to them, the letter carried a silent, universal language—an art that speaks without words, crossing frontiers and seas to strike directly at the heart. It required no translator, for it wrote in a tongue understood by all. Upon seeing the simple sketch Gerard had drawn between the two short paragraphs—a picture of two hands clasped tightly—they understood immediately. Gerard was bidding them farewell. As they gazed upon the lines, recognizing his manner in every curve, he seemed present, saying goodbye. The women wept until the image blurred before them. Giles spoke "Poor Gerard!" in a voice unnaturally low, while even Cornelis and Sybrandt felt a fleeting remorse, sitting in somber silence.

Desperate to decipher the message without exposing their ignorance or vulnerability to the stranger, Kate timidly suggested, "The Dame Van Eyck?" "And so I will, Kate," the soldier agreed. "She has a good heart. She loves Gerard, too. She will be glad to hear of him. I was sharp with her when she came here, but I shall make my submission, and then she will tell me what my poor child says." Soon, Margaret Van Eyck stood before the hosier's door. Reicht ushered her into a room, warning, "Wait a moment; she is at her prayers." Inside, a young woman sat pensively by the stove. Rising gracefully, she made way for the visitor. "Thank you, young lady; the winter nights are cold, and your stove is a treat," the newcomer remarked warmly.

While warming her hands, Catherine scrutinized her companion furtively. The young woman wore a plain wimple, yet her gown was trimmed with fur—a rare sign of superior rank or wealth in those times. What struck Catherine most profoundly, however, was the candor and modesty radiating from the girl's face. Feeling certain of sympathy from such a noble countenance, Catherine began to chatter nervously. "Now, what brings you here, young lady? It is a letter! A letter from my poor boy, far away in some savage land. I take shame to say that none of us can read it. I wonder if you can?" "Yes," the girl replied simply. "Can you, now? It is much to your credit, my dear. I dare say she won't be long, but every minute feels like an hour to a poor, longing mother." "I will read it to you," the girl promised. "Bless you, my dear; bless you!"

In her unfeigned eagerness, the girl failed to notice the suppressed urgency with which the hand was slowly extended to take the letter, nor the tremor that gripped the fingers as they closed around it. "Come, then, read it to me, pray. I am wearying for it," Catherine implored. "The first words are, 'To my honoured parents,'" the girl began. "Ay! And he always did honour us, poor soul," Catherine sobbed. "'God and the saints have you in His holy keeping, and bless you by night and by day. Your one harsh deed is forgotten; your years of love remembered.'" Catherine laid her hand over her bosom and sank back in her chair, overcome by one long, rending sob. "Then comes this, madam. It speaks for itself: 'a long farewell.'" "Ay, go on; bless you, girl, you give me sorry comfort. Still, 'tis comfort." "'To my brothers Cornelis and Sybrandt—Be content; you will see me no more!'" "What does that mean? Ah!" "'To my sister Kate. Little angel of my father's house. Be kind to her—for poor Gerard's sake: since grief to her is death to me.'"

At the mention of Margaret Brandt, the dam broke. "That is Margaret Brandt, my dear—his sweetheart, poor soul. I've not been kind to her, my dear. Forgive me, Gerard!" Nature, resenting the girl's struggle to maintain unnatural composure, suddenly gave way. She sank from her chair, collapsing into insensibility, the letter clutched in her hand, her head resting heavily on Catherine's knees.

Experienced women possess a stoicism that renders them immune to panic when a sister falls; they do not hastily conjure specters of moral failing, but rather diagnose the immediate physical collapse with the clinical detachment honed by observation. Catherine moved with the brisk efficiency of one who knows the body's fragility better than its secrets. She lowered the girl until her head rested flat upon the floor, flung open the shutter to invite the raw air, and loosened the corset that constricted the victim's breath. Only after these mechanical acts of rescue were complete did she turn to the threshold and command, her voice ringing with authoritative clarity: "Come here, if you please."

Margaret Van Eyck and Reicht entered to find the scene stark: the girl prone and pale, Catherine thrashing her hands in a futile rhythm of distress. "Oh, my poor girl! What have you done to her?" cried the elder woman, her composure fracturing under the weight of maternal instinct.

"Me?" Catherine snapped, her anger rising like steam from a boiling kettle. "What has happened, then?"

"Nothing, madam; nothing more than is natural in her situation," Reicht replied, attempting to soothe the storm.

Margaret Van Eyck flushed with a heat that betrayed her ire. "You do well to speak so coolly," she retorted, her eyes narrowing, "you that are the architect of her situation."

"That I am not," Catherine declared bluntly, her stance rigid. "Nor is any woman born."

"Was it not you and your husband who kept them apart?" Margaret pressed, her voice trembling with suppressed accusation. "And now he has fled to Italy all alone. 'Situation' indeed! You have shattered her heart amongst you."

"Why, madam? Who is it then? In Heaven's name!" Catherine recoiled slightly, scandalized. "To hear you, one would think this was my Gerard's lass. But that cannot be. This fur never cost less than five crowns the ell; besides, this young gentlewoman is a wife—or ought to be."

"Of course she ought," Margaret insisted, her tone sharpening. "And who is the cause she is none? Who stood before them at the very altar?"

"God forgive them, whoever it was," Catherine said gravely, shaking her head. "Me it was not, nor my man."

"Well," the other woman conceded, her anger softening into concern as she observed the girl's breathing deepen, "now you have seen her, perhaps you will not be quite so bitter against her, madam. She is coming to, thank Heaven."

"Bitter against her?" Catherine laughed, a dry, incredulous sound. "No, that is all over. Poor soul! Trouble behind her and trouble ahead; and to think of my setting her, of all living women, to read Gerard's letter to me. Ay, and that was what made her go off, I'll be sworn. She is coming to. What, sweetheart! Be not afraid, none are here but friends."

They lifted the girl into an easy chair. As color began to creep back to her cheeks and lips, Catherine drew Margaret Van Eyck aside, lowering her voice to a conspiratorial whisper. "Is she staying with you, if you please?"

"No, madam."

"I wouldn't let her go back to Sevenbergen tonight, then."

"That is as she pleases. She still refuses to bide the night."

"Ay, but you are older than she is; you can make her. There, she is beginning to notice."

Catherine leaned forward, pressing her mouth to Margaret Van Eyck's ear for a fleeting instant. It seemed insufficient time to whisper a word, far less a sentence, yet between women, communication flashes across the chasm of silence like lightning or pure thought itself. The old lady stiffened, her eyes widening in horror, and whispered back urgently.

"It is false! It is a calumny! It is monstrous! Look at her face. It is blasphemy to accuse such a face."

"Tut! Tut! Tut!" Margaret dismissed the warning with a wave of her hand. "You might as well say this is not my hand. I ought to know; and I tell you it is so."

Then, to Margaret Van Eyck's profound astonishment, she rose and took the girl by the neck, kissing her warmly, a gesture of fierce affection. "I suffered for Gerard, and you shed your blood for him, I do hear; his own words show me that I have been to blame, the very words you have read to me. Ay, Gerard, my child, I have held aloof from her; but I'll make it up to her once I begin. You are my daughter from this hour."

Another warm embrace sealed this hasty compact, and the woman of impulse vanished into the room. Margaret lay back in her chair, a feeble smile stealing over her face. Gerard's mother had kissed her and claimed her as kin; but the next moment, she saw her old friend regarding her with a vexed, troubled air.

"I wonder you let that woman kiss you," Margaret murmured, half-reproachful.

"Mother, or no mother, you would not let her touch you if you knew what she whispered in my ear about you," Margaret continued, her voice low.

"About me?" Margaret asked faintly.

"Ay, about you, whom she never saw till tonight."

The old lady proceeded, with hesitant choice of language, to draw Margaret into the vortex of her indignation, when an unlooked-for interruption silenced her. The young woman slid from the chair to her knees, bowing her head, and began to pray piteously for pardon. From the fervor of her words and the agony of her manner, a bystander would have gathered that she had inflicted some cruel wrong, some intolerable insult, upon her venerable friend.

The gathering at the hosier's dwelling hung suspended in a fragile equilibrium, the little party seated around the table digesting the weight of recent revelations. The atmosphere shattered only when their mother entered, her presence cutting through the murmurs like a blade. She swept a piercing gaze across the circle, her eyes searching for purchase, before laying the letter flat upon the wood. With deliberate slowness, she traced the inked lines with a trembling finger, feigning literacy to anchor herself and the room in the illusion that she could decipher the script. Then, abruptly, she lifted her head, locking eyes with Cornelis and Sybrandt. Their gazes dropped, unable to meet hers. In that instant, the tempest that had simmered beneath the surface erupted, crashing down with violent clarity.

Catherine seemed to inflate, swelling like an agitated bird ruffing its feathers against the wind. From her lips poured a torrent—a *Rhone* and *Saône* of wisdom and nonsense, of grand invectives and petty insults, a verbal deluge no man born of earth could muster in a single stream, let alone a woman. Her speech was a chaotic mosaic of accusation and revelation: "For ages I harbored suspicions that you kindled the flame between Gerard and your father, setting that old rogue, Ghysbrecht, upon the path. Now, here stand Gerard's own words, written in blood and ink, proving your treachery. You have driven your own flesh and blood into a distant, foreign land, robbing the mother who bore you of her darling, the pride of her eye, the joy of her heart. Yet, you remain consistent from end to end. When we were all boys, my others were a comfort; you were a curse—mischievous, sly, requiring a woman half a day merely to mend your torn garments. Why? Work wears cloth, but play cuts it. Prudence arrives with the beard, but none came to you. You were the last to retire and the last to rise, for honesty sleeps early and industry wakes betimes. Where two lie-in-beds exist in a house, there dwells a pair of ne'er-do-wells.

Often I have sat watching your ways, bewildered by your origins. You bear no resemblance to your father, nor are you akin to me save as a wasp differs from an ant. Surely you were altered in the cradle, or perhaps the cuckoo dropped you upon my floor, for you possess neither our hands nor our hearts. Of all my blood, none but you ever mocked those God afflicted. Behind my back, I heard you sneer at Giles for lacking size, and at my lily Kate for failing the strength of a Flemish mare. If you can mock men and women thus, surely you will rob a church next! For in the eyes of Him who created Kate and Giles, and in mine who suffered for them as I suffer for you, you are paltry, unfeeling, treasonable curs!

No, I will not hush, my daughter; the cup has overflowed. It takes a monumental effort to turn a mother's heart against the sons she has nursed upon her knees. Many times I have blinked, refusing to see too clearly, biting my tongue lest your father discover what I knew; he would have cast you to the door without hesitation. But now, the cup is full. And whence came this wealth? This past month you have rolled in gold. You earned not a single coin. May I never hear from another mouth how you acquired it. Since that night you lingered so late, returning with a swollen head, Cornelis, sloth and greed are ill-matched companions. Those who love money must sweat or steal. Well, if you plundered any poor soul, it was likely a woman, I swear it; for a man would drive you with his bare hand.

It matters not; it serves one purpose. It reveals how you intend to manage our affairs once they fall into your hands. I have watched you, my lads, for some time. You have squandered a groat today between you. I spend scarce a groat a week yet sustain you all, good and bad. I renounce waiting for the shoes that might walk behind your coffins. This shop and this house shall never be yours. Gerard is our heir—the poor Gerard you have banished and strived to destroy. Never address me as 'mother' again! Yet, you have rendered him tenfold dearer to me. My poor lost boy! Soon I shall see him again, hold him in my arms, set him upon my knees. Yes, you may stare! You are too cunning, yet not cunning enough. You severed the stalk, but you left the seed—the seed that shall outgrow you and outlive you. Margaret Brandt is swift, and this child is Gerard's, and what belongs to Gerard belongs to me. I have prayed the saints it may be a boy, and it shall be—it must be.

Kate, when I learned the truth, my bowels yearned over her unborn child as if it were my own. He is our heir. He will outlive us. You will not; for a corrupt heart within a carcass is like the worm in the nut, swiftly reducing the body to dust. So, Kate, retrieve Gerard's bib and tucker from the drawer you know well. One day we shall carry them to Sevenbergen. We shall borrow Peter Buyskens' cart and go to comfort Gerard's wife under her burden. She is his wife. Who is Ghysbrecht Van Swieten? Can he interpose himself between a couple and the altar, sundering those united by God and the priest? She is my daughter, and I am as proud of her as I am of you, Kate. As for you, stay out of my sight for a while, for you resemble the black dog in my eyes."

Cornelis and Sybrandt seized the implicit command and retreated, their movements sluggish, weighed down by a triad of emotions: remorse, impenitence, and a festering hate. They avoided her gaze as diligently as possible. For many days thereafter, she spoke not a word—not good, not bad, not indifferent—to either of them. Thus, she liberated her spirit.

To understand the architecture of Catherine's domestic universe, one must first grasp the precarious balance she maintained between duty and desire. She was a woman of impeccable housekeeping, yet her very virtue rendered her perpetually anxious; she feared that even a single day's absence would unravel the fragile tapestry of her home. Thus, her planned journey to Sevenbergen was deferred day by day, postponed until the domestic tide receded sufficiently to allow passage. Finally, fortune aligned: Peter Buyskens' mule was occupied elsewhere, clearing the path for departure. Yet, fate intervenes where human planning falters. One morning, Eli summoned the entire household to witness a confrontation.

"Eli asked her publicly, before the gathered kin, if it were true she harbored thoughts of visiting Margaret Brandt."

"Ay, my man," she admitted softly.

"Then I forbid it," Eli declared, his voice cutting through the room like a blade.

"Oh, do you?" she challenged, her color rising.

"I do."

"There is nothing further to discuss, then," she murmured, flushing deeper.

"Not a word," Eli replied, his sternness absolute.

Once the family dispersed, Catherine retreated to the privacy of her chamber, where she turned her severity inward, directing her wrath not at Eli, but at her own capitulation. She berated herself for behaving like a cat cornered by a robin—a creature driven to panic by mere proximity—or a hen that cannot lay an egg without announcing its intent to the whole flock. "Next time," she vowed to herself, "if we must pursue something amiss, let us act immediately, in the heat of the moment, rather than deliberating and speaking aloud. If questioned later, we might claim ignorance, feign innocence, and escape scrutiny. For two pence, I would have gone to Sevenbergen openly, amidst their teeth and judgment."

Such defiance, wild and picturesque, staggered Kate. "Nay, mother," she pleaded gently, "with patience, father will relent."

"And so will Michaelmas," Catherine retorted, her spirit hardening. "But when? I was so eager for you to see the girl. Only then could we have united our counsel regarding her. Let them speak what they will; no judge or beast can be truly known except by sight. Were I to bear fifty more sons, I would never thwart one's affection until I had laid eyes upon the object of their desire and verified the truth. Perhaps I should have considered this before condemning Gerard's fancy. But life is a relentless school, the lesson never finished. We discard one fault only to embrace another, stumbling blindly here and there until we stumble into our graves, and there ends our tale."

"Mother," Kate interjected timidly.

"Well, what brings this shadow to your face? No good tidings, surely. What terrorizes this poor wretch?"

"An avowal she intends to make," Kate faltered, her voice barely audible.

"Now, there is a noble sentiment for you," Catherine exclaimed, pride swelling in her chest. "Gerard taught you that phrase, I give you my oath. Come forth with your confession."

"Well then, truthfully, I have seen her."

"And?"

"And I spoke with her as well."

"And you withheld this from me? Now wonders are merely dust."

"Mother, you burned with such hatred against her. I waited until I could speak without provoking greater anger."

"Ay," Catherine sighed, a mixture of sadness and bitterness coloring her tone. "Like mother, like daughter; cowardice is our bane. I occasionally buffet the others, or how would the house endure? But did you, Kate, ever receive a harsh word or a cruel glance from your poor mother, that you..." She paused, seeing her daughter's distress. "Nay, I will not have you weep, girl. Ten to one you acted rightly. Rise, brave heart, and tell me everything. Better late than never. First and foremost, when and how did you travel to Sevenbergen with your crutches, and I remain unaware?"

"I never set foot there in my life," Kate confessed. "And, mammy dear, to say that I never wished to see her—that I refused—is false. But I never went, nor did I seek her out."

"There now," Catherine disputed, shaking her head. "Did I not say it was entirely unlike my girl to seek someone unknown to me? Come, I am all agog."

"Thus it happened. News reached my ears, by whatever means, and pray, good mother, on your knees, never ask me how. Gerard was a prisoner in the Stadthouse tower."

"Ah."

"Under the pretext of Father's command."

Catherine exhaled a sigh that bordered on a moan. "Blackest than I imagined," she whispered faintly.

"Giles and I ventured out at night to encourage him. And there, at the foot of the tower, stood a brave maiden, utterly strange to me, I swear, on the same mission."

"Lookee there now, Kate."

"At first, we frightened one another profoundly, haunted by his notorious reputation and our minds filled with demons. In the moonlight, we paled with fear. But in the next instant, I cried, 'You are Margaret.' And she replied, 'And you are Kate.' Think on it!"

"Did anyone ever? 'Twas Gerard! He must have spoken endlessly of you to her, and her to you."

In return for this revelation, Kate bestowed upon Catherine one of nature's finest gifts—the composite kiss. She pressed a single kiss to her mother's cheek, conveying four distinct meanings simultaneously: first, complete correctness; second, acknowledgment of her mother's sharp intuition; third, the sweetness of their minds aligning again after discord; fourth, and so on.

"Now then, speak freely, child. Gerard is not here. Alas, what am I saying? Would to Heaven he were."

"Well then, mother, she is comely, and her likeness bears her little resemblance to the truth."

"Eh, dear; listen to the youth! I value good deeds, not good looks. Does she love my boy as he deserves to be loved?"

"Sevenbergen lies farther from the Stadthouse than we do," Kate reflected. "Yet she arrived there before me."

Catherine nodded, understanding dawning.

"Nay, more," Kate continued, her voice gaining strength. "She had already secured his release before I arrived. Yes, she lowered him from the captive's tower."

Catherine shook her head in disbelief. "The highest tower for miles! It is not feasible."

"'Tis true nonetheless. She and an old man she accompanied devised means and wit to lower him via a rope. There he hung, suspended from his prison, and our Giles ascended it. When I first beheld the rope hanging there, I remarked, 'This is magic.' But when the frank lass's arms embraced me, and her bosom beat against mine, and her cheeks grew wet, then I realized, 'Tis not magic: 'tis love.' For she is not like me; she is robust and capable. And, dear heart, even I, poor frail creature, sometimes feel I could move the world for those I cherish: I love you, mother. And she loves Gerard."

"God bless her for it! God bless her!" Catherine cried out.

"But..."

"But what, lamb?"

"Is her love founded on certainty and honesty? 'Tis most strange; yet that very quality which warms your heart has somewhat cooled mine toward her. Poor soul. She is no wife, you know, mother, when all is said and done."

"Humph! They stood at the altar together."

"Ay, but they departed as they arrived: maid and bachelor."

"The parson claims so?"

"Nay, for that I know not."

"Then I will accept no man's word but his own in such a tangled skein." After a moment of reflection, she added, "Nevertheless, you are right, girl. I shall go to Sevenbergen alone. A wife I am, but not a slave. We are all in darkness here. She holds the key. I must question her, and no one else—not even you. I will not carry a lily to a house marked with a stain, no, not to a palace of gold and silver."

As Catherine pondered this resolve, she felt increasingly drawn to Margaret. Moreover, she was "all agog" with curiosity, a potent passion shared by all, and nearly omnipotent for those like Catherine who do not slake it with books. Finally, one bright day after dinner, she whispered to Kate, "Keep the house from falling apart, if you can," donned her finest kirtle and hood, paired with scarlet clocked hose and new shoes, and marched briskly toward Sevenbergen, troubling no man's mule.

Upon arrival, she inquired where Margaret Brandt resided. The first person she approached shook his head. "The name is strange to me." She walked slightly farther and addressed a girl of about fifteen standing at a doorway. "Father," the girl called out into the house, "here is another after that magician's daughter." The man emerged and informed Catherine that Peter Brandt's cottage lay just outside the town on the eastern side. "You may see the chimney from here," he indicated, pointing upward. "But you will not find them there, neither father nor daughter; they have left the town this week, bless you."

"Say not so, good man, and me walking all the way from Tergou."

"From Tergou? Then you must have met the

To reconstruct the fractured geography of their sorrow, Denys seized the morrow's quiet hour to unveil the remainder of his tale, carefully shielding the names of Cornelis and Sybrandt from the stain of accusation. "It is not for me to blacken them," he declared, his voice carrying the weight of a soldier's code, "for they descend from noble stock. Yet Gerard views them as enemies in this affair. Since I am Gerard's comrade, and since it is the iron law of war to withhold truth from the foe—even if that truth is merely a lie—I cannot speak ill of them."

Catherine exhaled a sigh that seemed to carry the dust of their shared grief, offering no rebuttal.

The adventures Denys recounted ignited a tumult of agitation and profound mourning within them. They wept bitterly at the memory of the friends' separation, a wound Denys himself could barely articulate without stumbling. Yet, eventually, the sharp edges of despair smoothed into a radiant, trembling hope: the expectation of Gerard's swift return. Denys shared this confidence wholeheartedly, yet he cautioned them that hope must not blind them to the final instructions of the departed. "If Gerard returns next week and finds no Margaret," he warned, his tone sharpening, "what figure shall he cut? How shall he explain the absence of the woman he loved?"

In this moment, Catherine felt a kinship toward the absent Margaret stronger than ever before. Her anxiety mirrored Denys's; she yearned to find the girl and offer kindness before Gerard's arrival. However, she could not align with his strategy of abandoning their neighborhood to scour the land. "She must have confided in someone regarding her destination," she reasoned gently. "These are not dishonest souls fleeing the country; they owe not a single stiver in Sevenbergen. Dearest heart, Denys, you cannot hunt the entirety of Holland for one girl."

"Can I not?" Denys retorted, his expression grim. "We shall see." After a pause for reflection, he proposed a division of labor: she would remain here, her senses alert and wide-open, while he would ransack every town across Holland if necessity demanded. "But she will not stray many leagues from here. There are three of them. Three do not fly as swiftly, nor travel as far, as one."

"That is sense," Catherine conceded, though she pressed him immediately to visit the Demoiselle Van Eyck. "She and our Margaret were bosom friends. She knows where the girl has gone, provided she chooses to speak." Eager to depart, Denys moved to leave, but Catherine, with a deft turn of her hand, adjusted her dress to appear slightly neater and compelled him to accompany her.

They were ushered into a richly furnished chamber where the elderly lady sat in gracious reception. Upon hearing their purpose, the tapestry slipped from Margaret Van Eyck's hands. "Gone? Departed from Sevenbergen without telling me? The ungrateful girl."

This reaction startled the visitors. "What? You know nothing? When was she last here?"

"Perhaps ten days ago," the old lady replied, her voice tinged with irritation. "I had taken out my brushes, after so many years, intending to paint her portrait. I did not finish it, though; for certain reasons."

Catherine lamented the strangeness of such a departure—"bag and baggage"—without permission or explanation. "Was there ever anything so untoward? Just when our hearts were warmest toward her, and here comes Gerard's companion from the ends of the earth bearing comfort, only to find her missing, while Gerard himself awaited her return. What am I to do? Surely she did not vanish thus without cause. Can you not provide us with a clue, my good demoiselle? Pray, tell us."

"I possess no clue to give," the elder lady snapped, her patience fraying.

"Then I can," announced Reicht Heynes, emerging from the doorway with cheeks flushed with indignation.

"So you have been listening all along, have you?"

"What are my ears for, mistress?"

"True enough. Very well, then, shed the light of your wisdom upon this dark matter."

"There is no darkness here that I perceive," Reicht declared. "And the clue, if you call it a double-stranded cord, has both ends lying right here in this room. Oh, mistress, I wonder at you sitting there feigning ignorance."

"Marry, come up," the mistress blushed, her complexion rivaling the servant's. "So it was I who drove the foolish girl away."

"You played your part, mistress. What manner of greeting did you afford her on her last visit? Could she fail to notice it, feeling so friendless? And you said, 'I have changed my mind about painting you,' turning your nose up at her."

"I did not turn up my nose. It is not shaped like yours for gazing heavenward."

"Oh, all noses can follow the heart's inclination, for that matter. Poor soul. She did come to my kitchen. 'I am not to be painted now,' she said, tears filling her eyes. She spoke no further. But I understood perfectly what she meant. I had seen you."

"Well," Margaret Van Eyck admitted, her defiance softening into confession, "I do confess as much, and I make you the judge, madam. Know that these young girls can achieve nothing of their own volition; they are most adept at mimicking their sweethearts. Your Gerard is reasonably skilled in many arts, including the illuminator's craft. Margaret was his pupil, a patient one. What marvel? Having a woman's eye for color and a lover to emulate. 'Tis a trick I despise in my heart: for thereby the great art of color, which should be royal, aspiring, and free, becomes a poor slave to the petty crafts of writing and printing, fettered and imprisoned, reduced to match the littleness of books, destined to go to church in a rich fool's pocket. Nevertheless, affection rules us all. When the poor wench brought me her thorn leaves, lilies, ivy, dewberries, ladybirds, and butterfly grubs—all the scum of Nature stuck fast in gold-leaf like wasps in a honey-pot—and alongside her diurnal book, showing she had pored over each singular page for a hundred, or one hundred and fifty, or two hundred hours, certainly I was wroth. That an immortal soul, countless hours of labor, and much manual skill should be flung away on Nature's trash—leaves, insects, grubs—and on barren letters. Yet, having bowels, I was forced to restrain my anger, damming my better feelings, and looked kindly at the work to suggest improvements. I said, 'Since Heaven, for our sins, has doomed us to spend time, soul, and color on great letters and little beetles, omitting such small fry as saints and heroes, their acts and passions, why not present the scum naturally?' I told her, 'The grapes I saw walking abroad hung in the air, not stuck in a wall; and even these insects, and Nature's slime in general, do not pass their noxious lives wedged miserably in metal prisons like flies in honey-pots and glue-pots, but crawl or hover at large, infesting the air.' 'Ah, my dear friend,' she replied, 'I see now whither you drive; but this ground is gold; whereon we may not shade.' 'Who said so?' I asked. 'All teachers of this craft,' she answered; and (to make an end of me at once, I trow) 'Gerard himself!' 'That for Gerard himself,' I retorted, 'and all the gang; give me a brush!'

"Then I chose to shade her fruit and reptiles with a color false in nature, but true relative to that monstrous ground of glaring gold. In five minutes, out came a bunch of raspberries, stalk and all, that almost flew into your mouth; likewise a butterfly grub she had so truly rendered it might turn the stoutest stomach. My lady threw her arms around my neck and exclaimed, 'Oh!'"

"Did she now?"

"The little love!" observed Denys, managing to wedge in a word at last.

Margaret Van Eyck stared at him, then smiled. She continued to recount how, step by step, she had been led to promise to resume the art she had abandoned with a sigh upon her brothers' deaths—to paint the Madonna once more, with Margaret as the model. Incidentally, she even revealed the alchemy by which girls are turned into saints. "'Thy hair is adorable,'" I said. "'Why, 'tis red,' she replied. 'Ay,' I said, 'but what a red! How brown! How glossy! Most hair is not worth a straw to us painters; thine is the artist's very hue. But thy violet eyes, which smack of earth, being now languid for lack of one Gerard, now full of fire in hopes of the same Gerard, these will I lift to heaven in fixed and holy meditation. And thy nose, which does already somewhat aspire that way (though not so piously as Reicht's), will I debase a trifle, and somewhat enfeeble thy chin.'"

"Enfeeble her chin? Alack! What may that mean? You go beyond me, mistress."

"'Tis a resolute chin. Not a jot too resolute for this wicked world; but when you come to a Madonna? No thank you."

"Well, I never. A resolute chin."

Denys. "The darling!"

Catherine stood paralyzed by a dread that tightened her throat, realizing with crushing clarity that Gerard must arrive home within a week, certainly no later than a month. In that interim, how was she to confess the terrible truth—that she had failed to watch over his betrothed? The uncertainty regarding the girl's fate hung over them like a storm cloud, occasionally coalescing into a sickening, tangible horror. "Oh, Kate," she groaned, her voice trembling, "what if she has already thrown herself into the water?" Her mother's response was immediate, cutting through the silence with the blunt wisdom of experience. "Margaret would never be so wicked," Catherine protested, clinging to the hope of her friend's virtue. "Ah, my lass, you know not the chaotic nature of young women unmoored from maternal guidance," her mother replied, her tone shifting from comfort to grim warning. "They are capable of throwing themselves into the depths for a man whom the next stranger might cure in a single week. I have seen them leap into the canal with the resolve of iron, only to scream for mercy moments later, unable to command their own minds even over such trifles. Worse still, there are times when their bodies ail in ways unknown to any other creature, stringing their emotions so tightly that the patience inherent in their souls vanishes entirely, sending them plunging into the water without a second thought. Thus, I declare that men are monsters." "Mother!" Catherine cried out, recoiling at the accusation. "Monsters, and no less," her mother insisted, her eyes hardening. "They construct these very canals merely to tempt poor women. They know we possess no desire to cut our throats, nor do we rate our skin or our lives so highly that we would willingly spill blood. As for hanging, while she waits for the nail to be fixed and the noose to be fashioned, she retains the luxury of changing her mind. But a jump into a canal is akin to jumping into bed; the water does all the work, whether you wish it or not." The mother paused, her gaze drifting to the window as if recalling a distant memory. "Consider me, the mother of nine. Was I not once eager to make a hole in our canal right here and now?" "Nay, Mother, I shall never believe you capable of such folly." "You may believe it, though," her mother countered, a wry smile touching her lips. "It happened in the first year of our household. Eli had not yet discovered my weak stitches, nor I his; we pulled in contrary directions until we made a rent in our peace. I ran crying to a gossiping fool like myself, sharing secrets meant only for the saints, and lo, one of our precious canals lay directly in my path. Did they take us for teal? How tempting it looked! I told myself, 'Since he has allowed me to leave his door in a quarrel, he shall see me drowned next, and then he will change his key. He will weep a proper flood, and I shall look down from heaven—and yes, I forgot I should be elsewhere—watch him mourn, and oh, that would be sweet indeed.' My feet were already dancing on the edge, ready to plunge, until suddenly I stopped." She shook her head, the image fading. "I had a new gown in progress, nearly finished, and I chose to go home instead. What was Eli's first word upon seeing me? 'Let that flea stick in the wall, my lass,' he said. 'Not a word of what angered you was true, save this: I love thee.' Those were his exact words, spoken during our first quarrel. I remembered them, and I flung my arms around his neck, sobbing briefly, thinking of the canal. He was no colder to me than I to him, being a man and a youth; the warmth returned between us. That embrace was infinitely sweeter than lying in the freezing water, saving my wedding kirtle and the fine new shoes crafted by old John Bush, his uncle who still runs the shop today. And what was my grief compared to hers?" Little Kate pressed forward, hoping that Margaret's love for her aging father would anchor her. "He is father, mother, and everything to her, you know." "Nay, Kate," her mother dismissed gently, "they forget all such bonds in a moment of despair when the sky itself turns black above them. I place greater faith in the unborn than in him who is ripe for the grave to keep her from mischief. For truly, it goes sore against our nature to die when a little innocent pulls at our hearts, begging to live, feeding upon our very veins." "Very well, then, keep your heart high, Mother," Catherine urged, adding internally that these fears were likely exaggerated. She concluded by solemnly entreating her mother to cease naming the sex of Margaret's infant. It was considered unluckily superstitious, a notion reinforced by every gossip in town. "Dear heart, as if there were not as many girls born as boys," she reasoned. Yet this reflection, though logically sound, met with a clamorous rejection. "Do you possess the cruelty to threaten me with a girl?!" her mother exclaimed, her composure fracturing. "I want no more girls while I have you. What use would a lass be to me? Can I sit her on my knee and see my Gerard again as I can with a boy? I tell thee, it is all settled." "How can that be?" Catherine asked, bewildered. "In my mind," her mother declared firmly. "And if I am to be disappointed in the end, it is not for you to disappoint me beforehand by telling me it cannot be a child, but only a girl."

Margaret Brandt had always occupied a solitary orbit within Sevenbergen, her reserve mistaken by the town for arrogance. This misinterpretation had bred a fertile soil for jealousy and malice, and she knew, with a chilling clarity, how these poisoned hearts would eventually triumph over her when the veil of her condition could no longer be maintained. The prospect gnawed at her, a relentless hunger that consumed her nights and stole the light from her days. Driven by this terror, she retreated into the shadows of her home, avoiding the harsh glare of daylight even during those rare excursions necessary for survival.

Yet, in the quiet sanctuary of her own conscience, Margaret's moral compass remained undeviating, unlike the confused judgments of the unlearned observers who might mistake her plight for something lesser. While she lacked the intricate knowledge of contemporary legal statutes, she understood the fundamental sanctity of a betrothal: it was a binding contract, immutable as any document sealed with witness and ink. She knew that marriage to another would have been annulled by both Church and State, and that children born of such unions were legitimate. However, the weight crushing her simple medieval spirit lay elsewhere. The contract itself—the physical proof of her union—did not exist in her possession. Instead, it rested in the hands of Gerard, who was currently lost to her, far beyond reach.

She loathed herself for the catastrophic oversight that had surrendered her to the mercies of public rumor. Though she had never encountered Horace's famous verse regarding the sting of praise, she possessed a distinctly Horatian capacity for clear, hard, positive judgment—a trait strangely characteristic of her sex when emotion does not cloud perception entirely. She assessed the world with ruthless precision. With her marriage lines hidden in Italy, invisible to the eye, they could never counterbalance the undeniable evidence of her visible pregnancy and the future sight of her child. What narrative could withstand slanderous tongues? "I possess my marriage lines, but I cannot display them to you." Which woman would believe such a fabrication? Or even feign belief? As one of the most modest souls in Holland, Margaret sought the approval of women, not men. Slander often attacks the female sex with impunity, but here, slander wore the mask of truth. The concept of the "strong-minded woman" had not yet been conceived; Margaret, though naturally authoritative as the mistress of a household, was fragile as water in moments of crisis, particularly in this instance. Like the elite of her gender, she was a mere leaf, trembling at every gust of worldly opinion, whether true or false.

Immense suffering resides in few words. Few pages of description penned by a man could convey the agony of a virtuous woman foreseeing herself paraded as frail, unless that woman were already virtuous enough to require no such aid. Had her heart been frail, she might have brazened out the accusation. But she possessed no such shield. She was pure as snow, watching the darkness approach with terrifying inevitability. The poor girl sat listless for hours, moaning with an internal anguish that echoed in the hollow rooms. Often, when her father spoke and she offered mechanical replies, her cheek would ignite like fire, causing the old man to wonder what words had disturbed her. Nothing. His speech was less than air to her; it was the ever-present dread that painted her face with the color of shame, regardless of the topic at hand.

Shame and fear reached their zenith the night she returned from Margaret Van Eyck's house. Her condition had been discovered, and by women of her own sex. The old artist, secluded as she was, might remain silent, but Catherine, a gossip at the center of a bustling family and a tight-knit neighborhood? A single spark of hope flickered. Catherine had spoken kindly, even lovingly. The situation allowed for no middle path; Gerard's mother, once roused, would become either her greatest ally or her deadliest foe. Margaret waited in racking anxiety for news, but none arrived. Hope evaporated. Catherine was not her friend. Consequently, the urge to expose herself grew, fueled by the absence of kindness to balance the natural impulse to babble. Soon, the desire to flee this neighborhood took root, gnawing until it became a wild, passionate obsession.

But how to persuade her father? Old people cling to their roots. He was ancient and infirm, unable to abandon his abode. The only recourse was to make him her confidant; fleeing without him would be impossible. Thus, she oscillated between an uncontrollable urge to vanish and an invincible aversion to speak to a man, even her father, vibrating in a suspense filled with living torture. Then, one day, the fatal thought emerged: "End all!" Words foolishly chosen are not always foolish deeds. One of Catherine's constant fears, the city's numerous canals, tempted this wavering girl sorely. Standing on the bank one afternoon, she gazed into the calm, deep water. It appeared an image of repose, mirroring her exhaustion. No more trouble. No more fear of shame. If Gerard had not loved her, she doubted she would have stopped there. As it was, she knelt by the water's edge and prayed fervently to God to purge such wicked thoughts from her soul.

"Oh! Selfish wretch," she whispered, tears streaming down her face, "to leave thy father. Oh, wicked wretch, to kill thy child, and cause thy poor Gerard to lose all the pain and peril he undertook for thy sake. I will tell my father everything, yes, before this sun sets."

With eager haste, lest her resolve dissolve before arrival, she hastened home. In domestic matters, the learned Peter was simple as a child, while Margaret, since the age of sixteen, had governed the house gently but absolutely. It was therefore a strange inversion to see the young, despotic mistress addressing him with faltering, irresolute tones—tones usually reserved for Martin Wittenhaagen or the little maid who washed vessels for a pittance.

"Father, I would speak to thee."
"Speak on, girl."
"Wilt you listen to me? And—and—not—and try to excuse my faults?"
"We have all our faults, Margaret, thou no more than the rest of us; but fewer, unless parental feeling blinds me."
"Alas, no, father: I am a poor foolish girl, that would fain do well, but have done ill, most ill, most unwisely; and now must bear the shame. But, father, I love you, with all my faults, and will not you forgive my folly, and still love your motherless girl?"
"That ye may count on," said Peter cheerfully.
"Oh, well, smile not. For then how can I speak and make you sad?"
"Why, what is the matter?"
"Father, disgrace is coming on this house: it is at the door. And I the culprit. Oh, father, turn your head away. I—I—father, I have let Gerard take away my marriage lines."
"Is that all? 'Twas an oversight."
"'Twas the deed of a mad woman. But woe is me! That is not the worst."

Peter interrupted her. "The youth is honest, and loves you dear. You are young. What is a year or two to you? Gerard will assuredly come back and keep troth."
"And meantime know you what is coming?"
"Not I, except that I shall be gone first for one."
"Worse than that. There is worse pain than death. Nay, for pity's sake turn away your head, father."
"Foolish wench!" muttered Peter, but he turned his head.

She trembled violently, her cheeks aflame, beginning to falter. "I did look on Gerard as my husband—we being betrothed—and he was in so sore danger, and I thought I had killed him, and I—oh, if you were but my mother I might find courage: you would question me. But you say not a word."
"Why, Margaret, what is all this coil about? And why are thy cheeks crimson, speaking to no stranger, but to thy old father?"
"Why are my cheeks on fire? Because—because—father, kill me; send me to heaven! Bid Martin shoot me with his arrow! And then the gossips will come and tell you why I blush so this day. And then, when I am dead, I hope you will love your girl again for her mother's sake."

"Give me thy hand, mistress," said Peter, a little sternly.
She extended her hand, trembling. He took it gently, his face etched with anxiety, and began to feel her pulse.
"Alas, nay," she said. "'Tis my soul that burns, not my body, with fever. I cannot, will not, bide in Sevenbergen."
Wringing her hands impatiently, she added, "Be calm now," said the old man soothingly, "nor torment thyself for nought. Not bide in Sevenbergen? What need to bide a day, as it vexes thee, and puts thee in a fever: for fevered thou art, deny it not."
"What!" cried Margaret, "Would you yield to go hence, and—and ask no reason but my longing to be gone?" Suddenly throwing herself on her knees beside him, in a fervor of supplication she clutched his sleeve, then his arm, then his shoulder, imploring him to quit this place without asking why.
"Alas! What needs it? You will soon see it. And I could never say it. I would liever die."
"Foolish child, who seeks thy girlish secrets? Is it I,

Among the strangers of this foreign land, Margaret Brandt discovered a comparative happiness, a fragile sanctuary amidst the chaos of displacement. Yet, fortune, capricious as ever, soon spun a thread of unexpected relief. A civic dignitary, plagued by a whimsical and erratic illness that led him from physician to physician until he teetered on the brink of death, summoned Peter. The old healer found the man reduced to a husk, drained by bloodletting and purging. With a flourish of desperation, Peter emptied a battalion of bottles from the window, leaving the air thick with their scent; he whipped egg yolks into a frothy paste using neat Schiedam, administering it in cautious doses, followed by meat stewed in red wine and water, shredded with mild febrifuge herbs that caused no further harm. Slowly, the patient regained his footing, emerging as a grotesque hybrid of man and pillowcase. Being a voluble man, the dignitary spread Peter's reputation across every cobblestone street, granting the artist—who had long deserved renown in vain—a rapid ascent built entirely on luck.

Things appeared bright. The old man's pride was finally cheered, and his purse began to swell. Yet, he squandered much of this newfound wealth on sovereign herbs and choice drugs, intending to invest it all in the arcane arts. It was Margaret who intercepted this flow, white-mailing a portion of the gain. But the victory came too late; the happy excitement itself proved fatal.

One evening, as he bid her goodnight, his voice stumbled, inarticulate and fractured. By the next morning, he lay speechless, barely sensible.

Margaret, who had served as her father's attentive pupil for years, saw immediately that he had suffered a paralytic stroke. Trusting neither her own judgment nor the silence of the room, she ran to summon a doctor. One of those who had previously been obstructed by Peter's methods—and thus spared the fate of killing the dignitary—arrived cheerfully to confirm her fears. He advocated for immediate bleeding. She refused. "He was always against blooding," she insisted, "especially the old."

Peter survived, but he was never the same man again. His memory became a treacherous landscape, unreliable and foggy; naturally, he could no longer be trusted to prescribe. Patients, drawn by rumors of a new doctor, waited in vain for his convalescence. Misery stared her in the face, sharp and undeniable. Resolved to seek counsel and comfort, she turned to her cousin, William Johnson, from whom she had hitherto kept aloof due to pride and poverty.

She found him and his servant seated in the same room, neither appearing improved by liquor. Mastering every sign of surprise, she offered her greetings and announced her intent to discuss a family matter, glancing quietly at the servant to signal her presence. The woman took the cue, but not as expected.

"Oh, you can speak before me, can she not, my old man?"

At this familiarity, Margaret flushed crimson. "I cry you mercy, mistress. I knew not my cousin had fallen into the custom of this town. Well, I must take a fitter opportunity," she said, rising to depart.

"I wot not what ye mean by custom o' the town," the woman snapped, bouncing up. "But this I know: 'tis the part of a faithful servant to keep her master from being preyed on by his beggarly kin."

Margaret retorted sharply. "Ye are too modest, mistress. Ye are no servant. Your speech betrays you. 'Tis not till the ape hath mounted the tree that she shows her tail so plain. Nay, there sits the servant; God help him! And while so it is, fear not thou his kin will ever be so poor in spirit as to come where the likes of you can flout their dole." Casting a look of mute reproach at her cousin for remaining passive and silent throughout the exchange, she turned and walked out haughtily, refusing to shed a single tear until she reached home and allowed herself to think. Now, she faced the grim reality: two men to lodge and feed, plus another mouth arriving into the world.

Yet, this last arrival, though the most helpless of all, would become their best friend.

Nature was strong in Margaret Brandt—the same fierce instinct that drives beasts, birds, and insects to cunningly provide food and shelter for progeny yet unborn. Stimulated by this primal force, she sat and brooded, thinking intensely, calculating how to outrun destitution. Even with five gold pieces remaining, she saw starvation approaching with inevitable footsteps.

Her sex, when deviating from custom to think with male intensity, reasoned just as effectively as any man. She rose, ordered Martin to move Peter to another room, and transformed her own space into a sanctuary of order. She polished the glass globe, suspending it from the ceiling, dusted the crocodile, and nailed him firmly to the outside wall. After duly instructing Martin, she set him to play the role of a lounging sentinel at the street door, tasked with telling the crocodile-bitten visitors that a great, aged, and learned alchemist resided within, who in his moments of recreation amused himself by curing mortal diseases.

Patients soon arrived, demanding to see the leech. "That might not be," Margaret replied smoothly. "He is deep in his studies, searching for the grand elixir, and not even princes could command his speech. They must tell me their symptoms, and return in two hours." And oh, the mysterious powers of performance! When they returned, the drug or draught was invariably ready. For a worshipful patient, she would carefully scan his face, feel his pulse and skin, and listen to his tale, then walk softly to Peter's room. There, she would ponder how her father, whose system she had long observed, would treat the case. She would scribble an illegible scrawl filled with cabalistic letters, bring it down reverently, and show it to the patient. "Could he read that?" she would ask. The response was usually, "I am no reader," or, with admiration, "Nay, mistress, nought can I make on't."

"Ay, but I can. 'Tis sovereign. Look on thyself as cured!" If she had the materials on hand—and she was too good an economist not to favor those medicines already in her stock—she would sometimes allow the patient to watch her compound them, anxiously consulting the sacred prescription lest Great Science suffer in her hands. Thus, she sent them away relieved of cash but with pockets full of medicine and minds full of faith, humbugged to their hearts' content. *Populus vult decipi.* When they departed, she would retrieve two little boxes Gerard had made her; on one she wrote *Today*, on the other *Tomorrow*. Into *Today* she placed the smaller coins, and into *Tomorrow* the larger ones, alongside the few gold pieces that had survived the journey from Sevenbergen and the expenses of housekeeping in a strange place. In this way, she met current expenses and laid by for the rainy day she foresaw, mixing drugs with simples and vice with virtue. On this last score, her conscience pricked her sorely. After each day's comedy, she knelt and prayed God to forgive her "for the sake of her child." But lo and behold, cures were reported; thus, her conscience began to harden.

Martin Wittenhaagen had recently become a dead weight on her hands. Like most men who had endured great hardships, he had stiffened suddenly. Though less supple, he was as strong as ever, capable at his own pace of carrying the doctor herself round Rotterdam. Instead, he carried her slops.

In this new business, he displayed the qualities of a soldier: unreasoning obedience, punctuality, accuracy, dispatch, and drunkenness.

He fell among "good fellows"; the blackguards plied him with Schiedam, and he babbled and bragged. Doctor Margaret had risen very high in his estimation. All this brandishing of a crocodile for a standard, setting a dotard in ambush, disposing of slops, and taking good money in exchange struck him not as science but as something far superior: Strategy. He boasted in his cups and before mixed companies how "me and my General we are a biting of the burghers."

When this revelation had time to leaven the city, his General, Doctor Margaret, received a summons from the constables. They took her, trembling and begging subordinate machines to forgive her, before the burgomaster. Standing by his side were real physicians, a terrible row in long robes and square caps, accusing her of practicing unlawfully on the bodies of the Duke's lieges. At first, she was too frightened to speak. A novice like her, the very name of "Law" paralyzed her. But questioned closely, yet not so harshly as if she had been ugly, she told the truth. She had long been her father's pupil and had merely followed his system, curing many. "And it is not for myself in very deed, sirs," she pleaded, "but I have two poor helpless honest men at home upon my hands, and how else can I keep them? Ah, good sirs, let a poor girl make her bread honestly; ye hinder them not to make it idly and shamefully; and oh, sirs, ye are husbands, ye are fathers; ye cannot but see I have reason to work and provide as best I may." Ere this woman's appeal had left her lips, she would have given the world to recall it, standing with one hand upon her heart and one before

To truly measure the weight of this day, one must first understand the architecture of Margaret's solitude. It was a paradox of sweetness and bitterness, a day that simultaneously gifted her a soulmate and delivered the crushing verdict of abandonment. For the first time, the truth settled upon her like a physical blow: Gerard was alone in that strange, hostile land. The thought shattered any lingering hope she might have harbored; he would not be returning. Denys, however, served as a balm to this fractured spirit. He claimed her as his *she-comrade*, flooding her days with formulas of cheer and hilarities that momentarily suspended the gravity of her grief. She doted on him, treating him with a fierce protectiveness she extended equally to Martin, refusing to let either consume a single meal outside her sanctuary. With a feebly authoritative voice, she policed their tongues, forbidding the intrusion of vulgarity. "It spoils you, Denys," she chided, her tone shifting from affectionate to stern. "Good lack, to hear such ugly words emerge from so comely a head! Forbear, or I shall be angry; so be civil." Thus, Denys adopted a posture of exaggerated propriety, creating a ludicrous tableau where his innate politeness warred with the acquired ruffianism of his upbringing. Yet, as the proverb suggests, when it does not rain, it pours. Other forces soon sought to claim her friendship.

Margaret had written to Margaret Van Eyck, a letter steeped in humility and regret. She confessed that she knew she was no longer the favored daughter-in-law she once was, promising to keep her distance, yet unable to erase the memory of her benefactress's past kindness. She recounted the myriad battles she had fought merely to survive, concluding with a desperate plea: that her current residence remain hidden, especially from Gerard's people. "I do hate them," she wrote, her pen trembling with the residue of trauma. "They drove him from me. And even when he was gone, their hearts turned not to me as they would have, had they repented their cruelty to him." The Van Eyck household was thrown into perplexity. Finally, they made Reicht a confidante, allowing the secret to travel through him like fluid through a cylinder until it reached Catherine. "Ay, and is she turned that bitter against us?" the good woman asked, her voice thick with sorrow. "She stole our son from us, and now she hates us for not rushing into her arms. Nevertheless, it is a blessing she is alive and no farther away than Rotterdam." Meanwhile, the English princess, now the Countess Charolois, made a stately progress through the northern duchy, accompanied by her stepdaughter, the young heiress of Burgundy, Marie de Bourgogne. The old Duke, the most magnificent prince in Europe, unleashed his full splendor. Troops of dazzling knights and bevies of fair ladies, gorgeously attired, trailed behind the princesses, while minstrels, jongleurs, bards, musicians, actors, and tumblers filled the streets. Fencing matches, dances, and unbridled joy erupted in every town they touched. Richart invited all his people to gather in Rotterdam to witness the pageant.

Days passed in Rotterdam before fate intervened. Denys encountered Catherine accidentally in the street. After a warm exchange of greetings, he urged her to rejoice, boasting that he had finally located the *she-comrade* and crowed with triumph. But Catherine quickly cooled his ardor, reminding him that he would have found her far sooner by remaining quietly at Tergou rather than vagabondizing across Holland. "And being found, what the better are we?" she asked, her voice hardening. "Her heart is set dead against us now." "Oh, let that flea stick," Denys retorted impulsively. "Come with me to her house." No, she refused to enter a place where she expected an ill welcome. "Those who come unbidden sit unseated." Instead, she proposed that Denys act as mediator, bringing the estranged parties to a reconciliation. He accepted the office immediately, proceeding with great pomp and confident swagger. He trotted to Margaret's door and announced, "*She-comrade*, I met today a friend of yours." "You looked into Rotterdam, then, and saw yourself?" Margaret replied, her tone sharp. "Nay, 'twas a female, and one that seeks your regard; 'twas Catherine, Gerard's mother." "Oh, was it?" Margaret said, her expression closing off. "Then you may tell her she comes too late. There was a time I longed and longed for her; but she held aloof in my hour of most need. So now, we will be as we have been." Denys attempted to shake this resolution, coaxing her gently, but she remained bitter and sullen, impervious to persuasion. Then he scolded her sharply. At that, she dissolved into hysterics. Terrified by the result of his own eloquence and caught off guard, he allowed himself to be entrapped into a solemn promise never to revisit the subject. He returned to Catherine crestfallen and relayed the rejection. She flared with anger, informing the family how her overtures had been received. In response, the family flared back; the rift widened into a feud that burned fiercer each passing day. Only little Kate dared make excuses for Margaret.

The following day, another visitor arrived. Margaret found her household enslaved and degraded: Martin buried to his elbows in soapsuds, Denys ironing with clumsy ineptitude, and herself plaiting ruffs, though her eyes retained the sharp vigilance of a mistress surveying her raw levies. Into this scene stepped an old man, venerable at first glance but, upon closer inspection, keen and wizened. "Ah," cried Margaret, her reaction instantaneous. Swiftly, she turned her back on him, hiding her face with invincible repugnance. "Oh, that man! That man!" "Nay, fear me not," said Ghysbrecht calmly. "I come on a friend's errand. I bring you a letter from foreign parts." "Mock me not, old man," she retorted, turning slowly around. "Nay, see;" and he held out an enormous envelope. Margaret darted forward, snatching it with trembling hands and glistening eyes. It bore Gerard's handwriting. "Oh, thank you, sir, bless you for this," she whispered, forgiveness washing over her features. "I forgive you all the ill you ever wrought me." Pressing the letter to her bosom with one hand, she glided swiftly from the room, leaving Ghysbrecht behind. He departed without delay, casting a final scowl at Martha. Margaret was left alone with her letter for hours, the weight of the world suspended in those few folded pages.

When she descended once more, the transformation was absolute. The storm that had previously convulsed her spirit had settled into a profound, crystalline calm. Her eyes, though glistening with unshed tears, held no longer the feverish glare of bitterness; they were pools of serene resolve.

"Denys," she murmured, her voice soft as falling snow, "I possess the orders. I am to read my lover's letter aloud to his kin."

"And you shall never do that?" Denys challenged, his brow furrowed.

"Aye, I shall."

"I perceive," he observed, searching her face for a crack in her armor, "that some hidden sentiment in the missive has softened your heart toward them."

"Not a jot, Denys. Not a single particle. Yet, though I loathe them as one loathes poison, I shall not disobey my love. Listen to me, Denys: it is a sweetness beyond measure to obey, and the supreme sweetness lies in obeying one who stands afar, whose command carries no weight of force, but rests entirely upon the altar of my devotion. Ah, Gerard, my darling, were you here beside me, I might have sneered at your commands, dismissed your people as I have every reason to despise them. But now, if you had bidden me wade into the raging sea to read your sweet letter to the very jaws of the sharks, I would have done so without hesitation. Therefore, Denys, tell his mother I carry the letter. If she and hers are willing to listen, I am their humble servant; let them set their hour, and I shall seat them with the utmost courtesy, welcoming them as best I may."

Denys departed to deliver this tidings to Catherine. He found the household gathered around the table, the air thick with anticipation. Upon announcing the arrival of a long letter from Gerard, a wave of joy rippled through the room. Seizing the moment, Denys added, "Moreover, her heart has been softened; she intends to read it to you personally. You are to choose your own time."

"What does she suppose that none but she can decipher?" Catherine asked, her skepticism cutting through the festive atmosphere.

"Let her dispatch the letter, and we shall read it ourselves," someone suggested.

"Nay, but, mother," interjected little Kate, her voice trembling slightly, "perhaps she cannot bear to part with it from her grasp; she loves him so dearly."

"Does she think we intend to steal it?" Cornelis scoffed, his cynicism sharp. "She merely seeks to wedge herself into our fold by means of this correspondence."

Denys cast a look of utter disdain upon the speaker. "There speaks a base heart," he declared. "La camarade hates you all like venom. Oh, mistake me not, dame; I defend her not, for so it is. Yet, despite her spleen at a mere word from Gerard, she proffers to read you his letter with her own pretty mouth, possessing a voice like honey—it is indeed a fair offer."

"'Tis so, mine honest soldier," the patriarch, Eli, replied gravely. "It merits a civil response. Hold your tongues, ye women, and I shall answer her. Tell her that I, her father-in-law, setting aside all ancient grudges, do hereby thank her for this grace. Should she desire double thanks, let her send my son's letter by your faithful hand. I shall read it to his flesh and blood, and thereafter, I shall return to her with such surety and fidelity as I am Eli a Dierich, a William a Luke, free burgher of Tergou, like my forbears, and like them, a man of my word."

"Ay, and a man who is better than his word," Catherine cried out, her eyes shining with a mixture of pride and sorrow. "The only one I have ever encountered."

"Hold thy peace, wife," Eli commanded gently.

"Are you a man of sense, Eli, a dirk, a chose?" Denys shouted, unable to contain his frustration. "That she-comrade will be right glad to obey Gerard while avoiding facing you all, whom she detests as wormwood, save for your presence. Bless ye, the world has changed; she is wholly submissive today: 'Obedience is honey,' she quoth. In truth, it is a sweetmeat she cannot resist, consuming so little of it because of her fair face and her mellow tongue. Furthermore, with her capacity to fly into fits that terrify us soldiers to death when thwarted, and to chide us one moment only to pet us like lambs the next, she has forged two of the most crawling slaves ever seen out of two honest swashbucklers. I am the ironing ruffian, and the other washes."

"What comes next?" Eli asked, weary of the domestic turbulence.

"Why, whenever the brat enters the world, I shall rock the cradle, and the other knave shall wash the tucker and bib. So, I shall fetch the letter on the instant. Will you allow me to remain and hear it read?"

"Else our hearts were black as coal," Catherine answered, her tone leaving no room for refusal.

Thus, Denys hastened to retrieve the letter. He returned moments later, crestfallen.

"She will not yield it to me, nor to you, nor to any living soul," he reported.

"I knew she would not," Cornelis muttered.

"Whisht! Whisht!" Eli commanded, silencing the room. "Let Denys recount his tale."

"'Nay,'" Denys insisted, "'be ruled by me.'"

"Not I," she had replied.

"Well, but," Denys continued, recalling her words, "'that same honey Obedience ye spake of.'"

"You are a fool," she had retorted. "Obedience to Gerard is sweet, but obedience to any other body—whoever said that was sweet?"

"At last, she seemed to soften a fraction," Denys recounted, holding up the document. "She gave me a written paper for you, mademoiselle. Here 'tis."

"For me?" little Kate exclaimed, flushing crimson.

"Give that here!" Eli ordered, scanning the handwriting with intense scrutiny. He spoke almost in a whisper, "These be words from the letter. Hearken!"

"And, sweetheart, an if these lines should travel safe to thee, make thou trial of my people's hearts withal. Maybe they are somewhat turned towards me, being far away. If 'tis so they will show it to thee, since now to me they may not. Read, then, this letter! But I do strictly forbid thee to let it from thy hand; and if they still hold aloof from thee, why, then say nought, but let them think me dead. Obey me in this; for, if thou dost disrespect my judgment and my will in this, thou lovest me not."

A heavy silence descended upon the room as Gerard's words, copied faithfully by Margaret, were passed around and inspected.

"Well," Catherine said finally, breaking the quiet, "that is another matter. But methinks 'tis for her to come to us, not we to her."

"Alas, mother! What odds does that make?" little Kate pleaded.

"Much," Eli replied sternly. "Tell her we are too numerous to journey to her, and bid her hither, the sooner the better."

When Denys had withdrawn, Eli confessed privately to his wife that it was a bitter pill to swallow. "When that lass crosses my threshold, all the mischief and misery she hath wrought here will seem to tumble in at the doors in one heap. But what could I do, wife? We must hear the news of Gerard. I saw that in thine eyes, and felt it in my own heart. She is backed by our undutiful but still beloved son, and thus she stands stronger than we, bringing our noses down to the grindstone, the sly, cruel jade. But never heed. We shall hear the letter; and then let her depart unblessed, as she came unwelcome."

"Make your mind easy," Catherine soothed him. "She will not come at all." A tone of deep regret lingered in her voice.

Shortly thereafter, Richart arrived from Amsterdam, awaited hourly. Grave and dignified in his burgher's robe, gold chain, ruff, and furred cap, he was received not merely with affection but with profound respect; for he had risen a step higher than his parents, and such ascents were marked in medieval society almost as visibly as the stairs themselves. Admitted to the family council in due course, he displayed plainly, though not discourteously, that his pride was deeply wounded by their having deigned to treat with Margaret Brandt.

"I see the temptation," he remarked coolly. "But which of us hath not at times wished one way and done another?"

This remark cast a considerable chill over the older generation. Little Kate, sensing the tension, interjected quickly. "Vex not thyself, dear Richart. Mother says she will not come."

"All the better, sweetheart," Richart replied, his relief palpable. "I fear me, if she do, I shall hie me back to Amsterdam."

Just then, Denys popped his head into the doorway.

"She will be here at three on the great dial."
