They all looked at one another in silence.

"Nay, Richard," Catherine finally spoke, her voice cutting through the tension like a knife through silk, "for Heaven's sake, let not this single wretched woman drag us all into the mud. Has she not already poisoned the well with enough malice?"

"In truth, she has," Richard replied, his tone steady despite the storm brewing in the room. "Do not fear, good mother. Let her enter and read the letter of the poor boy she has bewitched with diabolical arts, and then let her depart. Grant me only your word that you will afford her no more than the barest, most constrained civility. We cannot avoid her presence, for she is in our own house; and I promise, I shall say no further word."

With this fragile truce established, they awaited the intruder. Catherine, for her part, armored herself in a spirit barely less hostile than war. When Denys arrived to announce that the household would not visit her but would instead receive her, her lips curled in disdain. She commanded him to note how every petty emotion within them swelled larger than their love for Gerard. "Very well," she muttered, her voice dripping with venom. "I possess no such excuse. Why then should I mimic the petty pride of that bourgeois merchant—the arrogance common to all the unlettered? I shall go to them solely for Gerard's sake. Oh, how I loathe them!"

Thus, poor, good-natured Denys carried the volatile ingredients of an explosion into a single dwelling.

Margaret prepared herself with the solemn gravity of a knight donning armor for battle—he to deflect physical blows, she to parry the invisible arrows of contempt aimed at her poverty or the ironies directed at her perceived extravagance. Her kirtle was woven from English cloth, a deep, somber blue, matched by her farthingale and hose, though the latter were of a glossy roan, a claret hue that spoke of hidden richness. There was not an inch of pretentious fur upon her frame, only plain, snowy linen wristbands and intricately plaited linen framing her neck from the bosom of her gown upward, forming a square collar rather than a circle. Two waves of her front hair escaped, styled in a manner Mary Queen of Scots would revive a century hence; yet, eschewing the silver nets that would have clashed with her current plight, the remainder of her head was veiled in a tiny, tight-fitting hood of dark blue cloth, hemmed with silver thread. Her shoes were red, but the roan petticoat and hose prepared the observer for a visual shock, accentuating the arch of her instep and the elegance of her foot.

Beauty, even in adversity, knows its own business.

Despite this striking appearance, she kept her enemies waiting until the clock struck three.

Finally, she moved forward, accompanied by her male comrade. Halfway across the threshold, she halted, her expression thoughtful. "Denys!"

"Well, she-general?"

"I must return home," she whispered, the plea etched into her voice.

"What? Have you left something behind?"

"What?"

"My courage. Oh! Oh! Oh!"

"Nay, nay, be brave, she-general. I shall walk beside you."

"Aye, but will you stay close when I arrive?"

Denys promised, and she resumed her march, though with cautious, tentative steps.

Meanwhile, inside, the household gathered, a tableau of conflicting emotions: mortification mingled with curiosity, panting affection twisted by aversion to the woman who came to satisfy those very feelings, yet driven by another curiosity to see what magic could bewitch Gerard and unleash such chaos.

At last, Denys entered alone, whispering, "The female comrade waits without."

"Bring her in," Eli commanded gravely. "Now, silence. No one speaks to her save me."

All eyes turned to the door in a deathly hush. A faint murmur drifted from the hallway—a clash between Denys's rough baritone and a woman's soft, mellifluous voice. Soon, the sounds ceased. Slowly, the door swung open, and Margaret Brandt stepped onto the threshold. Dressed as described, she appeared somewhat pale, yet radiating a profound calm and loveliness. She looked straight ahead, meeting no gaze.

They all rose except Kate, who remained mute and staring.

"Be seated, mistress," Eli said solemnly, gesturing to a chair reserved specifically for her.

She bowed her head and crossed the room. In doing so, her condition became undeniable—not merely in her form, but in the palpable languor that weighed upon her. Cornelis and Sybrandt despised her for it; Richard felt it marred her beauty. Yet, strangely, it softened the women.

She withdrew her letter from her bosom, kissing it with a tenderness reserved for solitude, before settling to read it with the air of one fulfilling a singular, sacred duty.

As she began, she noted they had isolated her, seating her alone like a leper. She glanced at Denys, lowered her hand to her side, and made a swift, furtive gesture inviting him closer.

He responded with an obedient start, as if she had shouted "March!", and stood at her shoulder like a sentinel. However, this zealous display revealed to the company that he had been ordered there, and at that revelation, she flushed crimson. Now, she began to read of Gerard, *their* Gerard, to their rapturous ears. Her voice was mellow, clear, so soft and earnest that her very soul seemed to cling to every precious syllable. It was the voice of a woman's bosom given speech by Heaven itself.

"I do not doubt, my Margaret, that long before this meets thy beloved eyes, Denys, my dearest friend, will have sought thee out and recounted the manner of our unexpected and most tearful parting. Therefore, I shall begin at that most sorrowful day. What befell him afterward, poor faithful soul, I fain would hear, but may not. Yet I pray for him day and night, next after thee, dearest. A friend more steadfast and loving had David never known than I in him. Be good to him, for poor Gerard's sake."

At these words, which struck Denys with unexpected force, he leaned his head against Margaret's high chair and groaned aloud.

She turned quickly in her seat, found his hand, and pressed it tightly.

So the sweetheart and the friend held hands while the sweetheart read.

"I proceeded forward, dizzy and disoriented, like one trapped in a nightmare. Presently, a gentleman approached with his servants, all mounted on horseback, and nearly rode over me. He drew rein at the brow of the hill and sent his armed men back to strip me. They robbed me civilly enough, taking my purse and the last copper coin, then rode gaily away. I wandered on, a stuporous, friendless pauper."

A collective sigh rippled through the room, followed by a sharp oath from Denys.

"Shortly thereafter, a strange dimness enveloped me. I lay down to sleep upon the snow. It was ill-advised, with packs of wolves lurking nearby. If I had loved thee as thou deservest, I would have shown more manhood. But oh, sweet love, the drowsiness that crawled over my desolate, benumbed senses was greater than nature intended. Thus I slept; and but for God's mercy, superior to mine own or thyself, from that sleep I never woke, so all say. I had slept an hour or two, I suppose, when a hand shook me rudely. I awoke to my troubles. Standing there was a serving maid in her holiday attire. 'Are ye mad,' she said, seemingly in anger, 'to sleep in the snow beneath wolves' noses? Are ye weary of life, and not long weaned? Come, now,' she said, more kindly, 'rise like a good lad;' so I did rise. 'Are ye rich, or are ye poor?' But I stared at her as one amazed. 'Why, 'tis easy of reply,' she said. 'Are ye rich, or are ye poor?' Then I gave a great, loud cry, causing her to start back. 'Am I rich, or am I poor? Had ye asked me an hour ago, I would have said I am rich. But now I am so poor that surely no earth bears upon her bosom one poorer. An hour ago I was rich in a friend, rich in money, rich in hope and the spirits of youth; but now the Bastard of Burgundy has taken my friend, and another gentleman my purse; and I can neither proceed to Rome nor return to the one I left in Holland. I am poorest of the poor.' 'Alack!' said the wench. 'Nevertheless, if ye had been rich, ye might have lain down again in the snow for any use I had for ye; and then I trow ye would soon fare out of this world as bare as ye came into it. But, being poor, you are our man: so come with me.' Then I went because she bade me, and because I cared no longer whither I went. She took me to a fine house nearby, into a noble dining hall hung with black tapestry. There was set a table with many dishes, but only one plate and one chair. 'Fall to!' she whispered. 'What, alone?' I asked. 'Alone? And which of us, think ye, would eat out of the same dish with ye? Are we robbers of the dead?' Then she queried where I was born.

To truly grasp the architecture of Margaret's recovery, one must first understand the absolute fragility of her universe at that precise moment. She lay prostrate, her head resting upon Catherine's arm, having been invaded by the honest, rough-hewn members of her family who stood around her like a phalanx of warriors, shouting words of crude encouragement. Among them roared Giles, a man of immense physical presence, declaring with booming certainty that Gerard was alive and well, citing the letter itself as proof—a document he proclaimed to be the largest mankind had ever seen, and furthermore, the most beautiful, moving, and smallest written thing in history.

"Ay, good Master Giles," Margaret sighed, her voice a feeble thread against the roar of the room. "He was alive. But how can I know what hath since befallen him? Oh, why did he leave Holland to wander among strangers fierce as lions? And why did I not drive him from me sooner, rather than part him from his own flesh and blood? Forgive me, you who are his mother!"

With trembling hands, she gently dislodged Catherine's arm and made a valiant, if futile, attempt to slide from the chair to her knees. Superior forces, however, conspired against her gravity-defying ambition; after a brief struggle, she found herself once again ensconced upon Catherine's bosom, a sanctuary of softness amidst the chaos. There, she extended the letter toward Eli, speaking faintly yet with a sweetness that cut through the tension. "I will trust it from my hand now. In truth, I am little fit to read any more—and loath to leave my comfort;" and she wound her other arm tightly around Catherine's neck, seeking anchor in the living.

"Read thou, Richart," commanded Eli, his voice cutting through the domestic drama. "Thine eyes be younger than mine."

Richart accepted the parchment. "Well," he remarked, scanning the script with a critical eye. "Such writing I have never seen. It is penned with a needle's point; and clear to boot. Why is he not in my counting-house at Amsterdam instead of vagabonding it out yonder!"

"When I came to myself," Richart began, his voice dropping into the cadence of the traveler, "I was seated in the litter, and my good merchant holding fast to my hand. I babbled nonsense, and then shuddered in silence for a while. He put a horn of wine to my lips."

"Bless him! Bless him!" cried Catherine, her relief palpable.

"Whisht!" Eli hushed her sharply.

"And I told him what had befallen. He examined my leg. It was sprained sore, swollen at the ankle; and all my joints were broken, as I could scarce keep up my hose. I said, 'Sir, I shall be but a burden to you, I doubt, and can make you no harmony now; my poor psaltery it is broken;' and I grieved over my broken music, the companion of so many weary leagues. But he patted me on the cheek, bidding me not fret; also, he laid my leg upon a pillow and tended me like a kind father."

"January 19," Richart continued, reading aloud. "I sit all day in the litter, for we push forward with haste, and at night the good, kind merchant sends me to bed, refusing to let me work. Strange! Whenever I fall in with men like fiends, the next moment God still sends me some good man or woman, lest I should turn away from humankind. Oh, Margaret! How strangely mixed they are, and how old I feel compared to what I was three months ago. And lo! If good Master Fugger has not already bought me a psaltery."

"Eli, my man," Catherine interrupted, her eyes gleaming with commercial acumen, "if that merchant comes our way, let us buy a hundred ells of cloth from him, and not haggle."

"That will I, take your oath on't!" Eli declared immediately.

As Richart prepared to continue, Kate glanced at her mother, a faint blush coloring her cheeks as she drew a piece of work from under her apron. With her head bowed slightly lower than necessity demanded, she resumed sewing. Her mother, observing this silent communion, produced a similar specimen from her pocket, and both women stitched in tandem while Richart read. These surreptitious creations, newly exposed to observation, were babies' caps, more than half finished, telling a silent tale of devotion. Horror! They resembled little monks' cowls in their shape and delicacy.

"January 20," Richart intoned. "Laid up in the litter, and as good as blind, but halting to bait, the Lombardy plains burst upon me. Oh, Margaret! A land flowing with milk and honey; all sloping plains, goodly rivers, jocund meadows, delectable orchards, and blooming gardens; and though winter, it looks warmer than poor beloved Holland at midsummer, making the wanderer's face shine and his heart leap for joy to see earth so kind and smiling. Here be vines, cedars, olives, and cattle plenty, but three goats to a sheep. The draft oxen wear white linen on their necks, and standing by dark green olive-trees, each one is a picture; and the folk, especially women, wear delicate straw hats adorned with flowers and leaves fairly imitated in silk, mixed with silver. This day we crossed a river prettily in a chained ferry-boat. On either bank was a windlass, and a single man, by turning it, drew our whole company to his shore, whereat I did admire, being a stranger. Passed over with us some country folk. And an old woman, looking at a young wench, hid her face with her hand and held her crucifix out like a knight holds his sword in tournament, dreading the evil eye."

"January 25," Richart paused, shifting the tone. "Safe at Venice. A place whose strange and passing beauty is well known to thee by report of our mariners. Dost mind too how Peter would oft fill our ears withal, we handed beneath the table, and he still discoursing of this sea-enthroned and peerless city, in shape a bow, and its great canal and palaces on piles, and its watery ways plied by scores of gilded boats; and that market-place of nations, *orbis*, non *urbis*, Forum, St. Mark, his place? And his statue with the peerless jewels in his eyes, and the lion at his gate? But I, lying at my window in pain, may see none of these beauties as yet, but only a street, fairly paved, which is dull, and houses with oiled paper and linen, in lieu of glass, which is rude; and the passers-by, their habits and their gestures, wherein they are superfluous. Therefore, not to miss my daily comfort of whispering to thee, I will e'en turn mine eyes inward, and bind my sheaves of wisdom reaped by travel. For I love thee so, that no treasure pleases me not shared with thee; and what treasure so good and enduring as knowledge? This then have I, Sir Footsore, learned, that each nation hath its proper wisdom, and its proper folly; and methinks, could a great king, or duke, tramp like me, and see with his own eyes, he might pick the flowers, and eschew the weeds of nations, and go home and set his own folk on Wisdom's hill. The Germans in the north were churlish, but frank and honest; in the south, kindly and honest too. Their general blot is drunkenness, the which they carry even to mislike and contempt of sober men. They say commonly, 'Kanstu niecht sauffen und fressen so kanstu kienem hern wol dienen.' In England, the vulgar sort drink as deep, but the worshipful hold excess in this a reproach, and drink a health or two for courtesy, not gluttony, and still sugar the wine. In their cups the Germans use little mirth or discourse, but ply the business sadly, crying 'Seyte frolich!' The best of their drunken sport is 'Kurlemurlehuff,' a way of drinking with touching deftly of the glass, the beard, the table, in due turn, intermixed with whistlings and snappings of the finger so curiously ordered as 'tis a labour of Hercules, but to the beholder right pleasant and mirthful. Their topers, by advice of German leeches, sleep with pebbles in their mouths. For, as of a boiling pot the lid must be set ajar, so with these fleshy wine-pots, to vent the heat of their inward parts: spite of which many die suddenly from drink; but 'tis a matter of religion to slur it, and gloze it, and charge some innocent disease therewith. Yet 'tis more a custom than very nature, for their women come among the tipplers, and do but stand a moment, and as it were, kiss the wine-cup; and are indeed most temperate in eating and drinking, and of all women, modest and virtuous, and true spouses and friends to their mates; far before our Holland lasses, that being maids, put the question to the men, and being wived, do lord it over them. Why, there is a wife in Tergou, not far from our door. One came to the house and sought her man. Says she, 'You'll not find him:

The air between Naples and Rome hung heavy with the scent of brine and impending doom. Two months prior to the quiet domesticity of Eli's home, the coastal townspeople had gathered on the beaches, their gazes fixed unblinkingly upon a vessel fighting a gale that blew with malicious intent against the shore. The ship danced on the edge of catastrophe; moments of respite sparked cheers from the crowd, only to be extinguished instantly when the wind and sea conspired to drive her closer, feeding a secret, grim satisfaction into the hearts of the spectators—a cruel delight in witnessing another's struggle.

Though scientifically unsound for open-sea navigation, the craft was a masterpiece of destruction, designed specifically to break upon the rocks. Her extravagant poop caught the wind like a trap, and her lines hung inverted, resembling a cocked hat turned inside out. To the observers on the sand, the battering timber seemed a living thing, a panting beast struggling against mortality. Yet, had they stepped onto her deck, they would have discovered she possessed not one heart, but a dozen throbbing with terror; not one nature, but a multitude of souls unraveling in that fearful hour.

Chaos reigned supreme. Mariners stumbled wildly, clutching ropes with erratic strength, their cries alternating between blasphemous curses and desperate pleas. Passengers huddled around the mast, a trembling mass of bodies—some seated, some kneeling, others prostrate, fingers digging into the bulwarks as the vessel pitched in the monstrous swells. Among them stood Gerard, a figure of striking comeliness, his cheeks ashen, his lips compressed in a battle between raw terror and stubborn self-respect. Clutching a shroud tightly, he winced with every roll of the sea, an ill-fated soul adrift in a storm of divine indifference.

Around him, the air vibrated with a cacophony of faith. Prayers and vows rose from the throng, creating a polytheistic frenzy where the number of deities invoked nearly equaled the number of humans present. The sailors, however, pinned their hopes to a singular goddess, shifting her titles fluidly from "Queen of Heaven" to "Star of the Sea," "Mistress of the World," and "Haven of Safety." On land, the chaos of belief was even more fractured. An English merchant vowed a pile of gold to Our Lady of Walsingham; a Genoese merchant pledged a silver collar worth four pounds to Our Lady of Loretto; a Tuscan noble promised ten pounds of wax candles to Our Lady of Ravenna. In their fervent diversity, they pledged allegiance not merely to the True Cross, but to specific iterations of the Cross located in various modern cities, each believing their unique offering held exclusive power.

Then, disaster struck with finality. A gust of unprecedented violence caught the sail at a fatal angle. The rotten shrouds snapped, and the mainsail tore free with a deafening crack. It descended slowly, shrinking and darkening until it vanished into the distance, fluttering half a mile offshore like a discarded sheet of paper. Before the helmsman could adjust the ship's heading, a massive wave slammed into her quarter, drenching the terrified crew to the bone and delivering a chilling prelude to death.

In the wake of this betrayal, vows erupted anew. One man swore to become a Carthusian monk if Saint Thomas spared him; another promised a pilgrimage to Compostela, barefoot and clad only in armor, if Saint James intervened. Others invoked Thomas, Dominic, Denis, and above all, Catherine of Siena. Amidst this religious riot, two small Neapolitan traders stood shivering. One shouted at the top of his lungs, vowing a waxen image of Saint Christopher weighing as much as himself if he reached land safely. His companion nudged him, whispering a warning: "Brother, take heed. If you sold all your worldly possessions at auction, it would not buy that much wax."

"Hold your tongue, you fool!" the first retorted, then lowered his voice. "Do you think I am joking? Let me reach land, and I will not give him a single rush-dip."

Elsewhere, others lay flat against the deck, begging the sea itself. "Oh, most merciful sea! Oh, sea most generous! Oh, bountiful sea! Oh, beautiful sea! Be gentle, be kind, preserve us in this hour of peril!" These pleas were met with wails of pure animal terror, echoing every time the ship lurched violently, now reduced to a mere plaything in the arms of the tremendous waves.

Yet, amidst the hysteria, a figure of profound stillness emerged. A Roman woman of humble station sat with her child at her half-bared breast. Silent in the midst of the wailing throng, her cheek pale as ash, her eyes unnervingly calm. Her lips moved occasionally in silent prayer, but she neither wept nor lamented, nor did she bargain with the gods. Only when the ship seemed truly vanishing beneath their feet, causing bearded men to squeak in panic, did she kiss her child. That was her entire response. She sat patiently, suckling him in the jaws of death, refusing to deny him any joy she could offer. *Moribundo?* Yes, there she sat, embodying Antiquity among the medieval. Sixteen centuries had failed to tarnish the old Roman blood flowing in her veins; the instinct of a forgotten race taught her to meet death with decent dignity.

Nearby, a gigantic friar stood on the poop, feet planted wide apart like the Colossus of Rhodes. He did not defy the peril surrounding him; he simply ignored it. With a loud, unwavering voice, he recited verses from the Canticles and invited the passengers to confess. Some knelt before him, and he heard them, laying hands upon their heads and absolving them as if they stood in a secure sacristy rather than a perishing ship. Gerard, drawn by an instinct that pulls the wavering toward the impregnable, moved closer and closer to the monk. Indeed, the courage of heroes facing flesh-and-blood odds might have paled beside that of the giant friar and his monumental composure. Here, amidst the collapse, two figures upheld the dignity of their race: a woman, tender yet heroic, and a monk steeled by religion against mortal fears.

With the sail gone, the sailors hacked down the useless mast just a foot above the deck, and it crashed over the side with its remaining rigging. For a fleeting moment, this seemed to relieve the strain. But now, deprived of canvas propulsion, the hull could no longer outrun the sea. The waves struck her repeatedly on the poop, the tremendous blows sounding less like impacts from liquid and more like strikes from a rocky mountain.

The captain abandoned the helm and appeared amidships, pale as death. "Lighten her!" he cried. "Fling everything overboard, or we shall founder before we strike, losing the one slim chance we have of life." As the sailors executed this order, the captain, himself pale and surrounded by faces demanding answers, spoke with a demeanor utterly alien to an English skipper in such peril. "Friends," he addressed them, "last night, when all was fair—too fair, alas!—a globe of fire approached the ship. When a pair appear, it is good luck; nothing can drown her on that voyage. We mariners call these fiery globes Castor and Pollux. But if Castor comes without Pollux, or Pollux without Castor, she is doomed. Therefore, like good Christians, prepare to die."

These words triggered a loud wail of despair. When a trembling passenger asked how long they had to prepare, the captain replied, "She may, or may not, last half an hour; beyond that, it is impossible. She leaks like a sieve. Bustle, men, lighten her!"

Desperate, the passengers seized everything on deck and hurled it into the churning water. Soon, they grabbed a heavy sack. Beneath it lay an old man, seasick and unconscious. They dragged it from under him; it rattled ominously. Two men pulled it to the side, and the owner shot upward with an unearthly shriek, pouncing on the bundle. "Holy Moses! What would you do? 'Tis my all! 'Tis the whole fruit of my journey! Silver candlesticks, silver plates, brooches, hanaps—"

"Let go, thou hoary villain!" cried the others. "Shall all our lives be lost for thy ill-gotten gear?" "Fling him in with it!" shouted one. "'Tis this Hebrew we Christian men are drowned for." Numbers soon wrested the bundle from him and heaved it over the side. It splashed into the waves. Its owner let out one cry of anguish, stood glaring with white hair streaming in the wind, and prepared to leap after it, had it not already sunk forever. He staggered to and fro, tearing his hair, cursing them, the ship, the sea, and all the powers of heaven and hell alike.

"Now," the captain cried, pointing, "see, there is a church in sight. Steer for that church, mate, and you, friends, pray to the saint, whoever he may be."

Thus, they steered for the church and prayed to the unknown deity it housed. A tremendous sea pooped the vessel, shattered the rudder, and jammed it immovable, flooding the deck.

Wild with superstitious terror, some turned

To survive the crushing weight of Rome, Gerard descended to the west bank of the Tiber, securing a lodging that felt less like a home and more like a temporary cell between the river and the city's indifferent throat. Every dawn, he ventured forth into the labyrinthine marketplaces, carrying a specimen of his script to every workshop known for executing commissions. The reception was a uniform chill, a collective rejection that tasted of ash in his mouth.

"We make our letters somewhat thinner than this," sneered one merchant, holding up a sheet of paper as if inspecting a diseased limb. "How dark your ink is," scoffed another, dismissing the very essence of his craft. But the core of their cruelty was not aesthetic; it was existential. "What avails this?" they demanded, their voices rising in a chorus of dismissal. "Scant is the Latin writ here now. Can ye not write Greek?"

"Ay," Gerard replied, his voice steady despite the hollow ache in his chest, "but not nigh so well as Latin."

"Then you shall never make your bread at Rome," the verdict fell, final and absolute.

Defeated but unbroken in spirit, Gerard returned to his quarters with a purse lightened by a high-priced loan for a beautiful Greek manuscript—a tangible debt for intangible hope. Yet, within two weeks, the Greek alphabet ceased to be a barrier and became a bridge. He worked until noon, pouring his soul into the ancient characters, then hunted for customers with renewed vigor. When he finally presented a specimen of Greek calligraphy surpassing anything they possessed, the traders merely shrugged. Greek and Latin were alike unsaleable; the city was choked with works from across Europe. They told him bluntly, "You should have come last year."

Desperation bred new strategies. Gerard purchased a psaltery, finding solace in music when commerce failed. His landlady, a woman of Sienna blessed by the Virgin, noticed the decline in his demeanor. On one occasion, she invited him to dine, her kindness cutting through the fog of his despair. When she gently probed the source of his despondency, he confessed his plight. Her response was sharp, laced with the cynicism of a survivor.

"Those sly traders," she declared, her eyes narrowing, "have writers in their pay. They receive a noble price for their work and pay a sorry one for yours. No wonder they blow cold on you. Methinks you write too well. How know I that? Say you. Marry—because you lock not your door, like the churl Pietro, and women will be curious. Ay, ay, you write too well for them."

Gerard sought clarification. "Why?"

"Your good work might put out the eyes of what they are selling," she explained simply.

Gerard sighed, the weight of her insight settling on his shoulders. "Alas, dame, you read folk on the ill side, and you so kind and frank yourself."

"My dear little heart," she countered, her tone shifting to something warmer, "these Romans are a subtle race. Me? I am a Siennese, thanks to the Virgin."

"My mistake was leaving Augsburg," Gerard admitted, the ghost of his homeland haunting his words.

"Augsburg?" she scoffed haughtily. "Is that a place to even toe the line of Rome? I never heard of it, for my part."

Yet, she reassured him that he would make his fortune regardless of the booksellers. "Seeing thee a stranger, they lie to thee without sense or discretion. Why, all the world knows that our great folk are bitten with the writing spider these many years, pouring out their money like water, turning good land and houses into writ sheepskins to keep in a chest or a cupboard. God help them, and send them safe through this fury, as He hath through a heap of others. In sooth, there has been somewhat less cutting and stabbing among rival factions, and vindictive eating of their opposites' livers, minced and fried, since Scribbling came in. Why, I can tell you two. There is his Eminence Cardinal Bassarion, and his Holiness the Pope himself. There be a pair who could keep a score such as thee a writing night and day. But I'll speak to Teresa; she hears the gossip of the court."

The following day, armed with intelligence from Teresa, she revealed five more signors afflicted by the "writing spider." Gerard took down their names, purchased parchment, and spent days crafting specimens. He left samples bearing his name and address at each door, hoping against hope.

Silence answered him.

Day after day passed, leaving him heartsick. Strangely, this was the exact moment Margaret was fighting tooth and nail against impossible odds in Rotterdam, arrested for curing without a license rather than killing with one. Back in Rome, Gerard saw ruin staring him directly in the face.

He retreated into the afternoons, picking up canzonets and mastering them, laying in playing cards to color, and striking off a meal per day. This last stroke of genius, born of necessity, landed him in fresh trouble.

In these *camere locande*, the landlady prepared all meals, though the lodgers provided the provisions. Her hostess quickly detected his culinary experiments. With a brusque opening that made him blush and shrink, she pacified herself immediately, appealing to his common sense. Was adversity a thing to be overcome on an empty stomach?

"Patienza, my lad! Times will mend; meantime, I will feed you for the love of heaven." (*Gratis.*)

"Nay, hostess," Gerard protested, "my purse is not yet quite void, and it would add to my trouble if true folk should lose their due by me."

"Why, you are as mad as your neighbor Pietro, with his one bad picture," she retorted.

"Why, how know 'tis a bad picture?"

"Because nobody will buy it. There is one that hath no gift. He will have to don casque and glaive, and carry his panel for a shield."

Gerard pricked up his ears. She elaborated. Pietro had arrived from Florence with money and an unfinished painting, renting an unfurnished room opposite Gerard's and furnishing it neatly. Upon completion, he received visitors and offers—liberal ones, in her opinion—but refused them so disdainfully that he made enemies. Since then, he had dragged the canvas through the streets, only to return it defeated. Recently, furniture had vanished from his room; now he wore a single suit and slept atop a great chest. She had discovered this only by peering through the keyhole, noting how vigilantly he locked his door. "Is he afraid we shall steal his chest, or his picture, that no soul in all Rome is weak enough to buy?"

"Nay, sweet hostess; see you not 'tis his poverty he would screen from view?"

"And the more fool he! Are all our hearts as ill as his? A might give us a trial first, anyway."

"How you speak of him. Why, his case is mine; and your countryman to boot."

"Oh, we Siennese love strangers. His case yours? Nay, 'tis just the contrary. You are the comeliest youth ever lodged in this house; hair like gold. He is a dark, sour-visaged loon. Besides, you know how to take a woman on her better side; but not he. Natheless, I wish he would not starve to death in my house, to get me a bad name. Anyway, one starveling is enough in any house. You are far from home, and it is for me, which am the mistress here, to number your meals—for me and the Dutch wife, your mother, that is far away. We two women shall settle that matter. Mind thine own business, being a man, and leave cooking and the like to us, that are in the world for little else that I see but to roast fowls, and suckle men at starting, and sweep their grown-up cobwebs."

"Dear kind dame, in sooth you do often put me in mind of my mother that is far away."

"All the better; I'll put you more in mind of her before I have done with you." And the honest soul beamed with pleasure.

Gerard, neither an egotist nor blinded by female partialities, saw his own grief mirrored in the proud misery of Pietro. The more he contemplated it, the more he resolved to share his humble means with that unlucky artist; Pietro's sympathy would surely repay him. He attempted to intercept Pietro, but without success.

One day, a groan echoed from the adjacent room. Gerard knocked, receiving no answer. He knocked again. A surly voice commanded him to enter.

Obeying somewhat timidly, he pushed open the door to find a garret furnished sparsely: a chair, a picture facing the wall, an iron basin, an easel, and a long chest. Coiled upon the chest was a haggard young man with a wonderfully bright, predatory eye. Nothing resembled a coiled cobra ready to strike the first comer more vividly.

"Good Signor Pietro," Gerard began, his voice softening, "forgive me that, weary of my own solitude, I intrude on yours. But I am your nearest neighbor in this house, and methinks your brother in fortune. I am an artist too."

"You are a painter? Welcome, signer. Sit down on my bed."

Pietro leaped off the chest and waved him into the vacant space with a magnificent display of courtesy. Gerard bowed and smiled, yet hesitated. "I may not call

About a week later, the two friends sat side by side, yet the air between them hummed with a dissonance that shattered any pretense of camaraderie. Pietro attacked his canvas with the erratic violence of a storm-tossed ship; he wrought miracles in moments, only to collapse into sullen inertia, cursing the very medium he had just conquered, before resuming his assault in a fresh fury to end it with a groan of defeat. In stark contrast, the shrewd Dutchman worked with a serene, unbroken rhythm, his hands moving with the calm precision of a clockmaker, a quiet smile fixed upon his lips as he observed the chaos. Plainly, Gerard, who had mastered every fool he ever befriended, had successfully harnessed the wild Onagra. Together, they painted playing cards—a trivial craft designed merely to boil the pot. Once finished, the indignant master seized his larger work to begin his daily pilgrimage in search of a buyer. Gerard implored him to take the deck as well, urging him to test the market. The merchant eyed the cards with the suspicion of a rattlesnake, but eventually, embracing Gerard in the warm, open style of Italy, he accepted them. First, however, he dried the final pieces under the fierce, golden sun of that blessed climate.

Left alone, Gerard sketched a few Greek letters and then stitched a tear in his hose. His landlady discovered him thus occupied and queried, with ironic amusement, whether there were no women in the house. "When you have finished that," she said, "come and speak with Teresa, my friend—the woman I mentioned to you, whose husband is a man of little worth, yet who boasts acquaintance with the great." Gerard descended, and who should meet him but the Roman matron. "Ah, Madam," he greeted her, "is it truly you? The good dame told me nothing of this. And the little fair-haired boy—is he well? Is he none the worse for his voyage in that strange boat?" "He is well," replied the matron. "But pray, what are you two discussing?" demanded the landlady, staring alternately at each of them with wide, suspicious eyes. "And why do you tremble so, Teresa mia?" "He saved my child's life," Teresa declared, struggling to compose her features. "Saved your child?" the landlady exclaimed. "My lodger? And he never uttered a word of such a thing! Are you not ashamed to look me in the face?" "Alas, speak not harshly to him," interjected the matron gently. Turning to her friend, she poured forth a glowing eulogy of Gerard's conduct, during which the young painter stood flushed like a maiden, scarcely recognizing his own actions, as gratitude painted them in hues far more radiant than reality. "To think you should ask me to serve your lodger, of whom I knew naught save that he bore your good word, oh, Fiammina; and that was sufficient for me. Dear youth, in serving you, I serve myself." What followed was an eager discourse between the two women regarding the strategies required to breach the impenetrable wall of fees, commissions, and legal chicanery that separated the patrons of art from the unknown artist in the Eternal City. Teresa smiled with a sad pity at Gerard's naive simplicity in having left specimens of his skill at the doors of the mighty without offering bribes. "What!" she cried. "Without promising the servants a share? Without even tipping them to allow the Signori to view your merchandise! It might as well have been thrown into the Tiber." "Well-a-day!" sighed Gerard. "Then how is an artist to find a patron? For artists are poor, not rich." "By fleeing to some city nobler and less greedy than this," Teresa advised. "The Roman court does not wish for sheep without wool." She fell into contemplation and promised to return the next day. The landlady congratulated Gerard warmly. "Teresa has something brewing in her head," she noted.

Scarcely had Teresa departed when Pietro returned with his picture, his countenance black as a thunderhead. Gerard exchanged a glance with the landlady and followed him upstairs to offer comfort. "What? Have they allowed you to bring home your masterpiece?" "As heretofore," Pietro grumbled. "More fools they, then." "That is not necessarily a bad thing." "Pray, what is the matter?" "They have bought the cards!" Pietro shouted, hammering the empty air furiously left and right. "All the better," Gerard replied cheerfully. "They flew at me for them. They were utterly enraptured. They tried desperately to conceal their longing, but failed miserably. I saw, I feigned, I pillaged; curse these boobies." He flung a dozen small silver coins onto the floor, leaped upon them, dancing with the basilisk-like eyes of a gambler, kicking them assiduously until they spun and scattered across the room. Down went Gerard on his knees, following the maltreated innocents directly, transferring them tenderly into his purse. "You should rather smile at their ignorance and put it to profit," he suggested. "And so I will," Pietro retorted, his indignation now concentrated and steaming. "The brutes! We shall paint a pack a day; we shall set the entire city gambling and ruining itself, while we live like princes upon its vices and stupidity." "There was one of the queens, though," Pietro continued, his tone softening, "that I had fain kept back. 'Twas you who limned her, brother. She had lovely red-brown hair and sapphire eyes, and above all, a soul." "Pietro," said Gerard softly, "I painted that one from my heart." The quick-witted Italian nodded, his eyes twinkling with understanding. "You love her so dearly, yet you leave her." "Pietro, it is precisely because I love her so dear that I have wandered this weary road." This intimate colloquy was abruptly interrupted by the landlady's cry from below: "Come down, you are wanted." He descended, and there stood Teresa once more. "Come with me, Ser Gerard."

Gerard walked in silence beside Teresa, his mind drifting into the contemplative haze reserved for artists, speculating on the trajectory of her intentions regarding him rather than voicing the inquiry. Eventually, she broke the quiet, revealing her plan of her own volition. A confidante had apprised her of a goldsmith's wife seeking a scribe; "Her establishment lies nearby; your journey shall be brief."

Thus, they hastened to the goldsmith's dwelling.

"Madam," Teresa addressed the woman, "Leonora informs me you require a writer. I have procured one of exceptional beauty for you—a man who rescued my child from the treacherous sea. Pray, regard him with favor."

The goldsmith's wife acknowledged the request in spirit alone. Her gaze locked onto Gerard's handsome visage, reluctant to sever its hold. Yet her verbal response was dismissive. "No, I hold no need for a writer. Ah! Now I recall, it was my gossip, Claelia, the sausage-maker, who sought one. She mentioned it to me, and I relayed it to Leonora."

Teresa offered a polite retreat and withdrew.

Claelia resided at a considerable distance. Upon reaching her threshold, she was absent. Teresa declared calmly, "I shall await her return," settling into a posture so still, so dignified, and so statuesque that Gerard began to surreptitiously sketch her profile. Just as Claelia reappeared, the tableau shifted.

"Madam, I learn from the goldsmith's wife, the virtuous Olympia, that you seek a writer," Claelia announced, seizing Gerard's hand and drawing him forward. "I have brought you a paragon of beauty; he delivered my child from the cruel embrace of the waves. For the sake of our Lady, cast your eyes upon him with benevolence."

"My good dame, my fair Sir," Claelia replied, "I possess no use for a writer. However, you jog my memory; it was my friend Appia Claudia who requested one but recently. She is a tailor residing in the Via Lepida."

Teresa retired with serene composure.

"Madam," Gerard interjected, "this pursuit seems destined to become tedious for you."

Teresa opened her eyes wide. "What endeavor has ever succeeded without a measure of patience?" she added softly. "We shall knock upon every door in Rome, and justice shall be ours."

"But, Madam, I perceive we are being shadowed. I noted a man trailing us, sometimes at a distance, sometimes in close proximity."

"I have observed it," Teresa replied coolly, though a faint blush stained her cheeks. "It is my poor Lodovico."

She paused, turned, and beckoned with a single finger. A figure approached, moving with evident reluctance.

Upon arrival, Teresa fixed her gaze upon him until he appeared sheepish.

"Lodovico mio," she commanded, "acquaint yourself with this young Sir, of whom I have frequently spoken. Know him and cherish him, for he is the very man who saved your wife and child."

These final words transformed Lodovico. Having previously bowed and grinned with artificial cheerfulness, his countenance instantly softened into an expression of profound gratitude, and he embraced Gerard with fervent warmth.

Yet, beneath this display, a dissonance lingered. The man's initial demeanor, coupled with his clandestine following of his wife, rendered Gerard uneasy under the embrace. Nevertheless, he asked, "Shall we accept your company, Sir Lodovico?"

"No, signor," Lodovico responded, "I do not travel that side of the Tiber."

"Then, Adieu," Teresa said, her tone laden with significance.

"When shall you return home, Teresa mia?"

"When I have completed my errand, Lodovico."

They resumed their journey in silence. Teresa now bore a somber, almost gloomy air.

Briefly stated, Appia Claudia proved merciful. She did not direct them across the river again, but merely a hundred yards down the street to Lucretia, keeper of the glove shop. Lucretia also required a writer, though Appia Claudia could not discern the purpose. Lucretia was a jovial little dame who welcomed them warmly, declaring she needed no scribe, keeping all her accounts memorized. "It was for my confessor, Father Colonna; he is utterly infatuated with them."

"I have heard of his excellence," Teresa remarked.

"Who has not?"

"But, good dame, he is a friar; he has taken vows of poverty. I cannot permit this young man to write without remuneration. He saved my child at sea."

"Did he indeed?" Lucretia cast an approving glance at Gerard. "Well, rest assured; a Colonna never lacks funds. The good father needs only utter the word, and the princes of his order will shower a thousand crowns into his lap. And what a confessor, dame! The finest in Rome. His mind wanders leagues and leagues away; he scarcely heeds your words. Indeed, I confess my sins to him no more readily than I would whisper them to that wall. Once, to test him, I confessed alongside the crowd that I had slain my lodger's infant daughter and baked her inside a pie. Well, when my voice ceased its confession, he jolted awake from his reverie, summoning a grave demeanor: 'My erring sister, recite three Paternosters and three Ave Marias kneeling, abstain from butter and eggs next Wednesday, and pax vobiscum!' Then off he went, hands clasped behind his back, appearing as though I were nonexistent."

Teresa waited patiently, then gently steered this digressive lady back to the objective: "Would you be so kind as accompany this worthy youth to the friar and advocate for him?"

"Alas! How can I abandon my shop? And what necessity exists? His door stands perpetually open to writers, painters, scholars, and all such creatures. Indeed, once he refused to receive the Duke d'Urbino because a learned Greek was secluded with him; the friar's head and the scholar's were pressed so closely over a dusty parchment newly arrived from Greece that one could overlay both cowls upon the pair. My wench Onesta told me. She frequents here primarily for conversation during her errands."

"This is the man for thee, my friend," Teresa affirmed.

"All you must do," Lucretia continued, "is proceed to his lodgings—my boy shall guide you—and inform Onesta that you come from me and are a writer. She will escort you to him. If you place a piece of silver in the wench's palm, it causes no harm; it stands to reason."

"I possess silver," Teresa said warmly.

"But wait," Lucretia cautioned, "heed one thing. Whatever this young man claims he can accomplish, he must be capable of doing, or let him avoid the good friar like pestilence. He is a ferocious beast against all bunglers. Indeed, but yesterday, someone presented him with a poorly carved crucifix. He exclaimed, 'Is this how you present the *Salvator Mundi*? Who died for you in mortal agony, and you dare begrudge Him careful craftsmanship? This slovenly trinket, a crucifix? But since it is a crucifix of some sort, and I am a holy man, I would dust your jacket with your crucifix!' Onesta heard every syllable through the keyhole; therefore, beware."

"Fear not, Madam," Teresa replied loftily. "I warrant his ability; he saved my child."

Gerard lacked the subtlety to grasp this assurance. Far from sharing Teresa's confidence, he pleaded for a postponement. Would it not suffice to delay until tomorrow?

"Here is a coward for you," Lucretia chided.

"No, he is not a coward," Teresa snapped, inflamed; "he is modest."

"I dread this high-born, fastidious friar," Gerard admitted. "Consider he has witnessed the handiwork of every writer in Italy, dear Dame Teresa. If you would merely allow me to craft a superior piece of work than I have thus far produced, I shall face him tomorrow."

"I consent," Teresa agreed.

They walked homeward together.

Not far from his own lodging stood a shop selling vellum. A magnificent white skin hung in the window. Gerard gazed at it wistfully, knowing he could not afford it, yet he hurried past nonetheless. Soon, however, he resolved where to procure vellum. Parting ways with Teresa at his doorstep, he rushed upstairs, retrieving the bond he had carried all the way from Sevenbergen, laying it upon the table with a sigh. He then prepared his chemicals to erase the ancient script; yet, recognizing this as his final opportunity to read it, he conquered his visceral aversion to illegible handwriting and proceeded to decipher the deed despite its detestable abbreviations. The document revealed that Ghysbrecht Van Swieten was to advance funds to Floris Brandt concerning a parcel of land, to be repaid via rental income.

On this realization, Gerard deemed it imprudent and improper to destroy the deed. Instead, he vowed to decipher every word at his leisure. Descending the stairs, he resolved to purchase a small sheet of vellum with his share of the card-money.

At the foot of the stairs, he encountered the landlady and Teresa conversing. At his appearance, the former cried out, "There he is. You are caught, Donna Mia. Observe what she

To understand the architecture of Gerard's ascent, one must first grasp the absolute poverty of his universe prior to the arrival of the Friar. He existed within a microscopic empire of two, subjugated by a man he was conditioned to call "Master"—a name that now feels like a relic of a darker, pre-renaissance age. That Master was a square-built, brutal artifact of a world he did not know, roughly sixty years of age, though time in the monastery seemed measured only by the decay of his humanity. His dark hair fell in matted, feral clusters, and a vast beard obscured his face, rendering him less a man than a beast draped in the ruin of whatever civilization had cast him out. He possessed a terrifying latent power, yet he spent his days in a state of stagnant lethargy, only stirring to exert his dominance over Gerard's labor—copying texts or hauling faggots of wood down the ravine.

Gerard's life was bound by the daily ritual of obedience, a silent contract written in ink and blood. He was permitted no education, no heritage, and almost no language beyond the vernacular of command. The Master utilized silence as a tool of oppression, rationing his words to orders and curses, ensuring Gerard's mind remained as barren as the rocky crags that caged them. Gerard became a taciturn creature, his intellect starved, left to gaze into the abyssal depths of the ocean or trace the cold geometries of the stars. He was kept as a beast of the field—valuable only for the calories his labor saved the Master, and perhaps to serve as a mirror for the Master's own desperate need for mastery.

Then came the intervention of Fra Colonna, a variable introduced into the equation that shattered the static equilibrium of Gerard's existence. To comprehend the magnitude of this shift, one must appreciate the intellectual landscape of the era. The true revivers of ancient learning and philosophy were two writers of fiction—Petrarch and Boccaccio. Their labors were not crowned with great, public, and immediate success; but they sowed the good seed; and it never perished, but quickened in the soil, awaiting sunshine. From their day Italy was never without a native scholar or two, versed in Greek; and each learned Greek who landed there was received fraternally. The fourteenth century, ere its close, saw the birth of Poggio, Valla, and the elder Guarino; and early in the fifteenth Florence under Cosmo de Medici was a nest of Platonists. These, headed by Gemistus Pletho, a born Greek, began about A.D. 1440 to write down Aristotle. For few minds are big enough to be just to great A without being unjust to capital B. Theodore Gaza defended that great man with moderation; George of Trebizond with acerbity, and retorted on Plato. Then Cardinal Bessarion, another born Greek, resisted the said George, and his idol, in a tract "Adversus calumniatorem Platonis." Pugnacity, whether wise or not, is a form of vitality.

Born without controversial bile in so zealous an epoch, Francesco Colonna, a young nobleman of Florence, lived for the arts. At twenty he turned Dominican friar. His object was quiet study. He retired from idle company, and faction fights, the humming and the stinging of the human hive, to St. Dominic and the Nine Muses. An eager student of languages, pictures, statues, chronology, coins, and monumental inscriptions. These last loosened his faith in popular histories. He traveled many years in the East, and returned laden with spoils; master of several choice MSS., and versed in Greek and Latin, Hebrew and Syriac. He found his country had not stood still. Other lettered princes besides Cosmo had sprung up. Alfonso King of Naples, Nicolas d'Este, Lionel d'Este, etc. Above all, his old friend Thomas of Sarzana had been made Pope, and had lent a mighty impulse to letters; had accumulated 5000 MSS. in the library of the Vatican, and had set Poggio to translate Diodorus Siculus and Xenophon's Cyropaedia, Laurentius Valla to translate Herodotus and Thucydides, Theodore Gaza, Theophrastus; George of Trebizond, Eusebius, and certain treatises of Plato, etc. etc.

The monk found Plato and Aristotle under armistice, but Poggio and Valla at loggerheads over verbs and nouns, and on fire with *odium philologicum*. All this was heaven; and he settled down in his native land, his life a rosy dream. None so happy as the versatile, provided they have not their bread to make by it. And Fra Colonna was Versatility. He knew seven or eight languages, and a little mathematics; could write a bit, paint a bit, model a bit, sing a bit, strum a bit; and could relish superior excellence in all these branches. For this last trait he deserved to be as happy as he was. For, gauge the intellects of your acquaintances, and you will find but few whose minds are neither deaf, nor blind, nor dead to some great art or science—"And wisdom at one entrance quite shut out." And such of them as are conceited as well as stupid shall even parade instead of blushing for the holes in their intellects.

A zealot in art, the friar was a skeptic in religion. In every age there are a few men who hold the opinions of another age, past or future. Being a lump of simplicity, his skepticism was as naive as his enthusiasm. He affected to look on the religious ceremonies of his day as his models, the heathen philosophers, regarded the worship of gods and departed heroes: mummeries good for the populace. But here his mind drew unconsciously a droll distinction. Whatever Christian ceremony his learning taught him was of purely pagan origin, that he respected, out of respect for antiquity; though had he, with his turn of mind, been a pagan and its contemporary, he would have scorned it from his philosophic heights.

Fra Colonna was charmed with his new artist, and having the run of half the palaces in Rome, sounded his praises so, that he was soon called upon to resign him. He told Gerard what great princes wanted him. "But I am so happy with you, father," objected Gerard. "Fiddlestick about being happy with me," said Fra Colonna; "you must not be happy; you must be a man of the world; the grand lesson I impress on the young is, be a man of the world. Now these Montesini can pay you three times as much as I can, and they shall too-by Jupiter." And the friar clapped a terrific price on Gerard's pen. It was acceded to without a murmur. Much higher prices were going for copying than authorship ever obtained for centuries under the printing press. Gerard had three hundred crowns for Aristotle's treatise on rhetoric. The great are mighty sweet upon all their pets, while the fancy lasts; and in the rage for Greek MSS. the handsome writer soon became a pet, and nobles of both sexes caressed him like a lap dog. It would have turned a vain fellow's head; but the canny Dutchman saw the steel hand beneath the velvet glove, and did not presume. Nevertheless it was a proud day for him when he found himself seated with Fra Colonna at the table of his present employer, Cardinal Bessarion.

They were about a mile from the top of that table; but never mind, there they were and Gerard had the advantage of seeing roast pheasants dished up with all their feathers as if they had just flown out of a coppice instead of off the spit: also chickens cooked in bottles, and tender as peaches. But the grand novelty was the napkins, surpassingly fine, and folded into cocked hats, and birds' wings, and fans, etc., instead of lying flat. This electrified Gerard; though my readers have seen the dazzling phenomenon without tumbling backwards chair and all. After dinner the tables were split in pieces, and carried away, and lo, under each was another table spread with sweetmeats. The signoras and signorinas fell upon them and gormandized; but the signors eyed them with reasonable suspicion. "But, dear father," objected Gerard, "I see not the bifurcal daggers, with which men say his excellency armeth the left hand of a man." "Nay, 'tis the Cardinal Orsini which hath invented yon peevish instrument for his guests to fumble their meat withal. One, being in haste, did skewer his tongue to his palate with it, I hear; O tempora, O mores! The ancients, reclining godlike at their feasts, how had they spurned such pedantries."

As soon as the ladies had disported themselves among the sugar-plums, the tables were suddenly removed, and the guests sat in a row against the wall. Then came in, ducking and scraping, two ecclesiastics with lutes, and kneeled at the cardinal's feet and there sang the service of the day; then retired with a deep obeisance: In answer to which the cardinal fingered his skull cap as our late Iron Duke his hat: the company dispersed, and Gerard had dined with a cardinal and one that had thrice just missed being pope. But greater honor was in store. One day the cardinal sent for him, and after praising the beauty of his work took him in his coach to the Vatican; and up a private stair to a luxurious little room,

To truly grasp the architecture of Fra Colonna's worldview, one must first confront the absolute poverty of his perception regarding the grandeur of the Eternal City. On those rare occasions when the artist Gerard was permitted to walk the streets alongside the friar, the world unfolded *en beau*—wrapped in the suffocating, golden haze of Roman imperial nostalgia. Yet, the friar, a man of stark realism, consistently drenched these romantic reveries in cold water.

"This place Rome?" Fra Colonna would scoff, gesturing to the cobblestones beneath their boots. "It is merely the tomb of mighty Rome." He dragged Gerard's attention downward, pointing out how twenty or thirty feet of ancient triumphal arches lay buried beneath the modern thoroughfares, and how contemporary palaces sprawled directly atop the severed heads of colossal columns. Coupling the cramped confines of the current city with the gigantic, skeletal vestiges of antiquity that poked through the soil like rotting teeth, the friar delivered a simile that cut deeper than any blade. "I tell thee," he intoned, "this village they call Rome is but as one of those swallow's nests ye shall see built on the eaves of a decayed abbey."

"Old Rome must indeed have been fair then," Gerard murmured, captivated by the ghost of history.

"Judge for yourself, my son," the friar countered, his voice dripping with cynical authority. "You see the great sewer, a work of the Romans in their very childhood, destined to outlast Vesuvius itself. You see the fragments of the Temple of Peace. Imagine, if you could, the Capitol with its twenty-five temples. Note this Monte Savello; what is it, if you please, but the ruins of the ancient Theatre of Marcellus? And as for Testaccio, one of the highest hills in modern Rome, it is but an ancient dustheap—the refuse of old Rome, where women flung their broken pots and pans until they formed a mountain."

*"Ex pede Herculem; ex ungue leonem."* From the foot, Hercules; from the claw, the lion.

Gerard listened with respectful deference, but when the holy friar proceeded by analogy to imply that the moral superiority of the pagan Romans was proportionately grand, he resisted stoutly. "Has then the world lost anything by Christ's coming?" he challenged, though he immediately flushed, sensing the weight of his own ingratitude toward his benefactor.

"Saints forbid!" the friar snapped, recoiling as if struck. "'Twere heresy to say so." Having made this direct concession, he quickly retreated into the labyrinth of subtle circumlocution, evading the theological trap until they reached the forbidden door via a spiral back staircase.

In the midst of their journey, they arrived at a church where a knot of agitated persons gathered in the porch. Inside, a demon was being exorcised. Fra Colonna possessed a peculiar tic: a curious, low moan that escaped him whenever facts contrary to Zeno or Epicurus were presented to him. This sound conveyed to those who knew it not merely strong dissent, but a profound pity for human credulity, ignorance, and error—especially when such blindness prevented men from seeing the merits of Paganism.

The friar moaned softly and declared, "Then come away."

"Nay, father, prithee! Prithree! I ne'er saw a devil cast out."

Reluctantly, the friar accompanied Gerard into the nave, giving a final, exaggerated shrug. There, huddled before the altar, knelt the demoniac, a scarf tied tightly around his neck, used by the officiating priest to hold him fast like a dog on a chain. Few attended; rumor had spread that the last demon expelled in this church fled no further than into the lap of one spectator—"like a coney ferreted out of one burrow running to the next."

When Gerard and the friar approached, the priest seemed satisfied that sufficient witnesses had assembled. He rose, breviary in hand, and began the ritual. First, he sought to identify the entity tormenting the victim.

"Come out, Ashtaroth. Oho! It is not you then. Come out, Belial. Come out, Tatzi. Come out, Eza. No; he trembles not. Come out, Azymoth. Come out, Feriander. Come out, Foletho. Come out, Astyma. Come out, Nebul."

"Aha! What, have I found ye? 'Tis thou, thou reptile; at thine old tricks. Let us pray!"

"Oh, Lord, we pray thee to drive the foul fiend Nebul out of this thy creature: out of his hair, and his eyes, out of his nose, out of his mouth, out of his ears, out of his gums, out of his teeth, out of his shoulders, out of his arms, legs, loins, stomach, bowels, thighs, knees, calves, feet, ankles, finger-nails, toe-nails, and soul. Amen."

The priest then rose from his knees, turning to the congregation with quiet geniality. "Gentlemen, we have here as obstinate a devil as you may see in a summer day." Facing the patient, he spoke with rigorous intensity, alternating between addressing the man and the fiend. Through the same trembling mouth, the victim alternated between hatred of the holy names and complaints of internal agony.

It was the priest who first confused the victim with the culprit in the public imagination, launching physical assaults—cuffing the man soundly, kicking him, and spitting repeatedly in his face. He then ignited a candle, deliberately dimmed it, and burned it until the flame seared his own fingers, dropping it hastily. Next, he produced the *custodia*, displaying the *Corpus Domini* within. He lit another candle, this time with caution, and addressed the demoniac in his human guise with civility, dismissing him and accepting the applause of the crowd.

"Good father," Gerard observed, "you have their names by heart. Our northern priests possess no such exquisite knowledge of the hellish squadrons."

"Ay, young man, here we know all their names, and eke their ways, the reptiles. This Nebul is a bitter hard one to hunt out."

With affable ease, the friar recounted several of his experiences, culminating in a tale from the previous day: driving a great hulking fiend out of a woman through her mouth, leaving behind nails, pins, and a tuft of hair, while the creature screamed in anguish, *"Tis not thou that conquerest me. See that stone on the window sill. Know that the angel Gabriel coming down to earth once lighted on that stone: 'tis that has done my business."*

The friar let out a characteristic moan. "And you believed him?"

"Certes! Who but an infidel would discredit a revelation so precise?"

"What, believe the father of lies? That is pushing credulity beyond endurance."

"Oh, a liar does not always lie."

"Ay, doth he whenever he tells an improbable story to begin, and shows you a holy relic, arming you against the Satanic host. Fiends—if any exist—are not so simple. Thou shouldst have answered him out of antiquity: *'Timeo Danaos et dona ferentes.'"*

"Some blackguard chopped his wife's head off on that stone, young man; you take my word for it." With a final flourish of dismissal, the friar hurried Gerard away.

"Alack, father, I fear you abashed the good priest."

"Ay, by Pollux," the friar chuckled, wiping sweat from his brow. "I blistered him with a single touch of 'Socratic interrogation.' What modern can parry the weapons of antiquity?"

Later that afternoon, after Gerard completed his day's work, a fine lackey appeared demanding his attendance at the Palace Cesarini. He was ushered into a noble apartment where a girl sat working on a tapestry. She rose, excused herself, and returned shortly with news that her mistress wished to see him.

An hour passed, the silence stretching taut, until Gerard began to fret. "These nobles think nothing of a poor fellow's time." Just as he resolved to slip out, the door swung open, admitting a superb beauty followed by two maids. It was the young princess of the house of Cesarini. She entered speaking loudly and haughtily to her attendants, but upon spotting Gerard, her voice softened to a feminine whisper. "Are you the writer, messer?"

"I am, Signora."

"'Tis well."

She seated herself on a high chair, almost a throne, while Gerard and the maids remained standing.

"What is your name, good youth?"

"Gerard, signora."

"Gerard? Body of Bacchus! Is that the name of a human creature?"

"It is a Dutch name, signora. I was born at Tergou, in Holland."

"A harsh name, girls, for so well-favoured a youth; what say you?"

The maids assented warmly.

"What did I send for him for?" the lady inquired with lofty languor. "Ah, I remember. Be seated, Ser Gerardo, and write me a letter to Ercole Orsini, my lover; at least he says so."

Gerard seated himself, taking out paper and ink, looking upward for instructions. The princess, perched on her elevated seat, gazed down at him

To capture the essence of Claelia's transformation, one must first witness the deliberate construction of her own mythos. She commissioned a full-length portrait, yet rejected the suggestion of her friend Pietro Vanucci with a sharp, calculated disdain. "'Twill be time to put a slight on the Gerardo, when his work discontents me," she declared, her voice cutting through the air like a blade. Gerard, aware of his own limitations as a colorist despite his mastery of line, proposed an alternative: a Roman statue. He demonstrated his ability to mimic marble on paper, coaxing her into consenting to the pose. However, the moment he insisted she don the tunic, toga, and sandals of antiquity, her medieval frankness surfaced. "Why, I had as lieve be presented in my smock," she retorted, her refusal grounded in a pragmatic rejection of historical alienation.

Gerard, undeterred, appealed to her aesthetic sensibilities, arguing that the ancient garb was free, ample, and noble—a fitting vessel for the Roman features and shapely limbs Heaven had bestowed upon her. "Alack! signorina," he pleaded, "you have surely never noted the ancient habit... most becoming your highness." Her response was a deferral, a strategic pause: "Come o' Saturday, and then I will say ay or nay."

This respite was consumed by the frantic tailoring of costumes that were a millennium out of date, tried on in her chamber to ensure they compensated for their temporal displacement. But Gerard's journey to this interview was abruptly halted by a vision in a shop window. There, lying open, was a copy of Lactantius. His eye, trained to recognize the fluid grace of the human hand, scanned the page. "That is fairly writ, anyway," he thought, expecting the familiar rhythm of ink.

Then, the realization struck with the force of a physical blow. It was not written; it was printed.

Gerard groaned, the sound echoing in the quiet street. "I am sped; mine enemy is at the door. The press is in Rome." He entered the shop, feigning nonchalance, asking how long the printing press had been present in the city. The merchant laughed, dismissing the idea entirely until Gerard pressed him regarding the specific volume. "Oh, the Lactantius; that was printed on the top of the Apennines."

"What, did the printing-press fall down there out o' the moon?" Gerard demanded, his horror palpable.

"Nay, messer," the trader replied, pointing to the title page. "It shot up there out of Germany. See!"

Gerard snatched the book, his eyes devouring the foreign script: *Opera et impensis Sweynheim et Pannartz Alumnorum Joannis Fust. Impressum Subiacis. A.D. 1465.* "Will ye buy, messer? See how fair and even be the letters. Few are left can write like that; and scarce a quarter of the price."

"I would fain have it," Gerard said sadly, clutching the threat of mechanized reproduction. "But my heart will not let me. Know that I am a caligrapher, and these disciples of Fust run after me round the world a-taking the bread out of my mouth. But I wish them no ill. Heaven forbid!" He fled the shop, driven by a singular urgency. "Dear Margaret," he whispered to the empty air, "we must lose no time; we must make our hay while shines the sun. One month more and an avalanche of printer's type shall roll down on Rome from those Apennines, and lay us waste that writers be."

With this dread anchoring his resolve, he rushed to the Princess's residence. He was ushered into an apartment new to him, a space of opulent isolation. A fountain played in the center, its waters masking footsteps on a floor paved with panther skins, dressed with hair to create a surface of absolute silence. An antechamber to her boudoir, separated only by a curtain of gorgeous tapestry, it was a theater staged for performance.

Left alone, Gerard grew uneasy, doubting if he had been led astray. These fears dissolved with the arrival of a light step behind the tapestry. The fabric parted, revealing a figure that transcended mere mortality. It was neither quite Venus nor quite Minerva, but a synthesis of both—nobler than the goddess of love, more womanly than the daughter of Jove. Dressed in toga, tunic, and sandals, she was a living relic, untouched by modernity. As for beauty, it was timeless.

Gerard started, the artist within him flushing with a mixture of shock and ecstasy. "Oh!" he cried innocently, gazing in rapture. This reaction, genuine and unguarded, added the final charm to his model. A light blush tinted her cheeks, her eyes brightened, and her mouth curved in a smile of delicious complacency at this tribute to her own allure.

After a prolonged silence, during which Gerard's eloquence reduced itself to staring, she broke the spell. "Well, Gerardo, thou seest I have made myself an antique monster for thee."

"A monster? I doubt Fra Colonna would fall down and adore your highness, seeing you so habited."

"Nay, I care not to be adored by an old man. I would liever be loved by a young one: of my own choosing."

Gerard settled his pencils, covering his canvas with stout paper, and began to work. Absorbed completely, he showed no mercy to his model. Finally, after nearly an hour in one rigid posture, she spoke, her voice faint. "Gerardo," she said, "I can stand so no more, even for thee."

"Sit down and rest awhile, Signora."

"I thank thee," she replied, sinking into a chair, turning pale and sighing. Gerard, alarmed by her distress and realizing his inconsideration, reached for water from the fountain. He prepared to splash it on her face, but she raised a white hand deprecatingly. "Nay, hold it to my brow with thine hand: prithee, do not fling it at me!"

Timidly, he applied the wet cloth to her forehead. "Ah!" she sighed, "that is reviving. Again." He repeated the action. She thanked him and signaled for a bell. A maid arrived, dispatched to fetch Floretta with orders to bring a large fan.

Floretta appeared, but the moment she drew near, the Princess's highbred nostrils flared like a bloodhorse's. "Wretch!" she shouted, springing to her feet with a sudden resurgence of vigor. Seizing Floretta by the left arm, she twisted it violently into her hair and, with her right hand, delivered three severe boxings to the girl's ears.

Floretta screamed and blubbered, receiving no mercy. The antique toga, now discarded, revealed a bare arm that seemed as powerful as it was beautiful, rising and falling like the piston of a steam engine. Heavy slaps resonated against Floretta's shoulders, driving her sobbing and screaming through the tapestry, where she continued to cry bitterly for some time.

"Saints of heaven!" cried Gerard, horrified. "What is amiss? what has she done?"

"She knows right well. 'Tis not the first time. The nasty toad! I'll learn her to come to me stinking of the musk-cat."

"Alas! Signora, 'twas a small fault, methinks."

"A small fault? Nay, 'twas a foul fault." With an amazing descent from fury to humility, she added, "Are you wroth with me for beating her, Gerardo?"

"Signora, it ill becomes me to school you; but methinks such as Heaven appoints to govern others should govern themselves."

"That is true, Gerardo. How wise you are, to be so young." She summoned the other maid, handing her a purse. "Take that to Floretta, and tell her 'the Gerardo' hath interceded for her; and so I must needs forgive her. There, Gerardo."

Gerard blushed at the compliment, unable to formulate a witty reply, and asked if he should resume the painting.

"Not yet; beating that hussy hath somewhat breathed me. I'll sit awhile, and you shall talk to me. I know you can talk, an it pleases you, as rarely as you draw."

"That were easily done."

"Do it then, Gerardo."

Gerard was taken aback. "But, signora, I know not what to say. This is sudden."

"Say your real mind. Say you wish you were anywhere but here."

"Nay, signora, that would not be sooth. I wish one thing though."

"Ay, and what is that?" she asked gently.

"I wish I could have drawn you as you were beating that poor lass. You were awful, yet lovely. Oh, what a subject for a Pythoness!"

"Alas! he thinks but of his art. And why keep such a coil about my beauty, Gerardo? You are far fairer than I am. You are more like Apollo than I to Venus. Also, you have lovely hair and lovely eyes--but you know not what to do with them."

"Ay, do I. To draw you, signora."

"Ah, yes; you can see my features with them; but you cannot see what any Roman gallant had seen long ago in your place. Yet sure you must

Gerard returned to consciousness, and with it, the crushing weight of despair settled upon his spirit. By the second day, fever had seized his brain, driving him into a ravishing delirium. Beside his bed, upon a stark table, lay his rich auburn hair, cascading long and luxuriant as a woman's tresses—a severed crown marking the end of his former self. One deadly symptom swiftly followed another, accelerating toward the grave. On the fifth day, the leech withdrew, declaring the patient beyond aid. As the sun dipped below the horizon that same evening, Gerard succumbed to a profound slumber. Whispers circulated among the townsfolk that he would awaken only to meet his final breath. Yet, an elderly gossip, whose counsel held sway due to her experience as a professional nurse, insisted that his youth might yet be his salvation, provided he could endure a single cycle of twelve uninterrupted hours.

On this promise, his aged landlady swept the room clear, leaving him to rest in solitude. She vowed a wax candle to the Virgin Mary for every hour he slept. Twelve hours passed. The good soul rejoiced, falling to her knees to thank the Virgin. Twenty-four hours elapsed. Doubt crept into the hearts of the nurses. At the thirtieth hour, she summoned the physician. "Thirty hours!" she cried anxiously. "Shall we wake him?" The doctor examined him closely, his brow furrowed. "His breath is steady, his hand moist," he observed. "There are learned leeches who would wake him merely to inspect his tongue, finding nothing but confusion; but we, being women, possess the wisdom to let nature take its course. When has sleep ever harmed a racked brain or a torn heart?"

When the clock struck forty-eight hours, the news spread, and the curious thronged the doors, requiring great effort to keep them at bay. They were admitted only to Fra Colonna and his companion, the gigantic Fra Jerome. These two men relieved the anxious women, sitting in silent vigil. Fra Colonna gazed upon his young friend with tears streaming silently down his cheeks, while Fra Jerome, beads clasped tightly in his hand, regarded the sleeper with the same calm detachment he had once shown the churning sea during their encounter hand-to-hand.

It was likely near the sixtieth hour of this unnatural slumber that the landlady nudged Fra Colonna with her elbow. He leaned forward. Gerard had opened his eyes as gently as if he had merely been dozing. He stared blankly, drawing himself up slightly in the bed. He reached for his head and discovered his hair was gone. He noticed Fra Colonna and smiled with a fleeting pleasure. But midway through the smile, his expression froze, and in an instant, his face convulsed with anguish unspeakable. A loud cry escaped his throat, and he turned his face firmly to the wall. His good landlady wept openly; she knew the hollow ache of awakening bereft. Fra Jerome began to recite canticles and prayers from his breviary. Gerard curled himself into the bedclothes, retreating further inward. Fra Colonna approached him, whimpering softly, reminding him that all was not lost. "The divine Muses are immortal," he urged. "You must transfer your affection to them; they will never betray you nor fail you like fragile creatures of clay." Overcome by his own emotion, the simple father hurried away.

Fra Jerome remained. "Young man," he said, his voice low and grave, "the Muses exist only in the minds of pagans and visionaries. The Church alone grants repose to the heart on earth and happiness to the soul hereafter. Has the earth deceived thee? Has passion broken your heart after tearing it apart? The Church opens her arms: consecrate thy gifts to her! The Church is peace of mind." He spoke these words solemnly at the threshold and departed the moment they were uttered.
