"The Church!" Gerard screamed, rising furiously and shaking his fist at the departing friar. "Malediction on the Church! But for the Church, I should not lie broken here, while she lies cold, cold, cold in Holland. Oh, my Margaret! Oh, my darling! My darling! And I must flee from thee for the few months thou hadst to live. Cruel! Cruel! The monsters allowed her to die. Death does not come without signs; these blind, selfish wretches saw them not, or cared not; but I had seen them, I who loved her. Oh, had I been there, I would have saved her, I would have saved her. Idiot! Idiot! To leave her for a mere moment!" He wept bitterly for a long time. Then, suddenly bursting into fresh rage, he cried vehemently, "The Church! For whose sake I was driven from her! My malediction be on the Church! And on the hypocrites who name it to my broken heart. Accursed be the world! Ghysbrecht lives; Margaret dies. Thieves, murderers, harlots, live forever. Only angels die. Curse life! Curse death! And whosoever made them what they are!"

The friar did not hear these mad and wicked words, catching only the raw yell of rage flung after him. It was fortunate, for had he heard them, he would have ordered the late shipmate burned in the forum with as little hesitation as one might roast a kid. The old landlady, having accompanied Fra Colonna downstairs, heard the raised voice and returned in some anxiety. She found Gerard dressing, tears streaming down his face. She remonstrated gently.

"What avail lies in my lying here?" he said gloomily. "Can I find here that which I seek?"

"Saints preserve us! Is he distraught again? What seek ye?"

"Oblivion."

"Oblivion, my little heart? Oh, but you are young to talk so."

"Young or old, what else have I to live for?"

He donned his finest clothes. The good dame protested. "My pretty Gerard, know that it is Tuesday, not Sunday."

"Oh, Tuesday is it? I thought it had been Saturday."

"Nay, thou hast slept long. Thou never wearst thy brave clothes on working days. Consider."

"What I did when she lived, I did. Now I shall do whatever erst I did not. The past is the past. There lies my hair, and with it my way of life. I have served one Master as well as I could. You see my reward. Now I'll serve another, and give him a fair trial too."

"Alas!" sighed the woman, turning pale. "What mean these dark words? And what new master is this whose service thou wouldst try?"

And with this horrible declaration on his lips, the miserable creature walked out. His cap and feather were set jauntily on one side, masking a sinister face pale as ashes, drawn down as if by premature age, his feeble limbs carrying him forward into the unknown.

A shadow descended upon a noble mind, extinguishing the polar star that had once been his pure, unrivalled love for Margaret. With that light quenched, he drifted aimlessly across a gloomy sea of hopeless expectation. Yet, his torment was not merely the paralysis of despair; it was compounded by a searing exasperation at the sheer waste of his own self-denial, fortitude, peril, and virtue. Every act of restraint burned like a brand, stinging him with the bitter realization that had he chosen the lazy, selfish comfort of home, he might have saved Margaret's life. These twin poisons, raging in the fevered blood of his youth, drove him mad and dismantled his morality. He rushed furiously into pleasure, diving headlong into vice as a drowning man lunges blindly into the enemy's gunfire.

In those days, pleasure was synonymous with destruction. Wine, women, gambling—whatever could procure an hour of frenzied excitement or a fleeting moment of oblivion—he consumed with reckless abandon. The substantial savings he had amassed for Margaret now funded his debauchery, and he quickly ascended to leadership among a circle of loose companions he had previously kept at arm's length. His heart rotted alongside his morals. He sulked violently against his old landlady for offering gentle counsel, eventually moving to a distant quarter of the city to escape both remonstrance and painful memories. As time wore on, his hand grew unsteady; the pen, once his faithful confidant, betrayed him. His patience eroded as the habits of indulgence took root, leading him to abandon painting in ink for portraiture in color. Yet, even this craft was abandoned whenever his idle cronies summoned him. Thus, he dove deeper into foul waters, chasing the hollow shell of oblivion.

It is not my duty to chronicle in minute detail the coarse vices in which this unfortunate young man now wallowed. My sole purpose is to establish the undeniable truth: he had become a rake, a debauchee, a drunkard, and one of the most wicked youths in Rome. Those who conceal or soften the wickedness of the virtuous, robbing the despondent sinner of hope through their lack of candor, are no lovers of truth or mankind. Enough said: the man was not born to do things by halves, nor was his vice partial.

News of his decline rippled through the city. His humble female acquaintances gossiped freely; his old landlady informed Teresa that he was going to the devil, praying her to locate him. Teresa relayed the tragic tale to her husband, Lodovico, urging him to search for the young man's current whereabouts. "Do you remember his face, Lodovico mio?" she asked. "Teresa, a man in my line of work never forgets a face, least of all a benefactor's. But you know I rarely venture abroad by daylight." Teresa sighed. "And how long will this continue, Lodovico?" "Until some cavalier drives his sword through me," he replied grimly. "They will not allow a poor fellow like me to pursue any honest trade."

Pietro Vanucci, who bore prosperity far worse than adversity, was one such figure. Having been ignominiously ejected by his landlady for keeping late hours, he encountered Gerard in the street. Despite Gerard's apparent transformation, Vanucci greeted him warmly and shortly thereafter secured lodging in the same house. He arrived accompanied by a lad named Andrea, who ground his colors and served as both pupil and model—a youth of rare beauty, sharp as a needle. Vanucci claimed a warm friendship for Gerard, though Gerard, in whom all warmth of sentiment seemed extinguished, accepted this overture with icy reserve. What a strange alliance it proved to be: Vanucci, a libertine, half-misanthrope, and open infidel, paired with a man who had lost his soul. They wandered the streets as unlikely companions, united by nothing but circumstance.

One day, having undermined his health, stripped his beauty, and squandered most of his fortune, Vanucci organized a raucous expedition up the Tiber in a boat drawn by buffaloes. Lorenzo de' Medici had introduced these beasts to Florence three years prior, but they were novelties in Rome. Nothing satisfied Vanucci, the "beggar on horseback," except to be pulled upstream by the brutes. Each libertine was required to bring a lady, who must be handsome, lest he pay a fine. The bearer of the loveliest damsel would be crowned with laurel and hailed as a public benefactor—a twisted interpretation of *vir bonus est quis?* They procured a splendid galley and twelve buffaloes, and soon the libertines and their female accomplices gathered at the embarkation point. But Gerard was absent.

They waited, first with patience, then with impatience. Vanucci offered an excuse: "I heard him say he forgot to procure a fardingale. Comrades, the good lad is hunting for a beauty worthy to stand among these peerless dames. Consider the difficulty, ladies, and be patient!" Finally, Gerard appeared in the distance, holding a female by the hand. "She is long enough," critiqued one woman from afar. "Gemini! What strides she takes!" exclaimed another. "Oh, it is wise to hasten into good company," Vanucci muttered, feigning concern.

When the pair approached, however, satire choked in their throats. Gerard's companion was a peerless beauty who extinguished the boatload like the rising sun swallows the stars. She was tall yet not towering, straight as a dart yet supple as a young panther. Her face was a perfect oval, her forehead pale, her cheeks a rich olive flushed with eloquent blood, and her glorious eyes framed by long, thick silken lashes seemingly designed to sweep up sensitive hearts. Her lips were saucy red, her teeth the whitest ivory. The women were visibly depressed by this wretched sight; the men were in ecstasy. They welcomed her with loud shouts and waving caps, and one enthusiast even knelt upon the boat's gunwale, hailng her of divine origin. Only his mistress, pulling his hair, and the goddess delivering a swift kick, stopped him. He lay supine, and the peerless creature frisked over his body without deigning him a glance, taking her seat at the prow. Pietro Vanucci sat in a collapse, glaring at her, his mouth agape like a dying codfish.

The drover cracked the reins, the ropes tightened, and the buffaloes moved upstream. "What think ye of this new beef, mesdames?" Vanucci boomed. "We ne'er saw monsters so vilely ill-favored," retorted a lady, shuddering at their nasty horns and foul nostrils held high. "Holes be they, not nostrils." "Signorina, the beeves are a present from Florence the beautiful. Would you look a gift beef in the nose?" queried another. "They are so dull," objected a lively lady. "I went up the Tiber twice as fast last time with but five mules and an ass." "Nay, that is soon mended," cried a gallant. Jumping ashore, he drew his sword and, ignoring the drivers' protests, charged the dozen buffaloes, goading them forward.

The animals snorted and whipped their tails but refused to accelerate. The boatload erupted in laughter. Finally, he goaded a patriarchal bull who instantly turned on the sword. With a furious jerk of his prodigious neck, the bull sent the man flying over his head. The spark described a bold parabola and crashed sitting, unconsciously waving his glittering blade, into the yellow Tiber. The laughing ladies screamed and wrung their hands—all but Gerard's fair companion. She uttered something resembling an oath, seized the helm, and steered the boat away. The gallant surfaced sputtering, gripped the gunwale, and was dragged aboard, dripping.

He glared around him, confused. "I understand not that," he said, peevishly, puzzled and thus discontented. Finding he was not slain, but rather his doublet perforated, they began to laugh louder than ever. "What are ye cackling at?" the spark demanded. "I desire to know how 'tis that one moment a gentleman is out yonder pricking African beef, and the next moment—" Gerard's lady interjected, "Disporting in his native stream." "Tell him not, a soul of ye," cried Vanucci. "Let him find out his own riddle." "Confound ye all," the spark lamented. "I might puzzle my brains till doomsday and ne'er find it out. Also, where is my sword?" "Ask Tiber!" the lady replied. "Your best way, signor, will be to do it over again; and, in a word, keep pricking African beef until your mind receives light. So shall you comprehend the matter by degrees, as lawyers mount heaven and buffaloes ascend the Tiber."

A chevalier remarked that the last speaker transcended the sons of Adam in wit as much as she did the daughters of Eve in beauty. At this, and indeed at all their compliments, Pietro Vanucci's behavior became peculiar. He ceased his staring and gaping bewilderment, coiling up snake-like on the bench, his mouth muffled, two bright eyes fixed on the lady, twinkling and scintillating most comically. Yet, he failed to interest or amuse her in return. Her glorious eyes and lashes swept him calmly at times, scarcely distinguishing him from the benches and things.

Soon

In a chamber draped in the opulent textures of wealth, where the floor was paved with the striped and spotted hides of beasts, a woman sat poised in a tableau of torment. Her arms stretched rigidly forward, fingers curled into claws of tension, mirroring the violent oscillation of her countenance. One moment her pallor suggested death, the next a flush of crimson burned across her cheeks, while her foot tapped a frantic, restless rhythm against the ground. Suddenly, the heavy curtain parted, drawn by a silent servant. The woman's brow flared with heat. The maid, trembling in awe, whispered, "Altezza, the man is here."

With a sharp command to admit him, the lady seized a small black mask and pressed it over her face. Instantly, the color drained from her visage, leaving a stark, spectral contrast between the jet-black fabric and the marble-white skin beneath—a sight both strange and terrifying. A man entered, bowing and scraping with the mechanical precision of a crowd-made figure: short hair, a roundish head, dressed plainly yet decently, possessing features neither handsome nor forbidding. There was nothing remarkable about him save for a pair of eyes that darted with singular, unsettling restlessness.

After a profusion of deferential bows, he halted opposite the lady, awaiting her pleasure. "They have told you for what you are wanted?" she asked.
"Yes, Signora."
"Did those who spoke to you agree as to what you are to receive?"
"Yes, Signora. 'Tis the full price; and purchases the greater vendetta: unless of your benevolence you choose to content yourself with the lesser."

"I understand you not," the lady replied, her voice cool despite the fever in her veins.
"Ah; this is the Signora's first lesson," the man corrected smoothly. "The lesser vendetta claims the death of the body only. We watch our man emerge from a church, or seize him in an innocent hour, and so deal with him. In the greater vendetta, however, we watch him, catching him hot from some unrepented sin, and thus slay his soul as well as his body. But this vendetta is not so run upon now as it was a few years ago."

"Man, silence me his tongue, and let his treasonable heart beat no more. But his soul I have no feud with."
"So be it, Signora. He who spoke to me knew not the man, nor his name, nor his abode. From whom shall I learn these?"
"From myself."

At this, the man displayed the first flicker of anxiety he had shown since entering. He entreated her to be cautious, particular, in this delicate part of the business.
"Fear me not," she said, her tone dropping to a dangerous whisper. "Listen. It is a young man, tall of stature, with auburn hair and dark blue eyes, and an honest face that would deceive a saint. He lives in the Via Claudia, at the corner house—the glover's. In that house lodge but three males: he; a painter short of stature and dark-visaged; and a young, slim boy. He that hath betrayed me is a stranger, fair, and taller than thou art."

The bravo listened with every sense alert, his restless eyes narrowing.
"It is enough," he murmured. "Stay, Signora; haunteth he any secret place where I may deal with him?"
"My spy doth report me he hath of late frequented the banks of the Tiber after dusk," she continued, her voice rising with venomous delight. "Doubtless to meet his light o' love, who calls me her rival; even there slay him! And let my rival come and find him; the smooth, heartless, insolent traitor."
"Be calm, Signora. He will betray no more ladies."
"I know not that. He weareth a sword, and can use it. He is young and resolute."
"Neither will avail him."
"Are ye so sure of your hand? What are your weapons?"

The bravo produced a steel gauntlet. "We strike with such force we need must guard our hand. This is our mallet." He then undid his doublet, revealing a coat of mail beneath, and finally laid his glittering stiletto on the table with a flourish. The lady shuddered at first, but presently she took it up in her white hand and tested its point against her finger.
"Beware, madam," warned the bravo.
"What? Is it poisoned?"
"Saints forbid! We steal no lives. We take them with steel point, not drugs. But 'tis newly ground, and I feared for the Signora's white skin."
"His skin is as white as mine," she said, a sudden, fleeting gleam of pity crossing her features. It vanished as quickly as it arrived. "But his heart is black as soot. Say, do I not well to remove a traitor that slanders me?"
"The Signora will settle that with her confessor. I am but a tool in noble hands; like my stiletto."

The princess appeared not to hear the speaker. Lost in the labyrinth of her own emotions, she muttered, "Oh, how I could have loved him; to the death; as now I hate him. Fool! He will learn to trifle with princes; to spurn them and fawn on them, and prefer the scum of the town to them, and make them a by-word." She looked up, her eyes blazing. "Why loiter'st thou here? Haste thee, revenge me."
"It is customary to pay half the price beforehand, Signora."
"Ah! I forgot; thy revenge is bought. Here is more than half," she said, pushing a heavy bag across the table to him. "When the blow is struck, come for the rest."
"You will soon see me again, Signora."

And he retired, bowing and scraping until he disappeared. The princess, burning with jealousy, mortified pride, and a profound dread of exposure—for until she knew Gerard, no public stain had fallen on her—sat where he left her. Masked, with her arms straight out before her and the nails of her clenched hand nipping the hard surface of the table, she resembled the fabled Sphinx. Or rather, a tigress coiled in ambush.

Yet, as the assassin faded from view, a chilling realization crept upon her. Moody misgivings heaved within her breast, precursors of vain remorse. *Gerard and Margaret were before their age,* she thought, the names echoing in the hollow room. *This was your true mediaeval.* Proud, amorous, vindictive, generous, foolish, cunning, impulsive, unprincipled—and ignorant as dirt. Power was the curse of such a creature. Forced to do her own crimes, the weakness of her nerves might have balanced the violence of her passions, making her bark worse than her bite. But power gave a feeble, furious woman male instruments. And the effect was as terrible as the combination was unnatural. In this instance, it had whetted an assassin's dagger for a poor, forlorn wretch just meditating suicide.

Two days after the turmoil I have sought to depict, Gerard wandered through one of Rome's most squalid thoroughfares, where the air itself seemed thick with the city's moral rot. A thunderstorm broke overhead, forcing him into a low, shadowed hostelry that smelled of stale beer and desperation. He ordered wine, and as the deluge continued outside, the alcohol quickly clouded his judgment, lulling him into a stupor. He dozed, his head resting heavily upon his hands, which were folded upon the scarred wooden table.

Time blurred until the room grew crowded with roughshod strangers, their raucous laughter and coarse speech tearing through the haze of his intoxication. It was then that his consciousness sharpened, drawn to the hushed, conspiratorial murmur of two figures seated nearby. One was a pardoner, a man whose very presence was a license to sin. Beside him sat another, dressed in garments that appeared clean yet modest enough to pass for a respectable tradesman. Yet, the manner in which he had slumped his wide-brimmed hat over his brow, obscuring every feature save for a bristling beard, betrayed his nature; he was a man who actively shunned the gaze of honest citizens and the scrutiny of the law.

Together, they were negotiating a transaction in the black market of salvation. Under the celestial contracts of that era, the crime to be absolved had not yet been committed—a loophole allowing the purchase of forgiveness before the act. The man with the slouched hat fretted over the escalating cost of these spiritual commodities. "If prices rise any further," he grumbled, "we poor souls shall be locked out of heaven entirely."

The pardoner dismissed his anxiety with a flat denial. "Indulgences have never been more affordable for prudent stewards of their souls."

"Who were they?" the buyer pressed, seeking clarity on the beneficiaries of such cheap grace.

"Why, those who sin according to the market logic of reasonable creatures," the pardoner replied, his tone dripping with condescension. "But should you be so perverse as to select a transgression the Pope has explicitly condemned, look to your own conscience, not mine!"

To demonstrate that even grave sins were within reach of all but the absolute dregs of society, the pardoner unrolled a parchment and recited the price list. It was a grotesque catalog of human misery, a document too vile for print here, yet preserved in the great libraries of history. Suffice it to say, the cost to extinguish a layman's life was remarkably lower than many offenses my readers might consider trivial. This revelation sealed the deal; with minor concessions from both sides, the transaction concluded. Money changed hands, and the aspiring soul was already absolved for the future murder of a commoner. The price for eliminating a cleric stood in stark, horrifying contrast. Not once did the word *assassination* leave either man's lips, sanitized by the euphemisms of commerce.

All this cacophony of vice drifted past Gerard's ears, but he remained buried beneath the table, his head unmoving, listening with a vacant, drunken stare. Only when the conspirators rose and dispersed did he lift his chin slightly, fixing a scowl upon the retreating silhouette of the purchaser.

"If Margaret lived," he muttered, the venom rising despite his foggy mind, "I would seize you by the throat and strangle you, you cowardly stabber. But she is dead. Dead. Dead. Let the whole world perish; it matters nothing to me. So let me die among the first."

When he finally stumbled home, a man wearing a slouched hat paced briskly back and forth on the opposite side of the street. "There goes that cur again," Gerard thought, his eyes narrowing. Yet, in his shattered mental state, the sight failed to register, leaving no mark upon his tormented soul.

Two nights following this event, Pietro Vanucci and Andrea sat in the dim chamber, awaiting supper for Gerard. The atmosphere thickened with tension until Pietro, growing peevish under the weight of uncertainty, glanced at the clock. It was well past nine o'clock. In a final, desperate gambit, he dispatched Andrea to Gerard's room, hoping against hope that the scholar had entered unnoticed. Andrea shrugged, accepting the mission with a mixture of duty and apprehension, and departed.

When he returned, Gerard was absent, but he carried something far heavier than a meal ticket: a folded slip of paper. Illiterate in the formal arts, as scholars of his era and charity boys of ours understand such distinctions, Andrea possessed a sharp visual acuity and had memorized the specific cadence of Pietro Vanucci's handwriting. "That is for you, I trow," he declared, swelling with a pride born of his limited but keen intelligence.

Pietro snatched the note, his eyes scanning the lines with a grim intensity before reading aloud, punctuated by biting satire intended to mask his dread. "'Dear Pietro, dear Andrea, life is too great a burden.'"

"So 'tis, my lad," Pietro muttered, though his voice lacked conviction, "but that is no justification for wandering abroad at supper-time. Supper is not a burden."

"'Wear my habits!'"

"Said the poplar to the juniper bush," Pietro retorted, forcing a laugh that died quickly in his throat. "'And thou, Andrea, mine amethyst ring; and me in both your hearts a month or two.'"

"Why, Andrea?" Pietro pressed, the irony failing to reach his eyes.

"'For my body, ere this ye read, it will lie in Tiber,'" Pietro continued, his tone shifting from mockery to a hollow recitation of doom. "'Trouble not to look for it. 'Tis not worth the pains. Oh unhappy day that it was born—oh happy night that rids me of it.'"

"'Adieu! adieu!'"

"'The broken-hearted Gerard.'"

Pietro set the paper down, dismissing it as "a sorry jest of the peevish rogue." Yet, the pallor of his cheeks and the violent trembling of his teeth betrayed the truth he tried so desperately to conceal.

Andrea, however, could not contain the storm within him. He filled the house with his anguished cries, his voice rising to a shrill crescendo. "O, miserable day! O, calamity of calamities! Gerard, my friend, my sweet patron! Help! help! He is killing himself! Oh, good people, help me save him!"

His alarm rippled through the household like wildfire. Soon, a crowd gathered, drawn by the sheer volume of his distress. But poor Andrea found himself powerless to animate their sluggish compassion. "Go down to the river?" someone suggested skeptically. "No. It is not their business." "What part of the river?" another countered. "It is a wild goose chase." The prevailing sentiment was rooted in superstition; it was ill-fortune to venture near the river after sunset, for too many ghosts haunted those banks throughout the long, watchful night.

Yet, a lackey who had been lingering opposite the house stepped forward, claiming he would accompany Andrea. This single offer sparked a chain reaction, turning three or four of the younger servants into a small, frantic band. Together, they moved toward the river.

As the lackey questioned Andrea, the boy, sobbing uncontrollably, recounted the letter and Gerard's increasingly erratic, moody behavior. The lackey, revealed to be a spy of Princess Claelia, understood the gravity of the situation immediately. Their Italian tongues flew rapidly, exchanging urgent details as they neared the Tiber. But the moment they felt the cool, damp air of the riverbank and caught the faint, metallic scent of the stream in the calm spring night, their conversation ceased abruptly. Silence descended upon them, heavy and ominous.

The moon hung high and clear in a cloudless sky, casting silver light that seemed to mock their desperation. Their footsteps echoed loudly on the stones, sounding like gunshots in the quiet. Their tongues were hushed, replaced by the ragged sound of breathing.

Suddenly, hurrying around a shadowed corner, they collided with a man. He froze, irresolute, at the sight of them. He was bareheaded, his dripping hair glistening unnaturally in the moonlight. With the next step, they saw his clothes were soaked through, clinging to his frame.

"Here he is!" cried one of the young men, unfamiliar with Gerard's face and figure.

The stranger turned instantly and fled. They chased him might and main, Andrea leading with reckless speed, the princess's lackey close behind. Andrea gained on him briefly, but in a heartbeat, the fugitive twisted into a narrow alleyway. Andrea shot past, unable to check his momentum, and the pursuers found themselves trapped in a labyrinthine maze. It was futile to chase a quick-footed fugitive who knew every inch of the city's underbelly; now, they could follow him only by the echo of his retreat.

Returning to their companions, they found them standing exactly where the man had paused, utterly confounded. "Are ye verily sure?" they demanded. "He had been in the river. Why, in the saints' names, did he flee at our approach?"

Then spoke Vanucci, his voice weary. "Friends, methinks this has nought to do with him we seek. What shall we do, Andrea?"

Here, the lackey interjected. "Let us track him to the water's side, to make sure. See, he hath come dripping all the way."

This advice was approved, and with little difficulty, they retraced the man's path. But soon, they encountered a new enigma. Having traveled scarcely fifty yards, the trail of water droplets veered sharply away from the river, directing them instead toward the gate of a large, gloomy building—a monastery. They stood irresolute before the dark pile, gazing at its imposing silhouette, sensing that it hid some terrible mystery.

Presently, Andrea let out a shout of realization. "Here be the drops again!" he cried. "And this road leadeth to the river."

They resumed the chase, and soon it became clear that the moisture was guiding them homeward. The track grew wetter and wetter, leading inexorably to the Tiber's edge. There, on the bank, a bucketful of water appeared to have been discharged directly from the stream.

At first, they shouted in triumph, believing they had made a discovery. But reflection quickly sobered them. It amounted to nothing. Certainly, a man had been in the water and emerged safely, but that man was not Gerard.

One of them recalled knowing a fisherman nearby who owned nets and drags. They located the fisherman and paid him liberally to sink nets in the river below the scene and drag them upstream and downstream, promising gold if he found the body.

Then, they ran vainly up and down the river, which flowed so calm and voiceless, guarding its strange secrets. Suddenly, Andrea, with a cry of renewed hope, sprinted back toward the house. He returned in less than half an hour.

"No," he groaned, wringing his hands in despair. "There is no sign."

"What is the hour?" asked the lackey, his face grave.

"Four hours past midnight," Andrea replied, his voice breaking.

"My pretty lad," said the lackey solemnly, placing a comforting hand on the boy's shoulder, "say a mass for thy friend's soul: for he is not among living men."

Morning broke, gray and indifferent. Worn out by fatigue and grief, Andrea and Pietro trudged home, their hearts sick with the certainty of loss. The days rolled on, mute as the Tiber itself, offering no answer regarding Gerard's fate.

It would have been a grotesque irony if the men who stood upon the riverbank, armed with such barren data, could have deciphered the handwriting etched into the mud beneath their feet. For upon that specific spot, an event had just transpired—a singular collision of fate so profound it stands without parallel in the annals of human history, destined perhaps to remain an unassimilated mystery until the final dissolution of time. Yet, the truth of this moment shall be recounted in stark brevity, woven from the threads of my own observation and the testimony of a participant in the tragedy.

Gerard, having penned his final, trembling adieu to Pietro and Andrea, descended to the river under the cloak of nightfall. His movements were dictated by a dogged, grim resolution, a psychological architecture common only to those who have resolved upon self-annihilation. With methodical precision, he stuffed his pockets with every ounce of silver and copper he possessed, weighting his soul with metal to ensure the certainty of his grave. Thus prepared, he hastened to a secluded bend of the stream, a place he had previously scouted for its isolation. While many, particularly the timid or the fearful, scan their surroundings to confirm the presence of a witness, this resolute wretch scanned the darkness solely to verify the absence of one.

To his mounting agitation, however, he detected a solitary silhouette leaning against the shadowed corner of an alleyway. Instinctively, he feigned a careless departure, strolling away with deliberate nonchalance, only to halt and return to the precise point of convergence. Indeed, the same figure emerged from a side street, drifting like a specter into the periphery. *Does he watch me?* Gerard wondered, a spike of paranoia piercing his despair. *Does he know the purpose of my pilgrimage?* He dismissed the notion as impossible, yet his heart hammered against his ribs. He moved briskly down the lane, navigated two streets, executed a wide detour, and circled back. The figure had vanished. But when Gerard swept his gaze across the moonlit expanse, certain once more, there he was—just a few yards behind, ostensibly adjusting the lacing of his boot.

Gerard realized immediately that he was being hunted. In the pale luminescence of the moon, he discerned a steel gauntlet resting in the sentinel's hand. The realization crystallized with icy clarity: this was an assassin. Strangely, the concept never crossed his mind that the life targeted was his own. He was unaware, blinded by circumstance, that he harbored an enemy in the world. Instead of flight, he turned and approached the bravo with a desperate, eager desperation.

"My good friend," Gerard cried out, his voice cracking with a plea born of madness, "sell me thine arm! A single stroke! Behold, here is all I possess;" and he thrust his handful of coin into the assassin's grasp. "Oh, pray thee! Pray thee! Perform one act of mercy, and liberate me from this hateful existence!" As he spoke, he undid his doublet, exposing his bare bosom to the cool air and the blade.

The man stared, unmoved by the offering or the vulnerability displayed. "Why do you hesitate?" Gerard shrieked, his composure shattering into fragments. "Do you lack compassion? Is the effort of lifting your arm too great? Is it because I am poor, unable to offer further gold? Wretched fool, you can only strike a man from behind; you dare not meet his gaze. Very well, then! Turn your head, shut your eyes, and hold your tongue!"

With a snarl of utter contempt, Gerard spun away from the stoic killer and hurled himself into the churning waters. "Margaret!"

At the heavy splash of his body breaking the surface tension, the bravo seemed to rouse from a trance-like stupor. He sprinted to the bank, driven by a strange, inexplicable compulsion, and plunged into the freezing current after the self-destroyer. What ensued next shall be chronicled by the assassin himself.

A woman carries her own burdens, distinct and heavy as any man's, yet the male pen, confined by its own narrow vision, rarely dares to map the labyrinth of female grief. We sketch the outline; it is the intelligence of the female reader who must breathe life into these cold contours, filling the silence with the truth of experience. Thus, I have merely indicated, rather than exhaustively chronicled, the trials Margaret Brandt endured until that pivotal dawn when she stepped into Eli's house as an adversary, deciphered her lover's letter, and emerged transformed into a friend. Now, the zenith of a woman's torment approached, compounded by the crushing weight of Gerard's absence. She made scant use of Eli's sudden benevolence; for this reserve, she always possessed a plausible pretext, concealing the genuine terror that gripped her heart: two men resided under that roof whose very presence triggered an instinctive shudder of dread. In their visages, she had read the script of wickedness and hatred, detecting the flicker of secret intelligence between them. Consequently, she preferred the sanctuary of her own hearth, where Catherine and her daughter were welcomed daily. Catherine, consumed by the anticipated arrival of new life, embodied the archetype of the female multiplier, dedicated to the propagation and rearing of the multiplied. Once she ceased the relentless expulsion of infants from every corner of the household, she hovered protectively around the remaining young ones, clucking anxiously as if multiplying by proxy itself. It was in this fervent spirit that she implored Eli to remain in Rotterdam while she returned to Tergou. "The poor lass hath not a single soul about her who understands aught of consequence," she argued. "What utility is there in a pair of soldiers? Such cattle ought to be cast out the moment the season turns." Need I recount that this counsel provided Margaret with profound solace? Alas, the poor soul was teetering on the brink of annihilation, her heart torn between impending death and the void left by Gerard. Yet, the scales of fate balanced themselves. Her destitution and her father's utter helplessness—circumstances that had once subjected her to such agonizing struggle—now served as her shield. Adversity's iron fist had forged her immunity against the lethargy that often paralyzes women of wealth, compelling her to stand perpetually vigilant, her hands never idle. She maintained this grim vigilance until the final hour, buoyed solely by Catherine's counsel. Thus, on a radiant evening as the sun dipped below the horizon, she lay weak as water, yet secure; a small face rested beside her, and the celestial realm of maternity opened wide above her. "Why dost thou weep, sweetheart? Suddenly?" "He is not here to witness it." "Ah, well, lass, he shall be here ere the babe is weaned. Meanwhile, God hath dealt with thee as kindly as with any woman born. Consider, too, that it might have been a girl; did not my very own Kate threaten me with one? And here we possess the bonniest boy in Holland, a sturdy lad indeed, for which the saints give thanks." "Ay, mother, I am but a sorry, ungrateful wretch to weep. If only Gerard were here to behold it. It is strange; I bore him well enough to endure his absence amidst my sorrow, yet oh, it seems so cruel that he cannot share my joy. Prithee, prithee, come to me, Gerard! Dear, dear Gerard!" She stretched forth her feeble arms. Catherine bustled about, deliberately averting her gaze, unable to suppress the tears that welled at hearing her own absent child invoked with such earnest longing. Soon, turning back, she discovered Margaret regarding her with a singular, piercing expression. "Didst thou hear nothing?" "No, my lamb. What?" "I cried out for Gerard, but now..." "Ay, ay, surely I heard that." "Well, he answered me." "Tush, girl: speak not such nonsense." "Mother, as certain as I lie here with his boy at my side, his voice returned to me, 'Margaret!' So. Yet, methought, it lacked his usual cheerfulness. Perhaps the distance dulls all tones, making even the joyful sound melancholy. Why art thou not happy, sweetheart? And I, so filled with joy this night? Mother, I seem never to have felt a pang of pain or known a care." Her sweet eyes turned, gazing lovingly upon the infant in silent rapture. That very night, Gerard threw himself into the Tiber. And in that same hour, she heard him speak her name, crying aloud within the jaws of death and despair. "Margaret!" Let those who can account for it explain; I cannot.

In the shadowed sanctuary of a Dominican convent, a solitary figure lay prostrate within a guest chamber, his spirit nearly extinguished by the violent, recurring spasms of nausea that had finally ceased, leaving him as fragile and broken as Margaret had been in the Dutch lands. Upon the hearth, a massive wood fire crackled, casting dancing shadows against the walls where the patient's garments hung suspended. Beside the bed knelt a giant of a friar, his voice resonating with a low, rhythmic cadence as he recited pious collects from his breviary. At intervals, the sufferer opened weary eyes to meet the monk's gaze, listening intently; at others, he slumped back, surrendering to a silent, agonizing moan. The monk dropped to his knees, his forehead pressed to the stone floor in fervent supplication, before rising to bid the dying man adieu. "Day breaks," the friar announced softly. "I must prepare for matins." "Good Father Jerome," the stranger rasped, "before you depart, how did I arrive here?" "By the hand of Heaven itself," the monk replied, his tone grave yet comforting. "You cast aside God's gift; He has restored it to you. Reflect upon this! You have tested the world and found only bitterness. Now test the Church! The Church is peace. *Pax vobiscum*." With that, he departed.

Gerard lay back, his mind reeling in a haze of wonder until exhaustion claimed him, dragging him into a fitful doze. When consciousness returned, a new guardian sat vigilantly beside him—a layman whose eyes darted with a small, restless energy, a stark contrast to the calm majesty of Friar Jerome. The man inquired earnestly of his condition. "Extremely weak," Gerard murmured. "Where have I encountered you before, sir?" "No worse for my gauntlet?" the other asked, his anxiety palpable. "I was prepared to strike you down, or both of us would have perished at the bottom of the Tiber." Gerard fixed him with a stare. "What? Was it you who saved me? How?" "Indeed, signor," the man explained, his voice trembling slightly. "I stood by the banks of the Tiber on some errand of my own. You approached me, begging desperately for a dagger's stroke. Yet, even as you spoke, I recognized you—the very signor who rescued my wife and child upon the sea." "It is Teresa's husband... and an assassin?!" Gerard exclaimed, horror cutting through his weakness. "At your service. Well, Ser Gerard, the next moment you hurled yourself into the Tiber, commanding me to stand aside." "I recall that clearly." "If it had been anyone else, believe me, I would have obeyed without hesitation, not moving a muscle. Men are my enemies; they might all hang on a single rope or drown in one river for me. But when you, sinking beneath the current, cried out 'Margaret!'..." "Ah!" "My heart screamed 'Teresa!' How could I return home and face her, knowing I had allowed you to die, thereby condemning her too? So I entered the water. Fortunately for us both, I swim like a duck. Seeing me approach, intent on mutual destruction, you attempted to seize me, hoping to end us together. But I circled you, and—accept my apologies—I struck the nape of your neck with my steel-gloved fist. You lost consciousness, and with great effort, fighting the strong current, I dragged your body ashore, insensible and filled with water. I carried you on my back to my own dwelling. 'Teresa will nurse him,' I thought, 'and she will be pleased with me.' Hard by this monastery, however, a holy friar, the largest I have ever beheld, intercepted us and demanded an explanation. I recounted the tale. He scrutinized you closely. 'I know this face,' he declared. 'It is Gerard, a fair youth from Holland.' 'The same,' I confirmed. Then his reverence said, 'He has friends among our brethren. Leave him with us! Charity is our office.' He further informed me that the convent possessed far greater means to tend to you than I did, which proved true enough. Thus, I negotiated permission to visit you daily, and here you are." As he spoke, the man cast a look upon Gerard that blended strange affection with profound interest. Gerard recoiled internally, feeling as though a serpent coiled within the room. Closing his eyes, he dismissed the intruder. "Ah, you wish to sleep," the man observed eagerly. "Then I withdraw." He retreated on tiptoe, promising his daily return.

Gerard lay with his eyes shut, though not in sleep, but in a state of deep, tormented contemplation. Saved from death by an assassin—was this not the finger of Heaven pointing at him? A Heaven he had once insulted, cursed, and defiantly rejected. Shivers ran through him at the memory of his blasphemies. He attempted to pray, finding his lips capable of forming the words, yet his heart refused to offer true devotion. "I am doomed eternally," he wailed, the despair echoing in his chest. "Doomed, doomed." Suddenly, the organ of the convent church erupted in rich, solemn harmony, filling the air. Rising from the stalls, the choir began a full service, their voices weaving a tapestry of sound. Among them hovered a voice that soared above the rest, towering toward the heavens—a sweet boy's voice, full, pure, and angelic. Gerard closed his eyes, letting the melody wash over him. The days of his own boyhood flooded back, unburdened by the earth's dross, free from the foul, fierce passions that had rent and corrupted his soul. Only peace, sweet and balmy peace. "Yes," he sighed, tears welling in his eyes, "the Church is peace of mind. Until I left her bosom, I knew neither sorrow nor sin." And the poor, torn, worn creature wept. Just as his grief peaked, the sweet and reverend face of one he had not expected to see again beamed upon him. It was Father Anselm. The good father had arrived at the convent only the previous night. Gerard recognized him instantly and cried out, "Oh, Father Anselm! You healed my wounded body in Juliers; now heal my hurt soul in Rome!" "Alas," Anselm replied gently, "you cannot." Sitting by the bedside, he placed a tender hand upon Gerard's head, first calming him with soothing words. Having learned the circumstances of Gerard's arrival, he then spoke to him with kindness tempered by solemnity, making him acutely aware of his crime and urging him toward repentance and gratitude for the Divine Power that had thwarted his will to damn his soul. "Come, my son," Anselm said, "first purge your bosom of its burden." "Ah, father," Gerard confessed, "in Juliers I could; then I was innocent. But now, impious monster that I am, I dare not confess to you." "Why not, my son? Do you think I have not sinned against Heaven in my time, and deeply? Oh, how deeply! Come, poor laden soul, pour forth your grief, pour forth your faults, withhold nothing! Do not lie oppressed and crushed by hidden sins." Soon, Gerard was kneeling at Father Anselm's feet, confessing every sin with sighs and groans of genuine penitence. "Your sins are great," Anselm acknowledged. "Your temptation was also great, terribly great. I must consult our good prior regarding your penance." The good Anselm kissed his brow and withdrew to confer with the superior. And lo! Gerard could pray now. He prayed with all the fervor of his heart. The phase through which this remarkable mind now passed may be summarized in a single word: Penitence. He turned with terror and revulsion from the world, begging passionately to remain within the convent. To him, the cloister was like a wounded bird returning, weary and battered, to its gentle nest. He passed his novitiate immersed in prayer, mortification, pious reading, and meditation. Meanwhile, the Princess Claelia's spy returned home to report that Gerard was certainly dead, the manner of his passing currently unknown. She seemed literally stunned. When, after a long interval, she found breath to speak, it was to lament her fate, cursed with such ready instruments of evil. "So soon," she sighed, "see how swiftly these monsters commit ill deeds. They come to us in our hot blood, first tempting us with their venal daggers, then enacting the mortal deeds we never dared conceive but for them." Within mere hours, her pity for Gerard and her hatred of his murderer rose to a fever pitch, indistinguishable from the fool's own blood heat. "Poor soul! I cannot call you back to life. But he shall never live, that traitor who slew you treacherously." She immediately deployed armed men into ambush, keeping them on guard throughout the day, ready to fall upon Lodovico in a specific antechamber the moment he came for his payment, hacking him to pieces. "Strike at his head," she commanded, "for he wears a private coat of mail; and if he escapes hence alive, your own heads shall answer for it." Thus she sat weeping over her victim, pulling the strings of machines designed to shed the blood of a second, having herself become the instrument to kill the first.

To truly comprehend the architecture of Gerard's redemption, one must first confront the visceral horror of his initial encounter with Lodovico. It was a penance born of self-imposed necessity, yet tainted by a revulsion so potent it felt akin to handling a slimy serpent. Yet, in the economy of grace, there is no greater sacrifice than the denial of one's natural instincts; and like all rational acts of contrition, this abhorrence quickly transmuted into acceptance. At first, the assassin's gaze upon the single life he had spared was a grotesque tableau—a mixture of ludicrous arrogance and pathetic vulnerability. But to open a door in a hardened heart is to invite a flood; once the aperture is breached, light inevitably pours through. Finding the act of preserving life strangely sweet, the miscreant gradually grew averse to extinguishing it. From this aversion sprang remorse, and from remorse, a nascent, trembling penitence.

Teresa, ever vigilant, reinforced this transformation. She threatened, not for the first time, to withdraw her presence entirely unless he vowed to lead an honest life. The good fathers of the convent lent their weight to this moral realignment, sending Lodovico and Teresa by sea to Leghorn, where Teresa held friends, while the assassin settled into the mundane rhythm of a porter. Initially, he confessed the work to be miserably dull, a far cry from the adrenaline-fueled chaos of his past. But methinks this drudgery of plodding labor was infinitely superior to the brief, violent excitement of being hewn in pieces by the Princess Claelia's myrmidons. His exile had saved the unconscious penitent from that gruesome fate; conversely, the princess, balked in her quest for vengeance, succumbed to a profound, brooding melancholy. She dismissed her old confessor and summoned a new one renowned for his piety, entrusting to him what she termed her "griefs." This new confessor was none other than Fra Jerome. She could scarcely have fallen into worse—or rather, better—hands.

Fra Jerome listened to her grievances with grim detachment, then seized her by the shoulders and shook the delusions from her mind with the rough efficiency due a kitchen-maid. For, on the path of divine duty, he feared neither the wrath of princes nor the fury of the sea. In a torrent of thunder and lightning, he exposed her as the criminal among criminals. "Thou art the devil," he declared, his voice cracking like a whip, "who with thy gold hast tempted one man to slay his fellow, and then, blinded by self-love, instead of blaming and punishing thyself, art thirsting for more blood of guilty men, though theirs is not so foul as thine."

At first, she resisted, claiming she was unaccustomed to being reprimanded by her confessors. But Jerome overpowered her spirit. He threatened her with the Church's curse, both temporal and eternal, tearing the scales from her eyes until she sank down, groveling in remorse and terror at the feet of this gigantic Boanerges. "Oh, holy father, have pity on a poor, weak woman," she cried, "and help me save my guilty soul. I was benighted for want of ghostly counsel like thine, good father. I wake as from a nightmare."

"Doff thy jewels," commanded Fra Jerome sternly.

"I will. I will."

"Doff thy silk and velvet; and clad in humbler garb than thy meanest servant, wend thou instant to Loretto."

"I will," said the princess, her voice faint.

"No shoes; but a bare sandal."

"No, father."

"Wash the feet of pilgrims, both those departing and those arriving. And to such as be holy friars, tell thy sin, and abide their admonition."

"Oh, holy father, let me wear my mask."

"Humph!"

"Oh, mercy! Bethink thee! My features are known throughout Italy."

"Aye. Beauty is a curse to most of ye. Well, thou mayst mask thine eyes; no more."

On this reluctant concession, she seized his hand, intending to kiss it, but he snatched it away with rude force. "What would ye do? That hand handled the Eucharist but an hour ago; is it fit for such as thou to touch it?"

"Ah, no. But oh, go not without giving your penitent daughter your blessing."

"Time enough to ask it when thou comest back from Loretto."

Thus, that marvelous occurrence by the banks of the Tiber left its indelible mark upon all the actors, much as prodigies are said to do. The assassin, softened by the miracle of having saved the life he was paid to take, turned from the stiletto to the porter's knot. The princess went barefoot to Loretto, weeping her crime and washing the feet of the base-born. And Gerard, carried from the Tiber into that convent as a would-be suicide, now passed for a young saint within its walls. Eyes loving yet experienced watched over him. Upon a probation shorter than usual, he was admitted to priest's orders. Soon thereafter, he took the monastic vows and became a friar of St. Dominic. Dying to the world, the monk parted with the very name by which he had lived in it, thereby severing the final link to earthly feelings. Here Gerard ended, and Brother Clement began.

"As is the race of leaves, so is that of men." In the quiet, unassuming shadow of a tailor's workshop in Rotterdam, a great soul budded unnoticed this year, while simultaneously, a colossal figure crashed to earth with deafening fanfare. At Bruges, Philip, Duke of Burgundy, Earl of Holland, and myriad other titles, lay wasting away in fever. Here, nature allowed the poor to fall ill and recover with equal indifference; but woe betide the wealthy in an era where a single Mr. Malady could decimate three, while Dr. Remedy struggled to save one. The Duke's affliction, unnamed in those days, is now known as diphtheria—a cruel, weakening scourge that claimed the old man. Consequently, Dr. Remedy resorted to the ancient, bloody art of leeching. The Duke turned icy cold, a testament to the failure of mercy. Desperate, the physician turned to the arcane sciences of his time. "Ho! This is grave," he cried, demanding, "Flay an ape incontinent and clamp it to the Duke's breast!" State officers scrambled in every direction, seeking a primate to counteract the bloodthirsty folly of their own kind. Perdition! No apes remained. Buffaloes, lizards, Turks, and leopards roamed free, but none were the requisite beast. "Why, there used to be an ape about," one officer muttered. "If I stood here, I saw him." Indeed, there had been; but a mastiff, driven by hunger, had mauled the sprightly creature for stealing its supper, fulfilling the grim human precept: *Be of your season*. In this emergency, the seneschal cast his despairing eyes around the room, and hope flickered. "Here is this," he whispered, holding up a specimen. "Surely this will serve: 'tis altogether apelike, doublet and hose apart." "Nay," snapped the Chancellor peevishly, "the Princess Marie would hang us. She dotes on this." Thus spoke our friend Giles, strutting unconsciously in cloth of gold, oblivious to the gravity of the moment. Dr. Remedy, growing impatient with the bureaucracy of life and death, commanded, "Flay a dog." "A dog is next best to an ape," he reasoned, adding the condition, "only it must be a dog of one color." They skinned a liver-colored hound and pinned its still-palpating heart to the sovereign's chest. The Duke died. Philip the Good, thus scientifically disposed of, left behind thirty-one children; of these, one, by some miracle of lineage, was legitimate and ascended to rule. The benevolent duke had provided for nineteen of the others; the remainder were forced to shift for themselves. According to the Flemish chronicles, the deceased prince descended from the kings of Troy through Thierry of Aquitaine, Chilperic, Pharamond, and the ancient monarchs of Franconia. Yet, this genealogy held no true distinction. Not a single prince of his day can be discovered who does not claim descent from Troy. "Priam" was merely medieval code for "Adam." The Duke's body was carried into Burgundy and interred in a noble mausoleum of black marble at Dijon. Holland rang with mourning for his passing, little dreaming that within its borders, something as famous as a new sun was being born that same year. That judgment has since been utterly reversed. All eyes now turn to the tailor's house, the site of the great birth of the fifteenth century. As for the house where the good duke breathed his last, "no one knows and no one cares," as the song goes. Why? For Dukes like Philip the Good come and go, leaving mankind neither a halfpenny wiser, nor better, nor different than they found it. But when, once in three hundred years, such a child is born to the world as Margaret's son, behold! A human torch ignited by fire from heaven; and "FIAT LUX"—Let there be Light—thunders from pole to pole.

To understand the trajectory of Clement's soul, one must first grasp the shifting tectonic plates of the ecclesiastical world in which he moved. The Dominicans, once the towering giants of European spirituality, were crumbling under the relentless pressure of their rivals, the Franciscans. Even in England, a land steeped in piety where the Black Friars had once ruled supreme, the tide had turned. In response, the order's leadership, driven by a desperate urgency to reclaim their dominion, sought to forge weapons of unparalleled zeal. They identified Clement as such a weapon, drawn to his rare linguistic mastery—a polyglot fluent in Latin, Italian, French, and both High and Low Dutch—and dispatched him to England to preach.

Yet, the path of the saint is rarely a straight line. Jerome, the shadow behind the throne of the Superior, intercepted this destiny. With the cunning of a guardian dog protecting a vulnerable pup, Jerome argued against sending the novice abroad. "Clement," he intoned, his voice dripping with feigned concern, "still carries the milk of the world in his veins. Let not his newborn zeal or his fragile humility tempt us to discard our ancient wisdom. Temper him first. Test him, lest one day we find ourselves leaning on a reed for a staff."

The Prior, recognizing the strategic necessity of the test, acquiesced. "It is well advised," he conceded, handing the burden to Jerome. "Take him in hand yourself."

Thus began the crucible. Jerome led Clement not to the pulpit, but to the muddy periphery of society. He dragged him to fields where youths played, knowing these grounds were haunted by the ghosts of Clement's past friends. Sure enough, Pietro Vanucci and Andrea appeared, casting casual glances at the two monks. They failed to recognize the dead friend in the shaven head of the friar. Clement flinched, a micro-tremor of recognition, before lowering his eyes to recite a *paternoster*.

"Would you not speak with them, brother?" Jerome probed, watching for a spark of attachment.

"No, brother," Clement replied, his voice steady despite the internal storm. "Yet it was good for me to see them. They remind me of the sins I can never repent enough."

Satisfied with the detachment, Jerome filed a cold report in favor of the novice. But the trials escalated from psychological probing to physical purgatory. Jerome subjected Clement to deathbed vigils, forcing him to confront the finality of mortality, and then thrust him into dungeons where the air itself seemed to rot. These were places of appalling darkness and pestilential stench, where men resembling wild beasts coiled in rags and despair. The trial was grueling; Clement's body suffered, yet his soul gathered every ounce of its power to offer comfort to the wretched, shining with a radiance that defied the filth. Jerome reported back: "His spirit is willing, but his flesh is weak."

"Good!" declared Anselm. "His flesh is weak, but his spirit is willing."

However, the ultimate test awaited, one witnessed by the chaotic theater of Rome itself. One morning, the city's principal streets were choked with a sea of heads, the avenues blocked by the anticipation of violence. It was an execution, but not for a common crime. The Governor of Rome had been slaughtered in his sleep at dawn. His hand, severed at the wrist, lay beside him alongside a throat slit clean and precise, his temples bruised by a blunt instrument. The murder was traced to his servant, and justice demanded a retribution as bloody as the act.

The criminal was dragged to the house of the victim and pinned to the wall for thirty minutes, a grotesque preview of the law's vengeance. Then, his left hand was hacked off, mirroring the governor's. A freshly killed bird was slit open and stuffed onto the bleeding stump—a macabre offering of some forgotten medical theory—before the mutilated man was hurried to the scaffold. There, a young friar knelt, praying with an earnest, affectionate fervor, pressing a crucifix close to the condemned man's eyes.

Suddenly, the executioner shoved the friar aside. With a heavy mallet, he struck the culprit, and immediately drove a blade from ear to ear. A cry of horror erupted from the crowd. The young friar swooned, collapsing into unconsciousness. A gigantic monk stepped forward, seizing the fainting youth and carrying him away like a child.

Brother Clement returned to the convent, his spirit crushed by the spectacle. He confessed to the Prior, tears streaming down his face. "Courage, son Clement," the Prior urged. "A Dominican is not forged in a day. You shall have another trial. And I forbid you to approach it fasting."

Clement bowed, accepting the mandate. He had not long to wait. A robber, a monster of wanton cruelty who had slain men for sheer malice, was brought to the scaffold. Clement spent his last night in prison with the man, accompanying him to the platform. He prayed with such intensity that the hardened ruffian wept and embraced him. Clement, though his flesh quivered with revulsion, reciprocated the embrace, holding the crucifix firmly before the dying man's eyes. As the rope tightened and the dark spirit departed, Clement continued to pray aloud until the hangman raised his hatchet. The crowd, indifferent to the living man's suffering, uttered piteous cries at each blow upon the corpse, feeling nothing. Clement shuddered but stood firm, immovable as a rock vibrating in the wind.

Then, Jerome's voice cut through the air. "Brother Clement, mount that cart and preach to the people. Quickly! Strike with all your force upon this iron while it is still hot, and save these souls!"

Clement's complexion drained and flushed in rapid succession; his breath hitched. Yet, he obeyed. With uncertain steps, he climbed the cart and faced the first crowd he would ever address. That sea of upturned faces seemed to dry his throat, hammer his heart, and shake his voice. But gradually, the magnitude of the occasion ignited within him. The sight of the eager multitude, coupled with his own surging zeal, transformed the pale monk. He recounted the robber's history, fresh from the prison cell, using the man's fate to illustrate the deadly rungs of folly and crime, warning them solemnly not to place their foot on the first step.

Alternating between thunderous denunciations of bloodshed and moving appeals to charity, his tremors vanished. He felt himself strung tight like a lute, gifted with an unsuspected power. He mastered the listening crowd, sensing their very pulse, playing sacred melodies upon them as if they were strings on a psaltery. Sobs and groans confirmed his sway over the mob, already stirred by the preceding tragedy. Jerome stared, bewildered, like a man watching a rocket launch unexpectedly. When Clement noticed the lack of approval in Jerome's gaze, he abruptly stopped and descended.

"It was my first endeavor," Clement apologized humbly. "Your command came upon me like a thunderbolt. Did I succeed? Correct me, and aid me with your experience, Brother Jerome."

"Hmph," Jerome murmured dubiously. After a long pause, he added sullenly, "Give the glory to God, Brother Clement. My opinion is that you are an orator born."

He relayed this assessment to headquarters, albeit with reluctance, for he was an honest friar, however disagreeable.

Soon, another trial arose involving Julio Antonelli, accused of sacrilege. Three witnesses placed him at the scene of the theft, while others provided a solid alibi. Caught in this contradiction, Antonelli was granted the trial by water. The clergy, hoping for his acquittal, recommended the hot water ordeal—immersing an arm in boiling water to retrieve a pebble—which was considered less terrifying than the alternative. But Antonelli lacked the nerve and chose the cold trial.

Jerome seized this opportunity to steel Clement further. Antonelli received the sacrament, was stripped naked on the banks of the Tiber, and bound hand and foot to prevent struggling, which would cause him to sink. He was lowered gently into the stream, floating momentarily with only his hair above the surface. A simultaneous roar from the crowds on either bank declared him guilty. But seconds later, the new ropes soaked in water grew heavy, dragging him down. Another roar proclaimed his innocence. Left submerged for the appointed time, he was drawn up gurgling, gasping, and screaming for mercy. After the requisite prayers, he was released, cleared of all charges.

On the bank, Clement had prayed earnestly throughout the experiment. Upon completion, he thanked God in a loud, slightly wavering voice. Later, he turned to Jerome. "Should not the man be compensated?"

"For what?" Jerome asked.

"For the pain, the dread, the suffocation. Poor soul, he lives, but has tasted all the bitterness of death. Yet he committed no ill."

"He is rewarded enough in being cleared of his fault," Jerome countered.

"But being innocent of that fault, he has nonetheless drunk Death's cup, though not to the dregs," Clement persisted. "And his accusers, less innocent than he, suffer naught."

Jerome replied sternly, "It is not in this world that men are truly punished, Brother Clement. Unhappy are those who sin yet escape suffering. Happy are those who endure such ills as earth can inflict; it is counted to them above, yes, a hundredfold."

Clement bowed his head submissively. "May your good words not fall to the ground, but take root in my heart

Catherine is not an unmixed blessing within a stranger's domain; her governing spirit is potent enough to transmute the very atmosphere of a home. Scarce does she cross the threshold before the utensils gleam with renewed vigor, the hearth sweeps itself clean, the windows dilate to swallow more light, and the soul of an enormous cricket animates the dwelling. Yet this cricket is a relentless Busy Body—a title that carries the weight of tyranny. Possessing no discrimination, it seeks to rectify everything and everyone. While many things improve under such correction, nothing improves universally; indeed, everything resists being set aright save for the final, silent sanctuary of the grave.

In Margaret's house, Catherine rearranged every chair and table, perhaps for the better, yet her ambition soon escalated to overturning the living furniture itself. As Margaret's confinement drew near, Catherine treacherously summoned the aid of Denys and Martin. When the poor, simple-minded men earnestly inquired what service they could render, she directed them to a brief walk. Thus far, acceptable. But she hinted that extending this promenade into the middle of the following week would be even preferable. This was hardly ingratiation. The subsequent behavior of the strong under the yoke of the weak might have appeased a she-bear with three cubs, one of whom was already sickly. Generally, they slipped out at daybreak and returned like thieves in the night; if caught indoors, they moved about like cats walking on a wall tipped with broken glass, wearing expressions of awe-struck subjugation and a pervasive air of depression. Yet, all was insufficient. Their mere presence was ill-timed, jarring against Catherine's frayed nerves. Did instinct whisper that a pair of depopulators had no business in a house housing multipliers twain? The breastplate offers no armor against a female tongue, and Catherine drove infinite pins and needles of speech into them.

In short, when Margaret descended the stairs, she found the kitchen swept of heroes. Martin, old and stiff, had retreated no farther than the street, carrying off his baggage—a stool—and sitting upon it in the open air. Margaret saw he was exposed to the sun but failed to realize he had become a fixture within that luminary. Seeking Denys, she declared, "Good, kind Denys; he will be right pleased to see me about again." Catherine, wiping a bowl with now superfluous vigor, informed her that Denys had departed for his friends in Burgundy. "And high time! He hasn't been nigh them this three years, by all accounts." "What, gone without bidding me farewell?" Margaret asked, lifting two tender eyes like full-blown violets. Catherine reddened; this new perspective pricked her conscience. Yet, giving a little toss, she dismissed the guilt: "Oh, you were asleep at the time; and I would not have you wakened."

"Poor Denys," Margaret sighed, and visible dew gathered on her open violets. Catherine, observing from the corner of her eye without overt acknowledgment, turned her attention to the surviving depopulator. Suddenly, she lavished hospitality and tenderness upon Martin, old and stiff in more ways than one. "No, thank you, dame," he replied. "I have got used to out o' doors. And I love not changing and changing. I meddle wi' nobody here; and nobody meddles wi' me." "Oh, you nasty, cross old wretch!" Catherine screamed, passing in a moment from treacle to the sharpest vinegar, before flouncing back into the house.

On calm reflection, she allowed herself a little cry. Then, half-reconciling herself to her conduct, she vowed to be so kind that Margaret should never miss her plagues of soldiers. Feeling still uneasy, she dispensed all regrets through a process both simple and sovereign: she took the child and washed him. From head to foot, she scrubbed him in tepid water, and heroes, along with their grievances, became as dust in an ocean of soap and suds.

While this celestial ceremony proceeded, Margaret could not remain quiet. She hovered around the fortunate performer, determined to have an apparent hand in it, if not a real one. She dipped her finger into the water—to pave the way for her boy, presumably, for she could not deceive herself into thinking Catherine would allow her to settle the temperature. During the ablution, she knelt opposite little Gerard, prattling to him with amazing fluency, taking care not to articulate like grown-up people; after all, how could a cherub understand their ridiculous pronunciation?

"I wish you could wash out THAT," she said, fixing her eyes on the little boy's hand. "What?" "What, have you not noticed? On his little finger." Granny looked, and there, indeed, was a little brown mole. "Eh, but this is wonderful!" she cried. "Nature, my lass, you're strong; and meddlesome to boot. Have you noticed such a mark on anyone else? Tell the truth, girl!" "What, on him? Nay, mother, not I." "Well then he has; and on the very spot. And you never noticed that much. But, dear heart, I forgot; you haven't known him from child to man as I have. I've had him hundreds of times on my knees, just as this, and washed him from top to toe in luke-warm water." Swelling with conscious superiority, she looked down at Margaret as a woman beyond competition. Catherine gazed upward from her dizzy height and moralized. She differed from other busy-bodies in that she occasionally reflected—not deeply, or of course she would take care not to print it.

"It is strange," she remarked, "how things come round and about. Life is but a whirligig. Leastways, we poor women, our lives are all cut upon one pattern. Wasn't I for washing out my Gerard's mole in his young days? 'Oh, fie! Here's a foul blot,' quoth I; and scrubbed away at it until I made the poor wight cry; so then I thought 'twas time to give over. And now says you to me, 'Mother,' says you, 'do try and wash out o' my Gerard's finger,' says you. Think on't!"

"Wash it out?" cried Margaret. "I wouldn't for all the world. Why, it is the sweetest bit in his little darling body. I'll kiss it morn and night till he that owned it first comes back to us three. Oh, bless you, my jewel of gold and silver, for being marked like your own daddy, to comfort me." She kissed little Gerard's little mole, but she could not stop there; she presently had him sprawling on her lap, kissing his back all over again and again, seeming to worry him as a wolf worries a lamb. Catherine watched on, smiling. She had seen many such savage onslaughts in her day. This little sketch indicates the tenor of Margaret's life for several months. One or two small things occurred to her during that time which must be told; but I reserve them, since one string will serve for many glass beads.

But while her boy's father was passing through those fearful tempests of the soul, ending in the dead monastic calm, her life might fairly be summed in one great blissful word—Maternity. You, who know what lies in that word, enlarge my little sketch: see the young mother nursing and washing, dressing and undressing, crowing and gambolling with her first-born. Then, swifter than lightning, dart your eye into Italy, and see the cold cloister; see the monks passing like ghosts, eyes down, hands meekly crossed over bosoms dead to earthly feelings. One of these cowled ghosts is he, whose return, full of love, and youth, and joy—that radiant young mother awaits.

In the valley of Grindelwald, the traveler finds on one side the perpendicular Alps, all rock, ice, and everlasting snow, towering above the clouds and piercing to the sky; on his other hand lie little everyday slopes, green as emeralds, studded with cows and pretty cottages, and life. Whereas those lofty neighbors stand leafless, lifeless, inhuman, sublime. Elsewhere, sweet commonplaces of nature are apt to pass unnoticed; but fronting the grim Alps, they soothe, and even gently strike, the mind by contrast with their tremendous opposites. Such, in their way, are the two halves of this story, rightly looked at: on the Italian side, rugged adventure, strong passion, blasphemy, vice, penitence, pure ice, holy snow, soaring directly at heaven. On the Dutch side, all on a humble scale and womanish, but ever green. And as a pathway parts the ice towers of Grindelwald, aspiring to the sky, from its little sunny braes, so here is but a page between "The Cloister and the Hearth."

To truly grasp the architecture of Clement's spirit, one must first witness the absolute poverty of his universe at the precise moment of his transformation. He existed within a microscopic empire of two, subjugated by a man he was conditioned to call "Master." He was a square-built, brutal artifact of a world he did not know, roughly sixty years of age, though time on the island seemed measured only by the decay of his humanity. His dark hair fell in matted, feral clusters, and a vast beard obscured his face, rendering him less a man than a beast draped in the ruin of whatever civilization had cast him out. He possessed a terrifying latent power, yet he spent his days in a state of stagnant lethargy, only stirring to exert his dominance over my labor—collecting sea-birds or hauling faggots of wood down the ravine.

Our lives were bound by the daily ritual of bathing in a salt-water pool, barricaded from the shark-infested ocean by a ridge of jagged stone. Beyond this, my existence was an enforced void. I was permitted no education, no heritage, and almost no language. The Master utilized silence as a tool of oppression, rationing his words to commands and curses, ensuring my mind remained as barren as the rocky crags that caged us. I became a taciturn creature, my intellect starved, left to gaze into the abyssal depths of the ocean or trace the cold geometries of the stars. I was kept as a beast of the field—valuable only for the calories my labor saved him, and perhaps to serve as a mirror for his own desperate need for mastery.

I left him in the dark, initiating a careful descent down the rain-slicked precipices that formed the carceral walls of our world. Before I had traversed fifty paces, a sudden rupture of lightning tore the sky, pursued instantly by a shriek so guttural it arrested my breath. It was a sound stripped of all former sovereignty. I could not trace its origin through the howling wind, but then I heard the voice—his voice, diminished and frantic—commanding my return.

I obeyed, stepping back over the jagged stone, and found him frozen exactly where I had left him.

"You called me, master?" I asked, testing the word on my tongue.

"Yes, I did. Take my hand. Lead me to the cabin."

I guided him, marveling at the sudden frailty in his grip. The hand that had so often struck me down now clung to my shoulder with the desperate dependency of a drowning man. He found his bedding and collapsed onto it, a ruined monument of flesh.

"Bring the kid full of water," he gasped. "Quick!"

I brought the vessel. He plunged his face into it, bathing his head as if trying to wash away the fire of the heavens. Presently, he fell back against the damp wood, a heavy groan escaping his chest. "O God! It is all over with me," he whispered into the gloom. "I shall live and die in this cursed hole."

"What is the matter, master?" I asked.

He offered no answer, sinking instead into a rhythmic liturgy of curses and moans. When he finally fell still, I slipped out again into the dying tempest.

***

The new pope favored the Dominican order. The convent received a message from the Vatican, requiring a capable friar to teach at the University of Basel. Now Clement was the very monk for this: well versed in languages, and in his worldly days had attended the lectures of Guarini the younger. His visit to England was therefore postponed though not resigned; and meantime he was sent to Basel; but not being wanted there for three months, he was to preach on the road.

He passed out of the northern gate with his eyes lowered, and the whole man wrapped in pious contemplation.

Oh, if we could paint a mind and its story, what a walking fresco was this barefooted friar!

Hopeful, happy love, bereavement, despair, impiety, vice, suicide, remorse, religious despondency, penitence, death to the world, resignation.

And all in twelve short months.

And now the traveler was on foot again. But all was changed: no perilous adventures now. The very thieves and robbers bowed to the ground before him, and instead of robbing him, forced stolen money on him, and begged his prayers.

This journey therefore furnished few picturesque incidents. I have, however, some readers to think of, who care little for melodrama, and expect a quiet peep at what passes inside a man. To such students things undramatic are often vocal, denoting the progress of a mind.

The first Sunday of Clement's journey was marked by this. He prayed for the soul of Margaret. He had never done so before. Not that her eternal welfare was not dearer to him than anything on earth. It was his humility. The terrible impieties that burst from him on the news of her death horrified my well-disposed readers; but not as on reflection they horrified him who had uttered them. For a long time during his novitiate he was oppressed with religious despair. He thought he must have committed that sin against the Holy Spirit which dooms the soul forever. By degrees that dark cloud cleared away, *Anselmo juvante*; but deep self-abasement remained. He felt his own salvation insecure, and moreover thought it would be mocking Heaven, should he, the deeply stained, pray for a soul so innocent, comparatively, as Margaret's. So he used to coax good Anselm and another kindly monk to pray for her. They did not refuse, nor do it by halves. In general the good old monks (and there were good, bad, and indifferent in every convent) had a pure and tender affection for their younger brethren, which, in truth, was not of this world.

Clement then, having preached on Sunday morning in a small Italian town, and being mightily carried onward, was greatly encouraged; and that day a balmy sense of God's forgiveness and love descended on him. And he prayed for the welfare of Margaret's soul. And from that hour this became his daily habit, and the one purified tie, that by memory connected his heart with earth.

For his family were to him as if they had never been.

The Church would not share with earth. Nor could even the Church cure the great love without annihilating the smaller ones.

During most of this journey Clement rarely felt any spring of life within him, but when he was in the pulpit. The other exceptions were, when he happened to relieve some fellow-creature.

A young man was tarantula bitten, or perhaps, like many more, fancied it. Fancy or reality, he had been for two days without sleep, and in most extraordinary convulsions, leaping, twisting, and beating the walls.
