The village musicians had only excited him worse with their music. Exhaustion and death followed the disease, when it gained such a head. Clement passed by and learned what was the matter. He sent for a psaltery, and tried the patient with soothing melodies; but if the other tunes maddened him, Clement's seemed to crush him. He groaned and moaned under them, and groveled on the floor. At last the friar observed that at intervals his lips kept going. He applied his ear, and found the patient was whispering a tune; and a very singular one, that had no existence. He learned this tune and played it. The patient's face brightened amazingly. He marched about the room on the light fantastic toe enjoying it; and when Clement's fingers ached nearly off with playing it, he had the satisfaction of seeing the young man sink complacently to sleep to this lullaby, the strange creation of his own mind; for it seems he was no musician, and never composed a tune before or after. This sleep saved his life. And Clement, after teaching the tune to another, in case it should be wanted again, went forward with his heart a little warmer.

On another occasion he found a mob haling a decently dressed man along, who struggled and vociferated, but in a strange language. This person had walked into their town erect and sprightly, waving a mulberry branch over his head. Thereupon the natives first gazed stupidly, not believing their eyes, then pounced on him and dragged him before the podesta. Clement went with them; but on the way drew quietly near the prisoner and spoke to him in Italian; no answer. In French, German, Dutch; no assets. Then the man tried Clement in tolerable Latin, but with a sharpish accent. He said he was an Englishman, and oppressed with the heat of Italy, had taken a bough off the nearest tree, to save his head. "In my country anybody is welcome to what grows on the highway. Confound the fools; I am ready to pay for it. But here is all Italy up in arms about a twig and a handful of leaves."

The pig-headed podesta would have sent the dogged islander to prison; but Clement mediated, and with some difficulty made the prisoner comprehend that silkworms, and by consequence mulberry leaves, were sacred, being under the wing of the Sovereign, and his source of income; and urged on the podesta that ignorance of his mulberry laws was natural in a distant country, where the very tree perhaps was unknown. The opinionative islander turned the still vibrating scale by pulling out a long purse and repeating his original theory, that the whole question was mercantile. "Quid damni?" said he, "Dic; et cito solvam." The

Clement exhaled, a long, weary sound that seemed to carry the weight of every unspoken warning history had ever issued. A flicker of doubt crossed his mind—a rare intrusion for a man of such rigid conviction—regarding the wisdom of engaging with a spirit so violently passionate. Yet, even in his youth, he had absorbed the profound, silent lessons of the cloister. He knew the ancient rule: when faced with the turbulent eddies of human difficulty, one must cease all action and speech, allowing the waters to settle until clarity emerges. Thus, he chose silence, offering only a prayer for divine illumination. His sole physical act was a gentle withdrawal of his foot, a retreat meant to signal safety.

But the penitent woman reacted with a ferocity that defied reason. She flung her arms around his ankle, her body convulsing in a desperate embrace, and erupted in a cry of raw anguish, weeping openly over the limb she sought to hold. In that moment, the crushing agony of shame mingled with her contrition, flushing her pale skin with a vivid crimson that climbed high to her throat. She lay trembling at his feet, a living testament to her brokenness.

"My daughter," Clement spoke softly, his voice steady amidst her storm. "Take courage. Cease tormenting yourself over this Gerard, for he is nothing. As for me, I am Brother Clement, whom Heaven has dispatched to you today solely to comfort you and aid you in saving your soul. Since you have named me your confessor, I now claim your obedience."

"Oh, yes," she sobbed, her voice thick with emotion. "Leave this pilgrimage immediately and return to Rome. Penitence abroad holds little value. There, amidst the fires of our daily lives, lie the specific temptations we must conquer or perish; we must not flee to hide among strangers, masking our faces, when the true battle lies with those closest to us. It is easy to wash the feet of masked travelers, but hard to remain meek and charitable with the people who surround us."

"I swear, I will never, never touch her again."

"Nay," Clement corrected gently. "I speak not merely of servants, but of dependents, kin, and friends. This is your penance: at the last hour of the night and the first after Matins, recall your sin and God's boundless goodness; cultivate humility and gentleness toward the flaws of those near you. The world courts the rich, but you must seek the poor—not the beggar, for they are often dishonest or falsely destitute. Rather, find those who blush to ask for your help yet desperately need it. To give to them is to lend directly to Heaven. And regarding your future..."

"Me? I will never marry."

"You shall marry within the year," Clement declared firmly. "I entreat and command you to wed a man who fears God. For you are but clay; matched poorly, you will become nothing. But united with a worthy husband, by God's grace, you may live as a pious princess and die a saint."

"You."

He then urged her to rise and begin the good work he had assigned. She rose to her knees, removed her veil, and cast an eloquent, searching gaze upon him before lowering her eyes in meek submission. "I will obey you as I would an angel," she whispered. "How happy I am, yet how unhappy; for oh, my heart tells me I shall never look upon you again. I cannot leave until I have dried your feet."

"It is unnecessary," Clement replied. "I have excused you from this bootless penance."

"'Tis no penance to me," she insisted, her voice trembling. "Ah! If you will not let me dry your poor feet, then you do not forgive me."

"So be it," Clement said, resigning himself to her persistence. *Levius quid foemina,* he thought internally—the lighter burden women bear. Yet, these fragile creatures, who naturally gravitate toward the small just as celestial bodies turn to the massive, possess moments of startling angelic intelligence. Once the princess had finished drying the friar's feet, she gazed at him with tears shimmering in her beautiful eyes, murmuring with a singular tenderness, "May I have masses said for her soul?" she added timidly.

A faint blush colored the monk's cheek, and moisture gathered in his cold blue eyes. It was a reaction born of surprise, coming from a woman he had just rated so lowly. "It is a gracious thought," he admitted. "Do as thou wilt; often such acts return to the doer like blessed dew. Remember, I am your confessor, not hers; it is your soul I must strive to save, or woe betide my own. My daughter, my dear daughter, I see good and evil angels battling for your soul this very day, right now. Fight on your own side. Dost thou remember everything I commanded?"

"Remember!" the princess affirmed. "Sweet saint, every syllable of yours is engraved in my heart."

"But one final word," Clement continued. "Pray much to Christ, and little to his saints."

"I will."

"And that is the finest counsel I can offer you. With that, we part. You go to the place that suits you best—your father's house—and I return to my holy mother's work."

"Adieu," the princess faltered.

"Adieu, thou whom I have loved too well, hated too ill, known and revered too late; forgiving angel, adieu—for ever."

The monk caught the words, though they broke forth only as a sigh. "For ever?" he cried aloud, his voice suddenly ardent. "Christians live 'forever,' and love 'forever,' but they never part 'forever.' They separate, as the earth and sun do, only to meet more brilliantly in a little while. You and I part here for this earthly life. But what is our life? Merely one line in the grand story of the Church, whose children we both are; one handful in the sands of time, one drop in the ocean of eternity. Adieu—for the brief moment called 'a life!' We part in trouble, yet we shall meet in peace; we part as creatures of clay, yet we shall meet as immortal spirits; we part in a world of sin and sorrow, yet we shall meet where all is purity and divine love, where no ill passions exist, but only Christ and His saints arrayed in white. There, in the turning of an hourglass, in the breaking of a bubble, in the passing of a cloud, she, thou, and I shall meet again; and sit at the feet of angels, archangels, apostles, and saints, beaming with joy unspeakable in the light of God's shadow upon His throne, FOREVER—AND EVER—AND EVER."

Thus they parted. The monk stood erect, his eyes turned heavenward, glowing with the sacred fire of zeal; the princess withdrew slowly, turning repeatedly to cast lingering glances of awe and tender regret upon that inspired figure. She departed home subdued and purified. Clement, in due course, arrived in Basel and took up his duties, teaching in the university and preaching throughout the town and its environs. He led a life that could be summarized in two words: deep study and mortification. By Basel, he had advanced significantly in holy zeal and knowledge. The brethren of his order began to perceive in him a true descendant of the saints and martyrs.

When little Gerard was barely three months old, a messenger arrived, flushed with haste from Tergou, bearing news for Catherine. "Go back now," she commanded him, her voice sharp with resolve, "and tell them I cannot come, and I will not: they have taken Kate." The boy departed, and Catherine resumed her own lamentation. "There, child, I must go; they are all thrown into confusion, at sixes and sevens. This is the third time they have asked, and tomorrow my man will come himself to drag me home by the ear, with a flea in his pocket." She then recounted her trials with infants, instructing Margaret on how to manage every conceivable emergency, and pressed money into her hands. Margaret declined with gratitude, but Catherine insisted, her tone turning to anger. Margaret offered excuses so logically sound that Catherine dismissed them with a calm, icy contempt; to her mind, they lacked the necessary feminine spirit. "Come, out with your heart," she demanded, "as we part today; and perhaps we shall never see one another's face again." "Oh! Mother, do not say that." "Alack, girl, I have seen it so often; it comes into my mind now at every parting. When I was your age, I never harbored such a thought. Nay, we were all destined to live forever then: so, get it out." "Well, then, Mother—I would rather not have told you—but your Cornelis must say to me, 'So you are come to share with us, eh, mistress?' Those were his words. I told him I would be very sorry." "Beshrew his ill tongue! What does it signify? He will never know." "Most likely he would sooner or later. But whether or not, I will take no begrudging bounty from any family; unless I saw my child starving—and Heaven only knows what I might do." "Nay, Mother, give me but thy love—I prize that above silver, and they grudge me not that, by all I can find—for not a single stiver of money will I take from your house." "You are a foolish lass. Why, were it me, I'd take it just to spite him." "No, you would not. You and I are apples from the same tree." Catherine yielded with a gracious air; and when the moment of separation truly arrived, embraces and tears erupted from both sides.

Once she was gone, the child cried bitterly. All attempts to pacify him failing, Margaret suspected a pin and searched between his clothes and his skin, finding a gold angel uncomfortably lodged against his spine. "There, now, Gerard," she said to the babe, "I thought Granny gave in rather suddenly." She took the coin, wrapped it in a scrap of linen, and laid it at the bottom of her box, bidding the infant observe that she could be as resolute as Granny herself. Catherine had informed Eli of Margaret's foolish pride and how she had baffled it. Eli declared Margaret was right and Catherine wrong. Catherine tossed her head in disagreement; Eli pondered silently.

Margaret was not without domestic anxieties. She still had two men to feed and could not work as hard as she had done. She had enough to do to keep the house, the child, and cook for them all. But she had a little money laid by, and she used to tell her child that his father would be home to help them before it was spent. With these bright hopes and that treasury of bliss, her boy, she spent some happy months. Time wore on; and no Gerard came. Stranger still, no news of him reached them. Then her mind grew disquieted, and contrary to her practical nature, she was often lost in sad reverie, sighing in silence. And while her heart was troubled, her money melted away. Thus it was that one day she found the cupboard empty and looked into her dependents' faces; at the sight of them, her bosom swelled with pity. She appealed to the baby whether she could let Grandfather and poor old Martin go hungry, and went to retrieve Catherine's angel. As she unfolded the linen, a tear of gentle mortification fell upon it. She sent Martin out to exchange it. While he was gone, a Frenchman arrived with one of the dealers in illuminated manuscripts, who had previously offered her such a poor price. He told her he was employed by his sovereign to collect masterpieces for her Book of Hours. She showed him the two finest things she possessed; he was charmed by one of them—the flowers, raspberries, and creeping vines shaded by Margaret Van Eyck. He offered her an unheard-of sum. "Nay, flout not my need, good stranger," she replied; "three mouths reside in this house, and none to fill them but me." Curious arithmetic! Left out Number One. "I'd outdo thee not, fair mistress. My princess charged me strictly, 'Seek the best craftsmen'; but I will make no hard bargains; make them content with me, and me with them." The next minute Margaret was on her knees, kissing little Gerard in the cradle and showering four gold pieces upon him again and again, relating the entire occurrence to him in very broken Dutch. "And oh, what a good princess! Wasn't she? We will pray for her, won't we, my lambkin, when we are old enough?"

Martin returned, furious. "They will not change it. I trow they think I stole it." "I am beholden to thee," said Margaret hastily, almost snatching the bundle from Martin, wrapping it up again, and restoring it to its hiding place. Ere these unexpected funds were spent, she returned to her ironing and starching. In the midst of this, Martin sickened and died after an illness of nine days. Nearly all her money went to bury him decently. He was gone, leaving an empty chair by her fireside; for he had preferred the hearth to the sun since the Busy Body had departed. Margaret would not allow anyone to sit in this chair now. Yet whenever she let her eye dwell too long upon it, vacant, it was sure to cost her a tear.

Now there was no one to carry her linen home. To do it herself, she must leave little Gerard in the charge of a neighbor, but she dared not trust such a treasure to mortal hands; besides, she could not bear him out of her sight for hours on end. So she set inquiries on foot for a boy to carry her basket on Saturdays and Mondays. A plump, fresh-colored youth named Luke Peterson, who looked fifteen but was actually eighteen, came forward, blushing and twiddling his cap, to ask if a man would not serve her turn as well as a boy. Before he spoke, she was telling herself, "This boy will just do." But she took the cue and said, "Nay; but a man will perhaps seek more than I can well pay." "Not I," said Luke warmly. "Why, Mistress Margaret, I am your neighbor, and I do very well at coopering. I can carry your basket for you before or after my day's work, and welcome. You have no need to pay me anything. 'Tisn't as if we were strangers, ye know." "Why, Master Luke, I know your face, for that matter; but I cannot call to mind that ever a word passed between us." "Oh yes, you did, Mistress Margaret. What, have you forgotten? One day you were trying to carry your baby and also your pitcher full of water; and I said, 'Give me the baby to carry.' 'Nay,' says you, 'I'll give you the pitcher, and keep the bairn myself;' and I carried the pitcher home, and you took it from me at this door, and you said to me, 'I am muckle obliged to you, young man,' with such a sweet voice—not like the folk in this street speak to a body." "I do mind now, Master Luke; and methinks it was the least I could say." "Well, Mistress Margaret, if you will say as much every time I carry your basket, I care not how often I bear it, nor how far." "Nay, nay," said Margaret, coloring faintly. "I would not put upon good nature. You are young, Master Luke, and kindly. Say I give you your supper on Saturday night, when you bring the linen home, and your dawn-meat on Monday; would that make us anywhere near even?" "As you please; only say not I sought a couple of diets for such a trifle as yon."

With chubby-faced Luke's timely assistance and the health and strength which Heaven granted this poor young woman to balance her many ills, the house ran pretty smoothly for a while. But the heart became more and more troubled by Gerard's long, and now most mysterious, silence. Then that mental torturer, Suspense, began to tear her heavy heart with his hot pincers, until she cried often and vehemently, "Oh, that I could know the worst."

Whilst she was in this state, one day she heard a heavy step mounting the stairs. She started and trembled. "That is no step that I know. Ill tidings?" The door opened, and an unexpected visitor, Eli, came in, looking grave and kind. Margaret eyed him in silence, her agitation increasing. "Girl," he said, "the Skipper is come back." "One word," gasped Margaret, "is he alive?" "Surely I hope so. No one has seen him dead." "Then they must have seen him alive." "No, girl; neither dead

The evening meal at Tergou was interrupted not by the clatter of cutlery, but by the violent intrusion of Luke Peterson, a man battered by the elements and coated in the dust of a hurried journey. Bursting into the room, he announced with urgent breathlessness that Mistress Catherine was required immediately in Rotterdam. When he stumbled over her name, correcting himself from "Catherine" to "Kate" and finally settling on "Margaret," the household erupted in a cacophony of disbelief. Yet, Luke stood firm, recounting the divine command he had received: a plea from the heavens itself, delivered through the voice of Eli the hosier's wife, demanding the presence of the mother for the sake of God's love. He had not waited for dawn; he had ridden straight into the night.

The revelation shattered the domestic tranquility. "Holy saints! He has come home," someone exclaimed, though the logic of the situation defied immediate comprehension. Conjectures flew like sparks from a flint, until Kate, trembling with timid courage, suggested the young messenger might hold the key. Luke confirmed it with grim simplicity: the babe was dying, consumed by a wasting sickness that had peaked and pined relentlessly.

In an instant, the atmosphere shifted from confusion to fury. Catherine, her face turned toward her husband, erupted in a tirade of righteous indignation, threatening a quarrel that would echo through their decades of marriage if anyone dared question her resolve. Eli, however, dismissed the threat with characteristic pragmatism, offering to borrow Peter's mule so she might depart without delay. With a blessing that masked her terror, Catherine seized Luke, dragging him into the cart. On the road, she interrogated him with a ferocity that alternated between suspicion and seductive urgency, stripping his thoughts bare until he confessed everything he knew.

Upon arriving at the door, Margaret awaited them, a specter of pallor and agitation. She threw her arms around Catherine's neck, her eyes imploring forgiveness and salvation. Catherine scanned her daughter-in-law's face, then fixed her gaze on the failing child, and declared, "Lucky you sent for me. The child is poisoned."

"Poisoned! By whom?" Margaret cried, panic rising in her throat.

"By you," Catherine answered, her voice cutting through the room. "You have been fretting."

"Nay, indeed, mother! How can I help fretting?" Margaret protested, her voice breaking.

"Don't tell me," Catherine snapped, her tone devoid of mercy. "A nursing mother has no business to fret. She must turn her mind away from her grief to the comfort that lies in her lap. Know you not that the child pines if the mother vexes herself? This comes of your reading and writing. Those idle crafts befit a man; but they keep all useful knowledge out of a woman. The child must be weaned."

"Oh, you cruel woman!" Margaret screamed, the accusation tearing from her lips. "I am sorry I sent for you. Would you rob me of the only bit of comfort I have in the world? Nursing my Gerard, I forget I am the most unhappy creature beneath the sun."

"That you do not," Catherine retorted, her eyes hardening. "Or he would not be the way he is."

"Mother!" Margaret pleaded, the desperation in her voice raw.

"'Tis hard," Catherine relented slightly, though her expression remained stern. "But bethink thee; would it not be harder to look down and see his lovely wee face looking up at you out of a little coffin? And how could you face your other troubles with your heart aye full, and your lap empty?"

"Oh, mother, I consent to anything. Only save my boy."

"That is a good lass. Trust to me! I do stand by, and see clearer than thou."

Yet, there remained another consent to be gained—the babe's—and he proved far more refractory than his mother. Margaret tried to feign regret at the child's misbehavior, claiming he loved her too well, but Catherine was a match for them both. Observing a healthy young woman seated outside her own door with an infant nearby, Catherine approached her and proposed a temporary exchange. Would the stranger nurse the pining child for the nonce until Margaret could prepare to wean him? The young woman smiled, popped her own child into the cradle, and stepped into the house.

With a graceful curtsey, she accepted the infant from Catherine's hands. She examined the fragile form, filled with pity, and began to purr over it, proceeding to nurse it with the effortless grace of a mother who had never known loss. Margaret, paralyzed by the sheer assurance of this stranger, cast a rueful look at Catherine before bursting into uncontrollable sobs. The visitor glanced up, her composure unbroken.

"What is to do? Wife, ye told me not the mother was unwilling."

"She is not: she is only a fool. Never heed her," the stranger said firmly, addressing Margaret. "And you, Margaret, I am ashamed of you."

"You are a cruel, hard-hearted woman," Margaret sobbed, her defenses crumbling.

"Them as take in hand to guide the weak need be hardish," the stranger replied bluntly. "And you will excuse me; but you are not my flesh and blood; and your boy is."

After allowing this harsh truth to settle, she softened her tone. "Come now, she is robbing her own to save yours, and you can think of nothing better than bursting out a-blubbering in the woman's face. Out fie, for shame!"

"Nay, wife," the nurse said gently. "Thank Heaven, I have enough for my own and for hers to boot. And prithee wyte not on her! Maybe the troubles o' life ha' soured her own milk."

"And her heart into the bargain," Catherine added, her voice relentless.

Margaret locked eyes with the stranger, then lowered her gaze, defeated. "I know I ought to be very grateful to you," she sobbed, turning her head away to avoid witnessing the intolerable sight of another woman nursing her Gerard, a child who drew no distinction between this new mother and the one he had rejected.

"You are very welcome, my poor woman," the nurse replied warmly. "And so are you, Mistress Catherine, which are my townswoman, and know it not."

"What, are ye from Tergou?" Catherine asked, recognizing the accent. "All the better. But I cannot call your face to mind."

"Oh, you know not me," the woman said humbly. "My husband and me, we are very humble folk by you. But true Eli and his wife are known of all the town; and respected. So, I am at your call, dame; and at yours, wife; and yours, my pretty poppet; night or day."

"There's a woman of the right old sort," Catherine murmured as the door closed behind the departing stranger.

"I HATE her," Margaret whispered, the words vibrating with terrible fervor. "I HATE her. I HATE her."

Catherine merely laughed at the outburst. "That is right," she said. "Better say it, as set sly and think it. It is very natural after all. Come, here is your bundle o' comfort. Take and hate that, if ye can," she added, placing the child in Margaret's lap.

"No, no," Margaret refused, turning her head halfway from the baby; she could not bring herself to turn the other half. "He is not my child now; he is hers. I know not why she left him here, for my part. It was very good of her not to take him to her house, cradle and all..." Her voice dissolved into a cascade of wails.

"Ah! well, one comfort, he is not dead," Catherine soothed, letting her cry in peace. "This gives me light: some other woman has got him away from me; like father, like son."

For a time, Catherine allowed Margaret to mourn, occasionally taking Gerard out for fresh air when she needed Joan's assistance. Margaret never objected, nor did she express the slightest incredulity, yet she remained in tears whenever they returned. This period of connivance was short-lived, however. Soon, Margaret became as eager to wean little Gerard as Catherine had been to intervene. The process was completed, and the child recovered his health and vigor. Yet, another trouble descended upon him directly with the onset of teething. Here, Catherine's experience proved invaluable. Amidst her own grief regarding the missing father, Margaret found moments of bliss watching her son's tiny teeth emerge.

"Teeth, mother? I call them not teeth, but pearls of pearls," Margaret would exclaim. Each pearl that peeked and sparkled on his red gums represented, in her eyes, the greatest feat nature had ever achieved. Her companion shared the illusion. If we had told them that standing corn was equally admirable, Margaret would have transformed into a reproachful gazelle, and Catherine would have thrown us out of doors; thus, the arrival of each tooth was announced with a shriek of triumph by whichever of them happened to be the fortunate discoverer.

Catherine gossiped with Joan, learning that she was the wife of Jorian Ketel of Tergou, a man who had once served Ghysbrecht Van Swieten but had fallen out of favor, returning to Rotterdam, his native place. His friends had secured him the position of sexton to the parish, and combined with carpentry, he managed quite well. In return, Catherine told Joan whose child she had nursed, detailing the saga of

To grasp the architecture of this tragedy, one must first confront the absolute volatility of a single, calculated falsehood. A lie is not merely a blow with a curtal axe; its consequences are unpredictable, ramifying far beyond the scope of human calculation. While a physical strike leaves a wound that can be anticipated, a lie breeds chaos without bounds, often costing lives and laying waste to entire households. In this story, the repercussions of one heartless fabrication were tremendous, yet the architects of that deceit reaped little reward. The brothers, who concocted the lie solely to diminish the number of claimants to their father's estate, found themselves displaced rather than enriched. They watched as little Gerard seized the place in his mother's heart that rightfully belonged to them. Worse still, Eli publicly declared that, believing Gerard lost and likely dead, he had already provided by will for both the orphaned boy and Margaret, his son's widow. At this revelation, a silent, lethal warning passed between the betrayers—a glance that spoke volumes. Cornelis stood on the brink of speaking the truth—that Gerard was most likely alive—but caught his mother's gaze and swallowed his words.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the other partner in this treachery, was now a failing man. He sensed the period approaching when all his accumulated wealth would slip from his grasp, leaving only his misdeeds to cling to his soul. Too intelligent to fully deceive himself, he had never been free from gusts of remorse. In delivering Gerard's letter to Margaret, he had compromised his integrity. "I cannot give up land and money," his giant Avarice commanded. "I will cause her no unnecessary pain," his dwarf Conscience pleaded. Thus, after tampering with the seal and discovering there was not a syllable regarding the deed, he delivered the document himself, making a merit of it to balance his conscience on a scale common to the self-accusing judge. The birth of Margaret's child shocked him, casting his treachery in a new, damning light. If his forged letter were to take effect, he would be responsible for the dishonor of a woman who was the daughter of one friend, the granddaughter of another, and from whom he was unjustly withholding her ancestral lands. These thoughts preyed upon him during that twilight of life when bodily strength decays and the memory of old friendships resurfaces, filling him with gloomy horrors. Yet, he dared not confess, for the cure was an honest man who would have forced him to disgorge everything. With him, Avarice was an ingrained habit, while Penitence remained but a fleeting sentiment.

Matters reached a breaking point one day when Ghysbrecht, returning from the town hall to his home, found a woman waiting in the vestibule, a child cradled in her arms. Veiled, she appeared ashamed, prompting him to address her with magisterial condescension. Upon hearing his tone, she lowered her veil and met his gaze directly. It was Margaret Brandt. Her sudden appearance and intense manner startled him, and he could not conceal his confusion. "Where is my Gerard?" she cried, her bosom heaving. "Is he alive?" "For aught I know," he stammered. "I hope so, for your sake. Prithee, come into this room. The servants!" "Not a step," Margaret retorted, seizing him by the shoulder with the energy of an excited woman. "You know the secret of that which is breaking my heart. Why does not my Gerard come, nor send a line for these many months? Answer me, or all the town shall hear, let alone your servants. My misery is too great to be sported with."

In vain he insisted he knew nothing of Gerard. She countered that those who had sent her to him had told her a different tale. "You do know why he neither comes nor sends," she stated firmly. At this, Ghysbrecht turned paler and paler, summoning all his remaining dignity. "Would you believe those two knaves against a man of worship?" "What two knaves?" she asked keenly. He faltered, "Said ye not...?" Realizing his failure, he broke down. "There I am, a poor old broken man, whose memory is shaken. And you come here, confusing me so, I know not what I say." "Ay, sir, your memory is shaken, or surely you would not be my enemy," she replied sharply. "My father saved you from the plague when none other would approach; he was ever your friend. My grandfather Floris helped you in your early poverty and loved you, man and boy. Three generations of us you have known; and here stands the fourth. This is your old friend Peter's grandchild, and your old friend Floris' great-grandchild. Look down on his innocent face, and think of theirs!"

"Woman, you torture me," Ghysbrecht sighed, sinking onto a bench. But she saw her advantage immediately. Kneeling before him, she placed the boy on his knees. "This fatherless babe is poor Margaret Brandt's, who never did you ill, and comes of a race that loved you. Nay, look at his face. It will melt you more than any word of mine. Saints of heaven, what can a poor desolate girl and her babe have done to wipe out all memory of thine own young days, when thou wert guiltless as he is, that now looks up in thy face and implores thee to give him back his father?" Holding the child high with her arms beneath him, she smiled under the old man's eyes. He cast a wild look of anguish upon the child, then another upon the kneeling mother, before starting up and shrieking, "Avaunt, ye pair of adders!" The sting of her words gave his old limbs a momentary vigor. He walked rapidly, wringing his hands and clutching at his white hair. "Forget those days? I forget all else. Oh, woman, woman, sleeping or waking I see but the faces of the dead, I hear but the voices of the dead, and I shall soon be among the dead. There, there, what is done is done. I am in hell. I am in hell." Unnatural force ended in prostration. He staggered, and but for Margaret's support with one disengaged arm, would have fallen. She cried for help. Servants rushed in and carried him away in a state bordering on syncope. The last sight Margaret had of him was his old, furrowed face, white and helpless as the hair hanging down over the servant's elbow. "Heaven forgive me," she whispered. "I doubt I have killed the poor old man."

Yet, this attempt to penetrate the torturing mystery left it as dark, or darker, than before. As she pondered every word, her suspicion was confirmed that Ghysbrecht did know something about Gerard. "And who were the two knaves he thought had done a good deed, and told me? Oh, my Gerard, my poor deserted babe, you and I are wading in deep waters." The visit to Tergou cost more than she could afford, and a customer fled owing her money. Compelled once more to unfold Catherine's angel, she descended the stairs with it in hand only to find loose silver on the table accompanied by a written line: *For Gerard his wife*. She fell with a cry of surprise upon the writing, which soon rose into a cry of joy. "He is alive. He sends me this by some friendly hand." She kissed the note repeatedly and tucked it into her bosom.

Time rolled on, bringing no further news of Gerard, yet about every two months a small sum in silver found its way into the house. Sometimes it lay on the table; once it was flung through the bedroom window in a purse; another time it rested at the bottom of Luke's basket. He had stopped at the public-house to speak with a friend. The giver or his agent was never detected. Catherine disowned it; Margaret Van Eyck swore she had no hand in it; so did Eli. Whenever the funds arrived, Margaret would say to little Gerard, "Oh, my poor deserted child, you and I are wading in deep waters." She applied at least half of this modest, yet vital supply, to dressing the little Gerard beyond his station in life. "If it does come from Gerard, he shall see his boy neat." All the mothers in the street began to sneer, particularly those with children dressed in rags. Months rolled on, and a dead sickness of heart succeeded these keener torments. She returned to her first thought: "Gerard must be dead. She should never see her boy's father again, nor her marriage lines." This last grief, somewhat allayed by Eli and Catherine recognizing her betrothal, now revived in full force, as others refused to look favorably upon her story. Often she moaned over her boy's illegitimacy. "Is it not enough for us to be bereaved? Must we be dishonored too? Oh, that we had ne'er been born."

A change took place in Peter Brandt. His mind, clouded for nearly two years, seemed now to be clearing; he experienced intervals of intelligence, during which he and Margaret talked of Gerard until he wandered again. One day, returning after an absence of several hours, Margaret found him conversing with Catherine in a way he had not done since his paralytic stroke. "Eh, girl, why must you be out?" she asked. "But indeed I have told him all; and we have been a-crying together over thy troubles." Margaret stood silent, looking joyfully from one to the other. Peter smiled

To comprehend the architecture of Margaret's grief, one must first understand the absolute fragility of her universe at this moment of loss. She existed within a microscopic empire of three, subjugated by the inevitable decay of time itself. Her husband, once a figure of wisdom and learning, had been reduced by illness to a helpless infant, a ruin of the man who had once fed her soul. Catherine attempted to construct a fortress of platitudes around her, urging her to view the death as a merciful release, a natural ripening of the tree. But such logic was a weaponless shield against the visceral reality of absence.

Margaret's resistance was not born of stubbornness, but of a profound psychological fracture. "Such cares are all the joys I have," she confessed, her voice trembling with the weight of systemic abandonment. "They fill my desolate heart, which now seems void as well as waste." The empty chair became a physical manifestation of her trauma, an ache in her bosom that mirrored the hollow space left by a father she had loved with the ferocity of a mother protecting her own child. She had sat beside him, watching him fade, thinking, *But for me thou wouldst die of hunger.* Now, the cycle of survival had broken, leaving her isolated in a landscape of mourning where even piety felt insufficient to bridge the chasm between life and death.

In the silence that followed, Catherine finally abandoned her role as the dispenser of common sense. Her bowels yearned over the young widow, and she spoke not from the safety of convention, but from the raw necessity of human connection. "Waste not thine youth upon my son Gerard," Catherine urged, her tone shifting from maternal concern to strategic intervention. "Either he is dead or he is a traitor. It cuts my heart to say it; but who can help seeing it? Thy father is gone; and I cannot always be beside thee." Here, the narrative pivoted on the intersection of personal vulnerability and social survival. Catherine identified the structural isolation threatening Margaret and proposed a solution rooted in the resilience of the marginalized: marriage.

She introduced Luke Peterson, a figure previously relegated to the periphery of their domestic sphere—the boy who carried baskets, the Rotterdam lad described by others as "half man, half sponge." Yet, Catherine saw in him a capable anchor. "I've made inquiries for you," she declared, dismantling the class barriers that usually defined their interactions. "They all do say he is a capable workman, and never touches a drop." Seeing Margaret's initial perplexity, Catherine pressed further, revealing the hidden currents of affection that flowed beneath the surface of their daily routines. "Fancy will not be bridled... I wormed it out of him near a twelvemonth ago."

When Margaret protested, fearing she would be marrying into the shadow of Gerard's potential betrayal, Catherine reframed the union not as a replacement, but as a necessary fortification. "A lone woman is lost without a man about her to fetch and carry her little matters," she argued, invoking the harsh realities of gendered labor and economic dependence. "The best use you can put him to is to marry him." This was a critique of the societal expectation that women remain solitary victims of circumstance; instead, Catherine championed agency through partnership.

Yet, the rigid structures of honor and loyalty proved difficult to dismantle. Margaret, afflicted by the "busybody's protection," initially rejected the proposal, viewing Luke's advances as a transgression against the sanctity of her lost love. "An, Gerard, thou hast but me," she cried, refusing to believe her husband dead until she saw his tombstone. This emotional stasis created a tension that threatened to fracture the household entirely. Luke, the great soft fellow, found himself cast out, meeting a cold reception where he had once found kindness. He fell to whimpering, asking if he had offended, his innocence colliding with the sharp edges of social rejection.

Catherine, however, refused to let sentimentality override pragmatism. She exposed the mechanics of courtship that Margaret had ignored: Luke's weekly visits, his presence on Saturdays, the unspoken vows exchanged in the quiet moments of shared labor. "Promises are pie-crusts," she dismissed the notion of waiting indefinitely for a ghost. "Humour her in everything, and then, if Poor Gerard come not home and claim her, you will be sure to have her—in time." Her advice was a stark reminder of the precarious position of women in a patriarchal society; without a living partner, they were vulnerable to starvation and despair.

Despite Catherine's insistence, Margaret maintained her boundary, telling Luke he needed not trouble himself to visit anymore. Luke accepted this dismissal with a sad resignation, rising to leave the house he had called home. But the next Saturday, he returned, seated on the doorstep, blubbering openly. He admitted that every other place seemed strange compared to the warmth of that threshold. Catherine relayed this to Margaret, who sighed, recognizing the tragedy of a man who knew his place but lacked the courage to claim it. "Poor Luke, he might come in for her, if he could know his place, and treat her like a married wife," Margaret observed, her voice softening.

When Luke hesitated, citing the difficulty of breaking his resolve, Catherine delivered her final verdict with the clarity of someone who understood the stakes of survival. "Sit outside like a fool, when a word will carry you inside," she commanded. "Now you humour her in everything, and then, if Poor Gerard come not home and claim her, you will be sure to have her." The scene closed not with a resolution of romance, but with a hard-won acknowledgment of reality: in a world devoid of men, the only path forward was to seize the hands of those willing to stand beside them, transforming loneliness into a shared, resilient existence.

Brother Clement had labored in Basel for over a year, weaving his voice through the cobblestones and confessional booths of the city, when Jerome appeared before him. Dust coated the younger friar's robes, but his eyes burned with a triumphant clarity. "Glory be to God, Brother Clement," Jerome declared, his tone vibrating with anticipation. "You may now journey to England alongside me."

"I stand ready, Brother Jerome," Clement replied, his demeanor shifting instantly from weary scholar to eager pilgrim. "I have awaited your summons for months. In the quiet intervals between teaching and prayer, I have diligently cultivated the English tongue."

"Tactful indeed," Jerome acknowledged. He explained that his delay had been necessary to secure extraordinary faculties from the Pope: the authority to collect funds for the Church's cause in England and to administer sacraments within the secular monasteries. "Now, gird your loins," Jerome commanded, his voice hardening into steel. "Let us depart and strike a decisive blow for the Church, and against the encroaching influence of the Franciscans."

Thus began their pilgrimage down the Rhine, a river that served as both artery and boundary. In the bustling metropolises, they shared the pulpit, their voices merging in a chorus of reform. Yet in the smaller hamlets, they diverged, taking opposite banks of the winding waterway until they converged once more at a preordained meeting point. Both were masters of rhetoric, yet their styles operated on distinct frequencies. Jerome's oratory was noble and imposing, a towering edifice of logic, though occasionally constrained by rigid dogma and a monotony that risked alienating the common ear. Clement, conversely, possessed a fluidity that defied categorization. His speech was a tapestry of variety, tender and gently persuasive, curling around the listener's reason and heart like smoke in a draftless room. Yet, when the moment demanded, Clement ascended. He soared with a prophetic intensity that left Jerome grounded. *Vehemens ut procella, excitatus ut torrens, incensus ut fulmen*—violent as a storm, roused as a torrent, inflamed as lightning—he thundered, flashed, and swept all before him with the rushing waves of his eloquence.

Jerome possessed the scholarly humility to admit such disparity, citing five reasons why he withheld samples of Clement's fiery Latin: the difficulty of translation, the brevity of time, prior publication, the existence of superior imitators, and the literary axiom that while uniformity suits peas, it constitutes a vice in books. Nevertheless, Clement employed a subtle strategy: he secretly investigated the specific sins plaguing each town before ascending the rostrum. Jerome, however, remained unbending, scorning the detour required to tailor his message to local vices.

At a great town situated several leagues from the Rhine, they climbed the same pulpit in succession. Jerome addressed the vanity of dress, a theme dear to his heart. He spoke with biting satire, and the congregation listened with complacent nods, for vanity was not their primary failing. Then Clement stepped forward to confront their true demon: drunkenness. It was a festering wound in these lands, shielded by clerical hypocrisy and popular prejudice. Clement delivered merciless blows drawn from Holy Writ and grim reality. He identified alcohol not merely as a vice, but as the nurturing mother of all other crimes, particularly theft and murder.

He recounted a recent parricide committed by a man intoxicated, and described how a gang of drunkards had roasted one of their own comrades alive in a neighboring village. "Your late prince," Clement proclaimed, his voice rising to a crescendo, "is rumored to have died of apoplexy, yet you know the truth: he died of drink. Last month, one of your aldermen perished miserably, drowned in his own stupor within a muddy puddle."

His gaze swept the crowd, piercing the collective shame. "Your children's backs go bare so that you may fill your bellies with the poison that turns you into the worst of beasts—silly as calves, yet fierce as boars. This deprives your families of sustenance and drives your souls straight to hell. I tell you, your town, yes, your entire nation, would sink to the bottom of human degradation if your women drank as you do. How long will they remain temperate, resisting nature to oppose the example of their husbands and fathers? Vice never stands still. Amend yourselves, or watch them descend to your level. Already in Bohemia, women drink alongside men."

"How does a drunken woman appear?" he demanded, the question hanging heavy in the air. "Would you wish to see your wives, your mothers, reduced to such a state?"

A roar of horror erupted from the assembly. Medieval audiences had not yet mastered the art of passive amusement; they reacted viscerally. "That strikes home!" cried the friar, seizing the momentum. "Do you suppose it shocks the All-Pure God less to witness a man, His noblest creation, transformed into a drunken beast, than it shocks you, creatures of sin and unreason, to see a woman degraded into something no better than yourselves?"

Clement concluded with two stark tableaux: the chaotic wreckage of a drunkard's household versus the serene order of a sober man's life. The details were so vivid, so brutally true, that the women fell into a collective swoon of "Ohing" and "Ahing," murmuring, "Eh, but that is a true word."

The discourse ignited a frenzy. Hearers clustered in knots; the men bristled with indignation. The women, however, rallied behind them, openly siding with the accused against the preacher. A married man, they argued, had a divine right to a drop; he needed it to sustain the labor of feeding his family. They refused to trade their sturdy drinkers for weak-willed "milksops." The hypocrisy was palpable.

That very evening, a mob of men cornered nearly a hundred individuals near Brother Clement, stuffing his wallet with coins and plucking roses from their own heads to offer him, kissing his habit and blessing him for undertaking the arduous task of curing their "sots."

Jerome observed the scene with a furrowed brow. "Drunkenness is not heresy, Clement," he remarked critically. "To dedicate an entire sermon to it seems... earthly."

As they continued their journey, Jerome discovered to his astonishment that Clement's serments penetrated the populace far deeper than his own. They compelled listeners to listen, to think, to weep, and occasionally, to amend their ways. "He possesses the art of sinking to their peg," Jerome mused, watching the crowds react. "Yet he retains the capacity to soar high enough at times."

On the whole, the phenomenon puzzled Jerome, who harbored a secret sense of superiority over his tenderer brother. Eventually, after traveling approximately two hundred miles, the situation ceased to puzzle him and began to displease him. He struggled to suppress the sentiment as petty and unworthy. "Souls differ like locks," Jerome reasoned aloud to himself, attempting to rationalize the divergence. "Preachers must differ like keys, or the Church remains locked shut, unable to open for God to pass through. And certainly, this novice holds the key to these northern souls, being himself a northern man."

And so they descended slowly along the Rhine, sometimes drifting a few miles downstream, but mostly walking the banks, preaching, teaching, and hearing confessions in the scattered towns and villages. Finally, they reached Düsseldorf.

There lay the small quay where Gerard and Denys had once boarded their boat upstream. The friars disembarked upon it. The streets stretched out before them, familiar and unchanged, save for the presence of "The Silver Lion." Nothing had altered in the cityscape, yet everything had shifted within the traveler who walked through it barefoot. His heart was calm and cold, his hands folded across his breast, his eyes bent meekly toward the earth—a true son of Dominic and the Holy Church, returning to a world he knew intimately, yet forever changed by the fires he had kindled.

"Eli," Catherine commanded, her voice cutting through the domestic haze with the precision of a surgeon's blade, "answer me one question like a man, and I shall ask no more today. What is wormwood?"

Eli faltered, momentarily paralyzed by the sudden demand upon his faculties, as if struck by a physical blow. Yet, he rallied quickly, offering a definition rooted in mere sensation rather than spirit. "It is something that tastes main bitter."

"That is a fair answer, my man, but not the one I seek," she replied, her eyes hardening.

"Then answer it yourself," he challenged, retreating behind the shield of his ignorance.

"And shall," Eli grunted, the weight of her scrutiny pressing down on him. "Wormwood is—to have two in the house a-doing nought, but waiting for thy shoes and mine."

The shaft struck home with devastating accuracy. Catherine's expression hardened further. "Methinks waiting for their best friend's coffin, that and nothing to do, are enow to make them worse than Nature meant. Why not set them up somewhere, to give 'em a chance?"

Eli hesitated, admitting his willingness but voicing a fear born of experience: "But I am afraid they will drink and gamble their very shelves away."

"Nay," Catherine countered, her tone leaving no room for doubt. "Dost thou take me for a simpleton? Of course I mean to watch them at the starting, and drive them with a loose rein, as the saying is." Her gaze sharpened, piercing the veil of his hesitation. "Where didst thou think of doing this? Not here; to divide our own custom."

"Not likely," Eli muttered. "I say Rotterdam against the world. Then I could start them."

Oh, self-deception! The true motive driving this entire maneuver was not merely commerce, but the desperate, aching proximity to little Gerard. Following many heated discussions and eager promises of amendment on these specific terms, Catherine departed for Rotterdam, accompanied by Kate, to hunt for a shop. Seeking a change of air and purpose, they soon located a premises in a respectable street, though it stood sadly out of order. Undeterred, they seized the opportunity; the dilapidation allowed them to secure it at a fraction of the cost. Instantly, they set to work, scrubbing the floors until they gleamed, fitting proper shelves for the burgeoning business, and rendering the dwelling-house habitable once more.

Meanwhile, Luke Peterson found himself constantly approaching Margaret, inquiring what he might do to assist her. Her response invariably fell in a tone laden with sorrowful resignation: "Nothing, Luke, nothing."

"What, you that are so clever, can you think of nothing for me to do for you?" he pleaded, his hope flickering like a candle in a draft.

"Nothing, Luke, nothing," she repeated, closing herself off.

But eventually, she varied her reply, opening a narrow crack in her defenses: "If you could make something to help my sweet sister Kate about."

The slave of love, consumed by devotion, consented joyfully. Soon, Luke had fashioned a small cart for Kate, cushioning it with care, and even yoked himself into it. At eventide, he drew her out of the town and along the pleasant boulevard, with Margaret and Catherine walking beside them. On the surface, it appeared a happier party than reality warranted. Kate, however, enjoyed the moment keenly; little Gerard was placed in her lap, and she doted on him with a tenderness that transformed the scene into something ethereal—a cherub carried by a little angel, or a rosebud resting in the cup of a lily.

Yet, beneath this fragile peace, the vulgar jeered. They mocked Luke, asking how a thistle tasted and questioning whether his mistress could not afford a horse with four legs instead of a man. Luke did not mind these taunts; he bore them with stoic silence. But Kate minded them for him.

"Thou hast made the cart for me, good Luke," she said, her voice trembling slightly. "'Twas much. I did ill to let thee draw me too; we can afford to pay some poor soul for that. I love my rides, and to carry little Gerard; but I'd liever ride no more than thou be mocked for't."

"Much I care for their tongues," Luke declared, his jaw set in defiance. "If I did care, I'd knock their heads together. I shall draw you till my mistress says give over."

"Luke, if you obey Kate, you will oblige me," Margaret interjected softly.

"Then I will obey Kate," Luke affirmed.

An honorable exception to the prevailing mockery was Jorian Ketel's wife, Joan. She spoke with a prophetic gravity that silenced the crowd. "That is strength well laid out, to draw the weak. And her prayers will be your guerdon; she is not long for this world; she smileth in pain."

These were the words of Joan, speaking of death and suffering. Single-minded Luke, however, dismissed such spiritual currency. "I do not want the poor lass's prayers," he stated firmly. "I do it to please my mistress, Margaret."

After that, Luke often pressed Margaret to give him something substantial to do, without success. But one day, seemingly tired of his persistent importuning, she turned on him. With a look and an accent that conveyed a depth of emotion I should in vain try to convey, she delivered her final command: "Find me my boy's father."

"To Mistress, they all whisper he is dead."

"Not so. They feed me still with hopes."

"Aye, to your face, but behind your back, the tongues are sharp enough to carve ice; they all say he is dead."

At this revelation, Margaret's tears began to flow, a silent deluge washing over her features. Luke whimpered for company, possessing the broad frame of a man but the fragile, fluttering heart of a girl.

"Prithee, weep not so, sweet mistress," he pleaded, his voice thick with empathy. "I'd bring him back to life an I could, rather than see thee weed so sore."

Margaret confessed she thought she wept because they were so double-tongued with her, weaving lies between their smiles. She recovered herself, laying her hand firmly on his shoulder, and spoke with a solemnity that cut through the gloom. "Luke, he is not dead. Dying men are known to have a strange sight. Listen, Luke! My poor father, when he was a-dying, and I, simple fool, was so happy, thinking he was going to get well altogether... he said to mother and me—he was sitting in that very chair where you are now, and mother was as might be here, and I was yonder making a sleeve—he said, 'I see him!' I see him! Just so. Not like a failing man at all, but all o' fire. 'Sore disfigured—on a great river—coming this way.'"

"Ah, Luke, if you were a woman, and had the feeling for me you think you have, you would pity me, and find him for me. Take a thought! The father of my child!"

"Alack, I would if I knew how," said Luke, his shoulders slumping under the weight of the impossible. "But how can I?"

"Nay, of course you cannot. I am mad to think it. But oh, if any one really cared for me, they would; that is all I know."

Luke reflected in silence for some time, the quiet stretching until it felt heavy enough to crush. "The old folk all say dying men can see more than living wights. Let me think: for my mind cannot gallop like thine. On a great river... Well, the Maas is a great river." He pondered, his brow furrowing as he mapped the geography of hope against the map of reality.

"Coming this way? Then if it 'twas the Maas, he would have been here by this time, so 'tis not the Maas. The Rhine is a great river, greater than the Maas; and very long. I think it will be the Rhine."

"And so do I, Luke; for Denys bade him come down the Rhine. But even if it is, he may turn off before he comes anigh his birthplace. He does not pine for me as I for him; that is clear. Luke, do you not think he has deserted me?" She wanted him to contradict her, to offer the comfort of denial, but he said, "It looks very like it; what a fool he must be!"

"What do we know?" objected Margaret imploringly, her voice trembling.

"Let me think again," said Luke. "I cannot gallop."

The result of this meditation was a plan born of desperation. He knew a station about sixty miles up the Rhine, where all the public boats put in; and he would go to that station, and try and cut the truant off. To be sure, he did not even know him by sight; but as each boat came in, he would mingle with the passengers, and ask if one Gerard was there. "And, mistress, if you were to give me a bit of a letter to him; for, with us being strangers, mayhap he won't believe a word I say."

"Good, kind, thoughtful Luke, I will (how I have undervalued thee!). But give me till supper-time to get it writ." At supper, she put a letter into his hand with a blush; it was a long letter, tied round with silk after the fashion of the day, and sealed over the knot.

Luke weighed it in his hand, with a shade of discontent, and said to her very gravely, "Say your father was not dreaming, and say I have the luck to fall in with this man, and say he should turn out a better bit of stuff than I think him, and come home to you then and there—what is to become of me?"

Margaret coloured to her very brow. "Oh, Luke, Heaven will reward thee. And I shall fall on my knees and bless thee; and I shall love thee all my days, sweet Luke, as a mother does her son. I am so old by thee: trouble ages the heart. Thou shalt not go 'tis not fair of me. Love maketh us to be all self."

"Humph!" said Luke. "And if," resumed he, in the same grave way, "yon scapegrace shall read thy letter, and hear me tell him how thou pinest for him, and yet, being a traitor, or a mere idiot, will not turn to thee—what shall become of me then? Must I die a bachelor, and thou fare lonely to thy grave, neither maid, wife, nor widow?"

Margaret panted with fear and emotion at this terrible piece of good sense, and the plain question which followed it. But at last she faltered out, "If, which our Lady be merciful to me, and forbid—Oh!"

"Well, mistress?"

"If he should read my letter, and hear thy words—and, sweet Luke, be just and tell him what a lovely babe he hath, fatherless, fatherless. Oh, Luke, can he be so cruel?"

"I trow not but if?"

"Then he will give thee up my marriage lines, and I shall be an honest woman, and a wretched one, and my boy will not be a bastard; and of course, then we could both go into any honest man's house that would be troubled with us; and even for thy goodness this day, I will—I will—ne'er be so ungrateful as go past thy door to another man's."

"Ay, but will you come in at mine? Answer me that!"

"Oh, ask me not! Some day, perhaps, when my wounds leave bleeding. Alas, I'll try. If I don't fling myself and my child into the Maas. Do not go, Luke! do not think of going! 'Tis all madness from first to last."

But Luke was as slow to forego an idea as to form one. His reply showed how fast love was making a man of him. "Well," said he, "madness is something, anyway; and I am tired of doing nothing for thee; and I am no great talker. To-morrow, at peep of day, I start. But hold, I have no money. My mother, she takes care of all mine; and I ne'er see it again."

Then Margaret took out Catherine's gold angel, which had escaped so often, and gave it to Luke; and he set out on his mad errand.

It did not, however, seem so mad to him as to us. It was a superstitious age; and Luke acted on the dying man's dream, or vision, or illusion, or whatever it was, much as we should act on respectable information.

But Catherine was downright angry when she heard of it. "To send the poor lad on such a wild-goose chase! But you are like a many more girls; and mark my words; by the time you have worn that Luke fairly out, and made him as sick of you as a dog, you will turn as fond on him as a cow on a calf, and 'Too late' will be the cry."

The two friars reached Holland from the south just twelve hours after Luke started up the Rhine. Thus, wild-goose chase or not, the parties were nearing each other, and rapidly too. For Jerome, unable to preach in low Dutch, now began to push on towards the coast, anxious to get to England as soon as possible. And having the stream with them, the friars would in point of fact have missed Luke by passing him in full stream below his station, but for the incident which I am about to relate.

About twenty miles above the station Luke was making for, Clement landed to preach in a large village; and towards the end of his sermon he noticed a grey nun weeping. He spoke to her kindly, and asked her what was her grief.

"Nay," said she, "'tis not for myself flow these tears; 'tis for my lost friend. Thy words reminded me of what she was, and what she is, poor wretch. But you are a Dominican, and I am a Franciscan nun."

"It matters little, my sister, if we are both Christians, and if I can aid thee in aught."

The nun looked in his face, and said, "These are strange words, but methinks they are good; and thy lips are oh, most eloquent. I will tell thee our grief."

She then let him know that a young nun, the darling of the convent, and her bosom friend, had been lured away from her vows, and after various gradations of sin, was actually living in a small inn as chambermaid, in reality as a decoy, and was known to be selling her favours to the wealthier customers. She added

Waiting for an earnest letter rarely leaves the mind in a state of suspended animation; rather, it acts as a catalyst, igniting a volatile mixture of hope and dread. Margaret, clutching her correspondence, poured her entire spiritual and emotional reservoir into validating her dying father's vision—or perhaps his delusion. Once the ink had dried and Luke departed, a crushing sense of disillusionment settled over her. She recoiled from her own credulity, her conscience pricking sharply regarding Luke's absence, while Catherine's reproaches only served to deepen the wound. The cost of these false elevations of spirit was a plunge into a despondency far darker than before.

In this hollowed-out state, she was discovered by Joan Ketel, a steadfast friend forged in recent trials. Joan entered radiating a singular, luminous idea. "Margaret, I hold the cure for thy malady," she declared, her voice bright against the gloom. "The hermit of Gouda—a wondrous saint. In his mood, he can foretell the future, discerning the threads of destiny before they are woven."

"Ay," Margaret murmured, her hope extinguished like a candle in a draft.

With considerable effort, Joan persuaded her to journey to Gouda. They carried a basket of butter and eggs, provisions for the ascetic, and walked toward the cave. Their bond, however, was rooted in a tragedy that had nearly claimed Jorian Ketel mere months prior. Six weeks ago, Jorian had succumbed to a violent bowel affliction, a torment that began with agonizing spasms. When the acute pain subsided, leaving him emaciated, a more insidious failure took hold: neither liquid nor solid could remain in his stomach for a single minute. The physicians, baffled, prescribed a chicken boiled with a golden angel, a remedy that shared the fate of the humblest fare—it passed through him untouched. Next, they steeped the thumb of St. Sergius in beef broth, yielding the same sterile result. Desperate, Joan wept to Margaret to borrow linen for a shroud.

"Let me see him," Margaret commanded, stepping into the chamber. She felt his pulse, a fleeting tremor against her fingers. "Ah!" she cried, her eyes widening. "They have not touched the root. Open the window! Thou art stifling him. Change all his linen."

The medieval wife protested, "Alack, woman, what for? Why foul more linen for a dying man?"

"Do as thou art bid," Margaret said, her voice dull with authority, and exited the room. Joan, compelled by the force of her friend's conviction, complied. Margaret returned with her apron heavy with a flowering herb. She prepared a decoction, tasting a spoonful herself with a feigned, hypocritical savor. "That is fair," Jorian managed, offering a feeble spark of humor. "Why, 'tis sweet, and now 'tis bitter."

She engaged him in conversation immediately after he drank. This bitter-sweet concoction became his anchor. Observing its effect, Margaret played her cards with the patience of her sex: she mixed a drop of Schiedam gin into the third spoonful and a beaten yolk of egg into the seventh. With this alchemy, she coaxed his body from Death's grasp. Finally, Nature, having been patted on the back rather than kicked beneath the bed, restored Jorian Ketel to his feet. Yet, the healer demanded a solemn oath from them both: never to reveal her guilty deed. "They would imprison me, tearing me away from my child," she had whispered. The simple herb that saved Jorian was known as sweet feverfew, gathered from his own garden, spotted by her eagle eye growing through the window lattice.

Thus, Margaret and Joan arrived at the hermit's cave, placing their offerings on the small stone platform. Margaret pressed her lips to the aperture designed for prayer and spoke: "Holy hermit, we bring thee butter and eggs of the finest quality. And I, a poor deserted girl, a wife in title yet no wife in truth, and mother of the sweetest babe, come to pray thee tell me whether he is quick or dead, true to his vows or false."

A faint, rasping voice drifted from the darkness: "Trouble me not with the trifles of earth, but send me a holy friar; I am dying."

"Alas!" Margaret cried, her heart breaking. "Is it even so, poor soul? Then let us enter to aid thee."

"Saints forbid! Thy voice is that of a woman. Send me a holy friar."

They retreated as they had come. Joan, unable to contain her outrage, snapped, "Are women imps of darkness then, that they must not approach a dying bed?"

But Margaret was too deeply bowed by grief to respond. Joan offered rough consolation, which went unheeded until she mentioned Jorian. "And Jorian will speak out ere long; he is just on the boil. He is very grateful to thee, believe it."

"Seeing is believing," Margaret replied, her tone quiet and laced with bitterness.

"Not but what he suspects thou might have saved him with something more extraordinary than yon common herb," Joan continued, weaving the tale of Jorian's gratitude. "'A man of my inches to be cured with feverfew,' he says. 'Why, if there is a sorry herb,' he remarks. 'Why, I was thinking of pulling all mine up,' he confessed. I told him remedies are none the better for being far-fetched; you and feverfew cured him when the grand medicines failed utterly. So I said, 'You may go down on your four bones to feverfew.' But indeed, he is grateful at bottom; you are all his thought and all his chatter. But he sees Gerard's kin approaching, good friends, and he said only last night—"

"Well?"

"He made me vow not to tell thee."

"Prithee, tell me."

"Well, he said: 'If I reveal what little I know, it will not bring him back, and it will set them all by the ears. I wish I had more headpiece,' he lamented. 'I am sore perplexed. But least said is soonest mended.' That is his favorite phrase; he returns to it from a mile away."

Margaret shook her head slowly. "Ay, we are wading in deep waters, my poor babe and me."

It was Saturday night, and Luke was nowhere to be found.

"Poor Luke!" Margaret sighed. "It was very good of him to undertake such an errand."

"He is one out of a hundred," Catherine responded warmly. "Mother, dost thou think he would be kind to little Gerard?"

"I am sure he would. So do thou be kinder to him when he returns! Wilt thou promise?"

"Ay."

***

Brother Clement, guided by the nuns, navigated a sharp bend in the river, striding with renewed vigor toward a station miles closer to the coast than where Luke lay in ambush for Gerard Eliassoen. The following dawn, he started early, reaching Rotterdam by noon. He hastened directly to the port, eager to meet Jerome. Upon arrival, he observed several monks of his order along the quay; he approached them, but Jerome was absent. He asked one if Jerome had arrived.

"Surely, brother," the monk replied.

"Prithee, where is he?"

"Where? Why, there!" the monk pointed, gesturing to a ship cutting through the waves in full sail. Clement noticed immediately that all the friars were gazing seaward.

"What, gone without me! Oh, Jerome! Jerome!" he cried, his voice cracking with anguish.

Several friars turned, staring at him. "You must be Brother Clement," one said eventually. On hearing this, they embraced him with brotherly warmth and handed him a letter Jerome had entrusted to them. It was a hasty scrawl, written in cold ink. The author informed him dispassionately that a ship was about to depart for England and that he loathed losing time. He urged Clement to follow if he wished, but advised him much better to stay behind and preach to his own countrymen.

"Give the glory to God, brother; thou hast a wonderful power over Dutch hearts; but thou art no match for those haughty islanders: thou art too tender," the letter read. "Know thou that on the way I met one who asked for thee under the name thou didst bear in the world. Be on thy guard! Let not the world catch thee again by any silken net. Remember, Solitude, Fasting, and Prayer are the sword, spear, and shield of the soul. Farewell."

Clement was profoundly shocked and mortified by this contemptuous desertion and the icy missive. He promised the good monks he would sleep at the convent and preach wherever the prior appointed, for Jerome had once raised him to the skies as a preacher. Yet, wounded to the quick, he withdrew abruptly, needing solitude to process the betrayal. He questioned himself: Was there some incurable fault in him, repulsive to so true a son of Dominic? Or was Jerome himself devoid of that Christian Love which St. Paul had placed above Faith itself? Shipwrecked together, saved on the same fragment of wreckage—his pupil, his penitent, his son in the Church—and now, for four hundred miles, his fellow traveler in Christ, only to be shaken off like dirt at the first opportunity with harsh, cold disdain.

"Why are worldly hearts no colder nor less trustworthy than this," he muttered. "The only one that ever truly loved me lies in a

The following day, a Sunday consecrated to commerce rather than contemplation, transformed Catherine's dwelling in the Hoog Straet into a hive of industrious anticipation. The shop stood unveiled and fully prepared, a testament to the relentless energy of Cornelis and Sybrandt, whose names were already etched above the threshold alongside their heraldic emblem: a white lamb suckling a gilded sheep. Eli had arrived bearing fresh provisions from his store, eager to fortify their enterprise before the official opening. Within the domestic sphere, the atmosphere shimmered with a feverish optimism; the parents' hearts burned with the heat of reinvention. They strolled through the garden, their steps synchronized by a shared resolve to abandon the drudgery of their agrarian past. "We are weary of the soil," Sybrandt declared, his voice ringing with the clarity of conversion. "It is far sweeter to mint coin than to squander it upon wild oats. Let us bind ourselves to business as tightly as wax to the seal."

Catherine's mother, her ears perpetually attuned to the currents of household fortune, intercepted this vow through an open lattice. She relayed the news to Eli: the evening supper would serve as an inaugural banquet, welcoming Margaret and her son. It was to be a rite of passage where seasoned merchants would dispense wisdom upon the converts fleeing the unremunerative fields of agriculture, and where vintage wine would toast the triumph of commerce over the earth. Margaret descended to assist her mother-in-law, driven by a dual necessity to aid the household and to shed the heavy languor that had settled upon her spirit. Both women emerged flushed, their complexions glowing with the vibrant red of embers, signaling a vitality long dormant.

Suddenly, the front door swung open, admitting Joan carrying a crisp salad harvested from Jorian's garden. "He cut it specifically for you, Margaret," Joan announced, her tone laced with playful rivalry. "You are his sole conversation; I fear I shall become jealous. I reminded him of today's feast, but alas, what a thunderous sermon echoed from the new church! Preaching? I have never witnessed such a spectacle until this hour."

"I wish I had been present," Margaret murmured, her voice tinged with a spiritual thirst. "I am parched, deprived of the dew from heaven."

"Why, he preaches again this afternoon," Joan countered, her eyes darting toward the street. "Yet, perhaps your duties demand you remain here."

"Not she," Catherine interjected firmly. "Go, if your heart inclines you."

"Indeed," Margaret hesitated, "but methinks I should not dampen the spirits at the table if I could attend, warmed by the fervor of a divine discourse."

"Then you must move with alacrity," Joan advised, scanning the horizon. "Observe the congregation gathering; look, even the holy friar approaches. Oh, blessed be our salvation, Margaret—the hermit! We have forgotten him entirely." With a burst of kinetic energy, the active woman launched herself from the doorway, crossed the cobblestones, and halted the friar mid-stride. She returned moments later, breathless. "Behold, I was determined to view him in person."

"What did he impart to thee?"

"He spoke thus: 'My daughter, I shall visit him ere sunset, God willing,'" Joan recounted, her voice softening. "His voice was the sweetest melody. Yet, oh, my mistresses, observe his gaunt cheeks, those hollow features of a young man, and his great eyes, remarkably akin to yours, Margaret."

"I possess a strong desire to hear him," Margaret confessed. "However, my cap lacks cleanliness, and the assembly will surely wear their pristine white mutches."

"There," Catherine instructed, reaching into a woven basket, "take my handkerchief. I cannot spare the child; I require him for my poor Kate, who suffers from one of her grievous days."

Margaret ascended the stairs, leaving the boy behind. She found Kate confined to bed, her body wracked with agony. "How fares thou, sweetheart?" Margaret asked gently. "No inquiry is necessary; thou art in profound pain. Yet, thou smilest. Behold, I have brought thee one thou lovest."

"Two, by my reckoning," Kate replied, her countenance radiant with an angelic serenity despite the torment. Suddenly, a violent spasm seized her, a convulsion that might have caused onlookers to roar like bulls in their distress.

"In your lap?" Margaret queried, responding to the suffering girl's gesture.

"Nay, he is too heavy, and thou art in such anguish," Kate protested weakly.

"I love him too dearly to feel his weight," was the tender rebuttal. Seizing this brief respite, Margaret attended to her own preparations.

"I am bound for the church," she announced, "to listen to a magnificent preacher."

Kate sighed, a sound of resigned longing. "A moment ago, I was eager to depart; such is my condition for the past month. Up and down, up and down, like the restless waves of the Zuyder Zee. I would sooner remain beside thee; simply say the word!"

"Nay," Kate pleaded, her eyes filling with tears. "Pray, go. Bring me back every syllable. Alas, that I cannot journey myself."

This plea resolved Margaret's hesitation. She kissed Kate, glanced beneath her eyelashes at the sleeping infant, and let out a small, sorrowful sigh. "I deem I must not," she whispered. "I could never bear to kiss him lightly; nor could my father endure waking a child by day or night. Speak to Aunt Kate the two new words thou hast acquired when it is thy pleasure to rise." With a final, loving glance over her shoulder, she departed, shutting the door with an inaudible click.

Downstairs, Catherine turned to Joan. "Will you lend me a hand to peel these vegetables?"

"That I will, dame," Joan responded promptly.

Cooking resumed with a silent, vigorous intensity. "Listen well, Joan," Catherine commanded, chopping rhythmically. "Those who assist me in preparing and serving the meat shall share in eating it; that is the law of our house."

"There exist harsher statutes in Holland than that," Joan retorted cheerfully. "Your will is my delight, mistress. My Luke awaits his supper in the air; fortunately, he is digging today."

Margaret descended the stairs. "Eh, woman, that is a grim trade indeed."

She had merely washed her face and arranged her hair, and suddenly, she appeared transformed. "Rotterdam, that for you!" Catherine exclaimed, snapping her fingers in admiration of the capital city's influence on beauty. "Give us a buss, hussy! Mind now, Eli will not wait for the Duke to join us. Loiter not after your church service concludes."

Joan and Catherine accompanied Margaret to the threshold, standing sentinel as she ventured down the street. Among the mundane routines of housewives, the act of departing home holds half the drama of any event. Catherine paused to comment on the procession. "There, Joan, it feels as though I had just dispatched my own daughter to church, standing guard to ensure her safety—a vigilance I have performed countless times. Joan, lass, she will not tolerate a single word spoken against our Gerard. If he remains alive, he has treated her cruelly; hence, my bowels ache for the poor wretch. Being older and wiser, I intend to wed her to that simple Luke, and there shall be an end to her suffering. What is one grandchild more?"
