To comprehend the architecture of Margaret's awakening, one must first understand the absolute sanctity of the space she inhabited—a cathedral under construction, a skeletal framework of faith waiting to be filled with divine light. It was a colossal edifice, far from completion, a testament to human endurance against the slow march of time. While the side aisles bore roofs, the grand mid-aisle and the sacred chancel remained open to the elements; yet, the pillars and arches stood in near-perfect symmetry, some already washed in the purity of fresh lime. Only a single window pierced the darkness with glass; the rest were gaping wounds in the stone, jagged apertures that should have invited chaos. But today, these imperfections forged a new kind of beauty.

It was a glorious summer afternoon, and the sunlight, fractured by the irregular openings, danced across the broken surfaces in marvelous, shifting forms. Beams of glory sliced through the dark, cool shadows of the side aisles, illuminating the white pillars beyond with a dazzling radiance. The central aisle itself was checkered with light and shade, the shadows offering a soothing coolness that defied the heat, while the lights shimmered like patches of amber diamonds animated by heavenly fire. Above, the blue sky vaulted the lofty nave, pressing close as if to embrace the congregation. The white caps of the women created a sea of innocence contrasting exquisitely with the vivid azure dome. Though the massive central aisle was crammed with souls, a profound stillness reigned; the words and the mellow, earnest voice of the preacher held them mute in a collective suspension of breath.

Margaret stood spellbound, captivated by the beauty, the devotion, and that great, overwhelming calm. Seeking refuge from the direct assault of the sun, she moved silently behind a pillar in the north aisle. Even there, unable to catch every syllable, a sweet, devotional languor crept over her, smoothing the turbulent waves of her heart until they ran like oil on water. Leaning against the stone, her eyes half-closed, she felt the goodness of the moment, drifting into a soft, dreamlike reverie.

Presently, she noticed a lady vacating an excellent seat opposite to escape the scorching rays pouring from the window. Seizing the opportunity, Margaret moved swiftly and softly, securing the spot. Now positioned beside a pillar in the south aisle, merely fifty feet from the pulpit, she was close enough to hear every word, though her attention was momentarily fractured by a peculiar sight. A shadow of a man's head and shoulders bobbed up and down with such drollery that she struggled to suppress a smile. Yet, it was not inherently funny; it was the sexton, Jorian Ketel, diligently digging. She confirmed this by glancing backward through the window, tracing the source of the movement.

As she watched Jorian, a specific tone of the preacher's voice struck her ear with such distinct force that it seemed to physically vibrate her very bones. Her hand flew instinctively to her bosom, startled by the sudden intensity of the thrill. Turning sharply, she sought the source, but the preacher's back was turned, revealing only the stark silhouette of his tonsure. She sighed; that shaved crown, being all she could see, effectively contradicted the profound depth of the voice emanating from it. Still, leaning forward with downcast eyes, she hoped for that accent to return. It did not. Instead, the entire voice grew strangely resonant, rising and falling as the speaker warmed, waking faint echoes of a thousand happy memories. She refused to look up, unwilling to dispel the melancholic pleasure the voice inspired.

Then, in the midst of an eloquent passage, the preacher stopped. Margaret almost sighed aloud; the soothing music had abruptly ceased. Could the sermon end already? No; she scanned the room, but the people remained motionless. Suddenly, many faces turned toward her. She looked behind her sharply, expecting a threat, but found nothing. Startled countenances nearby now fixed their gaze on the preacher. Following their视线, she saw him in the pulpit, his face resembling that of a staring corpse. The friar's eyes, naturally large and magnified by the hollow thinness of his cheeks, were dilated to a supernatural size, glaring directly at her from a bloodless visage.

She cringed, turning fearfully, convinced some terrible entity lurked near her. But there was nothing; she was merely the peripheral figure of a listening crowd. In this moment of confusion, the church erupted into commotion. Figures rose everywhere, craning forward; hundreds of agitated faces swung between the friar and Margaret. The rustling of caps turned rapidly created a deafening din, followed by shrieks from nervous women and the buzzing murmur of men. Seeing so many eyes leveled at her, Margaret shrank terrified behind the pillar, casting one scared, hurried glance at the preacher.

In that fleeting instant, she caught an expression on his stricken face that made her shiver violently. She turned faint and sank down onto a heap of chips left by the workmen, burying her face in her hands. The sermon resumed, but she heard only the sound, not the sense. Her mind spun in a whirlwind, refusing to coalesce into any thought other than this: *On that prodigy-struck face, I had seen a look stamped.* The recollection of that gaze now made her quiver from head to toe. For that look was **Recognition**.

After wavering for some time, the sermon concluded in a strain of exalted, feverish eloquence that drove the crowd to forget the preacher's strange pause and ghastly stare. Margaret mingled hastily with the throng and exited the church. As the congregation dispersed toward their homes, she paused at the door and retreated into the churchyard, walking straight to Peter's grave. Despite her poverty, she had provided him with a slab and a headstone. Sitting upon the stone, she kissed it tenderly, then threw her apron over her head to conceal her hair from the world.

"Father," she whispered, addressing the silent earth, "thou hast often heard me say I am wading in deep waters; but now I begin to think God only knows the bottom of them. I will follow that friar round the world, but I will see him at arm's length. And he shall tell me why he looked towards me like a dead man wakened; and not a soul behind me. Oh, Father, you often praised me here: speak a word for me there. For I am wading in deep waters."

Her father's tomb commanded a commanding side view of the church door. Upon that tomb, with her face veiled, she sat, waylaying the holy preacher, ready to confront the mystery that had just shattered her peace.

The sanctuary, checkered with shafts of sunlight and crowned by a canopy of celestial purple, acted as a balm to Father Clement, soothing and enchanting him much as it had done for Margaret. More profoundly, it transported his consciousness directly to the Architect of all benevolent purity. His gaze swept across the great nave, crowded with his compatriots—a thousand snow-white caps, intricately filigreed with gold. Though he had traversed hundreds of leagues, he had witnessed nothing comparable to this congregation save for the vast, silent expanses of winter snow. That morning, he had thundered with judgment; yet this sweet afternoon seemed discordant with such threats. His heart ached with compassion for the multitude; indeed, these poor souls heard scarcely more of divine love from the pulpit in those days than the heathens themselves. He proclaimed the glad tidings of salvation, and the people hung upon his gentle, earnest tongue. He was not among those preachers who gyrate in the pulpit like weathercocks atop steeples; rather, he moved the hearts of others far more than his own physical frame allowed. Yet, he did not entirely neglect those dwelling in the shadows. Warm with this theme, determined that not a single soul in that throng should miss the joyful message of Christ's love, he turned his attention toward the south aisle.

There, bathed in a luminous stream from the window, stood the radiant visage of Margaret Brandt. He gazed upon it without initial emotion; it merely numbed him, soul and body alike. Soon, however, the words died in his throat, and he trembled as he fixed his glare upon her. There she stood, her auburn hair washed in sunbeams, glittering like the halo of a saint, her face glowing doubly bright—its own inherent beauty intensified by the golden light framing it. Leaning lightly against a white column, she listened with tender, downcast lashes. He had seen her listen to him so a hundred times before. There was no alteration in her essence; this was the blooming Margaret he had left behind, merely a shade riper, more lovely. He started at her presence, his eyes wide with monstrous astonishment, his cheeks drained of color. The crowd faded from his sight. He heard, as in a dream, a rustling and rising throughout the church, yet he could not tear his prodigy-struck eyes from that face—all life, all bloom, all beauty, and that wondrous auburn hair glistening gloriously in the sun. He gazed, convinced she must vanish into thin air. She remained.

In an instant, she looked at him, fully. Her own violet eyes! At this revelation, he was beside himself, his lips parting to shriek her name, when she turned her head swiftly. Soon after, she vanished, leaving behind only one final glance. Though rapid as lightning, this encounter met his gaze and left her trembling, her mind whirling, while he panted, gripping the pulpit convulsively. For this glance, though devoid of explicit recognition, was that startled, inquiring, nameless, indescribable look that precedes acknowledgment. He made a mighty effort, muttering something incomprehensible to anyone nearby, then feebly resumed his discourse, stammering and babbling for a while. Gradually, forcing himself to view her absence as a vision from another realm, he rose into a state of unnatural excitement, concluding his sermon in a style of eloquence that electrified the simple minds present, bordering on rhapsody.

When the sermon ended, he sat upon the pulpit stool, terribly shaken. Presently, an idea very characteristic of the era seized him. He had sought her grave at Sevenbergen in vain. Now, she had appeared to him, revealing that she was buried here—probably hard by that very pillar where her spirit had manifested. Once adopted, this notion settled upon his mind with the certainty of fact. He felt he needed only to speak to the sexton, whom he saw to his great disgust working during the service, to learn the precise spot of her interment.

The church was now utterly empty. Descending from the pulpit, he stepped through an aperture in the south wall onto the grass and approached the sexton. He recognized him instantly, though Jorian never suspected the poor lad, whose life he had once saved, hidden beneath the guise of this holy friar. The loss of his shapely beard had wonderfully altered the outline of the boy's face. This change affected him even more than his tonsure, his short hair sprinkled with premature grey, and his cheeks thinned and paled by rigorous fasts and vigils.

"My son," said Friar Clement softly, "if you retain any memory of those whom you lay in the earth, pray tell me: is any Christian buried inside the church, near one of the pillars?"

"Nay, father," replied Jorian, "here in the churchyard lie buried all that are buried. Why?"

"No matter. Pray tell me then where lieth Margaret Brandt."

"Margaret Brandt?" Jorian stared stupidly at the speaker. "She died about three years ago, and was buried here."

"Oh, that is another matter," said Jorian. "That was before my time; the vicar could tell you, likely, if so be she was a gentlewoman, or at least rich enough to pay him his fee."

"Alas, my son, she was poor—and paid a heavy penalty for it—but born of decent folk. Her father, Peter, was a learned physician; she came hither from Sevenbergen—to die."

When Clement had uttered these words, his head sank upon his breast, and he seemed to possess neither the power nor the wish to question Jorian further. One doubts even if he knew where he stood; he was lost in the past. Jorian put down his spade, standing upright in the grave with his arms akimbo, saying sulkily, "Are you making a fool of me, holy sir, or has some wag been making a fool of you!" Having thus relieved his mind, he proceeded to dig again with a vigor that showed his somewhat irritable temper was ruffled.

Clement gazed at him with a puzzled but gently reproachful eye, for the tone was rude and the words unintelligible. Good-natured, though crusty, Jorian had not thrown up three spadefuls ere he became ashamed of himself. "Why, what a base churl am I to speak thus to thee, holy father; and thou standing there, looking at me like a lamb. Aha! I have it; 'tis Peter Brandt's grave you would fain see, not Margaret's. He does lie here; hard by the west door. There; I'll show you."

And he laid down his spade, putting on his doublet and jerkin to accompany the friar. He did not know there was anybody sitting on Peter's tomb. Still less did he realize she was watching for this holy friar. Pietro Vanucci and Andrea did not recognize him without his beard. Indeed, the fact remains that a beard which has never known a razor grows in a very picturesque and characteristic form, becoming a defining feature of the face; so that its removal may, in some cases, prove an effectual disguise.

While Jorian adjusted the buttons of his doublet and tightened the lacing of his jerkin, preparing to descend to Peter's tomb, his tongue refused to remain silent. "They used to call him a magician out in Sevenbergen," he began, his voice carrying the weight of gossip sharpened by grief. "And they say he bestowed a final touch of his craft upon them before departing. He claimed to have seen Margaret's son descending the Rhine clad in noble attire, laden with wealth, yet his visage marred by a foreign blade, lacking the full complement of limbs he departed with. But, dear heart, nothing came of it. Margaret still wastes away waiting for her boy; and Peter lies as still as his neighbors, save that she has placed a stone slab over him to keep him fixed in his grave—as you shall witness."

Jorian pressed both palms against the rough edge of the fresh mound, rising slightly as if to peer deeper into the earth, when the friar's hand, trembling with suppressed emotion, settled upon his shoulder. "How long since Peter Brandt passed?" the monk whispered, his voice strained.

"About two months. Why ask?"

"And his daughter interred him, you say?"

"Nay, I performed the burial rites, though she bore the cost and commissioned the stone."

"Then—but he acknowledged only one daughter, Margaret?"

"No more, at least, that he ever admitted."

"Then you believe Margaret is... is she alive?"

"Believe? Why, I should be dead without such faith. Riddle me that."

"Alas, how can I doubt? You love her!"

"Not more than reason demands, being a husband and father to four sturdy knaves like myself. Nay, the truth is simpler: she saved my life scarcely six weeks past. Had she perished, I could not have endured. Blessed be your heart, I could barely keep food upon my stomach; physicians declared me lost. My Joan remarked, 'Tis time to buy thee a shroud.' 'I dare say, so 'tis,' I replied, 'but try borrowing one first.' Then in came my lady, this Margaret—who, by your reckoning, died three years ago—she opened the casements, shifted me from my prone position, and cured me in the blink of an eye. But with what? Herein lies the pinch; with the most despicable herb, plucked from my own garden, sweet feverfew. An herb, quotha, 'tis a weed; leastways, it was a weed until it restored me. Yet now, whenever I pass my hunch, I doff my cap and declare, 'Fly service to ye!' Why, how now, Father? You wax pale, then flush red, then turn ashen. Pray, what is the matter? What, in Heaven's name, troubles you?"

"The surprise—the joy—the wonder—the terror," Clement gasped, his composure fracturing.

"Why does it concern thee? Are you of kin to Margaret Brandt?"

"Nay; but I knew one who loved her with such ferocity that her death nearly extinguished his soul, body and spirit. And yet you claim she lives. And I believe you."

Jorian stared, the silence stretching taut between them before he spoke gravely. "Father, you have interrogated me thoroughly, and I have answered with truth. Now, for our Lady's sake, answer me but two questions. Did you, in very sooth, know one who cherished this poor girl? Where?"

Clement hovered on the brink of confession, yet the memory of Jerome's letter caused him to recoil, shrinking from the name he had once worn in the world. "I knew him in Italy," he murmured.

"If you knew him, you can reveal his name," Jorian urged cautiously.

"His name was Gerard Eliassoen."

"Oh, but this is strange. Stay, what prompted you to assert Margaret Brandt was dead?"

"I stood beside Gerard when a missive arrived from Margaret Van Eyck. It informed him that the woman he loved lay buried. Let me sit, for my strength fails me. Foul play! Foul play!"

"Father," Jorian said, his tone shifting from inquiry to resolve, "I thank Heaven for sending you to me. Sit; you resemble a specter, fasting yourself to weakness. My mind misgives me; I feel I hold the key to this riddle. If I do, there are two knaves in this town whose heads I would fain batter to pieces as I crush this mold beneath my boot." He clenched his teeth, hoisting his long spade high above his head and bringing it down furiously upon the earth several times.

"Foul play? You have never spoken a truer word in your life. If you know where Gerard hides now, lose no time in showing him the trap they have set. Mine is but a dull head, but sometimes the slow hound puzzles out the scent. Go on. I believe you and I have seized two ends of the same stick, and a dreadfully foul one at that."

After dispensing with the usual preliminaries of his station, Jorian cut straight to the core of the narrative. Employed by the burgomaster of Tergou to repair the flooring of an upper chamber, he had grown curious during a sudden visit to retrieve his tools. Hearing loud arguments below, he had propped himself upon his stomach and listened. The burgomaster spoke of a letter Cornelis and Sybrandt intended to deliver to Gerard, replacing one carried by Hans Memling. "'It seems their will was good, but their stomach was small,'" the official had boasted, producing a drawer brimming with silver to embolden the thieves. "If they succeeded, each should fill their hand with as much as it could hold."

"Well, Father," Jorian continued, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper, "I paid little heed then, save for the bargain itself, which haunted my dreams all night. Consider this: next dawn, who should I behold emerging from their homes but Cornelis and Sybrandt, each sporting a blackened eye? 'Oho,' I muttered, 'what mark has Hans Memling left upon you? Well now, I hope that is all you have suffered for your pains.' Did they not hasten to the burgomaster's residence? I retreated to my hiding place."

At this juncture, the monk's nostrils flared, his restless eyes betraying the tension coiling within him.

"Well, Father," Jorian pressed on, "the burgomaster summoned them to that very room. He held a letter in his grasp. Though I am no scholar, I possess as many eyes as the Pope, and I witnessed the drawer open. Those two knaves thrust in a hand each and drew it out overflowing. Saints in glory, how they struggled to cram more in, greed gnawing at them! And Sybrandt—he had daubed his palm in something viscous, I suspect glue, managing to drag one or two pieces away stuck fast to his skin. He! He! He! 'Tis a sin to laugh. Thus, fortune favored the wrong side as custom dictates; they completed the trick, yet how they executed it remains unknown until Judgment Day. Go on, they left their immortal treasures in that drawer. Well, they secured a handful of silver; the Devil won the worst of that bargain. There, Father, that weighs light on my mind. Often I yearned to confide in someone, but dared not speak to the women; otherwise, Margaret would have been left friendless in the world. For those two black-hearted villains are the favorites. Is it not always so? Have they not recently acquired a splendid new establishment in this very town, on the Hoog Straet? There you may view their sign: a gilt sheep and a lambkin. A pair of wolves suckling their dam would be nearer the mark. And there the entire family feasts today; oh, 'tis a fine world. What, not a word, holy father? You sit like stone, offering not even a curse upon them, the stony-hearted miscreants. Was it not enough that the poor lad stood alone in a strange land? Must his own flesh and blood conspire to bury the sole blessing his enemies spared him? And consider her, pining and wasting these years, seated at the window gazing down the street for Gerard! So constant, so tender, so true: my wife insists no woman ever loved a man more faithfully than she loves the boy those villains have torn from her side. Not a single day passes without her weeping salt tears for him. And when I reflect that, but for those two greedy, lying knaves, that winsome lad—whose life I saved—might walk beside her today, the happiest soul in Holland; and that sweet lass, who saved my life, might rest her cheek upon her sweetheart's shoulder, the blissest in Holland instead of the saddest; oh, I thirst for their blood, the nasty, sneaking, lying, cogging, cowardly, heartless, bowelless—how now?"

The monk leaped wildly to his feet, his complexion draining to a livid hue of fury and despair. With both hands clenched and raised high, he charged headlong from the spot. Such was the magnitude of this inarticulate eruption of rage that Jorian's petty anger evaporated at the sight, leaving him standing in stunned dismay after the human tempest he had unleashed.

Absorbed in his shock, Jorian felt his arm seized by a small, tremulous hand. It was Margaret Brandt. He started; her presence there, at that precise moment, seemed profoundly unnatural. She

Supper-time descended upon the household, casting a warm, domestic glow over the gathered family. The board was laden, the air thick with the savory perfume of cooking meats, yet a profound silence hung over the scene. Margaret was absent. To Catherine's astonishment, Eli declared he would wait for her return.

"Why, I told her you would not wait for the duke," someone remarked, the irony sharp in the quiet room.

"She is not the duke," Eli replied, his voice steady despite the tension coiling in his chest. "She is a poor, good lass who has waited not minutes, but years, for a graceless son of mine. You may set the meat upon the board; we shall eat without further loss of time once she arrives."

The aroma of the smoking dishes proved irresistible. Relenting to the sensory pull of hunger, Eli gestured. "She will come if we begin," he insisted. "They always do." He motioned for Mistress Joan to take her seat. "You are not here as a slave, I trust, but as a guest." Just then, a quick, urgent step echoed outside, signaling arrival. "There, I hear her," Eli announced, pulling out his chair. "Sit, and fall to."

The covers were drawn back, and the knives were raised in anticipation of the feast. But the figure that burst through the doorway was neither the beloved daughter nor the feared Duke. Instead, a Dominican friar stood framed in the light, his face a mask of livid rage. In a single, fluid motion, he crossed the threshold and slammed himself against the narrow table, positioning his towering form directly between Cornelis and Sybrandt. With both hands hovering inches above their shrinking heads, like raptors circling helpless prey, he unleashed a torrent of invective.

It was an era eloquent in its cursing, and this particular diatribe was so dense, so minutely detailed, so blighting and terrible that the weight of it threatens to crush the page itself.

"Cursed be the lips," he shrieked, his voice cracking with supernatural intensity, "that spoke the lie declaring Margaret dead! May they rot before the grave and kiss white-hot iron in hell thereafter! Doubly cursed be the hands that forged those fraudulent letters; may they be severed by the hangman's blade and forced to handle the fires of damnation forever! Thrice accursed be the cruel hearts that conceived this damnable falsehood, designed to sever true love eternally! May they sicken and wither on earth, joyless, loveless, and hopeless, reduced to dust before their natural time, consumed by eternal flame!"

His wrath expanded, consuming everything. He cursed the very meat awaiting their consumption and every atom of their physical being, from the crown of their hair to the soles of their feet. Turning then to the cowering pair, who had nearly hidden themselves beneath the tablecloth, he reached into his bosom, tore forth a letter, and hurled it down before his father.

"Read this, thou hard old man who imprisoned thy son!" he commanded, his voice trembling with righteous fury. "Read, and behold the monsters thou hast spawned into the world! The memory of my wrongs and hers shall dwell with you all forever! I shall meet you again at the Judgment Day; on this earth, thou shalt never see me more."

With the speed of a ghost, he vanished as abruptly as he had arrived, leaving behind a tableau of stunned horror. The family sat rigid, cold, and pale as statues encircling the untouched, steaming meal.

Then, the door opened again. Margaret entered, her eyes meeting the scene of petrified men and women surrounding the unbroken feast. She gasped, relief washing over her features. "Oh, joy! All are here; no blood hath been shed. Oh, you cruel, cruel men! I thank God he hath not slain you."

At the sight of her, Catherine emitted an eloquent scream before turning her head away, unable to bear the emotional surge. But Eli, having just scanned the false letter and beginning to grasp the magnitude of the deception, saw the other victim enter at that precise moment, her own sorrows mirrored in her sweet, pale countenance. A transport of rage seized him. He sprang to his feet, shouting, "Stand clear! Let me reach these traitors! I will hang for them!"

In an instant, he drew his short sword and lunged forward.

"Flee!" screamed Margaret. "Flee!"

They scrambled, howling, slipping under the table and crawling out the opposite side. Yet, before they could reach the door, the furious old man swept around, intercepting their path. Catherine merely screamed and wrung her hands—a futile gesture, for notable figures are often paralyzed at such critical junctures. Blood would surely have flowed had not Margaret and Jorian seized the elderly man's arms, holding him fast with all their might until the fugitives escaped the house. Only then did they release him, and he went vainly raging after them into the street.

They were a furlong distant, fleeing like hares. Inside, the old man hacked down the board bearing their names, dragged it indoors, and flung it into the fireplace. Catherine sat rocking herself, her apron pulled over her head in despair. Joan had rushed to her husband. Margaret embraced Catherine, her arms tight around the grieving woman's neck, pale and panting yet striving to offer comfort.

But it was impossible. "Oh, my poor children!" Catherine wailed. "Oh, miserable mother! 'Tis a mercy Kate was ill upstairs. There, I have lived to thank God for that!" she cried, bursting anew into sobs. "It would have killed her. He had better have stayed in Italy, coming home only to curse his own flesh and blood and set us all by the ears."

"Oh, hold your chatter, woman," Eli snapped angrily. "You remain on the side of the wrongdoer. You are cheaply served; your weakness made these rogues what they are. I intended to correct them in their youth; for sore ills, sharp remedies. But you sided with their faults and undermined me, baffling wise severity." He turned to Margaret. "And you, Margaret, cease comforting her when you ought to comfort yourself. What is her hurt compared to yours? But she never possessed a grain of justice under her skin, and never will. So come to me, that am thy father henceforth."

It was a command. Margaret kissed Catherine goodbye and tottered toward him. He placed her on a chair beside him, and she rested her feeble head upon his honest breast, though not a tear fell—it was sorrow too deep for tears. "Poor lamb," he murmured.

After a pause, true Eli spoke in a broken voice to Jorian and Joan. "Come, good folks, we are in a little trouble, as you see; but that is no reason you should starve. For our Lady's sake, eat. Add not to my grief the reputation of a churl. What the dickens!" he added, attempting a sudden, ghastly display of stout-heartedness. "The more knaves I have the luck to rid myself of, the greater my need of true men and women to help me clear the dish and cheer my eye with honest faces where else there are gaps. Eat, I entreat you."

Catherine, sobbing, backed his request. Poor, simple, antique, hospitable souls! Jorian, whose appetite, especially since his illness, was keen, prepared to act on this invitation. But Joan whispered a word in his ear, and he instantly recoiled. "Nay, I'll touch no meat that Holy Church hath cursed."

"In sooth, I forgot," Eli apologized humbly. "My son, who was reared at my table, hath cursed my victuals. That seems strange. Well, what God wills, man must bow to."

The supper was carried out into the yard. Heavy sadness reigned in Eli's house that night.

Meanwhile, where was Clement? He lay stretched full-length upon the floor of the convent church, his lips pressed against the lowest step of the altar. He existed in an indescribable state of terror, misery, penitence, and self-abasement, through which struggled faint gleams of joy that Margaret was alive. Night fell, finding him there weeping and praying; morning would have found him there too. But suddenly, he remembered that, absorbed in his own wrongs and Margaret's, he had committed another sin besides intemperate rage: he had neglected a dying man.

He rose instantly, groaning at his accumulated wickedness, determined to repair the omission. The weather had shifted; rain poured down heavily. As he cleared the town, he heard wolves bay—they were on his trail. Yet Clement was himself again, or nearly so. He thought little of danger or discomfort, driven solely by the shameful omission of religious duty he sought to rectify. He moved stoutly forward through the rain and darkness. As he walked, he often beat his breast and cried, "Mea culpa! Mea culpa!"

To grasp the labyrinthine architecture of a mind such as Clement's—one so sensitive, so burdened by a tender conscience, and animated by a loving heart and a devout soul—one must recognize that the internal storm raging within those few hours defies the very limits of human expression. Imagine the man; then, transpose your spirit entirely into his shoes. Were I to chronicle every nuance of that spiritual tempest, I would require a volume unto myself. Instead, I shall recount his next two decisive acts, which stand as the barometers of his state of mind once the initial, fierce convulsion of the soul had subsided.

Having spent the night beside the dying hermit, exchanging holy consolations in a sacred exchange of life and faith, Clement did not turn toward Rotterdam. No, his destination was Tergou. There, he intended to confront his mortal foe, the burgomaster, wielding the parchment—a document whose own history was a romance in itself—as the instrument to force restitution and restore Margaret to her rightful inheritance.

He arrived heated and dust-choked, pausing at the town fountain to consume his black bread and quench his thirst with cool water. Yet, midway through this austere meal, a female servant burst forth, breathless, imploring him to attend to her dying master. With a swift motion, he returned his bread to his wallet and followed her in silence. She led him to the Stadthouse. At the threshold, seeing her enter, he recoiled with a faint shudder, a visceral reaction to the proximity of sin. But the shock passed almost instantly; he composed himself and stepped inside, ascending the stairs until they reached the chamber where his deadly enemy lay propped against pillows, already resembling a corpse.

Clement stood in the doorway, his eyes tracing the trajectory of the tower, the wood, and the letter. Then, in a whisper barely audible over the silence, he spoke: *Pax vobiscum!* A slight tremor ran through him as the words left his lips. The sick man, however, greeted him with the eager desperation of a drowning man finding shore. "Thank Heaven, thou art come in time to absolve me from my sins, father, and pray for my soul, thou and thy brethren."

"My son," Clement replied, "before absolution comes confession. In this act, there must be no reservation, for it is the measure of thy soul's welfare. Consider carefully wherein thou hast most grievously offended God and the Church, while I offer a prayer for wisdom to guide thee."

Clement knelt and prayed. Rising, he addressed Ghysbrecht with apparent calm: "My son, confess thy sins."

"Ah, father," the sick man gasped, "they are many and great."

"Great, then, let thy penitence be commensurate," Clement urged. "So shalt thou find God's mercy great."

Ghysbrecht clasped his hands, beginning his recitation with the outward appearance of profound contrition. He admitted to eating meat during mid-Lent, to frequent absences from Mass on the Lord's Day and saints' days, and to trifling with various religious observances, enumerating them with scrupulous fidelity. When he finished, the friar spoke quietly, "'Tis well, my son. These be faults. Now, to thy crimes. Thou hadst done better to begin with them."

"Why, father, what crimes lie to my account if these be none?" Ghysbrecht asked, bewildered.

"Am I confessing to thee, or thou to me?" Clement countered, his tone sharpening slightly.

"Forgive me, father! Surely, I confess to you. But I know not what you term crimes."

"The seven deadly sins, art thou clear of them?"

"Heaven forefend I should be guilty of them. I know them not by name."

"Many commit them all yet cannot name them," Clement observed. "Begin with that one which leads to lying, theft, and murder."

"I am quit of that one, any way. How call you it?"

"AVARICE, my son."

"Avarice? Oh, as to that, I have been a saving man all my days; but I have kept a good table, and not altogether forgotten the poor." Ghysbrecht paused, his face clouding. "But, alas, I am a great sinner. Mayhap the next will catch me. What is the next?"

"We have not yet concluded with this one," Clement warned sternly. "Bethink thee, the Church is not to be trifled with."

"Alas! Am I in a condition to trifle with her now? Avarice? Avarice?" He looked utterly puzzled, an image of innocence masking a lifetime of greed.

"Hast thou ever robbed the fatherless?" the friar inquired.

"Me? Robbed the fatherless?" Ghysbrecht gasped, feigning ignorance. "Not that I mind."

"Once more, my son, I am forced to tell thee thou art trifling with the Church. Miserable man! Another evasion, and I depart, leaving thee to the fiends who will straightway gather round thy bed to drag thee down to the bottomless pit."

"Oh, leave me not! Leave me not!" shrieked the terrified old man. "The Church knows all. I must have robbed the fatherless. I will confess. Who shall I begin with? My memory for names is shaken."

The defense was skillful, yet in this instance, it failed completely.

"Hast thou forgotten Floris Brandt?" Clement asked, his voice stony.

The sick man reared himself up in bed, seized by a pitiable terror. "How knew you that?"

"The Church knows many things," Clement replied coldly, "and by many ways that are dark to thee. Miserable impenitent, you called her to your side, hoping to deceive her. You said, 'I will not confess to the cure but to some friar who knows not my misdeeds. So will I cheat the Church on my deathbed, and die as I have lived.' But God, kinder to thee than thou art to thyself, sent to thee one whom thou couldst not deceive. He has tried thee; He was patient with thee, warning thee not to trifle with Holy Church; but all is in vain. Thou canst not confess, for thou art impenitent as a stone. Die, then, as thou hast lived. Methinks I see the fiends crowding round the bed for their prey. They wait but for me to go. And I go."

He turned his back, but Ghysbrecht, driven to the extremities of terror, grabbed him by the frock. "Oh, holy man, mercy! Stay. I will confess all, all. I robbed my friend Floris. Alas, would it had ended there; for he lost little by me; but I kept the land from Peter his son, and from Margaret, Peter's daughter. Yet I was always going to give it back; but I couldn't, I couldn't."

"Avarice, my son, avarice. Happy for thee 'tis not too late."

"No; I will leave it her by will. She will not have long to wait for it now; not above a month or two at farthest."

"For which month's possession thou wouldst damn thy soul forever, Thou fool!"

The sick man groaned, pleading with the friar to be reasonable. But Clement, firm yet gentle, persisted with infinite patience, slowly detaching the dying man's greedy grip from another's property. There were moments when his patience was tested to the breaking point, tempting him to reach into his bosom and produce the deed he had carried specifically for this purpose. Yet, recalling yesterday's violent outbreak, he held himself in check against choler. Furthermore, he conquered a personal repugnance toward the man so intense that his own flesh crept beneath his habit as he struggled with this miser for his soul. Finally, without uttering a single word regarding the deed itself, he succeeded in winning the dying man to make full and prompt restitution.

How the restitution was executed will be briefly recounted elsewhere, along with the curious effects produced upon Ghysbrecht by the act, and the precise terms upon which Ghysbrecht and Clement parted ways. I promised to relate two acts of the latter, indicative of his restored mental clarity. This is the first. The second is told in two words.

As soon as he was quite certain that Margaret had regained her own and was thus a wealthy woman—he simply disappeared.

The day following that catastrophic rupture, the small dwelling on the Hoog Straet stood as a mausoleum of silence, heavier than any tombstone. Within it, Catherine, once the very embodiment of bustling vitality, moved with the sluggish grace of a ghost. Her eyes, rimmed with the raw red of weeping, betrayed a spirit crushed under the weight of recent tragedy. Driven by a desperate, final hope, she ventured to the convent to seek Gerard, attempting to lay the cornerstone of a reconciliation before the mortar of time could harden forever. Yet, the gatekeeper met her with the cold indifference of bureaucracy; Gerard had departed in the dead hours of the previous night, vanished into the city's labyrinthine shadows with no promise of return.

Sighing, a sound that seemed to deflate her very lungs, Catherine returned to find Margaret. There sat the younger woman, a figure of profound desolation, clutching her child so tightly it appeared she sought to fuse flesh and bone, terrified that the world might snatch him away as easily as it had taken everything else. Life had lost its flavor for Margaret, reduced to a bitter ash in her mouth.

"Catherine pleaded with her to accompany her back to the Hoog Straet, hoping her presence might bridge the chasm between them."

"Why?" Margaret sighed, her voice hollow. "You must admit, she is the architect of this ruin."

"Nay, nay," Catherine insisted, her tone pleading rather than argumentative. "We are not so devoid of charity. Eli loves you dearly; perhaps your touch will soften his hardened heart."

"If you believe I can effect a change," Margaret murmured, lifting her weary head, "then I shall come."

Upon their arrival, they found Eli engaged with a carpenter, hammering a new signpost into the timber. The name of Cornelis and Sybrandt had been effaced, replaced by a fresh inscription: *Margaret Brandt*. The irony cut deeper than any blade. Despite her enduring affection for Margaret, the sight of her name etched in permanent ink felt like a physical laceration to Catherine.

"The bane of one becomes the sustenance of another," Catherine whispered, the ancient proverb tasting like bile.

"Can he justify spending my inheritance unjustly?" Margaret retorted, her voice icy despite the tremor in her hands.

"You are a soul of goodness," Catherine replied, searching for comfort in the impossible.

"Ay, precisely," Margaret answered, her bitterness sharp as glass. "Which makes him the stronger, for he holds the purse strings."

The following day, Giles arrived, bringing with him the usual current of gossip. Catherine spun a tale woven entirely in favor of the ostracized siblings, painting them as victims of fraternal betrayal and paternal tyranny, begging for his pity. But Giles's prejudices flowed in a contrary river. He listened patiently, then delivered his verdict with blunt clarity: the scoundrels had escaped punishment far too lightly; they deserved execution at Margaret's very doorstep. Dismissing them with scornful laughter, he crowed triumphantly about the return of his favorite nephew.

"I shall demonstrate to him," Giles declared, his eyes gleaming with vindictive joy, "the true value of having a brother at court—one with a heart loyal to friendship and a mind capable of charting the path to justice."

"Bless thee, Giles," Margaret murmured softly, her gaze fixed on the floor.

"Thou hast always been his steadfast ally, dear Giles," little Kate added, her young voice tinged with helplessness. "But alas, I fear nothing you can offer will reach him now."

When Giles departed, the house settled back into its mournful silence. Just then, a well-dressed stranger entered, asking quietly if Margaret Brandt resided within.

"Do you hear, lass? You are summoned," Catherine announced briskly, her gossip instinct overriding her grief.

"Well, mother," Margaret said, rising with a listless resignation, "here I am."

A shuffle of feet echoed near the threshold, and a pale, trembling figure was ushered into the room. It was Ghysbrecht Van Swieten. At the sight of him, Catherine screamed, throwing her apron over her head in horror, while Margaret recoiled, turning her face away as if looking upon a plague.

From the stranger's lips emerged a voice weak as dry leaves: "Good people, a dying man comes to beg your forgiveness."

"To witness the fruits of your labor, you mean," Catherine spat, removing her veil and bursting into fresh sobs.

"There, there, she faints! Look to her, Eli, quickly!"

"Nay," Margaret whispered, her strength failing, "the sight of him has struck me blind. That is all. Pray, let him speak and depart; for he is the murderer of me and my lineage."

"Alas," Ghysbrecht gasped, struggling to maintain his composure, "I am too frail to stand, and no one bids me sit."

Eli, who had followed the intruder inside, stepped forward, his voice a mixture of sullen resentment and forced apology. "Well, Burgomaster, 'tis not our custom to leave a guest standing while we sit. But man, man, you have wrought us unspeakable woe." Even as he spoke, the honest man's voice began to quake with suppressed rage, fighting the urge to unleash fury upon the man who had destroyed his home.

Then, a voice arose that rarely spoke in vain within those walls—that of little Kate. "Father, Mother," she said gently, "my duty to you is clear, yet this course is wrong. Death balances all ledgers. Do you not see death written across his face? I shall not live long, good friends; and his time is shorter still."

Moved by her plea, Eli hurried to set a chair for their dying adversary with his own hands. Ghysbrecht's attendants helped him settle into it.

"Fetch the boxes," Ghysbrecht commanded.

Two chests were brought in, and the attendants withdrew, leaving their master alone amidst the family he had so grievously wounded. Every eye turned toward him save Margaret's. With unsteady fingers, Ghysbrecht unlocked the lids. From one, he produced the title deeds to properties in Tergou.

"This land and these houses belonged to Floris Brandt, and by right, they belong to thee, his granddaughter. I usurped them for a debt long since paid in full with interest. Now, with penitent tears, I restore them to their rightful owner." He paused, his voice thick with emotion. "In this other box lie three hundred and forty golden angels—the rents and fines I collected from that land exceeding Floris Brandt's debt to me. I kept them accounted for, intending to remain just one day longer, but Avarice held me fast, tempting me against my conscience. I was not born dishonest; yet, you see the proof."

"Well, truly!" Catherine exclaimed, her greed momentarily overriding her sorrow. "And you, the Burgomaster! You have whipped countless thieves in your day. However," she amended, reconsidering, "'tis better late than never.' What, Margaret, are you deaf? This good man has restored your birthright. You are a wealthy woman. Alack, what a mountain of gold!"

"Keep the land and the gold," Margaret said, her head still averted, her voice barely audible. "Give me back my Gerard, whom he stole from me through his treachery."

"Alas!" Ghysbrecht lamented. "Would that I could! What can I do? It cost me a fierce internal battle. I rose from my sickbed to deliver this personally, lest some misfortune intervene between you and your rights."

"Old man," Margaret said, her voice breaking, "since thou, whose idol is coin, hast done this, may God and the Saints, as I hope, forgive thee. As for me, I am neither saint nor angel, but merely a poor woman whose heart thou hast shattered. Speak to him, Kate, for I am like the dead."

Kate pondered for a moment, then her soft, silvery voice drifted through the room like a soothing melody. "My poor sister bears a sorrow that riches cannot heal. Give her time, Ghysbrecht; 'tis not in nature for her to forgive thee completely. Her boy is fatherless; she is neither maiden, wife, nor widow; and the blow fell but two days hence that laid her heart open to bleed."

A single, heavy sob escaped Margaret, the only response to these words.

"Therefore, grant her time!" Kate continued, her voice gaining strength. "And ere thou diest, she will forgive thee all, yea, even to please me, who may not linger long behind thee, Ghysbrecht. Meanwhile, we, whose wounds are sore but not as deep as hers, pardon thee, a penitent and a dying man. And I, for one, will pray for thee from this hour. Go in peace!"

Her oracle had spoken; it was sufficient. Even Eli invited him to share a crust of bread and a cup of wine to strengthen him for his journey. But Ghysbrecht declined, declaring that his act of restitution was already a cordial to his soul.

"Man sees but a short distance ahead, neighbor," he said, his voice fading. "This land I clung to with such desperation was a bed of nettles to me all along. It is gone, and I feel happier, lighter, for the loss."

Calling his men, they lifted him into the litter. As he was carried away, Catherine gazed upon the recovered wealth with a

The news arrived swiftly, carried on the wings of rumor and sorrow: Margaret Van Eyck was dead, her spirit extinguished and interred beneath the earth. Yet, in the ledger of her passing, death was not the final period; rather, it was a comma preceding a testament of profound generosity. A will, drafted a year prior to her demise, stipulated that after the settling of funeral rites and the distribution of specific gifts to Reicht Heynes, her entire estate devolved to her beloved daughter, Margaret Brandt. With maternal foresight, she instructed her child to maintain Reicht in the household until such time as he chose to wed, binding him to her legacy as long as he remained unmarried.

Through this instrument, Margaret Brandt inherited not merely a furnished dwelling, but a repository of art that, viewed through the lens of modern valuation, would constitute a fortune. Among the canvases hung within those walls stood one painting that held a supremacy over any gallery collection. It depicted "A Betrothal." The composition captured a moment suspended in eternal gravity: the groom's face bore the solemn weight of the covenant, while the bride wore a demure, placid expression of quiet acceptance. Though the bulk of the masterpiece was executed by Margaret Van Eyck herself, the remaining elements were the handiwork of Jan. Every accessory was rendered with exquisite precision, a technical marvel that continues to astound observers centuries later.

Margaret Brandt dispatched a message to Reicht, instructing him to remain in the house until she felt the courage to re-enter a space haunted by the ghost of a lost lover—the face and voice that once filled its corridors. She charged him with a singular, sacred duty: the protection of the painting, specifically the diptych hidden within a small cupboard.

Before the dust of the funeral had fully settled, Luke Peterson returned home, bearing the shocking tidings that Gerard had taken the habit of a monk. The revelation nearly unhinged his reason. Overcome with a chaotic surge of joy mixed with disbelief, he rushed to Margaret.

"Heed, mistress," he declared, his voice trembling. "If he cannot marry you, I can."

Margaret recoiled slightly. "You?" she queried, confusion clouding her features. "Why, I have seen him."

"But he is a friar," Luke insisted, desperation edging his tone.

"He was my husband, and the father of my boy long before he donned the grey robe," Margaret countered, her voice firm despite the tremor in her hands. "And I have seen him. I have seen him."

Puzzled by her conviction, Luke proposed a solution born of legal pragmatism. "Let me tell you what," he suggested. "I possess a cousin who is a lawyer. I shall inquire of him regarding your marital status."

"Nay," Margaret retorted sharply. "I shall ask my own heart, not a lawyer. Thus, this is your regard for me? To drag me into the town's gossip? Oh, fie!"

"That discussion has already occurred without a word from me," Luke muttered defensively.

"But not by those who seek my respect," she snapped, her eyes flashing. "And if you persist in this path, never approach me again."

"Ay," Luke sighed, the fight draining from his shoulders. "You are like a dove to everyone else, yet a hard-hearted tyrant to me."

"'Tis your own fault, dear Luke, for courting me," she explained, her tone shifting from anger to a weary pity. "That is the barrier preventing me from treating you with the kindness I desire, Luke, my bonny lad, listen closely. I am wealthy now; I can bring happiness to my friends, though not to myself. Cast your eyes upon the street, upon the parish. There are countless women fairer than I, twice told, untouched by the spoils of weeping. Look higher; choose freely. Speak to the maiden yourself, and I shall speak to her mother; they shall not refuse you. Take my word for it."

"I perceive your intent," Luke said, flushing crimson. "But if I cannot secure your affection, I accept none of your coin. I served you when you were as destitute as I, and poorer. No; if you would rather be a friar's paramour than an honest man's wife, you are not the woman I believed you to be. Let us part ways without malice. Seek your comfort on that distant road, where no woman has yet found solace. As for me, I shall live and die a bachelor. Good evening, mistress."

"Farewell, dear Luke," she whispered, her voice thick with emotion. "May God forgive you for speaking thus to me."

For several days, Margaret lived in a liminal state, dreading the inevitable confrontation with Gerard almost as intensely as she craved it. She reasoned internally that his absence was understandable; perhaps he needed time to process the shock. Surely, the knowledge that he was a father would eventually override his monastic vows. "And," she confessed to her own reflection, "if this meeting does not destroy me, I feel I shall emerge stronger afterward than I am now."

However, as day succeeded day without a sign of him, a chilling suspicion began to infiltrate her thoughts. Was his absence deliberate? Had he retreated to the cold, calculating minds of his fellow monks, who had commanded him to sever all ties with her forever? The convent, she feared, proved as merciless to true lovers as the grave itself. At the mere thought, the vitality seemed to drain from her veins.

Now, for the first time, a deep, burning indignation intertwined with her grief and terror. "Could he have truly loved me?" she wondered, the accusation stinging. "To flee from me and his child without a single word? Why does this poor Luke value me more than he does?"

Amidst this turmoil, Giles burst in, roaring with triumph. "I have hit the mark! Our Gerard is the Vicar of Gouda!"

A brief chronicle of the dwarf's rise at court serves to contextualize this extraordinary achievement. Months prior to his ascent, his intellect had begun to bud. He traced the transformation to a specific date, the eighth of June, when, balancing on a tightrope laden with a week's worth of laundry in the drying yard, a sudden crack echoed within his skull. Lo! Intellectual chains were shattered. At court, his sharp wit, blunt speech, cavernous voice, and diminutive stature forged him into a figure of immense power; without his small size, he feared they would have consigned him to the gallows. Both the young Duchess of Burgundy and Marie, the heir apparent, doted upon him, as great ladies have always adored dwarves. The court poet melted butter by the six-foot rule, pouring it down his back in quantities sufficient to stew Goliath. Even the poet's rough dictations were amplified, versified, and softened by Giles's touch.

The resulting verbosity traveled to Eli via letter, titled: *"The high and puissant Princess Marie of Bourgogne her lytel jantilman hys complaynt of y' Coort, and praise of a rusticall lyfe, versificated, and empapyred by me the lytel jantilman's right lovynge and obsequious servitor, etc."*

Yet, Giles reached his zenith through a fortunate alchemy of mind and muscle. The day preceding a grand tournament, he challenged the Duke's giant to a contest of strength. The challenge elicited a grimace of amusement and raised expectations.

Giles erected a lofty pole. At the appointed hour, he ascended it like a squirrel, using sheer arm strength to form a right angle with his body, holding the position effortlessly. Then, he slid down with such velocity that the High and Puissant Princess squealed, burying her face in her hands to avoid witnessing the demise of her pocket-Hercules.

The giant managed only about ten feet before looking ruefully upward and downward, descending in a sweat-drenched retreat to explain the discrepancy. "It was not the dwarf's superior strength, but his smaller frame," he argued.

The spectators met this excuse with raucous derision. The fact remained: the dwarf excelled at climbing poles; the giant excelled at excuses. In essence, Giles had gauged their intellects, likely with his own body as the measuring stick.

"Come," Giles declared, "if you resort to that, I shall wrestle you, my lad, provided you allow me to blindfold your eyes."

Smarting from defeat and hoping to reclaim victory through deception, the giant readily agreed.

"Madam," Giles announced, addressing the court, "do you see yonder blind Samson? Upon my signal, he shall perform a low obeisance and remove his cap for me."

"How can that be possible, being blinded?" a lady-in-waiting inquired.

"I wager on Giles for one," the princess proclaimed confidently.

"That is my affair."

As bets were placed on both sides, Giles struck the giant squarely in the breadbasket. The giant doubled over—a perfect obeisance—and his bonnet fell off.

The company erupted in delighted cheers at this delicate stroke of wit. Giles seized the opportunity to flee. The giant, recovering his breath and tearing off his bandages, gave chase. But it was too late. Giles had prepared a narrow door in the wall, sized perfectly for a man but impassable for a giant, camouflaged so artfully it resembled solid masonry. He tore it open and darted through headfirst, leaving behind only a verse scrawled in large letters upon the reverse side of his trick door:

*Long limbs, big

Yes, the hermit of Gouda was the vicar of Gouda, and he knew it not, so absolute was his seclusion that the veil between his inner sanctum and the world outside had become impenetrable. My reader understands that the moment the frenzy of his passion passed, he was seized with a remorse so profound it felt like a physical betrayal. Yet, perhaps only those who have ascended to the highest peaks of religious spirit and then tumbled violently from them can truly grasp the terror that took possession of him. He felt like a man whom his own self-confidence had betrayed to the very edge of a precipice, staring into the abyss of his own inadequacy.

"Ah, good Jerome," he cried, his voice trembling with a mixture of shame and relief, "how much better you knew me than I knew myself! How bitter yet wholesome was your admonition!"

Accustomed to scouring his own heart, he saw immediately that the true source of his fury was Margaret. "I love her then better than God," he confessed despairingly, the words tearing from his throat like a confession of treason. "Better than the Church. From such a love, what can spring to me, or to her?" He shuddered at the thought, a cold wave washing over him. "Let the strong battle temptation; 'tis for the weak to flee. And who is weaker than I have shown myself? What is my penitence, my religion? A pack of cards built by degrees into a fair-seeming structure; and lo! one breath of earthly love, and it lies in the dust. I must begin again, and on a surer foundation." He resolved to leave Holland at once, spending years of his life in some distant convent before returning. By that time, the temptations of earthly passion would be doubly baffled; older and a better monk, he should be more master of his earthly affections. And Margaret, seeing herself abandoned, would marry, and love another. The very anguish this last thought cost him revealed the self-searcher and self-denier that he was, proving he was indeed on the path of religious duty.

But in leaving her for his immortal good and hers, he was not to neglect her temporal welfare. Indeed, the sweet thought that he could make her comfortable for life, rich in this world's goods—a luxury she was not bound to despise—sustained him in the bitter struggle it cost him to turn his back on her without one kind word or look. "Oh, what will she think of me?" he groaned internally. "Shall I not seem to her of all creatures the most heartless, inhuman? But so best; ay, better she should hate me, miserable that I am. Heaven is merciful, and giveth my broken heart this comfort: I can make that villain restore her dowry, and she shall never lose another true lover by poverty. Another? Ah me! ah me! God and the saints to mine aid!"

How he fared on this errand has been related. But first, as you may perhaps remember, he went at night to shrive the hermit of Gouda. He found him dying, and never left him until he had closed his eyes and buried him beneath the floor of the little oratory attached to his cell. It was the peaceful end of a stormy life. The hermit had been a soldier, and even now carried a steel corselet next his skin, declaring he was now Christ's soldier as he had once been Satan's. When Clement had shriven him and prayed by him, the dying man, in his turn, sought counsel from one who was passing into eternity in such a pious frame. The hermit advised him to be his successor in this peaceful retreat. "His had been a hard fight against the world, the flesh, and the devil, and he had never thoroughly baffled them till he retired into the citadel of Solitude."

These words, coupled with the hermit's pious and peaceful death—which speedily followed and set, as it were, the seal of immortal truth upon them—made a deep impression upon Clement. Nor in his case did they encounter any prejudice to combat; the solitary recluse was still profoundly revered in the Church, whether immured as an anchorite or anchoress in some cave or cell belonging to a monastery, or hidden in the more savage but laxer seclusion of the independent hermitage. And Clement knew more about the hermits of the Church than most divines of his time; he had read extensively on the subject at the monastery near Tergou, devouring their lives with wonder and delight in the manuscripts of the Vatican, and conversing earnestly about them with the mendicant friars of several nations. Before printing presses existed, these friars were the great circulators of those local annals and biographies which accumulated in the convents of every land. Then his teacher, Jerome, had been three years an anchorite on the heights of Camaldoli, where for more than four centuries the Thebaid had been revived; and Jerome, cold and curt on most religious themes, was warm with enthusiasm on this one. He had pored over the annals of St. John Baptist's abbey, round about which the hermit's caves were scattered, telling him the names of many a nobleman and many a famous warrior who had ended his days there a hermit, and of many a bishop and archbishop who had passed from the see to the hermitage, or from the hermitage to the see. Among the former was the Archbishop of Ravenna; among the latter, Pope Victor the Ninth. He told him too, with grim delight, of their multifarious austerities, and how each hermit set himself to find where he was weakest, attacking himself without mercy or remission until, even there, he was strongest. And how seven times in the twenty-four hours, in thunder, rain, or snow, by daylight, twilight, moonlight, or torchlight, the solitaries flocked from distant points, over rugged precipitous ways, to worship in the convent church—at matins, at prime, tierce, sexte, nones, vespers, and compline. He even, under eager questioning, described to him the persons of famous anchorites he had sung the Psalter and prayed with there—the only intercourse their vows allowed, except with special permission. Moncada, Duke of Moncada and Cardova, and Hidalgo of Spain, who in the flower of his youth had retired thither from the pomps, vanities, and pleasures of the world; Father John Baptist of Novara, who had led armies to battle but was now a private soldier of Christ; Cornelius, Samuel, and Sylvanus. This last, when the great Duchess de' Medici obtained the Pope's leave, hitherto refused, to visit Camaldoli, went down and met her at the first wooden cross. There, surrounded as she was with courtiers and flatterers, he remonstrated with her, persuaded her, and warned her not to profane that holy mountain, where no woman for so many centuries had placed her foot; and she, awed by the place and the man, retreated with all her captains, soldiers, courtiers, and pages from that one hoary hermit. At Basel, Clement found fresh materials, especially with respect to German and English anchorites; and he had even prepared a "Catena Eremitarum" from the year of our Lord 250, when Paul of Thebes commenced his ninety years of solitude, down to the year 1470. He called them *Angelorum amici et animalium*, i.e., Friends of Angels and Animals.

Thus, though in those days he never thought to be a recluse, the road was paved, so to speak; and when the dying hermit of Gouda blessed the citadel of Solitude, where he had fought the good fight and won it, and invited him to take up the breast-plate of faith that now fell off his own shrunken body, Clement said within himself: "Heaven itself led my foot hither to this end." It struck him, too, as no small coincidence that his patron, St. Bavon, was a hermit, and an austere one, a cuirassier of the solitary cell.

As soon as he was reconciled to Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, he went eagerly to his abode, praying Heaven it might not have been already occupied in these three days. The fear was not vain; these famous dens never wanted a human tenant long. He found the rude stone door ajar; then he made sure he was too late; he opened the door and went softly in. No; the cell was vacant, and there lay the hermit's great ivory crucifix, his pens, ink, seeds, and, *memento mori*, a skull; his cilice of hair, and another of bristles; his well-worn sheepskin pelisse and hood; his hammer, chisel, and psaltery, etc. Men and women had passed that way, but none had ventured to intrude, far less to steal. Faith and simplicity had guarded that keyless door more securely than the houses of the laity were defended by their gates like a modern gaol, complete with iron bars at every window, and the gentry by moat, bastion, *chevaux de frise*, and portcullis.

As soon as Clement was fairly in the cell, there was a loud flap, and a flutter, and down came a great brown owl from a corner, whirling out of the window and driving the air cold on Clement's face. He started and shuddered. Was this seeming owl

To comprehend the architecture of Clement's spiritual resurrection, one must first witness the absolute desolation of his universe at the nadir of despair. He existed within a microscopic empire of solitude, subjugated by a silence so profound it bordered on the physical. He was a square-built, brutal artifact of a world he had abandoned, roughly thirty years of age, though time in the cave seemed measured only by the decay of his hope. His dark hair fell in matted, feral clusters, and a vast beard obscured his face, rendering him less a man than a beast draped in the ruin of whatever civilization had cast him out. He possessed a terrifying latent power, yet he spent his days in a state of stagnant lethargy, only stirring to exert his dominance over his own labor—chipping stone or fighting shadows.

Their lives were bound by the daily ritual of prayerless groaning, barricaded from the living world by a ridge of jagged stone and the weight of his own sin. Beyond this, his existence was an enforced void. He was permitted no education, no heritage, and almost no language save the Latin of the liturgy, which he recited mechanically. The silence utilized him as a tool of oppression, rationing his words to curses and commands, ensuring his mind remained as barren as the rocky crags that caged him. He became a taciturn creature, his intellect starved, left to gaze into the abyssal depths of the cave or trace the cold geometries of the stars. He was kept as a beast of the field—valuable only for the calories his labor saved him, and perhaps to serve as a mirror for his own desperate need for mastery.

One day, as he lay there sighing and groaning, prayerless, tuneless, hopeless, a thought flashed into his mind. What he had done for the poor and the wayfarer, he would do for himself. He would fill his den of despair with the name of God and the magic words of holy writ, and the pious, prayerful consolations of the Church.

Then, like Christian at Apollyon's feet, he reached his hand suddenly out and caught, not his sword, for he had none, but peaceful labour's humbler weapon, his chisel, and worked with it as if his soul depended on his arm.

They say that Michael Angelo in the next generation used to carve statues, not like our timid sculptors, by modelling the work in clay, and then setting a mechanic to chisel it, but would seize the block, conceive the image, and at once, with mallet and steel, make the marble chips fly like mad about him, and the mass sprout into form. Even so Clement drew no lines to guide his hand. He went to his memory for the gracious words, and then dashed at his work and eagerly graved them in the soft stone, between working and fighting.

He begged his visitors for candle ends, and rancid oil.

"Anything is good enough for me," he said, "if 'twill but burn." So at night the cave glowed afar off like a blacksmith's forge, through the window and the gaping chinks of the rude stone door, and the rustics beholding crossed themselves and suspected deviltries, and within the holy talismans, one after another, came upon the walls, and the sparks and the chips flew day and night, night and day, as the soldier of Solitude and of the Church plied, with sighs and groans, his bloodless weapon, between working and fighting.

{*ton Satanan suntripson upo tous pothas ymwn*}

*Deus, qui miro ordine Angelorum ministeria, etc,* (the whole collect).

And underneath the great crucifix, which was fastened to the wall, he graved this from Augustine:

*O anima Christiana, respice vulnera patientis, sanguinem morientis, pretium redemptionis. Haec quanta sint cogitate, et in statera mentis vestrae appendite, ut totus vobis figatur in corde, qui pro vobis totus fixus est in cruce. Nam si passio Christi ad memoriam revocetur, nihil est tam durum quod non aequo animo toleretur.*

Which may be thus rendered: *O Christian soul, look on the wounds of the suffering One, the blood of the dying One, the price paid for our redemption! These things, oh, think how great they be, and weigh them in the balance of thy mind: that He may be wholly nailed to thy heart, who for thee was all nailed unto the cross. For do but call to mind the sufferings of Christ, and there is nought on earth too hard to endure with composure.*

Soothed a little, a very little, by the sweet and pious words he was raising all round him, and weighed down with watching and working night and day, Clement one morning sank prostrate with fatigue, and a deep sleep overpowered him for many hours. Awaking quietly, he heard a little cheep; he opened his eyes, and lo! upon his breviary, which was on a low stool near his feet, ruffling all his feathers with a single pull, and smoothing them as suddenly, and cocking his bill this way and that with a vast display of cunning purely imaginary, perched a robin redbreast.

Clement held his breath.

He half closed his eyes lest they should frighten the airy guest.

Down came robin on the floor.

When there he went through his pantomime of astuteness; and then, *pim, pim, pim*, with three stiff little hops, like a ball of worsted on vertical wires, he was on the hermit's bare foot. On this eminence he swelled and contracted again, with ebb and flow of feathers; but Clement lost this, for he quite closed his eyes and scarce drew his breath in fear of frightening and losing his visitor. He was content to feel the minute claw on his foot. He could but just feel it, and that by help of knowing it was there.

Presently a little flirt with two little wings, and the feathered busybody was on the breviary again.

Then Clement determined to try and feed this pretty little fidget without frightening it away. But it was very difficult.

He had a piece of bread within reach, but how get at it? I think he was five minutes creeping his hand up to that bread, and when there he must not move his arm.

He slily got a crumb between a finger and thumb and shot it as boys do marbles, keeping the hand quite still.

*Cockrobin* saw it fall near him, and did sagacity, but moved not.

When another followed, and then another, he popped down and caught up one of the crumbs, but not quite understanding this mystery fled with it, for more security, to an eminence; to wit, the hermit's knee.

And so the game proceeded till a much larger fragment than usual rolled along.

Here was a prize. *Cockrobin* pounced on it, bore it aloft, and fled so swiftly into the world with it, the cave resounded with the buffeted air.

"Now, bless thee, sweet bird," sighed the stricken solitary; "thy wings are music, and thou a feathered ray camdest to light my darkened soul."

And from that to his orisons, and then to his tools with a little bit of courage, and this was his day's work:

And so the days rolled on; and the weather got colder, and Clement's heart got warmer, and despondency was rolling away; and by-and-by, somehow or another, it was gone. He had outlived it.

It had come like a cloud, and it went like one.

And presently all was reversed; his cell seemed illuminated with joy. His work pleased him; his prayers were full of unction; his psalms of praise. Hosts of little birds followed their crimson leader, and flying from snow, and a parish full of Cains, made friends one after another with Abel; fast friends. And one keen frosty night as he sang the praises of God to his tuneful psaltery, and his hollow cave rang forth the holy psalmody upon the night, as if that cave itself was Tubal's surrounding shell, or David's harp, he heard a clear whine, not unmelodious; it became louder and less in tune. He peeped through the chinks of his rude door, and there sat a great red wolf moaning melodiously with his nose high in the air.

Clement was rejoiced. "My

To truly comprehend the architecture of Clement's spiritual collapse, one must first visualize the absolute poverty of his universe at that precise moment. He existed within a microscopic empire of isolation, subjugated by a hunger that had eroded his humanity into a square-built, brutal artifact. Time in the cell was not measured by hours, but by the decay of his faith and the relentless ticking of an hourglass that marked only his suffering. His dark hair fell in matted, feral clusters, and a vast beard obscured a face rendered less human than beast, draped in the ruin of whatever civilization had cast him out. He possessed a terrifying latent power, yet he spent his days in a state of stagnant lethargy, only stirring to exert his dominance over the shadows of his own mind.

Her arrival shattered this static void. Her attitude was one to excite pity rather than terror, in eyes not blinded by a preconceived notion. Her bosom fluttered like a trapped bird, and the red and white flushed violently across her cheeks. By the instinct of timid things, she pressed her hand against the rough wall, trembling so violently that the air seemed to vibrate with her fear. Yet, the marvelous mixed gaze of love, pious awe, pity, and tender memories that those purple eyes cast upon the emaciated and glaring hermit was an event in nature—a collision of worlds that defied the logic of his solitary existence.

"Aha!" he cried, his voice cracking with the strain of six months of silence. "Thou art come at last in flesh and blood; come to me as thou camest to holy Anthony. But I am ware of thee. I thought thy wiles were not exhausted. I am armed." With this, he snatched up his small crucifix and held it out at her, astonished, the candle in his other hand shaking violently, casting erratic, dancing shadows against the damp stone. "Exorcizo te."

"Ah, no!" she cried piteously, putting out two pretty, deprecating palms. "Alas! work me no ill! It is Margaret."

"Liar!" shouted the hermit, his voice rising to a shriek. "Margaret was fair, but not so supernatural fair as thou. Thou didst shrink at that sacred name, thou subtle hypocrite. In Nomine Dei exorcizo vos."

"Ah, Jesu!" gasped Margaret, in extremity of terror, "curse me not! I will go home. I thought I might come. For very manhood be-Latin me not! Oh, Gerard, is it thus you and I meet after all, after all?"

And she cowered almost to her knees, sobbing with superstitious fear and wounded affection, her form shrinking against the oppressive weight of his delusion. Impregnated as he was with Satanophobia, he might perhaps have doubted still whether this distressed creature, all woman and nature, was not merely artifice and fiend. But her spontaneous appeal to that sacred name dissolved his chimera; and let him see with his eyes, and hear with his ears.

He uttered a cry of self-reproach, and tried to raise her, but what with fasts, what with the overpowering emotion of a long solitude so abruptly broken, he could not. "What," he gasped, shaking over her, "and is it thou? And have I met thee with hard words? Alas!" And they were both choked with emotion and could not speak for a while.

"I heed it not much," said Margaret bravely, struggling with her tears; "you took me for another: for a devil; oh! oh! oh! oh! oh!"

"Forgive me, sweet soul!" And as soon as he could speak more than a word at a time, he said, "I have been much beset by the evil one since I came here."

Margaret looked round with a shudder. "Like enow. Then oh take my hand, and let me lead thee from this foul place."

He gazed at her with astonishment.

"What, desert my cell; and go into the world again? Is it for that thou hast come to me?" said he sadly and reproachfully.

"Ay, Gerard, I am come to take thee to thy pretty vicarage: art vicar of Gouda, thanks to Heaven and thy good brother Giles; and mother and I have made it so neat for thee, Gerard. 'Tis well enow in winter I promise thee. But bide a bit till the hawthorn bloom, and anon thy walls put on their kirtle of brave roses, and sweet woodbine. Have we forgotten thee, and the foolish things thou lovest? And, dear Gerard, thy mother is waiting; and 'tis late for her to be out of her bed: prithee, prithee, come! And the moment we are out of this foul hole I'll show thee a treasure thou hast gotten, and knowest nought on't, or sure hadst never fled from us so. Alas! what is to do? What have I ignorantly said, to be regarded thus?"

For he had drawn himself all up into a heap, and was looking at her with a strange gaze of fear and suspicion blended.

"Unhappy girl," said he solemnly, yet deeply agitated, "would you have me risk my soul and yours for a miserable vicarage and the flowers that grow on it? But this is not thy doing: the bowelless fiend sends thee, poor simple girl, to me with this bait. But oh, cunning fiend, I will unmask thee even to this thine instrument, and she shall see thee, and abhor thee as I do, Margaret, my lost love, why am I here? Because I love thee."

"Oh! no, Gerard, you love me not or you would not have hidden from me; there was no need."

"Let there be no deceit between us twain, that have loved so true; and after this night, shall meet no more on earth."

"Now God forbid!" said she.

"I love thee, and thou hast not forgotten me, or thou hadst married ere this, and hadst not been the one to find me, buried here from sight of man. I am a priest, a monk: what but folly or sin can come of you and me living neighbours, and feeding a passion innocent once, but now (so Heaven wills it) impious and unholy? No, though my heart break I must be firm. 'Tis I that am the man, 'tis I that am the priest. You and I must meet no more, till I am schooled by solitude, and thou art wedded to another."

"I consent to my doom but not to thine. I would ten times liever die; yet I will marry, ay, wed misery itself sooner than let thee lie in this foul dismal place, with yon sweet manse awaiting for thee." Clement groaned; at each word she spoke out stood clearer and clearer two things--his duty, and the agony it must cost.

"My beloved," said he, with a strange mixture of tenderness and dogged resolution, "I bless thee for giving me one more sight of thy sweet face, and may God forgive thee, and bless thee, for destroying in a minute the holy peace it hath taken six months of solitude to build. No matter. A year of penance will, Dei gratia, restore me to my calm. My poor Margaret, I seem cruel: yet I am kind: 'tis best we part; ay, this moment."
