"Part, Gerard? Never: we have seen what comes of parting. Part? Why, you have not heard half my story; no, nor the tithe, 'Tis not for thy mere comfort I take thee to Gouda manse. Hear me!"

"I may not. Thy very voice is a temptation with its music, memory's delight."

"But I say you shall hear me, Gerard, for forth this place I go not unheard."

"Then must we part by other means," said Clement sadly.

"Alack! what other means? Wouldst put me to thine own door, being the stronger?"

"Nay, Margaret, well thou knowest I would suffer many deaths rather than put force on thee; thy sweet body is dearer to me than my own; but a million times dearer to me are our immortal souls, both thine and mine. I have withstood this direst temptation of all long enow. Now I must fly it: farewell! farewell!"

He made to the door, and had actually opened it and got half out, when she darted after and caught him by the arm.

"Nay, then another must speak for me. I thought to reward thee for yielding to me; but unkind that thou art, I need his help I find; turn then this way one moment."

"Nay, nay."

"But I say ay! And then turn thy back on us an thou canst." She somewhat relaxed her grasp, thinking he would never deny her so small a favor. But at this he saw his opportunity and seized it.

"Fly, Clement, fly!" he almost shrieked; and his religious enthusiasm giving him for a moment his old strength, he burst wildly away from her, and after a few steps bounded over the little stream and ran beside it, but finding he was not followed stopped, and looked back.

She was lying on her face, with her hands spread out.

Yes, without meaning it, he had thrown her down and hurt her.

When he saw that, he groaned and turned back a step; but suddenly, by another impulse, flung himself

The hermit's gaze darted frantically between the slumbering infant and Margaret, his eyes wide with a shock that bordered on madness, fueled by the sheer impossibility of her return. In his left arm, the child slept peacefully; at his right knee stood Margaret, transformed from the pale, trembling girl he had dominated mere hours prior into an imperious vision of beauty. Her cheeks burned with color, her eyes sparkled with a triumphant light, and her lips parted in a sweetness that radiated a new, unreadable emotion: maternal pride.

He stared, transfixed, his wonder-stricken eyes oscillating endlessly between the sleeping form and the woman holding it.

"Us?" he gasped, the word tearing from his throat. Even as he spoke, his gaze continued to wander, unable to reconcile the reality before him.

Surprised in her own turn, Margaret cried out, her voice echoing the chaotic nature of an age defined by fleeting impressions rather than solid facts. "What!" she exclaimed. "Does not a father recognize his own child? And a man of God, too? Fie, Gerard, to pretend! Nay, thou art too wise, too virtuous not to know—why, I watched thee! Look at you both now! It is thine own flesh and blood held to thy heart."

Clement trembled violently. "What words are these?" he stammered, his voice breaking. "Is this... is this my angel?"

"Whose else? Since he is mine."

Turning his gaze upon the sleeping child, Clement displayed an expression beyond the power of ink to capture, his entire frame shaking as his eyes seemed to drink in the tiny vessel of love. Margaret's eyes followed his trajectory. "He bears no resemblance to me," she declared proudly. "Yet, at intervals, he is thy very image in miniature; observe this golden hair. Thine was precisely this hue at his age; ask thy mother elsewhere. And behold this mole upon his little finger. Now look at thine own; there! 'Twas thy mother who informed me thou wast marked so before him. And oh, Gerard, 'twas this very child who found thee for me! By that small mark upon thy finger, I recognized thee as his father when I watched above thy window and saw thee feeding the birds." Seizing the child's hand, she kissed it eagerly, pressing half of it into her mouth—a gesture born of Heaven-knows-what desperation—"Ah! Bless thee, thou didst find thy poor daddy for her, and now thou hast restored us to friendship after our brief quarrel; the first, the last. Thou wast very cruel to me but now, my poor Gerard, yet I forgive thee; for the sake of loving thy child."

"Ahh! Ah! Ah! Ah! Ah!" sobbed Clement, choking on his grief. Lowered by fasting and unnerved by prolonged solitude, the once-strong man succumbed to hysteria, teetering on the brink of fainting.

Margaret felt alarm rise within her, yet her experience tempered her fear with profound pity. "Nay, do not distress thyself so," she murmured soothingly, placing a gentle hand upon his brow. "Be brave! So, so. Dear heart, thou art not the first man to depart abroad and return enriched by a lovely little self more than he departed. As a man of God, take courage, and declare that He sends thee this to comfort thee for what thou hast lost in me; and that is not so grievous, my lamb; for surely the better part of love shall never cool toward thee, though it may in thine, and ought, being a priest and parson of Gouda."

"I? Priest of Gouda? Never!" Clement whispered in a faint voice. "I am a friar of St. Dominic: yet speak on, sweet music, tell me all that has befallen thee before we are parted again."

Some might have protested the necessity of parting at all, raising the eternal question of separation. Yet women like Margaret do not repeat their mistakes. It is exceedingly difficult to defeat them twice when their hearts are set upon a thing.

She assented, turning her back on the Gouda manse as if it were a place not worth returning to, and began her tale. She dwelt above all on the kindness shown to her by his parents. As she recounted her tribulations, his hand instinctively sought hers; often she felt him wince and tremble with suppressed rage, and often he pressed her hand, sympathizing with her in every vein.

"Oh, piteous tale of a true heart battling alone against such bitter odds," he said.

"It all seems small now that I see thee here again, nursing my boy. We have received a warning, Gerard. True friends like you and me are rare, and it is madness to part ere death divides us."

"And that is true," Clement admitted, caught off guard.

Then she urged him to recount his sufferings for her sake. He begged her to excuse him, and she consented; yet by asking questions, she quietly revoked his request and elicited the truth. Many a sigh she heaved for him, and more than once she hid her face in her hands, terrified by the perils he had faced, though they were now past. To console him for everything he had endured, she knelt, sliding her arms beneath the little boy to lift him gently. "Kiss him softly," she whispered. "Again, again, kiss thy fill if thou canst; he is sound. 'Tis all I can do to comfort thee until thou art out of this foul den and in thy sweet manse yonder."

Clement shook his head.

"Well," she said, "let that pass. Know that I have been sorely affronted for the lack of my lines."

"Who hath dared affront thee?"

"No matter; those who will do it again if thou hast lost them, which the saints forbid."

"I lose them? Nay, there they lie, close to thy hand."

"Where, where, oh, where?"

Clement hung his head. "Look in the Vulgate. Heaven forgive me: I thought thou wert dead, a saint in heaven."

She looked, and upon the blank leaves of the poor soul's Bible, she found her marriage lines.

"Thank God!" she cried. "Thank God! Oh, bless thee, Gerard, bless thee! Why, what is here, Gerard?"

On the other leaves were pinned every scrap of paper she had ever sent him—their two names once written together in jest, the lock of her hair she had given him, half a silver coin she had shared with him, and a single straw she had used to suck her soup the first day he ever saw her.

When Margaret beheld these proofs of love and signs of a gentle heart bereft, even her exultation at recovering her marriage lines was overwhelmed by gushing tenderness. She almost staggered, her hand flying to her bosom, leaning her brow against the stone cell wall and weeping so silently that he did not perceive her tears; indeed, she refused to let him see, feeling that to befriend him now she must remain the stronger, for emotion weakens resolve.

"Gerard," she said, "I know you are wise and good. You must have a reason for what you are doing, however unreasonable it may seem. Let us talk like old friends. Why are you buried alive?"

"Margaret, to escape temptation. My impious ire against those two had its root in the heart; that heart I must now deaden, and, Dei gratia, I shall. Shall I, a servant of Christ and of the Church, court temptation? Shall I pray daily to be led into it, and walk into it with open eyes?"

"That is good sense anyway," Margaret remarked, with a consummate affectation of candor.

"'Tis unanswerable," Clement sighed.

"We shall see. Tell me, have you escaped temptation here? Why I ask is, when I am alone, my thoughts become far more wild and foolish than in company. Nay, speak sooth; come!"

"I must confess I have been worse tempted here with evil imaginations than in the world."

"There now."

"Ay, but so were Anthony and Jerome, Macarius and Hilarion, Benedict, Bernard, and all the saints. 'Twill wear off."

"How do you know?"

"I feel sure it will."

"Guessing against knowledge. Here 'tis men who are sillier than we women. Wise in their own conceits, they refuse to see; their stomachs are too high to be taught by their eyes. A woman, if she went into a hole in a bank to escape temptation and found it there, would simply lift her farthingale and step out upon it, not even knowing how wise she was until she watched a man in like plight."

"Nay, I grant humility and a teachable spirit are the roads to wisdom; but when all is said, here I wrestle but with imagination. At Gouda, she I love as no priest or monk must love any save the angels; she will tempt a weak soul, unwilling, yet not loath to be tempted."

"Ay, that is another matter; I should tempt thee then? To what, i' God's name?"

"Who knows? The flesh is weak."

"Speak for yourself, my lad. Why, you are thinking of some other Margaret, not Margaret a Peter. Was ever my mind turned to folly and frailty? Stay, is it because you were my husband once, as these lines avouch? Think you the road to folly is beaten

To truly grasp the architecture of Margaret's second exile, one must first understand the absolute poverty of her universe at that precise moment. She existed within a microscopic empire of three, subjugated by a man she was conditioned to call "Father," yet spiritually estranged from him. He was a square-built, brutal artifact of a world she barely knew, roughly sixty years of age, though time in the cloister seemed measured only by the decay of his humanity. His dark hair fell in matted, feral clusters, and a vast beard obscured his face, rendering him less a man than a beast draped in the ruin of whatever civilization had cast him out. He possessed a terrifying latent power, yet he spent his days in a state of stagnant lethargy, only stirring to exert his dominance over the labor of the flock—collecting sea-birds or hauling faggots of wood down the ravine.

Her life was bound by the daily ritual of the church bells, ringing out across the landscape, a sonic barrier separating the sacred from the profane. Beyond this, her existence was an enforced void. She was permitted no autonomy, no heritage, and almost no language beyond the liturgy. The Father utilized silence as a tool of oppression, rationing his words to commands and curses, ensuring her mind remained as barren as the rocky crags that caged them. She became a taciturn creature, her intellect starved, left to gaze into the abyssal depths of the ocean or trace the cold geometries of the stars. She was kept as a beast of the field—valuable only for the calories her labor saved him, and perhaps to serve as a mirror for his own desperate need for mastery.

Margaret retreated to Rotterdam long ere Gerard awoke, leaving her boy behind her in the wake of a decision that would haunt her until her dying day. She dispatched the faithful, sturdy Reicht to Gouda directly, armed with a vicar's grey frock and a large felt hat, burdened with minute instructions on how to govern her new master. Then she went to Jorian Ketel; for she reasoned to herself, "he is the closest I ever met, so he is the man for me." In concert with him, she committed two mortal sly acts; yet, in my opinion, they were not virulent, though she believed them to be. If I am asked what these nameless deeds were, the answer is this: as she, who was 'but a woman,' kept them secret till her death, I, who am a man—"Verbum non amplius addam."

She deliberately kept away from the Gouda parsonage. Things that pass little noticed in the heat of argument sometimes rankle afterwards; and when she came to go over all that had passed, she was offended at Gerard thinking she could ever forget the priest in the same-time lover. "For what did he take me?" she asked herself. This raised a great shyness which she would not otherwise have felt, being downright innocent. Pride sided with modesty and whispered, "Go no more to Gouda parsonage."

She left little Gerard there to complete the conquest her maternal heart ascribed to him, not to her own eloquence and sagacity, and to anchor his father forever to humanity. But this generous stroke of policy cost her heart dear. She had never yet been parted from her boy an hour, and she felt sadly strange as well as desolate without him. After the first day it became intolerable; and what does the poor soul do, but creep at dark up to Gouda parsonage, and lurk about the premises like a thief till she saw Reicht Heynes in the kitchen alone? Then she tapped softly at the window and said, "Reicht, for pity's sake bring him out to me unbeknown." With Margaret, the person who occupied her thoughts at the time ceased to have a name, and sank to a pronoun.

Reicht soon found an excuse for taking little Gerard out, and there was a scene of mutual rapture, followed by mutual tears when mother and boy parted again. It was arranged that Reicht should take him halfway to Rotterdam every day, at a set hour, and Margaret meet them. At these meetings, after the raptures, and after mother and child had gambolled together like a young cat and her first kitten, the boy would sometimes amuse himself alone at their feet, and the two women generally seized this opportunity to talk very seriously about Luke Peterson. This began thus:

"Reicht," said Margaret, "I as good as promised him to marry Luke Peterson. 'Say you the word,' quoth I, 'and I'll wed him.'"

"Poor Luke!"

"Prithee, why poor Luke?"

"To be bandied about so, atwixt yea and nay."

"Why, Reicht, you have not ever been so simple as to cast an eye of affection on the boy, that you take his part?"

"Me?" said Reicht, with a toss of the head.

"Oh, I ask your pardon. Well, then, you can do me a good turn."

"Whisht! Whisper! That little darling is listening to every word, and eyes like saucers."

On this, both their heads would have gone under one cap. Two women plotting against one boy? Oh, you great cowardly serpents!

But when these stolen meetings had gone on for about five days, Margaret began to feel the injustice of it, and to be irritated as well as unhappy. She was crying about it when a cart came to her door, and in it, clean as a new penny, his beard close-shaved, his hands white as snow, and a little color in his pale face, sat the Vicar of Gouda in the grey frock and large felt hat she had sent him.

She ran upstairs directly, washing away all traces of her tears, and put on a cap which, being just taken out of the drawer, was cleaner, theoretically, than the one she had on, and came down to him. He seized both her hands and kissed them, and a tear fell upon them. She turned her head away at that to hide her own, which started.

"My sweet Margaret," he cried, "why is this? Why hold you aloof from your own good deed? We have been waiting for you every day, and no Margaret."

"You said things."

"What! When I was a hermit, and a donkey."

"Ay! No matter, you said things. And you had no reason."

"Forget all I said there. Who hearkens the ravings of a maniac? For I see now that in a few months more I should have been a gibbering idiot; yet no mortal could have persuaded me away but you. Oh, what an outlay of wit and goodness was yours! But it is not here I can thank and bless you as I ought. No, it is in the home you have given me, among the sheep whose shepherd you have made me; already I love them dearly; there it is I must thank 'the truest friend ever man had.' So now I say to you as erst you said to me, come to Gouda manse."

"Humph! We will see about that."

"Why, Margaret, think you I had ever kept the dear child so long, but that I made sure you would be back to him from day to day? Oh, he curls round my very heartstrings, but what is my title to him compared to thine? Confess now, thou hast had hard thoughts of me for this."

"Nay, nay, not I. Ah! Thou art thyself again; wast ever thoughtful of others. I have half a mind to go to Gouda manse, for your saying that."

"Come then, with half thy mind, 'tis worth the whole of other folk's."

"Well, I dare say I will; but there is no such mighty hurry," said she coolly (she was literally burning to go). "Tell me first how you agree with your folk."

"Why, already my poor have taken root in my heart."

"I thought as much."

"And there are such good creatures among them; simple and rough, and superstitious, but wonderfully good."

"Oh, I leave you alone for seeing a grain of good among a bushel of ill."

"Whisht! Whisht! And Margaret, two of them have been ill friends for four years, and came to the manse each to get on my blind side. But give the glory to God, I got on their bright side, and made them friends, and laugh at themselves for their folly."

"But are you in very deed their vicar? Answer me that."

"Certes; have I not been to the bishop and taken the oath, and rung the church bell, and touched the altar, the missal, and the holy cup before the church-wardens? And they have handed me the parish seal; see, here it is. Nay, 'tis a real vicar inviting a true friend to Gouda manse."

"Then my mind is at ease. Tell me oceans more."

"Well, sweet one, nearest to me of all my parish is a poor cripple that my guardian angel and his (her name thou knowest even by this turning of thy head away) hath placed beneath my roof. Sybrandt and I are that we never were till now, brothers. 'Twould gladden thee, yet sadden thee to hear how we kissed and forgave one another. He is full of thy praises, and wholly in a pious mind; he says he is happier since his trouble than e'er he was in the days of his strength.

To truly comprehend the sanctity of the final rites, one must witness them performed not merely as duty, but as a desperate anchor against the void of mortality. In the chamber of death, Gerard was never more fully a priest than when he tended the departing soul of his beloved, executing the last offices of the Church with the same mechanical precision he would have applied to the smallest parishioner. Yet, once the rituals concluded, the machinery of faith ground to a halt. He sat, stupefied by the sudden, tremendous blow, paralyzed by a grief he had not yet recognized as such. Only then, in a transport of religious fervor born of sheer necessity, did he kneel to thank Heaven for her Christian end. His singular, consuming thought became immediate extraction: to remove her from the gaze of strangers and inter her within the sacred soil of his own churchyard.

That very evening, a covered cart drawn by a single horse departed for Gouda, carrying a coffin and a broken-hearted man whose arms and chin rested upon the cold wood. The mourner's short-lived energy had been entirely expended in the arduous preparations; now, he lay crushed, clinging to the leaden box that held her remains. The driver, a silent figure of routine, walked beside the horse's head, offering no comfort, speaking only in whispers when he baited the animal, respectful of the mute agony radiating from the passenger. When they halted for the night, the landlord and the driver made a well-meaning attempt to coax the mourner away for rest and sustenance. But Gerard repulsed them, snarling like a faithful dog guarding its burden, refusing to be parted from the cold lead even as sleep claimed him. They were forced to draw a cloak over him and leave him in peace.

At noon the following day, the sorrowful cart arrived at the manse, greeted by a score of parishioners gathered with their trivial domestic troubles momentarily suspended. They had missed the parson already, sensing a shift in the spiritual atmosphere. Upon seeing the scene—their healer so utterly stricken—they raised a loud wail of grief. This collective sound pierced the lethargy of his woe, rousing him from his stupor. He looked around, recognizing the familiar faces, and tried to speak to them, crying out, "Oh, my children! My children!" but choked on the rawness of his anguish, unable to form further words.

Yet, despite all remonstrances, the next day he buried her himself. He read the service with a voice that trembled only occasionally, many tears falling upon the fresh earth of her grave. When the service ended, he remained standing like a statue amidst the silent congregation, who left the churchyard out of profound respect. He stood thus, lost in a waking dream, until the sexton—a man of common sense and limited wisdom—began to fill in the grave without due warning. At the sound of earth falling upon her, Gerard uttered a piercing scream. The sexton forbore from finishing his work. Gerard staggered, clutching his breast, and the sexton supported him, calling loudly for aid. Jorian Ketel, who had lingered nearby mourning his benefactress, ran into the churchyard. Together, the two men supported the shattered priest back to the manse. "Ah, Jorian! Good Jorian!" he gasped, his voice breaking. "Something snapped within me; I felt it, and I heard it; here, Jorian, here;" and he pressed his hand firmly against his heart.

A fortnight later, the air of the Dominican convent in the suburbs of Gouda grew heavy with the arrival of a pale, bowed specter. This stranger sought audience with Brother Ambrose, the deputy governor who held the reins of the house since the prior's recent death, awaiting the arrival of his successor. The intruder was Gerard, a man come to terminate the life he had begun in such turmoil. Though he presented himself as a novice on probation, the truth lay deeper in his weary bones: he was a failing man, acutely aware of his own dissolution, seeking only the peace of death amidst the kindness of Ambrose and the silent fellowship of brothers whose doors and hearts had always welcomed him. Yet, his old manse felt like a prison of ghosts; it was too saturated with the lingering echoes of her presence to sustain him further. Ambrose, recognizing the depth of his friend's value and the crushing weight of his sorrow, harbored a tender hope that healing might restore him to his parish. In this spirit of mercy, he assigned Gerard a comfortable cell above the gateway and strictly forbade fasting or any form of ascetic penance. But fate intervened swiftly; within mere days, the new prior arrived, bringing with him the frost of a very Tartar disposition. Initially consumed by the zeal to purge abuses and tighten the rigid chains of discipline, his nature shifted abruptly when news reached him that the vicar of Gouda had entered the order as a novice. With a sharp dismissal, he declared, "'Tis well; let him first surrender his vicarage, then remain—or depart. I will have no fat parsons in my house." The prior summoned Gerard, who complied, and the moment their eyes met across the threshold, a shock rippled through the room. "Clement!" cried one. "Jerome!" answered the other.

Jerome remained as stern and somber as his nature dictated, yet a profound softening had taken root in his regard for Gerard. Throughout his tenure in England, the absence of his younger brother had gnawed at him with a ferocity he had scarcely anticipated, leaving him to wonder constantly of Gerard's whereabouts. The tidings from Gouda initially bolstered his esteem; particularly the report that Gerard had stood firm against the Pope and the Minorites, defiantly resisting what Jerome termed the "infernal heresy of the immaculate conception." However, this admiration curdled into revulsion when a novice monk, eyes glistening with unshed tears, revealed the true cause of Gerard's wasting sickness. The image of a man dying for a woman shattered Jerome's composure, reigniting his contempt. Determined to extinguish this perceived scandal, Jerome besieged Clement's cell twice daily, deploying every ounce of his former influence and eloquence to urge the friar to abandon this unspiritual despondency. He pleaded that Gerard was robbing the Church of its greatest asset—its piety and its voice—at a moment of supreme crisis.

Gerard listened, nodding in approval of Jerome's logic, admiring the intensity of his rebuke, yet he confessed his own fragility. Visibly, he began to fade, drifting toward the land of the faithful. On one occasion, Jerome declared that he had heard Gerard's entire story and listened with a mixture of sorrow and pride. "Yet now," Jerome argued, his voice tight with disapproval, "you undo everything. This is the triumph of earthly passion. Had you simply yielded and repented, it would have been better than resisting while she lived, only to succumb utterly now, body and soul alike."

"Dear Jerome," Clement replied, his tone so sweet it drained the sting from Jerome's reproof, "perhaps you misunderstand me. There is no passion here, only a deep affection for which I shall not blush in this life, nor in the next. Consider, Jerome: does the poor dog that perishes of grief beside his master's grave commit a sin of passion? Neither do I. Passion would have saved my life but cost me my soul. She was my guardian angel; she upheld me in my duties and charities. Her countenance fortified me in the pulpit; her lips soothed me amidst ingratitude. She wove herself into every thread of goodness in my existence. After leaning upon her so long, I could not stand alone. And, dear Jerome, believe me, I am no rebel against Heaven. It is God's will to release me. When they cast the earth upon her poor coffin, something snapped within my bosom—a fracture that cannot mend. I heard it, and I felt it. From that hour, Jerome, no food I consumed held any flavor. With my eyes bandaged, I could no longer distinguish bread from flesh by taste alone."

"Holy saints!" Jerome exclaimed involuntarily.

"And from that same hour," Clement continued, his voice trembling with a serene resignation, "my deep dejection lifted, and I smiled again. I smile often. Why? I read it thus: He in whose hands lie the issues of life and death granted me that final minute the great summons. A cord of life snapped within me. He is merciful. I would have lived unhappily; but He said, 'No; enough is done, enough is suffered. Poor, feeble, loving servant, thy shortcomings are forgiven, thy sorrows hasten thine end; come thou to thy rest!' I come, Lord, I come!"

Jerome groaned, the weight of loss pressing down upon him. "The Church has ever relied upon her holy yet feeble servants," he murmured. "Now I would trade ten years of my life to save yours. But I perceive it cannot be. Die in peace."

Thus, within a few short days, Gerard lay dying in a state of such holy tranquility that more than one aged saint seemed present to gather his final words. That evening, he had seen Giles, begging him to ensure poor Jack did not starve, to oversee that Little Gerard's trustees fulfilled their obligations, and to convey kisses to his parents and dispatch Denys to his friends in Burgundy—"Poor thing," he had noted, "he will feel so strange here without his comrade." Following this, he shared a private audience with Jerome alone. What transpired between them remains obscure, yet the aftermath spoke volumes: Jerome emerged from the door with his hands clasped across his breast, his noble head bowed low, sighing heavily as he walked away.

The two monks who kept vigil until matins recounted that throughout the night, Gerard periodically erupted in pious ejaculations, praises, and thanksgivings. Once, however, they reported that he wandered, convinced he saw her walking in verdant meadows alongside other spirits clad in white, beckoning him forward. The monks smiled and waved back, though later admitted it might have been mere fancy. Just before dawn, they claimed to hear three light taps against the wall, spaced slowly and deliberately. The dying man heard them and whispered, "I come, love, I come."

It is certain that Gerard uttered these words and prepared for his departure. He summoned all the monks keeping watch. They gathered, hovering like gentle spirits around him, offering holy comfort. Some knelt in silent prayer, faces pressed to the floor; others tenderly supported his head. When one mentioned his life of self-denial and charity, Gerard silenced him. Addressing them all, he declared, "My dear brethren, take heed: he who dies here so happy holds not these new-fangled doctrines of human merit. Oh, what a wretched hour it would have been for me had I believed them! Nay, I hold, with the Apostles and their successors in the Church, the ancient Fathers, that we are justified not by our own wisdom, nor piety, nor the works wrought in holiness of heart, but by faith alone."

A profound silence fell, heavy with significance. "If you please, sweep the floor," the dying Christian commanded, his voice suddenly restored with supernatural clarity and force. Instantly, the monks obeyed, moved by awe and curiosity. "Make a great cross with wood ashes." They scattered the ashes upon the floor, forming a massive crucifix. "Now lay me down upon it, for thus shall I die." They gently lifted him from his bed and placed him upon the ash-cross.

"Shall we spread out thine arms, dear brother?" someone asked softly.

"Now God forbid! Am I worthy of that?" he replied. He lay silent, eyes raised in ecstatic vision. Presently, speaking half to them, half to himself, he murmured with a subdued, concentrated rapture, "Oh... I feel it buoyant. It lifts me, floating in the sky whence my merits had sunk me like lead."

Daybreak revealed his face cast upward in silent rapture, his hands joined in prayer, reminiscent of Margaret's. As the clock struck the hour of her death, he spoke his final word in this world: "Jesu!" And with that syllable, he fell asleep.

They laid him out for burial. Beneath his linens, they discovered a shirt woven of horsehair. "Ah!" cried the young monks, "Behold a saint!" Underneath the haircloth, they found a long, thick tress of auburn hair. Gasps filled the room, horror mingling with confusion, sparking a babel of voices—some condemning, some excusing. Into the fray stepped Jerome. Hearing the dispute, he turned to Basil, an ardent young monk crying out the loudest accusations of scandal.

"Basil," Jerome said firmly, "is she alive or dead that owns this hair?"

"How may I know, Father?" Basil stammered.

"Then, for all you know, it may be the relic of a saint?"

"Certes, it may be," Basil replied skeptically.

"You have then broken our Rule, which states: 'Put no evil construction on any act done by a brother which can be construed innocently.' Who are you to judge such a man as this was? Go to your cell and remain there for a week as penance." Jerome seized the lock of hair and carried it away. When the coffin was to be sealed, he cleared the cell and placed the tress upon the dead man's bosom. "There, Clement," he whispered to the still face. Setting himself a penance for the act, he nailed the coffin shut himself.

The following day, Gerard was interred in the Gouda churchyard. The monks followed him in procession from the convent. Jerome, clearly executing the deceased's wishes, read the funeral service. The grave was deep, lined with a lead coffin at the bottom. Poor Gerard, light as a feather due to his emaciation, was lowered and placed beside it. After the rites, Jerome addressed the crowd of parishioners who had gathered for a final farewell. Speaking of the departed's virtues, loud wails and weeping erupted, tears falling upon the coffin like rain.

When the monks returned to the refectory, Jerome assembled them and spoke thus: "We have today laid a saint in the earth. The convent will keep his trentals, but we shall feast, not fast; for our good brother is freed from the burden of the flesh; his labors are ended, and he has entered into his joyful rest. I alone shall fast and do penance; for to my shame I confess, I was unjust to him, knowing not his worth until it was too late. And you, young monks, do not be curious to inquire whether the lock he bore on his bosom was a token of pure affection or the

In obedience to a custom I detest yet lack the fortitude to defy, I pause here to gather the scattered fragments of humanity I have strewn across this narrative. Let it be understood that these minor figures hold no intrinsic claim upon my affection; they have merely served their function, convincing the reader that Gerard traversed the path from Holland to Rome among living souls rather than a procession of lifeless dolls. Eli and Catherine endured to a ripe old age, surviving so long that both Gerard and Margaret faded into the twilight of memory. Giles too was long-lived; he journeyed to the court of Bavaria and remained there at ninety, though he had metamorphosed into nothing more than brittle bone and leathery skin, his voice reduced to a hollow trumpet tone. Cornelis, unburdened by rivals and absolved long ago by a mother who clung to him with increasing desperation as her brood dispersed, waited endlessly for the deaths of his parents. Yet Catherine's sharp prophecy proved true: before she and her partner wore themselves out, this worthy man rusted away. At sixty-five, lying dying of natural causes in his mother's embrace—a hale woman of eighty-six—he had lain unconscious for a time before regaining consciousness in the very moment of his passing. Seeing her beside him, he spoke of transforming the shop and premises once they belonged solely to him. "Yes, my darling," the poor old woman cooed softly, and within another minute, he was clay. That clay was followed to the grave by every footstep he had ever waited upon.

Denys, shattered by his comrade's death, returned gladly to Burgundy, where a modest pension from the court sustained him until fate intervened, granting him an unexpected inheritance from a distant relative. For many years thereafter, he was known in his homeland as a crusty veteran, capable of recounting tales of war when inclined, yet a relentless critic of women. Jerome, repulsed by the northern laxity, retreated to Italy. With powerful patrons behind him, he ascended to become a mitred abbot by the age of seventy. He tightened the screw of discipline; his monks revered him and loathed him in equal measure. He ruled with an iron rod for a decade, and one night he died alone, for he had never discovered the key to a single heart. The Vulgate rested upon his pillow, the crucifix gripped in his hand, and upon his lips hovered something resembling a smile—not seen during his lifetime. Thus, I believe, in that terrible hour, he was not entirely solitary. *Requiescat in pace.* The Master serves many masters, and those servants possess myriad minds; occasionally, a faithful soul manifests as a surly one, just as it happens in these mortal dwellings.

The golden-haired lad, Gerard Gerardson, does not belong to fiction but to history. Other chronicles have recorded his birth in terms far different from mine. Above the tailor's house in the Brede Kirk Straet, the stones bear the inscription: *"HAEC EST PARVA DOMUS NATUS QUA MAGNUS ERASMUS,"* and scribes have penned half a dozen lives of him. Yet there remains something for them to accomplish. They have grasped no more of the *Magnum Erasmus* than any other pygmy can comprehend a giant, or a partisan a judge. First scholar and divine of his era, he was also the heaven-born dramatist of his century. Many of the finest scenes in this volume originate from his medieval pen, illuminating the pages where they appear; for the words of a genius so towering as his were not born to perish. Once their immediate impact upon mankind is fulfilled, they may seem to lie dormant; yet with every fresh shower of intelligence that Time pours upon their students, they demonstrate their immortal lineage. They revive, rising from the dust of great libraries; they bud, they flower, they bear fruit and sow seeds, generation after generation, age after age.
